# THE PATH OF THE ESTABLISHED DOCTRINES OF THE PERFECTED MASTERS <note>original: "Siddha-Siddhānta-Paddhati". This title refers to a foundational manual describing the philosophy and methodology of the Siddhas, or "perfected" beings.</note>
### AND
# OTHER WORKS OF THE <term>NĀTHA</term> YOGIS <note>The Nāthas are a mediaeval Indian lineage of yogis who emphasize physical and spiritual transformation, often credited with the development of Hatha Yoga.</note>

# FOREWORD

I have great pleasure in drawing the attention of the scholars and philosophers interested in the <term>Yoga</term> (the ancient Indian practice of physical, mental, and spiritual discipline) system of philosophy to this collection of ten works of the school of the system known as that of the <term>Nātha Yogīs</term> (Masters of the Yoga lineage) or <term>Kānphāṭā Yogīs</term> (literally "split-ear" Yogis, named for the large earrings worn through the cartilage of their ears), which the learned Dr. Kalyani Devi Malik has edited. She has procured manuscripts of these works from various locations with great diligence. To this collection, she has added a well-considered and thought-provoking thesis regarding the historical lives and dates of the primary figures associated with the origin and growth of this sect. She has also provided a thorough summary of the contents of each work, enhanced by the addition of insightful scholarly notes.

By doing so, she has performed a significant service to Indian spiritual culture. The <term>Nātha Yogīs</term> preserve the ancient <term>Haṭha-yoga</term> (the yoga of physical purification and energy balancing) tradition in an authentic form. The origins of this tradition reach back to prehistoric times, inhabited by the great <term>Siddhas</term> (perfected masters) of the <term>Māheśvara</term> sect (devotees of the Great Lord Shiva), such as the renowned grammarian <term>Pāṇini</term>. These masters left behind remarkable works for the benefit of humanity on scientific subjects—including grammar, linguistics, mathematics, medicine, and classical music—all based on intuitive knowledge gained through selfless devotion and personal sacrifice.

In the first quarter of this century, Dr. Gopinath Kaviraj published two works from this sect in the Prince of Wales Sarasvati Bhavan Text Series, though they were of secondary importance: *The Compendium of the Doctrines of Goraksa* <note>original: "Gorakṣa-siddhānta-saṁgraha"</note> and *The Compendium of the Doctrines of the Perfected Ones* <note>original: "Siddha-siddhānta-samgraha"</note>. The first of these is a 17th-century work compiled from extracts of over sixty different texts. The second is a summary of *The Manual on the Doctrines of the Perfected Ones* <note>original: "Siddha-siddhānta-paddhati"</note> by Nityanāth, who lived long after <term>Gorakṣanātha</term>. Gorakṣanātha's achievement in reviving the <term>Siddhā</term> (perfected master) tradition is as significant as that of the master Vasugupta in the revival of <term>Kāśmīr Śaivism</term> (a non-dualist philosophy from Kashmir) or the great philosopher <term>Śaṁkara</term> in the revival of <term>Advaita Vedānta</term> (the philosophy of non-duality). The former work contains extracts from the—

<summary>This foreword introduces a scholarly collection of ten texts belonging to the Nātha or Kānphāṭā Yogī tradition, edited by Dr. Kalyani Devi Malik. It highlights the historical importance of the sect in preserving ancient Haṭha-yoga and compares its foundational figures to other major Indian philosophical reformers.</summary>

<keywords>Nātha Yogīs, Kānphāṭā Yogīs, Haṭha-yoga, Gorakṣanātha, Siddha tradition, Indian philosophy, Dr. Kalyani Devi Malik, Śaṁkara, Advaita Vedānta</keywords>

--- Page 5 ---
<meta>This page continues the scholarly foreword, moving from the historical origins of the Nātha sect to a detailed bibliographic review of the specific texts included in this volume, focusing on the works attributed to the master Gorakṣnāth.</meta>

*<term>Siddha-siddhānta-paddhati</term> (The Path of the Doctrines of the Perfected Ones)* of <term>Gorakṣnāth</term> <note>Gorakshanath was a 10th-12th century master and the primary developer of the Haṭha Yoga tradition</note> himself. But the learned editress of this collection has placed before the public the full text of that work based on a copy of a manuscript at Jodhpur and added comparative notes based on the copies of two other manuscripts obtained from Hardwar and Tanjore. The language of the text is ungrammatical at places. But that is a usual feature of almost all the works of that sect as noted by Dr. Kaviraj in his Preface to the <term>GSS</term> <note>original: "Gopinath Kaviraj's Government Sanskrit Series"</note>. Such inaccuracies have not escaped the notice of Dr. Kalyani and she has wisely drawn attention to some of them by suggesting corrections for them in brackets. The authenticity of that work is vouched for not only by the fact of the authors of the previously-published works having incorporated extracts from it into their own writings, but also by the fact that Theodor Aufrecht has, in his *Catalogue of Catalogues* <note>original: "Catalogus Catalogorum," a major 19th-century bibliography of Sanskrit manuscripts</note>, mentioned it as one of the nine works ascribed to Gorakṣnātha.

Another of those works is the *<term>Yogamartaṇḍa</term> (The Sun of Yoga)*. It too forms part of this collection. These two supplement each other because whereas the *SSP* <note>Siddha-siddhānta-paddhati</note>, besides mentioning the characteristics of an <term>Avadhūtā Yogī</term> <note>a "shaken-off" ascetic who has renounced all worldly attachments and social conventions</note> who is fit to be a <term>Guru</term> (teacher), works out the full details of the <term>Piṇḍa-Brahmāṇḍa</term> equation—the concept that the human body (the microcosm) and the universe (the macrocosm) are identical—which is the very foundation of the <term>Siddhānta</term> (doctrine) of the Perfected Ones. However, it mentions only the bare outlines of the <term>Aṣṭāṅga-yoga</term> (Eight-Limbed Yoga), which is recommended as the method of operation for the actual realization of that equality and for the attainment of the <term>Siddhis</term> (supernormal powers) as the fruit of having meticulously gone through that hard discipline. In contrast, the *YM* <note>Yogamartaṇḍa</note> omits the theory of the equation but supplies the full details of the components of the said Yoga.

There is a third work in this collection, namely *<term>Amaraugha-prabodha</term> (Awakening to the Immortal Stream)*, purporting to have been composed by the same author. Although I cannot vouch for its authenticity as it is not one of the nine mentioned by Aufrecht and as I do not remember to have seen extracts made from it in any other work, I can recommend its study for the knowledge of the inter-relation between <term>Mantra-yoga</term> (yoga of sacred sound), <term>Laya-yoga</term> (yoga of dissolution), <term>Hatha-yoga</term> (yoga of physical force), and <term>Rāja-yoga</term> (the "Royal Yoga" of meditation), the methods to be followed in the practice of each of them, and the indispensability of the last to those who are anxious to see concrete results flowing from the practice of any kind of the first three varieties, etc.

Besides the above three there is in this collection a fourth work in Sanskrit named *<term>Yogaviṣaya</term> (On the Subject of Yoga)*. In verse 7 thereof its...

<summary>This page evaluates the authenticity and philosophical content of four key Sanskrit texts attributed to Gorakṣnāth, specifically focusing on the relationship between the body and the universe and the various branches of Yoga.</summary>
<keywords>Gorakṣnāth, Siddha-siddhānta-paddhati, Yogamartaṇḍa, Microcosm-Macrocosm, Eight-Limbed Yoga, Amaraugha-prabodha, Haṭha-yoga, Rāja-yoga, manuscript studies</keywords>

--- Page 6 ---
<meta>This page continues the bibliographic review of texts within the collection. After discussing Gorakṣnāth’s works, the author now examines a treatise attributed to Mīnanātha (also known as Matsyendranātha) and details its specific yogic teachings regarding the subtle body and the arousal of Kuṇḍalinī.</meta>

The author has given his name as “Mīnanātha, son of Umāśaṁkara,” while the colophon <note>a concluding statement in a manuscript containing details about its production</note>, probably added by a scribe, mentions it as a work, whose name has the initial letters “Bhaktisaṁ,” <note>likely referring to the *Bhaktisaṁyoga*</note> “composed by Matsyendranātha.” The learned editress <note>Dr. Kalyani Mallik</note> being of the view that the two were identical individuals has put it down as a work of “Mīnanāth, otherwise known as <note>original: "alias"</note> Matsyendranāth,” but I have my reasons for doubting that identity. I cannot mention them here at length just as I cannot enter into a discussion of the questions as to the connection between the doctrine of the <term>Siddhas</term> <note>the "Perfected Ones," a group of medieval yoga masters</note> and those of the <term>Mahāyāna Buddhists</term> and the <term>Tāntrikas</term>, on which she has expressed her own views, for fear of exceeding the legitimate limit of a Foreword. Apart from that this work is not mentioned by <term>Aufrecht</term> <note>Theodor Aufrecht, author of the *Catalogus Catalogorum*, a massive catalog of Sanskrit manuscripts</note> under the name “Mīnanātha.” That however is no reason for considering it unauthoritative because there is the above-mentioned internal evidence and the same in verse 5 also. 

As for its value as an additional source of information regarding the <term>Siddhānta</term> <note>the established doctrine or philosophical conclusion</note> of the Siddhas, it is a short treatise containing 33 couplets purporting to expound the nature of the “<term>Para-tattva</term>” <note>the Supreme Reality or Essential Principle</note> leading to the knowledge of the “<term>Kula</term>” <note>the power of the manifested world or the Goddess Shakti</note> and the “<term>Akula</term>” <note>the unmanifested reality or the god Shiva</note> and towards that end mentions the 6 “<term>Kaula Cakras</term>” <note>energy centers within the subtle body belonging to the Kaula tradition</note> which must be pierced in order to reach the “<term>Nirañjana</term>” <note>the "Stainless" or "Untainted" aspect of the divine</note> who is beyond them and the principal veins <note>referring to the *nāḍīs* or energy channels</note> through which the vital air passes, refers to the mystic letters imprinted in 6 of the vital parts commencing from the <term>Ādhāra</term> <note>the root energy center at the base of the spine</note> and ending with the <term>Ājñā</term> <note>the "command" center or third eye</note>, the 10 <term>Vāyus</term> <note>the "vital winds" or internal breaths governing bodily functions</note>, the 3 <term>Granthis</term> <note>the "knots" or psychic blocks in the subtle body</note>, the special process by which the <term>Kuṇḍalinī</term> <note>the spiritual energy often depicted as a coiled serpent</note> having 8 coils, each of which has a specific name given to it in this treatise only, can be aroused and be made to pierce the 6 <term>Cakras</term>, as indications whereof the lamp of knowledge blazes forth, a sound issuing from the <term>Brahmasthāna</term> <note>the "Place of Brahma," the highest energy center at the crown of the head</note> is heard and the <term>Śaṁkhinī Nāḍī</term> <note>a specific energy channel associated with the flow of nectar</note> showers nectar from the moon in the forehead, and recommends its utilisation in bathing the “<term>Cidrūpa Īśvara</term>” <note>the Lord in the form of pure Consciousness</note> before offering the mind-flower to Him. 

As to Him, he further explains that He, of the nature of the self or of pure knowledge can be visualised in the form of an all-pervading body, as the source of the sound “<term>Haṁsa</term>” <note>a mantra representing the breath and the individual soul</note>, or in that of any of the three <term>Lingas</term>, the symbols of <term>Śiva</term>, located in the lower, middle and upper parts of the body, and winds up the topic by saying that the said process is effective in eradicating...

<summary>This page evaluates a short treatise by Mīnanātha (Matsyendranātha), debating its authorship and detailing its unique descriptions of the subtle body, including the specific names for the eight coils of the Kuṇḍalinī. The text focuses on the internal yogic process of piercing the cakras to reach the supreme consciousness (Cidrūpa Īśvara).</summary>
<keywords>Mīnanātha, Matsyendranātha, Siddha doctrine, Kaula Cakras, Kuṇḍalinī coils, Para-tattva, Cidrūpa Īśvara, Śaṁkhinī Nāḍī, internal yoga, Sanskrit bibliography</keywords>

# VIII

curing all diseases if the involved movements of <term>Vāta</term> <note>the "wind" or kinetic energy principle in Ayurvedic medicine</note> and <term>Pitta</term> <note>the metabolic or "fire" principle in Ayurvedic medicine</note> are free from 18 faults. It thus contains something new which is not found in the previous works.

All the other 6 works in this collection are in either Hindi or in its source, Eastern Rajasthani. The first of them, which is in prose, purports to be the <term>Gorakṣa Upaniṣad</term> <note>a sacred text named after the sage Gorakhnath</note>, which is however probably a Hindi rendering of the Sanskrit work of that name mentioned in <term>Aufrecht's Catalogue</term> <note>referring to the *Catalogus Catalogorum*, a massive 19th-century catalog of Sanskrit manuscripts by Theodor Aufrecht</note> and utilised in the <term>GSS</term> <note>likely the *Gorakṣa Siddhānta Saṁgraha*, a compendium of the sect's doctrines</note> for supporting some parts of the <term>Siddhānta</term> <note>established doctrine or philosophical conclusion</note> expounded therein. The remaining 5 works are only popular songs showing that physical renunciation <note>giving up worldly life to become an ascetic</note> was considered essential by the votaries <note>devoted followers</note> of this sect in the period between 801 to 1500 A.D., to which the language thereof seems to belong.

I regret to have noticed some misprints in the said Sanskrit works. I understand that they have remained uncorrected because Dr. Kalyani <note>Dr. Kalyani Mallik, the editor of the volume</note> was required to entrust the work of proof-correction to another person on account of her illness and am assured that a list of corrections will be inserted before the copies of the book are bound up. Lastly, I should not fail to refer to the enterprise of Sri D. K. Gondhalekar, whose Poona Oriental Book House undertook to publish this work and make its copies available to those interested in the literature of the <term>Nātha Yogīs</term> <note>a medieval religious movement in India that combined aspects of Buddhism, Shaivism, and Yoga</note> at his own cost. As much credit is due to him for doing so as to the learned editress who made the collection and prepared its press-copy.

->P. C. DIVANJI.<-

Bombay. Dated 14th August, 1953.

# PREFACE

The present book is a collection from various regions of India of previously unpublished manuscripts concerning <term>Nāthism</term> <note>a medieval Indian religious and philosophical movement focused on Hatha Yoga and spiritual liberation, founded by the semi-legendary sage Gorakhnath</note>, which likely would have had little opportunity to be edited or published otherwise. I am now able to present these to the learned scholars who take an interest in this subject. I have also provided an introduction in the hope of clarifying certain facts.

Dr. B. B. Dutt, M.A., Ph.D. of <term>Calcutta University</term> <note>now known as the University of Calcutta, a major center for Indological studies</note>, has been very helpful in reviewing the manuscripts and their printing proofs. I owe him my special thanks.

I offer my sincere gratitude to my publishers, the <term>Poona Oriental Book House</term> <note>a prominent 20th-century publishing house in Pune specializing in Sanskrit and Indology</note> and its proprietor, Sree D. K. Gondhalekar; without their assistance, it would not have been possible to see this book in print. I hereby thank everyone who has in any way encouraged or assisted me in my work, especially Rao Bahadur P. C. Divanji, who contributed the <term>Foreword</term> <note>the introductory section preceding this preface</note>, compiled the list of corrections and abbreviations, and took great pains to help me in every way possible.

Any suggestions for the improvement of this work will be received with thanks.

KALYANI MALLIK

Calcutta. Dated 13th December, 1953.

<summary>This preface by the editor, Kalyani Mallik, introduces a collection of previously unpublished manuscripts on the Nath yogic tradition and acknowledges the scholars and publishers who made the 1953 publication possible.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the errata section from page XIII. While the previous page focused on corrections for the editor’s Introduction, this page begins the corrections for the "Original Texts"—the primary Sanskrit source material included in this edition. It specifically addresses the "Siddha-Siddhanta-Paddhati," a foundational manual of the Nātha tradition.</meta>

## ORIGINAL TEXTS :—

The learned editress has at page 27 of her Introduction noted that the texts as printed are “full of incorrect readings” and that “The language is not strictly grammatical.” Her explanation is that “this was apparently done on purpose, as it was easily understood by all. The <term>Sant poets<gloss>a group of influential saint-poets from the Bhakti movement who favored vernacular languages over formal Sanskrit</gloss></term> also followed this policy of preaching in the popular language, which was in their case the Hindi.” In view of the fact that some of the texts of the <term>Nātha cult<gloss>a medieval yoga tradition founded by Matsyendranath and Gorakhnath, focusing on Hatha Yoga and spiritual liberation</gloss></term> edited in the <term>Sarasvati Bhavan Texts Series<gloss>a renowned collection of scholarly editions of Sanskrit and Prakrit works published in Banaras</gloss></term> of Banaras are also similar, this explanation is plausible. At the same time, it must be admitted that some of the errors and irregularities may have been due to the carelessness or incompetence of the <term>scribes<gloss>copyists who manually transcribed manuscripts before the age of printing</gloss></term> who may have transcribed the previous manuscripts and that some divergences of the print from the press-copy may have remained uncorrected till the end. Whatever the cause or causes, the following corrections are suggested for consideration by the class of learned readers, for whose benefit this edition can be deemed to have been undertaken, in order that the texts may be able to convey to them intelligible notions about the principles of the Nātha cult.

### 1. THE SIDDHA-SIDDHANTA-PADDHATI <note>original: "Siddhasiddhāntapaddhatiḥ"; a core text of the Nātha tradition attributed to Gorakhnath, detailing the relationship between the individual body and the cosmic body</note>

<vocab>
- **Correction sheet** (śuddhapattram): A list of errata to fix errors made during the copying or printing of a manuscript.
- **Genitals** (meḍhra): Specifically referring to the male organ in technical descriptions of energy centers.
- **Vital breath** (prāṇā): The life force that moves through the body's channels.
- **Seed/Drop** (bindu): A vital fluid or energy point in the head, often equated with nectar or reproductive essence.
- **Root-lock** (mūlabandha): A yogic posture involving the contraction of the perineum.
- **Sky-moving seal** (khecharī): An advanced mudra where the tongue is turned back into the nasal cavity to catch the "nectar" of immortality.
- **Forceful Yoga** (haṭhayoga): The physical system of yoga focused on balancing the body's internal energies.
</vocab>

The corrections focus on key technical terms related to the subtle body, including breath channels (prana), the vital essence (bindu), and specific seals (mudras) like Khechari.</summary>

| ” | 17 | leading the breath there <note>original: *prāṇaṃ tacca vinīya*</note> | dissolving the breaths there <note>original: *prāṇāṃstatra vilīyya*</note> |
| ” | 18 | of those <note>original: *yeṣāṃ*</note> | of them <note>original: *teṣāṃ*</note> |
| ” | 19-22 | 151.2, 152.1-2, 153.1 | These lines belong to a single verse regarding the Etheric Concentration. <note>original: *nabho-dhāraṇā*; <term>Nabhas</term> or <term>Akasha</term> refers to the sky or ether element.</note> |

# INTRODUCTION

## A Short Account of the Nātha Yogīs

The Nātha Yogīs <note>original: *Nātha Yogī*; literally "Masters of Yoga." This refers to a specific medieval order of ascetics.</note> have played a very important role in the history of mediaeval Indian mysticism. These Yogīs worshipped God as "Nātha" or the Supreme Master <term>Nātha: a Sanskrit title meaning "Lord" or "Protector," often identifying the Supreme Being with the first teacher, Shiva.</term>, who according to their faith, transcends not only the finite, but the infinite as well. The worshippers of Nātha were adepts in the Yogic cult <note>Here, "cult" refers to a specific system of religious practice, particularly Hatha Yoga, focusing on physical and spiritual mastery.</note>, which was supposed to give them supernatural powers. Among them, Sree Guru Matsyendranāth <note>original: *Matsyendranātha*; "Lord of the Indra of Fish." He is the legendary first human teacher of the tradition.</note> and his most able disciple Sree Gorakhnāth or Gorakṣanāth <note>original: *Gorakṣanātha*; "Protector of the Senses" or "Lord of the Cows." He is the systematizer of the Hatha Yoga path.</note> have been known throughout India for their marvellous performances of miracles. Legends of the superhuman powers of the Nātha gurus <term>Guru: a spiritual teacher or guide who leads a disciple from darkness to light.</term> abound in Indian Literature — Sanskrit, Hindi, Marathi, Gujrati, Oriya, Nepalese and extensively in Bengali. Matsyendranāth, the foremost of the Nātha gurus was born in Bengal and some scholars mention that Gorakṣanāth too hailed from Eastern Bengal. Thus the Nātha gurus must have profoundly influenced the religious life of the people of Bengal in the mediaeval period and their cult naturally found expression in Bengali literature.

Besides popular legends and ballads, the contributions to Nātha philosophical texts, both in prose and poetry, by the Nātha gurus themselves are by no means negligible. Luipāda or Minanāth <note>original: *Luipāda* and *Mīnanātha*; alternate names for Matsyendranāth common in the Buddhist and Sahajiya traditions.</note>, which were other names by which Matsyendranāth was known, was one of the first verse writers in Bengali in the tenth century, and Gorakhnāth was probably the first prose writer in Hindi. Other important works by these two gurus are found in Sanskrit.

The Nātha gurus taught their doctrines to the rich and the poor alike. Kings have given up their thrones to preach the doctrines of the Nātha sect. The renunciation of king Gopīchandra <note>A legendary king of Bengal who gave up his kingdom at his mother's urging to seek spiritual liberation.</note> of Bengal, in the eleventh century, created a sensation all over India, which even at this distance of time continues to be echoed in poems and dramas, and in popular ballads sung by minstrels amongst rural folk. King Bhartṛhari <note>A celebrated king and poet of Ujjain known for his "Shatakas" (centuries of verses) on worldly life and renunciation.</note> of Ujjain in Western India also became a Yogi. Bhartṛhari’s renunciation of wealth and enjoyment to take the vow is associated with the same popular pathos as that of—

<summary>This introduction outlines the historical and geographical spread of the Nātha Yogī tradition, highlighting key figures like Matsyendranāth and Gorakhnāth and their influence on Indian vernacular literature.</summary>
<keywords>Nātha Yogīs, Matsyendranāth, Gorakhnāth, medieval mysticism, Bengal, Hatha Yoga, Luipāda, King Gopīchandra, King Bhartṛhari, religious literature</keywords>

--- Page 24 ---
<meta>This page continues the introduction to the Nātha tradition, focusing on the historical and legendary biographies of its most famous practitioners and their influence on later Indian mysticism.</meta>

king Gopichandra. In Western India songs of *Bhartṛhari* <note>Bhartṛhari was a legendary king and philosopher-poet who renounced his throne for the life of a yogi.</note> are as widely sung as those of *Gopīchandra* in Bengal.

*Bhartṛhari* was initiated by *Jālandhari* <note>Also known as Jalandharnath, one of the nine legendary masters (Naths) of the tradition.</note>, who according to the tradition of the *Yogīs*, was the prince of *Hastināpur* in Northern India. The same guru *Jālandhari* is known in Bengal as *Hādipā*, the menial and the miracle worker in the capital of *Maynāmati*, who was the mother of *Gopīchandra*. Later on *Hadipā* <note>The name Hādipā suggests a connection to a lower social class, illustrating the tradition’s tendency to challenge caste hierarchies.</note>, became also the guru of king *Gopīchandra*. *Caurangīnāth*, another *Nātha Yogī*, initiated by *Matsyendranāth*, was the son of king *Devapāla* of the *Pāla* Dynasty of Bengal.

That *Gorakhnāth* and other *Nātha* Gurus were not mythical persons can be proved from *Kabīr’s* verses. *Kabīr* <note>Kabīr (c. 1440–1518) was a famous mystic poet and saint whose work influenced the Bhakti movement.</note> was a well-known *Sant* poet <term>Sant: Derived from the Sanskrit word for "truth," a Sant is a mystic who has realized the ultimate reality through internal devotion.</term>, philosopher and preacher of the sixteenth century. He highly praised *Sree Gorakhnāth*, *Bhartṛhari* and *Gopīchand*, “United with the Universal Mind they revel”, said he. *Kabīr*, *Kamāl* and *Dādū* among the *Sant* poets were known as *Nirgunīs* <term>Nirgunī: A worshiper of the "Nirguṇa" or formless aspect of God, which exists without qualities or physical attributes.</term> as they worshipped the *nirguna* or the supreme aspect of God which is beyond the pale of attributes. But they are indebted to the *Nātha Yogīs* for certain practices of *Yoga*, e. g., *Śabda Yoga* <term>Śabda Yoga: The spiritual practice of focusing on the internal divine sound or vibration.</term>, *Shaḍ-chakras* <term>Shaḍ-chakras: The "six wheels" or energy centers within the human body believed to be awakened through yoga.</term> etc., and they have avowed their indebtedness to the *Yogīs*. The terminology used by both these sects as found in Hindi poetry, is remarkably alike. But the *Nātha* cult, like many other cults in India, lost its popularity in time, and is now confined to a small esoteric group of worshippers. While it is found that verses by the *Sant* poets are published by different compilers, we rarely find the verses and books written by the *Nātha Yogīs* being published. Scholars like Grierson, Levi, Tucci, Grunwedel, Briggs etc. have studied the legendary and partly the historical aspects of the *Nātha* School but not much of their writings. Shastri, Kaviraj, Bagchi, Sahidullah, Singh and some other scholars have tried to secure MSS. <note>MSS.: An abbreviation for manuscripts.</note> and study the underlying philosophy. The language used in these MSS. is often a mixture of languages, hence difficult to comprehend. Often the authorship of the same book is contested, therefore, it is difficult to ascertain the real author. But numerous books and MSS., exclusively on the *Nātha* cult, are found in well-known libraries and in possession of some *Nātha Yogīs* who are loathe to part with

<summary>This page explores the historical reality of the Nātha gurus, linking them to royal dynasties and tracing their profound influence on later Sant poets like Kabīr. It also notes the challenges scholars face in studying the tradition due to the scarcity and linguistic complexity of surviving manuscripts.</summary>
<keywords>Nātha Yogīs, Bhartṛhari, Gopīchandra, Kabīr, Nirguṇa, Śabda Yoga, Shaḍ-chakras, Pāla Dynasty, medieval mysticism, manuscript study</keywords>

--- Page 25 ---
<meta>This page continues the introduction to the Nātha tradition, transitioning from the historical evidence of the sect found in the poetry of Kabīr and the Sant poets to the specific legends and lineages of the school’s most famous figures, such as Gorakhnāth and Gopichandra.</meta>

their treasures. In this book, I am publishing for the first time a set of manuscript collections belonging to the Nātha Yogīs <note>The Nātha Yogīs are a medieval Indian ascetic group famous for developing Hatha Yoga and seeking physical immortality through spiritual practice.</note> which I secured from both Northern and Southern India. In my first book on this subject, *The History, Philosophical Doctrine and Practice of the Nātha School (of Yogīs)*—which was written in Bengali and published by Calcutta University in 1950—I provided a detailed account of these Yogīs and their practices, along with their fundamental philosophy.

### The Legends

Legends relating to the various *gurus* (preceptors) and their *shisyas* (disciples) are often contradictory, except in the cases of Sree Matsyendra and Sree Gorakhnāth. It is unanimously accepted by the various traditions and *paramparas* <term>Parampara: A Sanskrit term for an uninterrupted row or lineage, referring to the chain of spiritual instruction from a teacher to a student.</term>—that is, the lists of teachers and their disciples—that Gorakhnāth learned Yoga from his teacher Sree Matsyendranāth, who in turn learned it from Ādināth or the god Śiva <note>Ādināth means "The First Lord," the primordial source of yoga, often identified as the deity Śiva.</note>. The followers of Gorakhnāth are known as *Gorakhnāthis*, and also as *Kānphātās* (from *kān* meaning ears and *phatā* meaning slit), because they wear huge earrings by slitting the cartilage of both ears by about three-quarters of an inch, in imitation of Lord Śiva. These *kānphātā yogīs* are found even to the present day in different regions of India, and their places of pilgrimage are scattered all over the country.

Of Gorakhnāth’s disciples, King Jālandharnāth of Northern India and Queen Maynāmati of Comilla (located in East Bengal, now Bangladesh <note>The original text refers to the region as Pakistan, reflecting the geopolitical boundaries at the time of the book's 1950s context.</note>) have become famous in the ballads sung in honor of the Queen’s only son, King Gopichandra. Gopichandra’s renunciation of his throne is a familiar subject upon which ballads and dramas have been composed in the Bengali, Oriya, Nepalese, and Hindi languages. Gopichandra was the ruler of Bengal in the eleventh century. His mother, Maynāmati, was a disciple of Saint Gorakhnāth. Because Gopichandra’s span of life was destined to be short, Gorakhnāth ordered Maynāmati to compel her only son to become a Yogī and to accept Hādipā—a man of low caste and a scavenger—as his teacher. Hādipā was none other than Jālandharnāth, the disciple of Gorakhnāth, in disguise. As Maynāmati and Hādipā were both adepts at performing miracles, Gopichandra suspected some foul play at first and resented the idea of becoming Hādipā’s disciple. The two

<summary>This page outlines the legendary lineage of the Nātha Yogīs, starting from the deity Śiva down to Gorakhnāth, and introduces the famous story of King Gopichandra’s forced renunciation under the guidance of his mother and the disguised saint Hādipā.</summary>
<keywords>Nātha Yogīs, Gorakhnāth, Matsyendranāth, Kānphātā, King Gopichandra, Queen Maynāmati, Jālandharnāth, Hādipā, lineage, Hatha Yoga, Bengal history</keywords>

--- Page 26 ---
<meta>This page continues the narrative of the Nātha lineage, moving from the general history of the sect to the specific, miraculous legends of King Gopīchandra and the origins of the Great Knowledge passed down from the god Śiva.</meta>

queens Adunā and Padunā with the help of Gopīchandra himself, put several tests to the queen mother. She was made to walk on fire and on a bridge of hairs. She was drowned in water and given poison to swallow. By uttering the name of her Guru <note>A spiritual teacher or preceptor.</note>, queen Maynāmatī passed all ordeals and convinced him of the great powers of Yoga. To cut a long story short, Rājā <note>original: "Rājā" — King</note> Gopīchandra renounced his throne and accepted Hāḍipā as his guru. As a Yogī <term>Yogī: A practitioner of Yoga, typically one who has renounced worldly life to seek spiritual liberation.</term>, Gopīchandra suffered untold misery. The heart-rending tale of his renunciation became a popular theme, and ballad-singers still earn their living by singing ‘*Songs of Gopīchandra*’ <note>original: "Gopīchandrer Gān"</note> in certain parts of Bengal. Gopīchandra is known in a legend of Sind as Pir Patāo.

Hāḍipā, the performer of miracles, could order the coconuts to come down to him, and when he had devoured the kernel and drunk the water, could order the outer skins to return to the trees! The dead revived if he kicked them. Mother Earth brought a chair for him to rest and the son of Yama, the god of death, held an umbrella over him, if he went in the sun. The sun and the moon were his ear-rings, in other words, he was the perfect Yogī who had the control over *Prāna* and *Apāna* <note>original: "Prāna and Apāna" — The vital breaths; Prāna is the inward-moving breath and Apāna is the outward-moving breath.</note> or the vital airs. If Hāḍipā was such a great Yogī, what made him clean the streets of Gopīchandra’s capital? Legends show that he had coveted Mahādevī <note>The Great Goddess, the wife of Śiva.</note> (the wife of Śiva) who in the disguise of a beautiful woman was testing the characters of the Yogīs. It was by her curse that the Yogī worked as a Hāḍi <term>Hāḍi: Historically a member of a community assigned menial tasks; here it signifies the Yogī's humble service as a street cleaner.</term> or menial in the state of the beautiful Queen Maynāmatī and her son Gopīchandra.

In the same ballad, we find that Gorakhnāth was flying through mid-air by his yogic powers in search of a perfectly innocent child, to whom he could bequeath his gift of *Supreme Knowledge* <note>original: "Mahājñāna"</note> or supreme knowledge by which one can be free from fear of death and on finding Maynāmatī, initiated her. Sree Gorakhnāth had obtained this knowledge from his guru Matsyendranāth, who in turn had obtained it from Lord Śiva. While Śiva was expounding the theory to his consort Gaurī <note>Another name for the Goddess Parvati, meaning "The Fair One."</note> on the sea-shore, Gaurī had fallen asleep and Matsyendranāth in the form of a fish in the sea overheard Śiva. When discovered by Śiva he was initiated and named ‘Matsyendranāth’ i. e., the king of fishes. But at the same time he was cursed by Śiva’s consort that he would

<summary>This page recounts the legendary trials of Queen Maynāmatī and the renunciation of King Gopīchandra, while also explaining the supernatural lineage of the Nātha gurus starting from the god Śiva.</summary>
<keywords>Gopīchandra, Maynāmatī, Hāḍipā, Gorakhnāth, Matsyendranāth, Mahājñāna, Yoga miracles, Bengal folklore, Śiva, vital airs</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the exploration of the Nātha lineage legends, transitioning from the miracles of Hāḍipā to the famous story of how Gorakhnāth rescued his teacher, Matsyendranāth, from worldly distractions and the loss of his spiritual power.</meta>

for a time being, forget the knowledge thus obtained by deception. In the Bengali story of *The Victory of Gorakh* <note>original: "Gorakṣa Vijaya"</note> or *The Awakening of the Fish* <note>original: "Mīnachetan"</note> it is related how the great guru <note>A spiritual teacher or preceptor.</note> Matsyendranāth has temporarily forgotten this knowledge and has become immersed in love of the queens of Kadali in Assam, who are proficient in magic. There are only women in this state, sixteen hundred of them, all able to bewitch men who happened to come their way. This belief is still held in certain parts of Assam! Gorakhnāth, however, on receipt of this sad news of his guru’s downfall goes forth in the disguise of a woman, delivers him and changes the sixteen hundred women of Kadali to bats, where to this day they can be found in a cave. This rather farfetched story was invented in all probability to depict the greater powers of Gorakhnāth over his guru. Gorakh is said to have attained superhuman powers by reason of his perfection in Yogic practices. But at the same time Gorakh delivers his guru by uttering ‘Victory to Matsyendra’ <note>original: "Jai Matsyendra"</note> thus showing reverence to the guru though he may have fallen temporarily. This is the subject matter of the legend *The Victory of Gorakh* which means ‘the conquest of Gorakh’, or *The Awakening of the Fish*, which means ‘the awakening of Mīna’ (*Mina = Matsya = fish*). The books *The Victory of Gorakh* and *The Awakening of the Fish* are identical but published from different places. Both the works are in Bengali.

In the *Determining the Knowledge of the Kaula* <note>original: "Kaula-jñāna-nirṇaya"</note> in Sanskrit, edited by Bagchi, we find that the Lord Śiva had himself appeared on the earth, in the disguise of a fisherman as Matsyendra, to preach the doctrines of the Kaula sect <term>Kaula: An ancient Tantric tradition focused on the realization of the absolute through the worship of the Goddess and the integration of worldly and spiritual life.</term>. The Nātha Yogīs <term>Nātha Yogīs: A medieval religious movement in India that combined aspects of Buddhism, Shaivism, and Yoga, emphasizing the attainment of spiritual power and physical immortality.</term> were at one time all known as Kaulas, a special branch of the Tāntriks <term>Tāntriks: Practitioners of Tantra, a tradition of esoteric rituals and meditations used to channel divine energy.</term>, who were worshippers of the Goddess. Matsyendra initiated Gorakhnāth, though there is no mention of Gorakhnāth in the *Determining the Knowledge of the Kaula*.

Guru Gorakhnāth initiated Jālandharnāth. In a drama discovered in Nepāl on Gopīchandra’s renunciation, we find Jālandharnāth telling Gopīchandra that he was the king of Jālandhar and had given up his throne and seven hundred queens to become a yogī. Jālandhar later on became Gopīchandra’s guru.

Bhartṛhari, the king of Ujjain, became the disciple of Gorakhnāth. In some legends we find the mention of

<meta>This page continues the examination of the legendary figures within the Nātha tradition, focusing on the lineage connections between the Pāla Dynasty of Bengal, the transition of Buddhism into the Dharma cult, and the later Marathi tradition of the poet-saint Jñāneśvar.</meta>

Bhartṛhari <note>A legendary king and poet who renounced his throne to become a yogi.</note> is described as the brother of Queen Maynāmati and the uncle of Gopichandra.

Caurangīnāth, another disciple of Matsyendranāth, belonged to the Pāla Dynasty of Bengal. Caurangīnāth’s stepmother had his hands and feet cut off and his body thrown into a forest. Here he was discovered by Matsyendranāth, initiated by him, and restored to his former self after twelve years of self-discipline through <term>Yoga</term> <note>A system of physical and spiritual practices aimed at liberation.</note>. This story is narrated in the form of a drama titled *The Full Moon* <note>original: "Pūrnachandra"</note>, written in Bengali by the well-known late dramatist Girish Chandra De. Caurangīnāth is believed to be the son of Devapāl, and Queen Maynāmati is said to be the sister of Devapāl. This same Maynāmati encouraged Rāmāi Paṇḍit in the worship of <term>Dharma</term> <note>A deity representing cosmic law, part of a "Dharma Cult" that emerged from a blend of Buddhism and local traditions.</note>, a later form of Buddhism adopted at the time of conflict with the Muslims. Buddhist contacts with the Nātha cult are numerous. In Bengal, the figures Hāḍipā, Gorakh, and Matsyendra are claimed as Buddhist Saints, and they are supposed to be related to the Dharma movement. One of the Yogi castes of Bengal is still known as the *Yogis of the Dharma Clock* <note>original: "Dharmaghorī Yogī"</note>. In Nepal, Matsyendranāth is identified with the <term>Lord of Compassion</term> <note>original: "Avalokiteśvara"</note>, the fourth divine <term>Bodhisattva</term> <note>A being who seeks enlightenment for the sake of all living things.</note>, and his annual festival there is connected with an ancient Buddhist image. In a hymn composed in Bengali in honor of the god <term>Dharma</term>—who is of a non-Hindu origin—we find the meeting of the Saints Hāḍipā, Kānupā, Gorakhnāth, and Caurangi described. Rāmāi Paṇḍit is the chief priest of this <term>Dharma</term> worship and the author of its tenets known as *The Ancient Chronicle of the Void* <note>original: "Sunya-Purāna"</note>.

From the Marāṭhi tradition, we know that there was an old connection between Gorakhnāth and Jñāneśvar’s grandfather, Govindpanth. Jñāneśvar was the famous commentator of the <term>Song of the Lord</term> <note>original: "Gita"</note>, and his commentary is known as the *Wisdom of Jñāneśvar* <note>original: "Jñāneśvari"</note>; he was initiated into the Nātha cult by his elder brother <term>Lord of Renunciation</term> <note>original: "Nivṛttīnāth"</note>. Jñāneśvar was born in 1290. Bhave, in his work *The Eloquence of Maharashtra* <note>original: "Mahārāstra Sāraswat"</note>, mentions that the Nātha sect was well-known throughout the region of Maharashtra in the twelfth century. Bhave also repeats the tradition of Govindpanth’s connection with Gorakhnāth and says that Govindpanth’s father, who worked under Jaitrapāl of Bira in 1207, had his mind turned to religion by the Master <note>original: "Guru"</note> Gorakhnāth (*Volume I, page 30*).

<keywords>Caurangīnāth, Matsyendranāth, Maynāmati, Dharma cult, Buddhism, Jñāneśvar, Maharashtra, Gorakhnāth, Pāla Dynasty, Sunya-Purāna</keywords>

--- Page 29 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of the historical and legendary lineage of the Nātha yogis, moving from their connections with the Pāla Dynasty and the "Dharma Cult" mentioned on the previous page to their broader geographic spread and philosophical foundations in India and Nepal.</meta>

These legends and traditions help us to give the <term>Nātha</term> <note>A medieval Hindu tradition of "Masters" or "Lords" who practiced yoga and tantra to achieve immortality and spiritual liberation.</note> gurus some definite place in history. The stories of Gorakhnāth, Matsyendranāth, and Gopīchand had been current in Bengal and many other parts of India before the time of the conquest of Bengal by the Muslims in the thirteenth century. Later on, the songs of Mīnanāth (another name for Matsyendra) and Gorakhnāth became popular among the Muslims of East Bengal. In the <term>Vaisnava</term> <note>Relating to the worship of the god Vishnu.</note> love lyrics of Bengal, occasional references are found of the Gorakh-Yogī. In Nepal, it is believed that the <term>Lord of Compassion</term> <note>original: "Avalokiteśvara"</note> came to Nepal at the command of Buddha from somewhere outside Nepal. He came in the guise of a <term>Śaivite</term> <note>A worshiper of the god Shiva.</note> priest and is identified with Matsyendra. These Yogis are still found in Nepal, Bihar, Bengal, Uttar Pradesh, Gujarat, Maharashtra, and the Northern Deccan.

### The Origin of the Sect

The Nātha Yogis, who were worshippers of Lord Śiva, originated in Northern India as an expression of the revival of Hinduism during the second millennium AD. After the decline of Buddhism, <term>Śaktaism</term> <note>The tradition focusing on the worship of the Goddess or Divine Mother (Shakti).</note> and Vaisnavism, in which the personal element was predominant, the Nātha Cult flourished side by side with Islam. Union with Śiva was preached by Śankarācharya at an earlier period (788-850 AD, the dates of Śankara), but this impersonal ideal of *Non-Dualism* <note>original: "Advaitavāda"; the philosophical doctrine that the true self is identical with the ultimate reality.</note> had died out. The Nātha Yogis helped in reviving this ideal. To them, Śiva was the permanent principle, while the phenomenal world was attributed to <term>Śhakti</term> <note>The ever-active, feminine principle of divine energy.</note>, the ever-active principle.

On the above principle, the Nātha gurus preached their doctrines; but as to the origin of the Nātha Sect, we are still uncertain. A mass of legends have grown round the hoary name of Gorakhnāth, who is undoubtedly the greatest of the Nātha Yogis, but his birthplace and family history are still shrouded in mystery. Indian Yogis never reveal their past; this is an age-old custom. Except in the case of kings who have forsaken their thrones to become Yogis, we can never be certain of their ancestry. Gorakhnāth is known as the *Son of God* <note>original: "Iśvara-Santāna"</note>. In the *Compendium of the Doctrines of Goraksa* <note>original: "Goraksa-Siddhānta-Sangraha"</note>, published by the Sarasvati Bhavana Texts of Benares and edited by Dr. Gopinath Kaviraj, we find on page 40, the names of the nine Nāthas:

<summary>This page explores the historical presence of Nātha gurus across India and Nepal, explaining their role in reviving Hindu non-dualism after the decline of Buddhism and their philosophical focus on the principles of Śiva and Śhakti.</summary>
<keywords>Nātha gurus, Gorakhnāth, Matsyendranāth, Advaitavāda, Śhakti, revival of Hinduism, Śaivism, Bengal history, Nepal traditions, Iśvara-Santāna</keywords>

--- Page 30 ---
<meta>This page continues the exploration of the Nātha tradition, shifting from the general historical context in Bengal to the specific lineage of the "Nine Masters" and the widespread geographic reach of Gorakhnāth and his teacher Matsyendra across the Indian subcontinent and Nepal.</meta>

popularly known as the **Nine Masters** <note>original: "Nava-Nātha"</note>. In this list we find the names of the **Primal Lord** <note>original: "Ādināth," an epithet for the god Śiva</note> (Śiva) as the Guru, followed by Matsyendra, Udaynāth, Daṇdanāth, Santoshnāth, Kūrmanāth, Bhavanārji, and Gorakhnāth, who is called the son of God.

From all other sources, we find that Matsyendra is the teacher at whose feet Gorakhnāth spent twelve years and emerged as a great **practitioner of yoga** <note>original: "Yogī"</note> and mystic. The name of Matsyendra is very familiar in Bengal, as he was born in Eastern Bengal and preached in Kamarupa, Assam, where he was said to be ensnared by the queens of Kadali <note>A legendary kingdom of women in medieval Indian folklore.</note>.

Gorakhnāth, the master **yogi**, was known throughout Northern and Western India in the eleventh century and later; he was held in great reverence in Sikkim, Nepal, Hardwar, Nainital, Gorakhpore, Benaras (in the Uttar Pradesh), the Punjab, Kashmir, Peshawar, Sind, Beluchistan, Cutch, Bombay, Rajputana, Orissa, Assam, and Bengal. In the Punjab, there is a famous temple in the name of Gorakhnāth, known as **Gorakh’s Mound** <note>original: "Gorakh-Tilla"</note>. The liberal Emperor Akbar is said to have been initiated into the secrets of the practitioners of yoga, and he presented some villages with an annual income for the maintenance of this mound.

The followers of Gorakhnāth <note>original: "Gorakhnathis"</note> also visit the holy shrines of Gorakhnāth, Dharmanāth, and others in far-off places like Dhinodhar in Cutch, Koteśvar near Karachi in Sind, Hiṅglāj in Beluchistan, and Devī Pātān in Nepal. Hiṅglāj is under the care of Muslims and is considered to be beyond the borders of India. Therefore, upon their return home from Hiṅglāj, the practitioners are branded on the upper part of the right forearm with the **symbol of the union of the divine masculine and feminine** <note>original: "Śiva-Śakti" represented by the "Yonī–linga"</note> at Koteśvar. The followers of Gorakhnāth lost control of Koteśvar to the **Atits** <note>A sect of Shaivite ascetics</note> in the sixteenth century. The monastery at Dhinodhar is one of the most important centers in Western India; in this place, Dharmanāth, a disciple of Gorakhnāth, performed his penance for twelve long years. At Devī Pātān, the image of Matsyendra is worshipped. An annual chariot festival is still held at Pātān in honor of Matsyendra, who is the guardian deity of Nepal. Matsyendra, though a follower of Śiva, is worshipped in Nepal as an incarnation of the Buddha in the form of the **Lord Who Looks Down in Compassion** <note>original: "Avalokiteśvar"</note>, the fourth divine **Enlightened Being** <note>original: "Bodhisattva"</note>. However, his image as a follower of Śiva is also worshipped as

It highlights the syncretism between Hinduism and Buddhism in Nepal, where Matsyendra is identified with the Bodhisattva Avalokiteshvara.</summary>

<meta>This page discusses the cultural influence of Gorakhnāth in Nepal and Northern India, before transitioning into a historical analysis of his teacher, Matsyendranāth, using textual evidence from scholars like Dr. Bagchi and Abhinava Gupta to establish a timeline for the lineage.</meta>

**Devotion to Shiva** <note>original: "Śaivism"</note> was introduced in Nepal through him. Gorakhnāth holds the same respect as **the Lord of the Beasts** <note>original: "Paśupatināth," an important form of Shiva in Nepal</note>, who is the God of the Nepalese. The ‘Gurkhās’ of Nepal are named after **the Protector of Cows** <note>original: "Gorakṣa," an alternate name for Gorakhnāth</note>. Gorakhnāth’s name is engraved in Nepalese coins. Gorakhpore in the Uttar Pradesh is named after the great saint; temples in Gorakhpore and Chunar are dedicated to his name. In Bengal there is a place called *Gorakh’s Dwelling* <note>original: "Gorakh-Bāsuli"</note> near Dum-Dum, and also a place called *The Three Rivers* <note>original: "Tribeni"</note> near the river Hooghly where the **Nātha Yogis** assemble and worship the holy Gorakhnāth, uttering the prayer <note>original: "mantra"</note> *Shiva is Gorakh* <note>original: "Śiva-Gorakh"</note>.

## The Historicity and date of
## Matsyendranāth

Some manuscripts <note>original: "MSS."</note> found in Nepal have been published by Dr. P. Bagchi under the name of *The Determination of the Wisdom of the Kaula* <note>original: "Kaula-jñāna-nirṇaya," a foundational text of the Kaula tradition</note>. The authors of these are Matsyendranāth and Mīnanāth as found in the concluding signatures <note>original: "colophons"</note>. Dr. Bagchi has proved them to be the same person, and also shown that the language is not later than that of the eleventh century, and is a form of old Sanskrit. In one place it is mentioned that Matsyendra was a **divine incarnation** <note>original: "Avatār"</note> of Shiva. It is generally supposed that when a person is considered to be an incarnation, he must have lived at least a century earlier. This fact, along with the nature of the language, helps us to place Matsyendra sometime in the tenth century. Abhinava Gupta, the great scholar, in his treatise *The Light on the Tantras* <note>original: "Tantrāloka"</note> salutes Matsyendra as his **Teacher** <note>original: "Guru"</note> and greets him as ‘equal to Shiva’. Dr. K. C. Pandey has discussed the time of Abhinava Gupta and placed him in the second half of the tenth century and his writings not later than that of the beginning of the eleventh century. This also helps us to place Matsyendra not later than the tenth century. Abhinava does not mention Gorakhnāth.

In the commentary of *The Light on the Tantras* we find that Matsyendra was the founder of the fourth branch of a particular **Non-dualist Esoteric** <note>original: "Advaita Tāntrik"</note> Sect. This sect was founded in Kāmarūpa in Assam, where according to other traditions also, Matsyendranāth preached. This mention is made because Abhinava’s great teacher was also connected with this particular Esoteric Sect.

Bagchi in his introduction to *The Determination of the Wisdom of the Kaula* has shown from a manuscript that Matsyendra was born in Bengal and he was the founder of the fourth branch of the **Clan of the Yoginis** <note>original: "Yoginī Kaula," an ancient tantric tradition focusing on female energies</note> and preached his doctrines in Kāmarūpa.

<summary>This page highlights Gorakhnāth’s profound influence on Nepalese culture and uses historical manuscripts and the writings of Abhinava Gupta to date his teacher, Matsyendranāth, to the 10th century.</summary>
<keywords>Gorakhnāth, Matsyendranāth, Nepal, Śaivism, Abhinava Gupta, Kaula-jñāna-nirṇaya, Tantrāloka, Kāmarūpa, Yoginī Kaula, Gurkhas</keywords>

--- Page 32 ---
<meta>This page continues the historical investigation into the life of the great saint Matsyendranāth, moving from textual analysis of the Kaula tradition to legend and lineage-based dating, eventually transitioning into the timeline of his most famous disciple, Gorakhnāth.</meta>

In an old book of the **Yogis** <note>practitioners of Yoga</note> by **Jñāneśvar** <note>a 13th-century Marathi saint and philosopher</note> written in **Marathi** <note>the language of Maharashtra, India</note>, translated into Hindi and published from Hardwar as *Manifestation of the Yogi Tradition* <note>original: "Yogī-sampradāyāviṣkṛiti"</note>, we find that Matsyendra was thrown into the sea by his parents because he was born under an inauspicious star and was devoured by a fish. It is then that he overheard **Shiva** <note>the Hindu god of destruction and transformation, often considered the first teacher of Yoga</note> and was initiated by him, the time being the end of the **Brazen Age** <note>original: "Dvāpara Yuga," the third of the four ages of the world comprising 864,000 years</note>. Then he preached in **Kāmarupa** <note>an ancient kingdom in what is now Assam</note>. Gorakhnāth became his disciple, and in the **Era of Yudhistir 1939** <note>original: "Yudhistir Samvat 1939," a dating system named after the eldest of the Pandava brothers from the Mahabharata</note> he left his charge to Gorakhnāth and put himself in **eternal trance** <note>original: "Samādhi," the final meditative state in which a saint leaves their physical body</note> in the Girnar Hills.

In the **verses on spiritual practices** <note>original: "Caryā-padas," early Buddhist tantric songs</note> composed by **Perfected Teachers** <note>original: "Siddhāchāryas"</note> discovered by Shastri and published by him, we find a couplet in Bengali by **Mīnanāth** <note>another name for Matsyendranāth, meaning "Lord of Fish"</note>, quoted in the commentary. As we have mentioned before, those **spiritual verses** <note>original: "Caryās"</note> do not belong to any period earlier than the tenth century. From this too, we may place Matsyendranāth not later than the tenth century, if Matsyendra and Mīnanāth are considered to be the same person.

In the list of the 84 **Perfected Ones** <note>original: "Siddhas," legendary saints of the tantric tradition</note> who flourished between the tenth and twelfth centuries, we find the name and picture of Matsyendranāth, who is also known as **Mīnanāth of the Diamond Path** <note>original: "Mīnanāth Vajrapāda"</note>.

## **The Historicity and date of Gorakhnāth**

Gorakhnāth was the disciple of Matsyendranāth and as such he must belong to a period later than the tenth century, which date we have accepted for Matsyendranāth, the **teacher** <note>original: "guru"</note>.

Gorakh's disciple was Gainināth, and his disciple was Nivṛttināth, the elder brother of Jñāneśvar. Nivṛttināth, though older than Jñāneśvar by two years only, had initiated Jñāneśvar in the **Nātha sect** <note>a medieval Indian religious movement combining elements of Yoga, Buddhism, and Shaivism</note>. Jñāneśvar is a well-known saint of **Maharashtra** <note>original: "Mahārāṣtra," a region in Western India</note>, who wrote his commentary on the **Song of the Lord** <note>original: "Gitā"</note>, known as *The Wisdom of Jñāneśvar* <note>original: "Jñāneśvari"</note>, in 1290 A.D., as has been proved by scholars. From Jñāneśvar’s own writings the fact has been established that he was put in **eternal trance** <note>original: "Samādhi"</note> at his own request, only at the age of 21 years in 1296 A.D. This **sacred tomb** <note>original: "Samādhi"</note> can still be seen and is only a few miles off from Poona. Therefore Jñāneśvar’s date of birth is 1275 A.D.

<summary>This page establishes the historical timeline for Matsyendranāth and Gorakhnāth, using hagiographies and the lineage of the Marathi saint Jñāneśvar to place them between the 10th and 13th centuries.</summary>
<keywords>Matsyendranāth, Gorakhnāth, Jñāneśvar, Samādhi, Natha tradition, historical dating, Marathi literature, Siddhas</keywords>

--- Page 33 ---
<meta>This page continues the historical investigation into the life and chronology of Gorakhnāth and his lineage. While the previous page examined the legendary origins of Matsyendranāth and his appearance in early Marathi and Bengali literature, this section attempts to pin down specific dates using genealogical records and historical connections to royal dynasties.</meta>

If we accept the theory that the time between a **guru** <note>spiritual teacher</note> and his disciple is about a hundred years—considering their longevity, which was well-known—we may place **Gorakh** <note>Gorakhnāth, the most famous of the Natha saints</note> in the eleventh century on the following grounds:

| | | |
|----------|:---:|----------|
| **Nivṛtti's** date of birth | — | 1273 A. D. |
| **Jñāneśvar's** date of birth | — | 1275 A. D. |
| **Gainināth's** time | — | Around 1175 A. D. |
| **Gorakhnāth** | — | Around 1075 A. D. |

If we accept this date for **Gorakhnāth**, **Matsyendranāth** can be placed a century earlier, i.e., in the tenth century. Consequently, the facts mentioned before—the composition of the **Knowledge of the Kaula Tradition** <note>original: "Kaula-jñāna," a foundational tantric text</note> and the salutation of **Matsyendra** by **Abhinava** <note>Abhinavagupta, the famous 11th-century philosopher from Kashmir</note> in the eleventh century—can all be admitted.

**Gopichandra** was the king of **Bengal**; his father was **Manikchandra Raja**, the brother of **Dharmapāla**. The **Pāla dynasty** came to an end in Bengal in the eleventh century. The **Yogis** rose to power under the **Pālas** in Bengal, and **Manikchandra's** wife, **Maynāmati**, was a disciple of **Gorakhnāth**. Therefore, **Gorakhnāth** must belong to the eleventh century as well.

**Maynāmati** is spoken of as the sister of **Bhartṛhari**, who abdicated his throne in favor of his brother **Vikramāditya** (Chandragupta II) of **Ujjain**. **Vikrama** ruled from 1079 to 1126 A. D. <note>These dates specifically refer to Vikramāditya VI of the Chalukya dynasty, though the text associates him with the earlier Gupta king</note>. **Bhartṛhari** became a **Yogi**. One of the sub-sects of the **Split-Ear Yogis** <note>original: "Kanphāṭās," so named for the large earrings worn through the cartilage of the ear</note> is named after him. **Bhartṛhari** was a disciple of **Gorakhnāth**, so we can place **Gorakh** in the eleventh century.

The tradition in Bengal of **Maynāmati** supporting **Rāmāi Pandit** in the worship of **Dharma** <note>referring to the 'Dharma cult,' a regional religion in Bengal combining Buddhist and folk elements</note> gives us the historical data of the eleventh century. The treatise on **Dharma** composed by **Rāmāi** and known as the **Chronicle of the Void** <note>original: "Sūnya Purāna"</note> points to a sufficiently archaic language, determined by scholars to be from the eleventh century.

**Gorakhnāth** accepted both **Hindus** and **Muslims** <note>original: "Mohammedans"</note> as his disciples. **Baba Ratan Haji** was probably a **Muslim** follower of **Gorakhnāth** and is said to have converted a number of **Muslims** to the **Yogi's** creed. In **Kabul**, they are known as **faqirs** <note>wandering holy men or ascetics</note> of **Ratan Haji**. **Understanding the Unbeliever** <note>original: "Kafirbodha"</note> was perhaps written by **Ratan Haji** but was known as the work of **Gorakhnāth**. **Ratan Haji** belonged to the eleventh century by tradition, so **Gorakhnath** may be placed in the same century.

<summary>This page establishes a historical timeline for Gorakhnāth in the 11th century by cross-referencing royal lineages in Bengal and the religious traditions of the Nath and Dharma cults. It also highlights the inclusive nature of the movement, which attracted both Hindu and Muslim followers during this period.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the investigation into the historical dates of the Natha saints, moving from the chronology of Gorakhnāth to a detailed look at King Gopichandra, a central figure in the lineage's lore. The previous page established that Gorakhnāth likely lived in the 11th century based on the genealogy of Marathi and Bengali figures; here, the author provides evidence for Gopichandra’s existence through literary and archaeological records across India.</meta>

# The Historicity of Gopichandra

Throughout India, one finds plays, ballads, famous paintings, and—in the modern day—films depicting the renunciation of **Gopichandra**. His departure from his home is considered as emotionally powerful as that of the **Buddha** <note>Siddhartha Gautama, who famously left his palace to seek enlightenment</note>. In the Cambridge University Library, there is a copy of a Bengali drama written in the **Newari** language of Nepal <note>the historical language of the Kathmandu Valley</note> which was composed in the 17th century. The plot of this drama is partially similar to the Bengali ballad known as the *Song of Govindachandra* <note>original: "Govindachandrer Git"</note>. In 1873, the scholar Grierson collected and published the *Song of King Manikchandra* <note>original: "Manikchandra Rājar Gān"</note>, which tells the story of **Gopichandra’s** father; however, the narrative follows the same theme, focusing on **Gopichandra’s** decision to leave his royal life behind. There is also a Hindi version of **Gopichandra’s** song by **Laksandāsa**. In the Ambala District of the Punjab, this drama is still performed, and the wandering singers of the Rangpore District in Bengal still earn their livelihood by performing this mournful song. The famous painter **Ravi Varma** <note>Raja Ravi Varma (1848–1906), known for his realistic depictions of Indian epic scenes</note> captured this sorrowful departure on canvas. Several copies of dramatic texts concerning **Gopichandra’s** renunciation are found in Northern and Eastern Bengal, Orissa, the Punjab, Bhagalpore, and Benaras, written in the local languages of those regions.

Who was this widely known **Gopichandra**? Upon marrying **Maynāmati**, **Manikchandra** received **Meharkula** in **Tripura** (located in the Chittagong District) as a dowry. His ancestral throne and kingdom were located at **Vikrampore** in the **Dacca** District. Thus, he ruled over both territories. Their only son was **Gopichandra**, whose capital was at **Paṭṭikānagar** in Tripura. A range of hills in Tripura is still known today by the name **Maynāmati**. An inscription has been discovered there dated to 1219 A.D. which mentions the city of **Paṭṭikera**. **Gopichandra** had also leased a portion of Northern Bengal, which explains why the people of the Rangpore district are so familiar with the story of his renunciation. Dr. D. C. Sen <note>Dinesh Chandra Sen, a renowned historian of Bengali literature</note> collected these facts from various verses about **Gopichandra** and believes that **Gopichandra** belonged to the **Chandra dynasty** <note>a Buddhist dynasty that ruled parts of Bengal and Myanmar</note> and was not related to the **Pāla kings** of Bengal.

**Gopichandra** married the two daughters of King **Harischandra**, who likely belonged to **Savar** in the Dacca district. This **Harischandra** lived during the eleventh century. If we accept the fact that **Gopichandra’s** mother was a disciple

<summary>This page examines the widespread cultural presence of King Gopichandra’s renunciation story across India and uses literary and archaeological evidence to place him in the 11th century. It identifies his royal lineage as part of the Chandra dynasty of Bengal rather than the Pāla kings.</summary>

<keywords>Gopichandra, Maynāmati, Manikchandra, Tripura, Chandra dynasty, renunciation, Govindachandrer Git, Ravi Varma, Bengali ballads, medieval Indian history</keywords>

--- Page 35 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion on the dating of King Gopichandra, providing bibliographical evidence from various scholars and linking the Natha lineage to the end of the Pāla dynasty. It then transitions into the historical identity of Bhartṛhari, another king-turned-yogi, attempting to reconcile various legends with the established 11th-century timeline for Gorakhnāth.</meta>

...of Gorakhnāth, both Maynāmati and Gopichandra cannot belong to a period much later than the eleventh century.

*The Song of Gopichandra* <note>original: "Gopichandrer Gān"</note> has been published by the Calcutta University with two different renderings, one by a Hindu, the other by a Mohammedan. From Dacca <note>the modern capital of Bangladesh</note>, Dr. N. K. Bhattasali published two books under the titles *The Song of Gopichandra* <note>original: "Gopīchandrer Gīt"</note> and *The Song of Maynāmati* <note>original: "Maynāmatir Gān"</note>. Sivachandra Sil published *The Song of Govindachandra* <note>original: "Govindachandrer Gīt"</note>, but Govindachandra is the same person as Gopichandra.

These books, however, have all been written at a much later date, in about the 17th and 18th centuries. Gopichandra can be placed in the 12th century or earlier from the facts of Maynāmati being Gorakh’s disciple and Hāḍīpā <note>a prominent Natha master and leather-worker by caste</note> being the guru of Gopichandra. We have already discussed the relationships between Gorakh, Maynāmati, and Hāḍīpā in the legends concerning them. The **Pāla dynasty** <note>an imperial power in late classical India, known for their patronage of Buddhism and the arts</note> also came to an end in the twelfth century; Gopichandra and his father were contemporaries of the Pāla kings.

### The Historicity of Bhartṛhari, Jālandharnāth, Jñānesvar, and Gambhīrnāth

**Bhartṛhari**, who was once a king, became a Yogī. In Mahāraṣṭra <note>a state in western India</note> a drama has been written in 1904 A.D. which is known as *The Renunciation of Bhartṛhari* <note>original: "Bhartṛhari Nirvēda"</note>. Gray published it in an American Journal. Bhartṛhari is supposed to be the king of **Ujjain** <note>an ancient city in central India, a center of learning and pilgrimage</note>. But the Maharaja of Alwar, who is interested in the story of Bhartṛhari, failed to gather correct information from Ujjain. Moreover, it is said that Bhartṛhari and Vikram were brothers, and Bhartṛhari abdicated in favor of Vikram. 

Who is this Vikram? If it is the **Vikramāditya**, who was defeated by Sālibāhan, in whose name an era was established (1872 = 1951 A.D.) <note>referring to the calculation of the Vikrama Samvat calendar</note>, we arrive at an impossible conclusion. We have no intention of placing Gorakhnāth in the first century. If we accept Vikramāditya as **Chandragupta II** of Ujjain, who ruled from 1076 to 1126 A.D., we may arrive at a probable conclusion and place Bhartṛhari before the end of the eleventh century. The death of his devoted wife **Rani Piṅgalā** made him accept the discipleship of Gorakhnāth. This also corresponds to the date we have accepted for Gorakhnāth, i.e., the eleventh century.

<summary>The author dates the Natha figures Gopichandra and Bhartṛhari to the 11th and 12th centuries by analyzing literary publications, dynastic history, and genealogical legends. He argues that Bhartṛhari's renunciation aligns with the era of Gorakhnāth, rejecting earlier dates that would place them in the 1st century.</summary>

<keywords>Gopichandra, Maynāmati, Gorakhnāth, Bhartṛhari, Pāla dynasty, Vikramāditya, Natha Hagiography, Chronology, Ujjain, Rani Piṅgalā</keywords>

--- Page 36 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion on the historical figures and lineages of the Natha tradition, moving from the medieval legends of Jālandharnāth and Jñāneśvar to the more recent history of Gambhīrnāthji, before addressing the scholarly confusion regarding the identities of the primary masters, Matsyendranāth and Luipa.</meta>

Jālandharnāth was also known as Jālandharipā or Hāḍipā. One opinion is that he was the king of Jālandhar, and another is that he was born in Sind <note>a historical region in modern-day Pakistan</note>. He was by caste a Sudra <note>the traditional social class of laborers and artisans</note> and learned Yoga at Uddiyana <note>an ancient site in the Swat Valley known as a major center for Buddhist Tantra and magic</note>, the great center of Buddhist tantrikism and magic. We have already described the great powers of Hāḍipā. Jālandhar was praised in sixteen different books written by his follower Mānsingh, the ruler of Jodhpur. In the literature of the Siddhas <note>the "Perfected Ones" or semi-divine masters of yoga</note>, Jālandhar is known as Vichārnāth and Gopīchandra, his disciple, as Sṛingārīpāva. In the *Siddhānta Vākya* <note>original: "The Statements of Established Truth"</note> we find some questions and answers between Jālandhar and Gopichandra in which the ideal of the Nātha cult is described. Jālandhar is a disciple of Gorakhnāth and the guru of Gopichandra, so we may place him in the eleventh century.

Jñāneśvar has been dealt with before, and it is sufficient to mention here that he was a Mahārāṣtra saint and his books *Jñāneśvari* <note>a famous Marathi commentary on the Bhagavad Gita</note> and *Yogī-sampradāyaviṣkṛti* <note>original: "An Explanation of the Yogi Tradition"</note> have earned the fame he deserves. Jñāneśvar belonged to the thirteenth century and we have given our reasons when discussing the date of Gorakhnāth.

We shall now deal with a Yogi of modern times. He is Yogīrā̄j <note>the title "King of Yogis"</note> Sree Gambhīrnāthji who passed away not so long ago in 1917 A.D. He was initiated by a Mohant <note>the religious superior or head of a monastery</note> of Gorakhpore Temple. Gambhīrnāth was a non-Bengali, yet he had about six hundred Bengali disciples. Gambhīrnāth was in charge of the Gorakhpore Temple, but he never accepted the Mohant's post, which was offered to him. The character of this saint was spotless, and he was well-known for his charities at Kumbha Melas <note>major Hindu pilgrimage festivals</note> and elsewhere. In Gorakhpore a small but beautiful temple of white marble with a life-size image of the once handsome Gambhīrnāth done in white marble too, has been erected by his Bengali followers. Śāntināth and Nivṛttināth, two of his Bengali disciples, still reside in the temple grounds and carry on their studies of religious books. Thus the cult which originated with Matsyendra and Gorakhnāth, is still a living source of inspiration to many.

## **Matsyendranāth, Minanāth and Luipa—**
## **Are they Identical ?**

The names of the gurus and their sishyas <note>disciples</note>, as found in the different lists, are often confusing. In the history of the 84 Siddhas <note>the traditional group of eighty-four masters of the Tantric tradition</note> as found from

It also introduces a critical inquiry into whether several prominent masters in the Siddha lineage are actually the same historical individual.</summary>

<keywords>Jālandharnāth, Jñāneśvar, Gambhīrnāth, Gorakhpore Temple, Natha lineage, Matsyendranāth, 84 Siddhas, Gopichandra, Sudra caste, Uddiyana tantrism</keywords>
