<meta>This page continues the investigation into the historical identities of the Natha masters, specifically addressing the scholarly debate over whether the figures Matsyendranāth, Mīnanāth, and the Buddhist sage Luipā are, in fact, the same individual across different regional traditions.</meta>

In Tibetan sources, we find mention of practically all the important <term>Nātha gurus</term> <note>the masters of the "Lord" lineage, a medieval Indian movement of yoga practitioners</note>. **Matsyendra** is often called **Mīnanāth** and even **Luipā**. **Luipā** belonged to the East and was in the service of king **Dharmapāl** of the ninth century, and was the author of five books. By another version, he was an employee of the king of **Uddiyana**, which the scholar Bagchi has proved to be the same as the **Swat Valley** in the North-West Frontier of India. But how could **Luipā** belong to the East and at the same time be employed in the North-West and compose verses in **Bengali**? It is well-known that **Yogīs** in ancient times travelled on foot all over India, which might explain the position. On the other hand, the mystic cult with which his name was associated was in vogue in distant parts of India. So **Luipā's** name was associated both with **Uddiyana** and **Bengal**. The name **Luipā** in Tibetan means "the intestine of a fish." According to **Bengali** legends, **Matsyendra** was found in the womb of a fish. Tibetan pictures show that he was fond of eating the entrails of fish and was surrounded by fish. **Shastri** in a **Bengali** novel has given a vivid description of **Luipā's** fondness for the entrails which were supplied by a king of **Bengal**, who was **Luipā's** disciple.

**Luipā** is considered to be the <term>Ādiguru</term> <note>original: "the first or primordial teacher"</note> by the Tibetans; while **Matsyendra** is looked upon as such by Indians. So, we may conclude that **Luipā** and **Matsyendra**, both looked upon as **Ādigurus** and both having some connection with fish, are identical persons.

The word **Mīna** also means "fish," and in the colophons of the *Kaula-jñāna-Nirṇaya* <note>original: "The Determination of the Knowledge of the Kaula Tradition," a foundational Tantric text</note> we find the name of **Mīnanāth** once and, at the end, that of **Matsyendranāth**. Bagchi has concluded from other manuscripts that they are identical persons, the one name being a synonym of the other, and they are not related as father and son, as might be supposed from the above colophons.

The river **Bramhaputra** in **Assam** is known as ‘**Lohit**’ because of its red water <note>from the Sanskrit "Lohita," meaning red or blood-colored</note>. **Matsyendra**, who preached in **Kamarupa** <note>an ancient kingdom in what is now Assam</note>, may have been known as ‘**Lohipada**,’ that is, one belonging to the **Lohit** river. **Luipā** was probably the shorter form of ‘**Lohipada**.’ In **Bengal**, a small fish-catching contrivance made of cane is known as a **Lui**, which may also account for the name **Luipā** as the ‘catcher of fish with a **lui**.’ In the

<summary>This page explores the multi-regional identity of the Natha guru Matsyendranāth, providing evidence that he is synonymous with the figures Mīnanāth and Luipā through linguistic connections to fish and the geography of Assam and Bengal.</summary>
<keywords>Matsyendranāth, Luipā, Mīnanāth, Natha Sect, Dharmapāl, Uddiyana, Kamarupa, Kaula-jñāna-Nirṇaya, Bengali literature, Tibetan Buddhism</keywords>

--- Page 38 ---
<meta>This page continues the scholarly investigation into the identity of the Tantric master Matsyendranāth, arguing that he is the same figure as the Buddhist master Luipā and Mīnanāth. It explores their historical dating to the 10th century and the doctrinal similarities in their writings.</meta>

In the <term>Determination of Kaula Knowledge</term> <note>original: "Kaula-jñāna-nirṇaya," a foundational text of the Kaula school of Tantra</note>, he is also mentioned as **Macchaghna**, which means the 'killer of fish.'

So **Matsyendranāth**, **Mīnanāth**, and **Luipā** are identical and known under different names in different sources.

### The Doctrines of Luipā and Matsyendra

We have mentioned above that **Luipā** and **Matsyendra** flourished at the same time—namely, the tenth century AD—and that they are the same person. In the list available from Tibetan sources, we find **Luipā's** name at the very beginning. His name in <term>the refined language</term> <note>original: "Sanskrit"</note> would be **Matsyantrād** or **Matsyōdara**, which is associated with the intestines of a fish. **Luipā** is described as a Buddhist sage, originally belonging to the fisherman caste. While **Luipā** is considered to be the first <term>Perfected Preceptor</term> <note>original: "Siddhāchārya," a teacher who has attained spiritual and physical perfection</note>, **Matsyendra** was accepted as the first <term>Lordly Teacher</term> <note>original: "Nātha Guru"</note>.

The <term>Perfected Preceptors</term> wrote the <term>Verses of Conduct</term> <note>original: "Caryās," mystic songs of realization</note> which the scholar **Shāstrī** discovered in Nepal and published as being "thousand-year-old **Bengali** verses." **Luipā** is known to be one of the earliest of these verse-writers. **Luipā** wrote another book in collaboration with **Dipankar Srijñāna** <note>also known as Atiśa, a famous Buddhist scholar who traveled to Tibet</note>, from whose lifetime we can fix **Luipā's** date as the second half of the tenth century, and the date of the composition of this book as the beginning of the eleventh century.

The scholar **Bagchi** has published some manuscripts of <term>The Determination of Kaula Knowledge</term> by **Matsyendranāth** and has proved that these were composed in the eleventh century.

The doctrines preached by **Luipā** in these old verses and by **Matsyendra** (**Mīnanāth**) in the <term>Tantra of the Hero Beyond Totality</term> <note>original: "Akula Vira Tantra"</note>—found within the collection named <term>The Determination of Kaula Knowledge</term>—both relate to the ways of attaining the supreme bliss of <term>spontaneous absorption</term> <note>original: "sahaja samādhi," a state of easy, natural fixation of the mind in spiritual bliss</note>. It is curious to note that **Matsyendra** of the **Nātha** cult has been deified in Nepal as <term>The Lord Who Looks Down</term> <note>original: "Avalokiteśvar," the embodiment of Buddhist compassion</note>, and is still honored as such. We can only repeat, therefore, that **Luipā** and **Matsyendra** were one and the same person.

The doctrine of **Luipā** as found in the <term>Verses of Conduct</term> is as follows: **Luipā** says that the mind is solely responsible for creating the illusory world. The world as seen by human beings is

<summary>This page argues for the historical and spiritual identity of the figures Matsyendranāth and Luipā, dating them to the 10th century and linking their teachings on "sahaja samādhi" across Hindu and Buddhist traditions.</summary>
<keywords>Matsyendranāth, Luipā, Kaula-jñāna-nirṇaya, Siddhāchārya, sahaja samādhi, Avalokiteśvar, Tantra, Buddhist-Hindu synthesis</keywords>

--- Page 39 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion on the identity of the sage Luipā/Matsyendranāth, moving from historical dating to his core philosophical teachings. It contrasts the ritualistic yoga of earlier schools with the "Innate Way" (Sahaja) and explores the overlap between Buddhist and Hindu Tantric traditions.</meta>

[The nature of reality is] neither existent nor non-existent. It is like the shadow of the moon in the water. When the moon disappears, the shadow is no more: thus when the mind is destroyed by being merged in the <term>Innate state</term> <note>original: "Sahaja," referring to a state of spontaneous enlightenment or supreme bliss</note> or Supreme Bliss, the outer world disappears for the <term>Yoga practitioner</term> <note>original: "Yogī"</note>.

**Luipā** also insists that the elaborate practices of Yoga, which are concerned with <term>ritual seals</term> <note>original: "Mudrās," specific hand gestures or bodily positions</note> and <term>physical locks</term> <note>original: "Bandhanas," internal energetic contractions</note> should be abandoned, because in spite of such practices one has to face death as an ordinary man. Therefore, the <term>Innate Way</term> <note>original: "Sahaja"</note> or the "easy way" should be adopted.

The idea of the phenomenal world as an illusion is not Buddhistic in origin, neither are the <term>ritual seals</term> and the <term>physical locks</term>, as we shall see later. But **Luipā** was known as the **<term>First Diamond-Vehicle Preceptor</term>** <note>original: "Ādi Vajrāchārya"</note>. **<term>The Diamond Vehicle</term>** <note>original: "Vajrayāna"</note> is a later form of Buddhist tantrik worship. The question arises, "Was **Matsyendra** a Buddhist tantrik or a Saivite <note>a follower of the Hindu god Shiva</note> tantrik?" To this, we may reply that '<term>Tantra</term>' <note>a system of esoteric practices and rituals</note> is neither Hindu nor Buddhistic in origin. The ancient <term>scriptural doctrines</term> <note>original: "Agamic tenets"</note> are the common source of all tantrik worship, whether Hindu or Buddhist. Tantra consisted of the practices of <term>sacred chants</term> <note>original: "Mantras"</note>, **seals**, **locks**, etc., but gradually sexo-yogic practices found its way into it. Such practices for the attainment of Supreme Bliss were advised by Buddhists of the **Vajrayāna** school, and **Luipā** is said to be its first exponent.

On the other hand, the **<term>Lordly Teachers</term>** <note>original: "Nātha gurus"</note> have repeatedly advised their disciples to shun the company of women, though we do find some **seals** described in the books of the **Nātha** cult in which the company of women seems necessary. The names of these **seals** remind one of the cults of the <term>Innate Vehicle</term> <note>original: "Sahajayāna"</note> and the <term>Diamond Vehicle</term>.

**Dr. Barthwal** concludes that it is not to be understood that **Gorakhnath** totally abandoned Tantric practices, though he did change its perspective and made it a difficult test for the practitioner who aims to attain <term>spiritual perfection</term> <note>original: "Siddhi"</note>. So these **seals** were practiced under trying conditions. Continence was a very important means of attaining spiritual perfection. It is under **Gorakhnath's** advice that **Maynāmati** compelled her only son to separate from his two queens. When, by the Goddess' scheme, **Gorakh** was compelled to marry, he at once became a baby in her arms by his Yogic powers and wanted her to nurse him! In the **<term>Words of Gorakh</term>** <note>original: "Gorakh-Vāṇī"</note> and also in some Bengali ballads we find—

<summary>This page explores the transition from complex ritual yoga to the "Sahaja" (Innate) path, debating whether Matsyendranāth was a Buddhist or Hindu Tantric and highlighting Gorakhnath's emphasis on celibacy.</summary>
<keywords>Sahaja, Luipā, Gorakhnath, Vajrayāna, Tantra, Mudrās, Bandhanas, Siddhi, Matsyendranāth, celibacy, Nātha gurus</keywords>

--- Page 40 ---
<meta>This page continues the investigation into the religious identity of Matsyendranāth and his disciple Gorakhnāth. It transitions from general philosophical principles to specific physical practices and ethical behaviors that distinguish the Nātha tradition from the Buddhist Sahajiyā tradition, despite their outward similarities.</meta>

**Gorakh** compares a woman to a tigress who sucks human blood! Again, **Gorakh** says in a Hindi verse, “The tree by the river bank and the man by the side of a woman, verily they cannot expect to last long. The restlessness, the unfettered activity of the mind causes the back bone to wear away, and the body of the man perishes.”

In a book on the **Yoga of Forceful Effort** <term>original: Haṭha Yoga; a system of physical techniques designed to channel vital energy</term>, full descriptions of **Postures** <term>original: Āsanas; specific physical positions used in yoga</term> as practiced by **Matsyendra** and **Gorakh** are given. To try to hear the **Unstruck Sound** <term>original: Anāhata Nāda; a mystical inner sound heard during deep meditation</term> in the body by practice is another difficult process adopted and advised by the **Nātha masters** <note>original: "Gurus"</note>. The awakening of the **Dormant Power** <term>original: Kundalinī Śakti; often visualized as a coiled serpent at the base of the spine</term> and the **Discipline of the Body** <term>original: Kāya-sādhana</term>, which we shall discuss later, are not at all easy or **Innate** <term>original: Sahaja; meaning 'spontaneous' or 'natural'</term> ways of **spiritual practice** <note>original: "sādhanā"</note>.

**Matsyendra** as a fisherman¹ or as the **Fish-Killer** <note>original: "Macchaghna"</note>, and **Gorakh** as a killer of animals², could not possibly be Buddhists, whose principle of **Non-violence** <term>original: Ahimsā</term> is well-known. The **Nātha** practitioners of Yoga sacrificed animals in their temples³. This too is opposed to Buddhism.

Thus we find that the **Innate** way of the Buddhists and the doctrines of the **Nāthas** have not much in common, though in the **Scripture of the Familyless Hero** <note>original: "Akula-vīra-tantra"</note> in Bagchi’s edition of the **Determination of Secret Knowledge** <note>original: "Kaula-jñāna nirṇaya"</note>, **Matsyendra** does advise the following of the **Innate** <note>original: "Sahaja"</note> which is a state of equilibrium and is the same state of **Innate Meditative Absorption** <term>original: Sahaja Samādhi</term> found in Tantric Buddhism.

The very word **Forceful Effort** <term>original: Haṭha</term> indicates the theory of the Sun and the Moon, the details of which we shall presently discuss. Thus this theory as found in the **Nātha** cult, can be found in the Buddhist **Innate** <note>original: "Sahajia"</note> cult too. It is because of such general similarities and also the fact that the **Diamond Being** <term>original: Vajra-saṭṭa; a central figure in Tantric Buddhism</term> of the Buddhists is also known as the **Primal Lord** <term>original: Ādināth; a title usually reserved for the god Shiva</term> (the name also for **Shiva** <note>The Hindu god of destruction and transformation</note>) that the **Nātha** masters are often called Buddhists by mistake. But the **Nāthas** were actually **Shiva-worshippers** <term>original: Śaivites</term>, as we shall presently prove.

<keywords>Gorakhnāth, Matsyendranāth, Haṭha Yoga, Kundalinī, Ahimsā, Śaivism, Tantric Buddhism, Sahaja, Ādināth</keywords>

<meta>This page transitions from the physical disciplines and ethical distinctions of the previous page to the core philosophical foundations of the Nātha tradition, specifically their non-dualistic view of Shiva and Shakti and the concept of the "liberated" state.</meta>

## The Philosophical Doctrines of the sect and Successive Steps of their **Spiritual Discipline** <term>original: Sādhanā</term>

The **Nātha Yogis** who call themselves the **Lineage of Shiva** <note>original: "Śiva-gotra"</note>—meaning they originate from Shiva—worship Shiva in his inactive form. Yet, **Power** <term>original: Śakti; the active, feminine principle of the divine</term> is powerless without Shiva. This philosophy is based on the **Non-dualism** <term>original: Advaitavād; the philosophical position that there is no ultimate distinction between the individual soul and the absolute reality</term> (monotheism) of the early **Shiva-worshipping** <note>original: "Śaiva"</note> School. However, the conception of the **Master** <note>original: "Nātha"</note> was beyond the **Dual and Non-dual state** <term>original: Dvaita Advaita</term> and also beyond the **Form and the Formless** <term>original: Sākār and Nirākār</term>. These conceptions of the 'Master' and the union of **Shiva and Shakti** are well depicted in the works of the **Nātha tradition** <note>original: "Nāthism"</note>, both in Sanskrit as well as in Bengali. Shiva is pure consciousness and perfect rest, while Shakti is the aspect of change and evolution.

Beyond the sphere of virtue and vice, of pleasure and pain, is Shiva, representing Eternity in the midst of all changes. Sir John Woodroffe has justly described it as “the changeless principle of all our changing experience.”

The **Nātha Yogi** strives to attain the state where pleasure or pain, virtue or vice, friend or foe does not affect him. To him, fragrant sandal paste is of the same value as mud. This state is described as the **Liberated** <note>original: "Avadhuta"</note> state—that is, the state of freedom attained when one has rid oneself of all changing conditions.

->“He is called a Liberated One because he shakes off all transformations of nature.”¹<-
<note>original Sanskrit: सर्वान् प्रकृतिविकारानवधुनोतीत्यावधूत:</note>
->**The Compendium of Goraksha’s Doctrines**, page 1.<-
<note>original: Goraksa Siddhanta Sangraha</note>

As the yogīs state that the human body is the epitome of the universe, the life process is also divided into the two aspects of Shiva and Shakti. It is Shakti which leads to change and decay through **Worldly Enjoyment** <term>original: Bhog</term>, which resides in the region below the navel. The region of Shiva is above the navel and is the domain of rest or **Renunciation** <term>original: Tyāg</term>.

---

( 1 ) “Shakti is within Shiva; Shiva is within Shakti.”
<note>original Sanskrit: शिवस्याभ्यन्तरे शक्ति: शक्तेरभ्यन्तरे शिव:</note>
->**Compendium of the Doctrines of the Adepts**, Page 26<-
<note>original: Siddha Siddhanta Samgraha</note>
->Saraswati Bhavan Texts, Banaras.<-

“Shakti appears as expansion; Shiva appears as contraction.”
<note>original Sanskrit: प्रसरं भामते शक्ति: संकोचं भामते शिव:</note>
Refer to the Saraswati Bhavana Texts, Banaras.
No 13. **Compendium of the Doctrines of the Adepts**, page 36.
No 18. **Compendium of Goraksha’s Doctrines**.

The same idea is contained in the Bengali book **The Victory of Goraksha** <note>original: Gorakṣa Vijaya</note>, page 3, regarding the **Primordial** <note>original: Ādya</note> and the **Beginningless** <note>original: Anādya</note>.

<summary>This page outlines the non-dualistic philosophy of the Nātha Yogis, focusing on the union of Shiva (rest/consciousness) and Shakti (change/evolution) and the goal of reaching the "Avadhuta" or liberated state.</summary>
<keywords>Nātha Yogis, Shiva, Shakti, Advaita, Avadhuta, Goraksha Siddhanta Sangraha, Siddha Siddhanta Sangraha, non-dualism, renunciation, macrocosm-microcosm</keywords>

--- Page 42 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of the "Avadhūta" or the liberated soul, specifically focusing on how the Nātha Yogī masters the physical and subtle body to reach a state of spiritual equilibrium that transcends the dualities of the material world.</meta>

The **Liberated Soul** <term>original: Avadhūta; an ascetic who has moved beyond worldly attachments and social norms</term> is equally unaffected by **Renunciation** <term>original: Tyag</term> or **Enjoyment** <term>original: Bhog</term>. The **Masters of the Yoga tradition** <note>original: "Nātha Yogīs"</note> adopted some mystic procedure in performing certain **Ritual gestures** <term>original: Mudrās; symbolic hand or body positions used in meditation</term> for spiritual realisation which consisted of **Enjoyment** and **Renunciation** at the same time. These procedures, however, remain obscure to the uninitiated.

The beginnings of the **Union** <term>original: Yoga; the system of spiritual discipline aimed at joining the individual soul with the divine</term> system can be traced back to ancient times, but the path is not an easy one. It is only through self-discipline that one can rise above the empirical self and be one with **The Auspicious One** <note>original: "Śiva"; the supreme deity representing pure consciousness</note>. A course of both physical austerities and mental discipline has to be followed to achieve this state of Bliss and forsake the outer world which binds us in its alluring snares. Mystic syllables such as **OM** <note>The sacred primal sound of the universe</note> help the practitioners of Yoga to achieve this state by concentration on the **Sacred chant** <term>original: Mantra; a word or sound repeated to aid concentration in meditation</term>. The practitioner forgets the outer world and is in Union with the Unseen. The **Nātha masters** advocate this practice as is seen from their works. The concentration and constant repetition of the sacred chant leads one to the state of **Spontaneous chanting** <term>original: Ajapā-jap; a state where the mantra repeats itself naturally with the breath without conscious effort</term> which means that one has entered internal life. The chant is spontaneously repeated all the time.

Such a practitioner, with the help of **The Yoga of Force** <term>original: Haṭha Yoga; a system of physical techniques and breath control</term>, gains control over the vital airs, the **Inward breath** <term>original: Prāṇa</term> and the **Outward breath** <term>original: Apāna</term>, and is able to perform miracles. It would be an injustice to say that the masters **Haḍipā**, **Gorakhnath**, etc., practised control over the vital airs to gain some occult powers only. Their final aim was higher. It was the attainment of **Shiva-hood** <note>The state of becoming one with the divine consciousness of Shiva</note>, with physical Yoga as the means. 

The vital airs are often termed as the **Sun and the Moon**. These terms have been used both in the Buddhist and Brahminical texts of mysticism. There are other synonyms as found in the **Verses of Realization** <term>original: Caryāpadas; ancient mystical poems from the Tantric tradition</term>. In physical Yoga, **The Moon** <term>original: Candra</term> is the nerve on the left of the body and is known as **The Channel of Comfort** <term>original: Iḍā</term>, and **The Sun** <term>original: Sūrya</term> is **The Golden Channel** <term>original: Piṅgalā</term> on the right. They are also known as **Gaṅgā** and **Yamunā** <note>The names of the two most sacred rivers in India, used here as metaphors for the body's internal energy channels</note>. An individual soul can attain the state of Shiva by abandoning the left and the right through meditative practices and bringing them under control. The master **Haḍipā** makes the Sun and Moon his earrings; in other words, he has controlled them. The Sun and the Moon are the symbols of day and night; the inward and outward breaths are the two important functions of the body on which depend the notion of time. So when a practitioner controls

<summary>This page explains the Nātha Yogī practice of Haṭha Yoga, detailing how the control of vital breaths (Prāṇa and Apāna) and the balancing of internal energy channels (metaphorically the Sun/Moon or Gaṅgā/Yamunā) lead to a state of spiritual liberation and union with Shiva.</summary>
<keywords>Nātha Yogīs, Haṭha Yoga, Prāṇa and Apāna, Iḍā and Piṅgalā, Gorakhnath, Ajapā-jap, spiritual liberation, internal alchemy</keywords>


--- Page 43 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of the "Avadhūta" or the liberated soul, explaining how the practitioner transcends duality by merging the internal "Sun" and "Moon" to reach the final stage of consciousness.</meta>

Through these practices, he subdues time and becomes a **Yogi who has conquered Time** <term>original: Kālajayī Yogī; one who transcends the cycle of temporal existence</term>. By commingling the "Sun" and the "Moon" <note>Metaphors for the dualistic forces of the body, such as right and left energy channels or heat and coolness</note>, so that they can no longer work independently, the Yogi’s state of mind goes beyond the reach of the senses. This is **Meditative absorption** <term>original: Samādhi; the final state of meditative union and bliss</term> or the final state of the **Spiritual discipline** <note>original: "Yoga"</note> process.

This **Absorption** is the final state of bliss, or the **Non-dual** <term>original: Advaya; the synthesis of all opposites into a single unity</term> or **Two-in-one union** <term>original: Yuganaddha; an esoteric state found in Buddhism where duality is merged</term> state as found in esoteric Buddhism. The word **Non-dual** means a synthesis of all duality in the principle of unity or the achievement of a state where no "two" exists. This principle of non-duality was adopted by **Luipā** <note>One of the earliest and most revered Buddhist masters or "Siddhas" from the 8th-10th century</note> and others, who have given repeated warnings not to go either to the left or the right, but to follow the middle path. The middle nerve in the body is the most important nerve and is called the middle path by the **Enlightened Masters** <term>original: Siddhācāryas; teachers who have attained spiritual perfection</term>.

The human body was conceived as a microcosm—a miniature universe—in which everything found in the macrocosm has a parallel. In Hindu **Systemic spiritual texts** <note>original: "Tantrik Texts"</note> we find similar ideas. **Consciousness and Energy** <note>original: "Śiva and Śakti"; the divine masculine and feminine principles</note> reside in the body. In the lowest of the nerve centres lies **Energy** <note>original: "Śakti"</note> in its dormant state; the Yogi rouses it and makes it follow the **Central energy channel** <term>original: Suṣumṇā; the main subtle nerve running up the spine</term> and unite with **Consciousness** <note>original: "Śiva"</note> in the highest centre, the **Thousand-petalled lotus** <term>original: Sahasrāra</term> in the head. Thus **Consciousness** and **Energy** unite in a state of **Aesthetic Equilibrium** <term>original: Samarasa; a state where all experiences have the same "flavor" of bliss</term> or the **Non-dual** state, which is the **Two-in-one union** state of the **Followers of the Spontaneous Path** <note>original: "Sahajia Buddhists"</note>. 

The nerves on the left and right in the human body represent the principles of duality. The Yogi checks the separate functions of duality and unites them with the middle nerve to function together. This middle nerve or path leads the Yogi to ‘truth’, while the other two on the left and right lead him to pollution. When the two nerves are controlled, the **Unstruck sound** <term>original: anāhta nāda; the spontaneous spiritual sound heard within during deep meditation</term> within the recesses of one’s soul can be heard. By the control of the nerves, it is really meant that the **Downward life-force** <term>original: Apānā</term> and the **Upward life-force** <term>original: Prāṇa</term> should both be arrested by Yogic practice. They are then made to flow through the middle nerve and held there by a **Gesture** <note>original: "mudrā"</note> known as **Breath retention** <term>original: Kumbhaka</term>. Thus the mind of the Yogi becomes steady, his breaths are suspended and the senses are controlled, and he achieves his final aim of attaining **Meditative absorption**.

The **Perfected Masters** <note>original: "siddhas"</note> of Ancient India laid special emphasis on **Cultivation of the body** <term>original: Kāya-sādhana; physical discipline aimed at transforming the mortal body into a divine one</term>. The **Masters of the Natha lineage** <note>original: "Nātha Siddhas"</note>...

<summary>This page describes the physiological and spiritual process of achieving Samadhi by merging dualities—symbolized as the Sun and Moon or Shiva and Shakti—within the central channel of the body. It highlights how the body is viewed as a microcosm where breath control and energy manipulation lead to non-dual realization.</summary>
<keywords>Samadhi, non-duality, central channel, Shiva and Shakti, breath control, macrocosm, Natha Siddhas, Sahajia Buddhism</keywords>

--- Page 44 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion on the yogic conception of the human body as a microcosm, transitioning into the specific views of the Natha tradition regarding physical immortality and its historical relationship with esoteric Buddhism in Bengal.</meta>

...were no exception to this rule. To make the body free from impurities and thus be free from death, was one of the most important factors of the **Master** <term>original: Nātha; a medieval Indian yoga tradition focused on physical and spiritual perfection</term> school. The final aim of the **Perfected Masters** <term>original: Nātha Siddhas</term> was **Liberation while living** <term>original: Jivan-mukti; the state of being spiritually free while still in a physical body</term> or attaining liberation in this body, while living. This state of **Liberation while living** meant immortality for the **Yoga practitioner** <term>original: Yogī</term> in a trans-materialised or pure body. With such a body a **Yoga practitioner** can go wherever he likes and act as he pleases. He can help his disciples in distant lands, by flying through air, or by transmitting his spiritual guidance over a distance of thousands of miles. A particular sect of **Perfected Ones** <term>original: Siddhas</term> in Tibet are well known for such helps to their disciples.

The **Masters of the Lord of Mercury** <term>original: Raseśvara Siddhas; a school of alchemy where mercury is used to attain immortality</term> in India, and some preceptors in the West, are known to have used chemicals to make the body immutable. **Alchemy** <term>original: Rasāyana; the "path of essence," or the science of prolonging life and transmuting metals</term> at one time had gained prominence all over the world. The **Master Perfected Ones** <term>original: Nātha Siddhas</term> were known as a band of death-defying **Yoga practitioners** who traversed the three worlds. In the **Master Cult** <term>original: Nātha Cult</term>, the actual use of chemicals is not mentioned, but reference of the **Alchemical Master** <term>original: Raseśvara Siddha</term> is often found. It is, however, mentioned in the texts that nectar oozing from the Moon in the **Crown center** <term>original: Sahasrāra; the "thousand-petaled" energy center at the top of the head</term> in the head, where **Shiva** <term>original: Śiva; the masculine principle of pure consciousness</term> and **Shakti** <term>original: Shakti; the feminine principle of cosmic energy</term> unite, is drunk by the **Master Yoga practitioners** <term>original: Nātha Yogīs</term> to make them immortal. Thus the attainment of **Liberation while living** by having an immortal body through the practice of **Physical Yoga** <term>original: Haṭha Yoga; a system of yoga focusing on the mastery of body and breath</term> was the final aim of the **Masters**. We find no such idea of a **Living Liberated Yoga practitioner** <term>original: Jivan-Mukta Yogī</term> in **Spontaneous Buddhism** <term>original: Sahajiā Buddhism; an esoteric school emphasizing natural realization over ritual</term>, neither the idea of the culture of the body to attain the **Perfected body** <term>original: Siddha-deha</term>. In the **Mystical verses** <term>original: Caryāpadas; a collection of 8th–12th century tantric songs from Bengal</term> the perfection of the body or **Body culture** <term>original: Kāyasādhanā</term> is considered as a means only for realisation of **The spontaneous state** <term>original: Sahaja; the innate, natural state of enlightenment</term>. This **Spontaneous Buddhism** had once flourished in Bengal. The **Pala dynasty** <term>original: Pāla; the Buddhist rulers of Bengal and Bihar from the 8th to 12th century</term> kings of Bengal built **Monasteries** <term>original: Vihāras</term> and gave land grants to Buddhists. **Pattikera** <term>original: Paṭṭikēra; an ancient city in what is now modern Bangladesh</term> which had been identified with Gopichandra's capital, had a **Monastery** known as the **Golden Stupa** <term>original: Kanakstupa</term>. Tantrik Buddhist scholars **Tilopā**, **Nadapā**, **Advayavajra**, the nun **Mekhalā**, all of whom are mentioned in the list of the **84 perfected ones** <term>original: 84 siddhas</term> flourished in Bengal under the **Palas**. Thus we may conclude that many of the authors of the **Mystical songs** <term>original: Caryās</term>, also lived and preached during the **Pala** period. The **Mystical verses** embody the tenets of **Spontaneous Buddhism**, which forbid the practice of **Postures, gestures, and locks** <term>original: Āsana, Mudrās, Vandhanas</term> as prescribed by **Physical Yoga practitioners** <term>original: Haṭha Yogīs</term>.

The nerve centres called the **Energy centers** <term>original: cakras; focal points of subtle energy in the body</term> hold an important place in **Physical yoga** practice. Union of **Shiva** and **Shakti** is not

<summary>This page explores the Natha school's goal of physical immortality through Hatha Yoga and the drinking of internal nectar, contrasting it with the Buddhist Sahajia school of Bengal which viewed the body only as a tool for spontaneous realization.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the investigation of the Natha tradition's origins, specifically refuting the theory that they were originally a Buddhist sect and instead situating them firmly within the Shaivite (Shiva-worshipping) fold by examining their distinct rituals, physical markings, and dietary habits.</meta>

possible, if the **Yoga practitioner** <term>original: yogī; a student or master of the discipline of yoga</term> is unaware of the '**energy centers**' <term>original: cakras; the focal points of power within the human body</term>. A **Yoga practitioner** <term>original: yogī</term>, who knows the six **energy centers** <term>original: cakras</term> and has been able to unite the dormant **Divine Power** <term>original: Śakti; the feminine creative principle</term> with **the Lord Shiva** <term>original: Śiva; the masculine consciousness</term> (the process known as raising the **Coiled Power** <term>original: Kundalini</term> **Energy** <term>original: Śakti</term>), is known as a **follower of the Kaula path** <term>original: Kaula; a specialized branch of Tantra</term>. The **Kaula followers** <term>original: Kaulas</term> were **Shiva-worshippers** <term>original: Śaivas</term> and not Buddhists. Matsyendra calls himself a **Kaula** <term>original: Kaula</term> in his treatise *The Investigation of Kaula Knowledge* <term>original: Kaula-jñāna-nirnaya</term>.

That the **Natha Masters** <term>original: Nātha Yogīs; the lineage of masters centered on physical and spiritual mastery</term> were **Shiva-worshippers** <term>original: Śaivites</term> can be proved from the sect mark of the **symbol of the womb and phallus** <term>original: Yoni-linga</term>, the organs of generation symbolizing divine procreation, on their right forearm, and of the '**three horizontal ash lines**' <term>original: tripundra</term> (three horizontal lines drawn with sacred ashes) on their forehead. The **Natha Masters** <term>original: Nātha yogīs</term> visit **Shiva-dedicated** <term>original: Śaiva</term> shrines and pierce the cartilages of the ears to wear **Large Ritual Earrings** <term>original: Kundalas</term> like **Shiva** <term>original: Śiva</term>. They wear a woolen holy thread from which is hung a **sacred sound-pendant** <term>original: nāda</term> and a **purifier ring** <term>original: pabitri</term>, or a horn whistle and a brass ring which represent **Shiva** <term>original: Śiva</term> and **his Power** <term>original: Śakti</term>. The **Natha Masters** <term>original: Nātha Yogīs</term> do not necessarily shave their heads and beards like the Buddhists. Neither are they strictly vegetarians; they smoke and use **hemp** <term>original: siddhi; literally "attainment," used as an intoxicant favorite of the deity</term>, which is the favorite intoxicant of **Shiva** <term>original: Śiva</term>. Matsyendra went to Nepal dressed as a **Shiva-worshipping Practitioner** <term>original: Śaiva Yogī</term> to preach **the teachings of Shiva** <term>original: Śaivism</term>. Above all Matsyendra was known as a fisherman and was associated with fish even as a **Yoga practitioner** <term>original: yogī</term>, while non-injury is a special feature of Buddhism.

The **sacred chant** <term>original: mantra</term> of the **Natha Masters** <term>original: Nātha yogīs</term> is '**Shiva-Gorakh**', and they call themselves descendants of **Shiva** <term>original: Śiva</term>, and are thus known as belonging to the **Shiva Lineage** <term>original: Śiva Gotra</term> to the present day. **Gorakh** is deified as **Shiva** <term>original: Śiva</term>, but Matsyendra is deified in Nepal as **the Lord of Compassion** <term>original: Avalokitesvar; a Buddhist savior figure</term>. What can be the reason of this association of the first **Master** <term>original: Nātha</term> guru with Buddhism? Gorakhnath and some other **Master** <term>original: Nātha</term> gurus have been also identified with Buddhist **Naturalists** <term>original: Sahajiās; practitioners of a path emphasizing spontaneity</term>, and on the other hand, we find the names of Buddhist **accomplished masters** <term>original: siddhas</term> Sṛngāripāo and Ramanvajra, associated with those of the **Master** <term>original: Nātha</term> gurus, Gopichandra and Gorakhnath.

We have proved that the **Natha Masters** <term>original: Nātha Yogīs</term> were **Shiva-worshippers** <term>original: Śaivites</term>, why then were there these confused identifications? We may only suggest that the general similarity in practice and in rites and rituals between the Buddhist **Naturalists** <term>original: Sahajiās</term> and the **Natha Masters** <term>original: Nātha Yogīs</term> resulted in these confusions. The Bengali writings of Luipa-Minanath and the fact that Luipa belonged to the '**Red River**' <term>original: Lohita; referring to the Brahmaputra</term> country in **Assam** <term>original: Kamarupa</term> were also responsible for turning a **Kaula practitioner** <term>original: Kaula yogī</term> into a **Naturalist** <term>original: Sahajiā</term> Buddhist.

<summary>This page argues that the Natha Yogi tradition is fundamentally Shaivite rather than Buddhist, pointing to their specific use of ritual ash, earrings, and non-vegetarian habits. It explains the historical confusion between Natha gurus and Buddhist Sahajia masters as a result of overlapping tantric practices in eastern India.</summary>
<keywords>Natha Yogis, Shaivism, Matsyendra, Gorakhnath, Tantra, Kundalini, Sahajia, Kamarupa, ritual iconography</keywords>

--- Page 46 ---
<meta>This page continues the investigation into the origins of the Natha tradition, moving from physical sectarian markers discussed on the previous page to the linguistic and philosophical evidence found in early Bengali, Hindi, and Sanskrit texts.</meta>

We may mention here that while verses in old Bengali by **Minanath** <term>original: Mīnanāth; also known as Matsyendranath, the legendary guru of Gorakhnath</term> have been discovered, none has yet been found by **Gorakhnath** <term>original: Gorakhnāth; the 11th-century yogi and principal organizer of the Natha sect</term>. Gorakh’s **Teachings** <term>original: Upadeśas; spiritual instructions or lessons</term> are commonly found in Hindi and Sanskrit; some scholars even claim Gorakh to be the first Hindi prose writer.

It is not true that the esoteric Buddhist cult, which drifted away from original Buddhism, later on transformed itself into a **Shaivite** <term>original: Śaivite; relating to the worship of the god Shiva</term> cult or vice versa. The element of yoga as practised by both these schools, together with the rites and rituals were neither Buddhistic nor Brahmanic, they were of a common heritage. The similarities can also be traced in the **Jain Couplets** <term>original: Jaina Dohās; rhyming couplets used in medieval Indian literature to convey spiritual truths</term> of the tenth century. The uniformity in spirit, as well as in form with the Buddhist couplets of the same period, is rather striking. Aversions to scholarship and injunctions of religion as preached by the *Pundits* (the learned) are the special features of these couplets. The motto is that Liberation can only be attained when the mind is fixed on **abiding in the true form of the Lord** <note>original: nātha-svarūpeṇāvasthānam</note>¹ and not by outward practices or knowledge of *Shāstras* (scriptures).

The esoteric yogic practices in India have a common background in the idea of the ‘dual’ and ‘non-dual’ state. To destroy the dual and the non-dual state and attain Reality or **the deity of eternal bliss who resides beyond non-duality** <note>original: advaitopari sadānandṛ-devatā</note> ² is the aim. It is only when the **Yogi** <term>original: Yogī; a practitioner of yoga</term> transcends the outer mental processes that he can know God as the experienced ‘Reality’ through his own **spontaneous intuition** <term>original: Sahaja; the innate, natural state of enlightenment</term>. This idea is found in the Buddhist and Jain couplets and in the verses of the Nirgunis (those who worship God without form), like Kabir and Dadu. ‘One-ness’ with God is described as **Innate Absorption** <term>original: Sahaja Samādhi; the attainment of the final spiritual state through one’s own intuition</term> by the Buddhist Sahajiās, and as **Essential Oneness** <term>original: Samarasa; the realization of the unity of the universe amidst all its diversities</term> by the **Natha Yogis** <term>original: Nātha Yogīs; the lineage of masters centered on physical and spiritual mastery</term>. But the practice of the **Natha Yogis** <term>original: Nātha Yogīs</term> differ from that of the Sahajiā Buddhists, who endeavour to attain the non-dual state through the dual. The **Natha Yogis** <term>original: Nātha Yogīs</term> aim to attain that which is beyond the dual and the non-dual state, which yogis call the **Supreme Reality** <term>original: Parampada; literally 'the highest step' or ultimate goal</term>. In describing this state, **Jalandhar Nath** <term>original: Jālandhar Nath; a prominent master in the Natha tradition</term> in his **Statement of Final Doctrine** <term>original: Siddhāntavākya</term> says:

->**"The state that is the form of duality and non-duality, or even beyond both, is the Auspicious One for the yogis."** <note>original: dvaitādvaita-rūpaṃ dvayata uta paraṃ yogināṃ śaṃkaraṃ vā</note><-

(1) **Compendium of the Doctrines of Goraksha** <term>original: Goraksa Siddhānta Sangraha</term> P. 10
(2) *Ibid, P. 10*

<summary>This page argues that the Natha, Buddhist, and Jain traditions shared a common yogic heritage focused on intuitive reality rather than scholarly ritual, specifically highlighting the Natha goal of reaching a "Supreme Reality" beyond duality.</summary>
<keywords>Gorakhnath, Minanath, Sahaja, Samarasa, Parampada, Natha Yogis, Jalandhar Nath, non-duality, yoga philosophy, Goraksa Siddhanta Sangraha</keywords>

--- Page 47 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion on the esoteric yogic practices common to both Buddhist and Shaivite traditions, focusing on the ultimate spiritual state that transcends the concepts of "dual" and "non-dual" mentioned at the end of the previous page.</meta>

# 25

This state is beyond description as it is beyond the dual and the non-dual conception which are common, and so the yogis call this state **"He is just as He is; He is entirely distinct from everything; such a Master is the goal of the Great Adepts"** <note>original: yādṛśaḥ eva tādṛśa evaiti sarvavilakṣaṇa evaitādṛśo nātho mahāsiddhānāṃ lakṣyaḥ</note>: i. e., “It is what it is” (vide **Compendium of the Doctrines of Goraksa** <term>original: Goraksa-Siddhānta Sangraha; a Sanskrit collection of the philosophical tenets of the Natha sect</term>, p. 73.).

Another common background in mediaeval times was the idea of the human body being the epitome of the universe, thus naming the nerves and nerve centres in the body as rivers, mountains etc. The idea was to search for Truth within one’s self. If God was to be found in the universe, we may very well look for Him within ourselves. This was the central idea of the preachings in mediaeval times.

The reverence of the preceptor or **guru** <term>original: guru; a spiritual teacher or guide</term> was another striking feature in mediaeval times, though the conception of different schools vary from one another. The **Nātha yogi’s** <term>original: Nātha yogi; a member of the Shaivite ascetic tradition focused on Hatha Yoga</term> guru is addressed as **"the one whose essence consists of the primordial sound and the creative drop"** <note>original: nādavindukalātmane</note> i. e., he in whom *nāda* and *bindu* have mingled together, such a guru is as great as **Śiva** <term>original: Shiva; the Hindu god of destruction and transformation, and the supreme deity of the Nathas</term>. *Nāda* and *Bindu* are the audible and inaudible forms of sound, and as sound is taken as the basis of creation, the guru is considered to be as good as the Creator, and is honoured as such. This is the special feature of the guru in the Nātha cult. In **Sahajiā Buddhism** <term>original: Sahajiā Buddhism; an esoteric, "natural" path of Buddhism that emphasizes spontaneous spiritual experience</term> the guru is thought of in the form of **"the state of union"** <note>original: Yuganaddha; literally "yoked together," representing the non-dual union of wisdom and compassion</note> i. e., in the state of union with his consort (as that of the Buddhist icons).

**Gopichandra’s** <term>original: Gopichandra; a legendary king who became a yogi and is a central figure in Natha folklore</term> renunciation depict the unquestionable authority of the guru over his pupil. The rescue of **Matsyendranath** <term>original: Matsyendranāth; the founding guru of the Nathas who, according to legend, forgot his divine nature while in the company of women</term> from evil company by **Gorakhnāth** <term>original: Gorakhnāth; the most famous disciple and reformer of the Natha tradition</term> while uttering the guru’s name is another example where a guru, though fallen, is yet a guru to his disciple. Such is the high esteem in which a ‘guru’ was held by his **disciples** <term>original: śiṣyas; students or followers</term>.

Not only in the ‘guru’ aspect but also in some of their practices, the Sahajiā Buddhists thought of the *Yuganaddha-rupa* <note>original: "the form of the union of the two"</note>. But the Nātha Yogis were strict celibates and avoided the company of women, examples of which we find in the ballads in Bengal and elsewhere.

The story of the great yogī Matsyendra, being ensnared by the women of **Kadali** in **Assam**, only shows that worldly pleasure leads a man to death, his forgetfulness of ‘**yoga**’ <term>original: yoga; the discipline of uniting the individual soul with the divine</term> shows, we are by nature forgetful of our immortal self. The women

<summary>This page explores the core philosophical tenets of the Natha tradition, highlighting the role of the guru as a divine creator and the body as a microcosm of the universe. It contrasts the celibate path of the Natha yogis with the "union" practices of Sahajia Buddhism.</summary>

<keywords>Natha Yoga, guru, nada, bindu, Sahajia Buddhism, Yuganaddha, Gopichandra, Matsyendranath, Gorakhnath, macrocosm, celibacy, Assam</keywords>

--- Page 48 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion on the shared foundations of medieval spiritual movements, moving from the philosophical concepts of sound and energy in the body to the historical and legendary figures who shaped these traditions.</meta>

of **Kadali** <term>Kadali; a legendary kingdom of women in folklore, symbolizing worldly temptation and the "snares" of domestic life</term> represent the snares of everyday life. The story is an allegory, showing man’s oblivion of his true immortal state. **Gopichandra’s** <note>Gopichandra was a legendary king who renounced his throne to become a yogi</note> renunciation also shows that the **Yogī** <term>Yogi; a practitioner of yoga, specifically one seeking spiritual liberation</term> is in search of ‘truth’ and ‘immortality’ and as such women are thought of as hindrances on the path of **Yoga** <term>Yoga; here referring to the disciplined spiritual path of the Natha sect</term>.

Thus from various aspects the difference between the **Sahajiā Buddhists** <term>Sahajiā Buddhists; a sect that sought the "natural" or "spontaneous" (Sahaja) path to enlightenment</term> and the **Nātha Yogīs** is easily discernible, yet **Luipā** and **Matsyendranāth** are thought of as identical. **Luipā** wrote the **Dohās** <term>Dohās; poetic couplets or songs used to convey mystical teachings</term> of the **Sahajiā** cult, while **Matsyendra** called himself a **Kaula** <term>Kaula; a member of an esoteric Tantric tradition within Shaivism</term>. **Rajmohan Nātha** <note>A 20th-century scholar of the Natha tradition</note> suggests that there were two **Matsyendras**, one was also known as **Luipā** and wrote the **Mystic Songs** <note>original: Caryās</note> and **“The Determination of Kaula Knowledge”** <note>original: Kaula-jñāna-nirṇaya</note> and advocated the **Natural Way** <note>original: Sahaja</note>. The other was **Matsyendra-Minanāth** <note>Minanāth literally means "Lord of the Fish"</note> who was the first **guru** <term>guru; spiritual teacher</term> of the **Nātha** cult, and adhered strictly to **Nātha-Yoga** practices.

We would like to point out that in the **“The Determination of Kaula Knowledge”** both the names **Minanāth** and **Matsyendranāth** are found in the colophons, and also from different copies of the **“Tantra of the Non-Dual Hero”** <note>original: Akula-Vira-Tantra</note> manuscripts they have been proved by **Dr. P. Bagchi** <note>Prabodh Chandra Bagchi (1898–1956), a renowned scholar of Sino-Indian studies and Tantra</note> to be identical persons. Probably **Rajmohan Nātha** has overlooked this fact. It is true that the doctrine ascribed to **Luipā** is the **“Natural”** <note>original: Sahaja</note> way, as opposed to **Matsyendra’s** difficult practices but **Matsyendra** has also described the **“Natural”** state in his **“Tantra of the Non-Dual Hero.”** So not only **Luipā**, but **Matsyendra** also advocates the **Natural Way**. In the vernacular literature of the **Nātha Yogīs** we very often find mention of the **Natural State**. This **“Natural State”** is the same as the **“Natural State”** of the **Sahajiā Buddhist Songs**. But the **“Natural State”** of **Matsyendra’s** school is the union of **Śiva** and **Shakti** <term>Shiva and Shakti; the masculine and feminine divine principles whose union represents ultimate reality</term>, which was known only to **Kaulas** who were **Śaivas** <term>Shaivas; worshippers of the god Shiva</term>. So there is no reason to call the **Nātha-Yogīs** Buddhists.

It is a fact that since the advent of **Mahayāna Buddhism** <term>Mahayana; the "Great Vehicle" branch of Buddhism emphasizing the path of the Bodhisattva</term> in this country, in spite of differences in views, there was an innate harmony of all religious creeds. The **Nātha** doctrine had many ideas in common with the **Sants** <term>Sants; medieval poet-saints of North India</term>, the **Sufis** <term>Sufis; practitioners of Islamic mysticism</term>, the **Sahajiā Buddhists** and others. In determining the special features of the Philosophical Doctrine of the **Nātha Sect** and in following the steps of their **spiritual practice** <note>original: sādhanā</note>, I have taken help mainly of the authentic texts belonging to the **Nātha** school of **yogīs**. The Tantric practices and also the

<summary>This page explores the historical and philosophical connections between the Natha Yogis and Sahajia Buddhists, specifically addressing the debate over whether the legendary figures Matsyendranath and Luipa were the same person. It concludes that while these groups shared the concept of the "Sahaja" (natural) state, the Natha tradition is distinctly Shaivite, focused on the union of Shiva and Shakti.</summary>

--- Page 49 ---
<meta>This page continues the researcher's investigation into the origins and documents of the Natha tradition. Previously, the text explored the legendary figures of Luipa and Matsyendranath, discussing how their identities overlap across Buddhist and Shaivite traditions. Here, the focus shifts to the linguistic and textual evidence used to reconstruct the history of the sect.</meta>

The **Upanishads** <term>Upanishads; a collection of ancient Sanskrit texts that explore the philosophical foundations of Hinduism</term> have partially helped me reach a decision regarding the practices of the **Nātha yogīs** <term>Nātha yogīs; a medieval Indian monastic order known for their focus on physical and spiritual mastery</term>. These **Nātha yogīs** were a mystic sect who kept their doctrines and practices secret among their disciples. Consequently, there is no other way to determine the correct philosophical doctrine and practices of the **Nātha Sect** than by using their own texts and comparing them with those of other schools.

### Texts Belonging to the Sect and their Importance

The texts belonging to the **Nātha Sect** are mostly unpublished and lie scattered, unknown and neglected in various parts of India. At one time, the **Nātha gurus** <note>Guru; a spiritual teacher or master</note> traveled from one end of India to the other; therefore, a patient search for **manuscripts** <note>original: MSS., an abbreviation of the Latin "manuscriptum"</note> might yield wonderful results, and my own humble attempt in this area has not been in vain. It is, of course, a well-known fact to scholars that it is extremely difficult to find verifiable documents belonging to mystic sects or to judge the authenticity of their authors. 

The texts I have gathered so far come from Jodhpur, Hardwar, Benares, Tanjore, and Madras. These texts are full of incorrect readings, yet they are being published with certain notes in the hope that their doctrines may finally be brought to light. Even if the authorship is disputed in some cases, there is no doubt that these texts belonged to the **Nātha Sect**. The language used is not strictly grammatical, but this was apparently done on purpose so that it could be easily understood by everyone. The **Sant poets** <term>Sant poets; a group of medieval Indian poet-saints who focused on internal devotion rather than external rituals</term> also followed this policy of preaching in the common language, which in their case was Hindi. In the verses of **Gorakhnath** <note>A legendary 11th-century yogi and the primary founder of the Natha movement</note>, one finds a mixture of Hindi, partly **Rajasthani** and partly **Hindustani**; this style was popular throughout Northern India.

The verses of **Matsyendra** <note>A legendary figure considered the first human teacher of the Natha tradition</note> were written in Old Bengali, as discovered and published by **Shāstri** <note>Haraprasad Shastri (1853–1931), a prominent scholar who discovered the earliest examples of Bengali literature</note>. This work is a collection of verses by **Sahajiā Buddhists** <term>Sahajiā Buddhists; a group that practiced a form of Tantric Buddhism focused on the "natural" or "spontaneous" path to enlightenment</term>, and **Shāstri** describes **Nāthism** <term>Nāthism; the religious and philosophical tradition of the Natha sect</term> as another school of **Tantric Buddhism**. ¹

The language of these verses was unique and was known as **“intentional speech”** <term>Intentional speech; a cryptic, symbolic language (known in Sanskrit as Sandhyā-bhāṣā) used by mystics to protect their teachings from the uninitiated while conveying deep truths to disciples</term>, through which their inner doctrines were preached. Scholars have established that these verses date to between the tenth and twelfth centuries; the authors’

names appear in the list of the **84 siddhas** <term>84 siddhas; a legendary group of "perfected" beings in tantric tradition celebrated as masters of physical and spiritual power</term>. These verse writers were known as **Siddhācāryas** <note>original: "Siddhācāryas", meaning "Perfected Teachers"</note> and were mostly Buddhists, so Shastri published them as "**Bauddha Gān and Dohā**" <note>translated as "Buddhist Songs and Couplets"</note>; Bagchi published some **manuscripts** <note>original: MSS.</note> by **Matsyendranāth** under the caption "**Kaula-jñāna-nirṇaya**" <note>translated as "The Investigation of the Knowledge of the Kaula Sect"</note>.

**Gorakhnath's** "**Siddha-Siddhānta-Paddhati**" <note>translated as "The Path to the Doctrines of the Perfected Masters"</note> is now being published with some other texts, belonging to the **Nātha Sect**. "**Siddha-Siddhānta-Paddhati**" is mentioned in "**Haṭha-Yoga-Pradīpikā**" <note>translated as "Light on the Forceful Yoga"</note> as being uttered by **Sree Gorakhnath** and this tradition is still held by the sect. For obvious reasons this has remained a **sealed book** <note>meaning a secret or esoteric text intended only for initiates</note> to the layman, though it was published by the **Nāth-Bramhacharyāsram** <note>a religious hermitage or school for students of the Natha tradition</note> at Hardwar; it was meant only for the members of its own mystic sect and is unavailable. Repeated requests by Dr. Gopinath Kaviraj and Dr. P. Bagchi for a copy of the book have been fruitless. In 1942 my diligent search was rewarded in the form of a transcription of the **manuscript**. A scholar, Sree Giridharilal Vyasji of Benaras, had been to Hardwar, mixed with the yogis, earned their favour and thus obtained a copy of the book. Sree Vyasji had a vast library and helped me with books whenever I needed them. I am deeply indebted to him also for the transcription of "**Siddha-Siddhānta-Paddhati**". Since then I have searched and obtained copies of **manuscripts** of the same **Siddha-Siddhānta-Paddhati** from Jodhpore and Tanjore. In the Tanjore copy, after the **colophon** <term>colophon; an inscription at the end of a manuscript containing information about its production</term> are given four lines in praise of god **Rama**.

Jodhpore was once a very influential centre of **Nāthism**. Maharaja Mansingha of Jodhpore had himself written sixteen books on **Nāthism** and had several others written by his court poets about the middle of the nineteenth century. Dr. Singh gives the list of as many as twenty-five Hindi works about **Gorakhnath** and his cult as mentioned by the state library, Jodhpore. The Maharaja made a collection of **Gorakhnāth's** books and verses, a detailed note of which can be obtained from the Report on Search of Hindi **manuscripts**, 1902, Banaras University.

In Tanjore the **manuscript** is found in Telegu script, a copy of which has been obtained by me, together with another **manuscript** *Yoga-Mārtyanda* <note>translated as "The Sun of Yoga"</note>, by **Sree Gorakhath**. The very existence

In 1951, at Poona, through the help of Principal S. V. Dandekar, I secured a **Marathi Drama**, partly written in **Hindi** by B. Varve. In this drama, **Gorakhnath** is telling **Kanifa** <note>also known as Kanhapa, a prominent disciple in the Natha lineage</note> about **Gopichandra** <note>a legendary king of Bengal who renounced his throne to become a yogi</note>, the king of Sundarpur, near Bengal. **Gopichandra** has confined **Jalandhar** in a well. **Kanifa**, on the other hand, tells **Gorakh** that his guru, **Matsyendra**, is attracted by Queen **Parimala** of the "country of women" in **Maliyal** <note>the Malabar region of Kerala, often depicted in folklore as a land of enchantresses</note>. **Gorakh** says, “Well, you go to Bengal in search of your teacher and I shall rescue my teacher from **Maliyal** and come to Sundarpur.” They bid farewell with the words ***The Command***, ***The Command*** <note>original: "Adesh, Adesh"; the traditional greeting used by Natha Yogis to acknowledge the divine authority or will</note>.

In this drama we find names of places and of the queen which are unknown in Bengal; otherwise, this play is not of much value and is relatively modern.

A copy of the manuscript of "**Self-Knowledge**" <note>original: "Atmabodh"</note>, though obtained from Jodhpore, is not being published in this volume, as it can be found in Dr. Barthwal’s "**The Words of Gorakh**" <note>original: "Gorakh-Vāni"</note> in **Hindi**. I have also obtained a list of another ten books, namely: **The Measure of Fearlessness** <note>original: "Abhayamātra"</note>, **Instruction for the Infidel** <note>original: "Kafērbodh"</note>, **The Chain of Life-Force** <note>original: "Pransaṅkali"</note>, **Ancient History of Creation** <note>original: "Sṛiṣtipurān"</note>, **The Seven Days** <note>original: "Saptabār"</note>, **Instruction for Mankind** <note>original: "Narvébodh"</note>, **The Sixteen Lunar Dates** <note>original: "Soḍastithi"</note>, **The Vision of Instruction** <note>original: "Sikṣadarsan"</note>, and **The Five Measures of Divine Knowledge** <note>original: "Brahma jñāna Panchamātra"</note>. However, most of these have already been published by Dr. Barthwal in his **The Words of Gorakh**. **Instruction for Kabir** <note>original: "Kabirbodh"</note> has been mistakenly published as a work of the **followers of Kabir** <note>original: "Kabirpanth"</note>. I tried to obtain copies of the last two but have been unable to secure them until now. Scholars interested in this subject may do so and study them.

In publishing this volume, the intention is to demonstrate the importance of the manuscripts relating to the **Natha Sect** <term>Nātha Sect; a medieval tradition of yogis who sought physical immortality and spiritual liberation</term>, and at the same time to rescue the texts from perishable materials. In this age, a better understanding of the rites and rituals of the sect, which once held great influence over India, is also necessary. The works are mostly known to be by **Gorakhnath**, the foremost **yogi** of medieval India and the most powerful figure of the **Natha Sect**.

<summary>This page details the author's efforts to catalog and preserve Natha Sect manuscripts from various private and royal libraries across India and Nepal, specifically highlighting works attributed to Gorakhnath and Matsyendra.</summary>

--- Page 53 ---
<meta>This page continues the list of manuscripts being compared by the author, specifically concluding the mention of the Newari script manuscripts found in the library of the Royal Preceptor Hemraja in Nepal, before transitioning to a description of the primary manuscript used for this edition.</meta>

**The Path to the Doctrines of the Perfected Masters** <note>original: "Siddha–Siddhānta–Paddhati"</note> contains a brief explanation of the form of Yoga associated with the name of Gorakhnath. As such, it is a valuable work, though cryptic in nature. Except for the edition from Hardwar <note>a holy city in northern India</note>, as previously mentioned, no further attempt has been made to publish and preserve this original and authentic work.

In publishing this volume, I have used the manuscript from Jodhpur as my model for two reasons:
( i ) as it remains unpublished;
( ii ) as it contains many more details regarding the names of books of the Natha Tradition <term>Nātha Sect; a medieval yoga tradition in India founded by Matsyendranath and Gorakhnath</term>, and other matters, than the copies from Hardwar and Tanjore <note>modern Thanjavur</note>.

The different versions of the same manuscript obtained for comparison, all bearing the name of Lord Gorakhnath <note>original: "Sree Gorakhnāth"</note> in the closing remarks <term>colophon; an inscription at the end of a manuscript containing facts about its production</term>, have been used, and the variations are noted in the footnotes. A summary of the aforementioned manuscript was compiled by a man named Balabhadra at the request of Krishnaraja, but the date of its composition is unknown. This summary, known as **The Compendium of the Doctrines of the Perfected Masters** <note>original: "Siddha–Siddhānta–Saṅgraha"</note>, was published in Varanasi <note>original: "Banaras"</note> under the sponsorship of the Saraswati Bhawan <note>a famous research library</note> and was ably edited by Dr. Gopinath Kaviraj over twenty-seven years ago. Since then, no further attempt has been made to publish the original text. I have also made use of this summary in certain places.

The source texts are inaccurate in many places. There are redundant repetitions, unclear phrasing, and frequent violations of the rules of grammar. However, I have not taken much liberty in correcting them, though some corrections are provided within brackets. I hope this approach will be appreciated by readers and that allowances will be made for such defects.

### The Probable Dates of the Texts

The date of composition for the Bengali verses of Matsyendra <note>the legendary teacher of Gorakhnath and founder of the Natha tradition</note> has been established as the tenth century A.D. According to Dr. H. P. Shastri, who collected the verses from Nepal, they were composed in the ninth century; however, authorities differ on this matter and place them between the tenth and twelfth centuries. Matsyendra's **The Determination of the Knowledge of the Kaula Tradition** <note>original: "Kaula–jñāna–nirṇaya"</note> belongs to the eleventh century; it was written in archaic Sanskrit and was not strictly grammatical.

<summary>The author explains his editorial choice to use the Jodhpur manuscript of the Siddha-Siddhānta-Paddhati due to its unique detail and unpublished status. He also provides historical context regarding the dating of foundational Natha tradition texts by Matsyendra.</summary>

<keywords>Siddha-Siddhānta-Paddhati, Gorakhnath, Natha Tradition, Jodhpur manuscript, manuscript collation, Gopinath Kaviraj, Matsyendra, Kaula-jñāna-nirṇaya, historical dating</keywords>

--- Page 54 ---
<meta>This page continues the introduction to the edition, shifting from a discussion of the text's importance to the linguistic characteristics of the primary sources and the historical evidence used to date the work.</meta>

The <term>manuscripts</term> published in this volume are also written either in the old form of Sanskrit or an old form of Hindi, which is partly Rajasthani and partly Hindusthani.

It is difficult to ascertain the dates of these <term>manuscripts</term> as in the <term>colophons</term> <note>the concluding inscriptions of a manuscript</note> the original dates of composition are not given, but only the dates when the scribes have copied them. However, the probable dates of the texts will be between the fourteenth and seventeenth centuries, the time when the leaders of the sect had finished preaching their cult and had passed into oblivion. It was only then that their disciples became eager to preserve their teachings in writing.

Of the already published books belonging to the <term>Natha</term> <note>a medieval yoga tradition</note> sect, **The Enlightenment of Gorakh** <note>original: "Gorakhbodh"</note> is written in an old form of Hindi and is reported to date from the fourteenth century¹. Another book, **Light on Forceful Yoga** <note>original: "Haṭha-yoga-Pradīpikā"</note> by Svatmarama Muni, probably belongs to the fourteenth century but certainly not earlier².

In **Light on Forceful Yoga**, we find Lord Gorakhnath mentioned as the author of **The Path to the Doctrines of the Perfected Masters** <note>original: "Siddha-Siddhānta-Paddhati"</note>, so it must have been written earlier. A long list of yogis preceding the author of **Light on Forceful Yoga** is also given by him; there we find Matsyendra, Gorakh, Caurangi, and others mentioned.

The copies of the <term>manuscripts</term> collected by me belong to a much later date however. The present edition of **The Path to the Doctrines of the Perfected Masters** is based on the collation of the following:—

I. (a) **MS. from Jodhpore Library, Sardar Museum.** Complete up to the sixth <term>Teaching</term> <note>original: "Upadesha"; a chapter or section of the text</note>, written in <term>Devanagari</term> <note>the script used for Sanskrit and Hindi</note>, contains about 60 pages of <term>manuscript</term>. Author: Lord Gorakhnath.

->Copies by Balaram Sadhu in the year **1945.**<-
<note>original date: V. S. 2002; the Vikram Samvat calendar is approximately 57 years ahead of the Western calendar</note>
->Reference of this is given by me as (Yo)<-
<note>original: (यो)</note>

(b) **MS. from Tanjore.** This <term>manuscript</term> is in fairly good condition. After the <term>colophon</term> are given four lines in praise of the god Rama. Burnell's catalogue No. 9895C.

(2) Indian Historical Quarterly, Vol. XVI., 1940. Article on the date of **Light on Forceful Yoga** by P. K. Gode.

<meta>This page continues the cataloging of primary manuscript sources used for this edition, specifically detailing the physical characteristics and dating of three additional versions of The Path to the Doctrines of the Perfected Masters and a related work on yoga.</meta>

The left column palm-leaf manuscript of 73 leaves, measuring 9” by 1¼” with 5 lines to a page, of **The Path to the Doctrines of the Perfected Masters** <note>original: "Siddha-Siddhānta-Paddhati"</note> is one of three works contained in a single manuscript written in the same hand. In one of these works, the scribe identifies himself as “Seetārām.” In another manuscript copied by the same scribe, the date is given as Saturday, the second lunar day <note>original: "dwitiya"</note> after the New Moon in the month of Gemini <note>original: "Mithuna"</note> during the year of <term>Pramadi</term> <note>a specific year in the sixty-year Jovian cycle used in Indian calendars</note>. The most recent Pramadi year was 1939; judging by its appearance, this manuscript must date to 240 years (four cycles of 60 years) prior, which is 1699 A.D. In 1699, the New Moon of the month of <term>Jyestha</term> <note>the third month of the Hindu calendar</note> fell on a Thursday, meaning the second day was indeed a Saturday. Therefore, this manuscript must have been written around that time.

The script of the manuscript is Telugu; the number of <term>granthas</term> <note>a standard unit of measurement for Sanskrit texts, equal to 32 syllables</note> within it is 700, though Burnell’s catalogue records it as 550. This version has not been printed.

The above information was provided by Sr. K. Vasudev Sastri, B. A., the Research Scholar at the T. M. S. S. Library in Tanjore.

I have given the reference for this manuscript as (तं) <note>The letter "Ta," signifying the Tanjore manuscript</note>.

(c) **Copy from Hardwar.** Transcription in Devanagari characters; complete in six <term>upadeshas</term> <note>teachings or chapters</note>. Sree Gorakhnāth is mentioned as the author in the concluding inscriptions. In this copy, there is an extra verse <note>original: "sloka"</note> on **Forceful Yoga** <note>original: "Haṭha Yoga"</note> which is not found in versions (a) or (b).

I have given the reference for this manuscript as (ह) <note>The letter "Ha," signifying the Hardwar manuscript</note>.

(d) **Compendium of the Doctrines of the Perfected Masters** <note>original: "Sihdha-Siddhānta-Saṅgraha"</note> by Balabhadra; Edited by G. N. Kaviraj; Princess of Wales Saraswati Bhavana Texts No. 13, Banaras, 1925.

I have given the reference for this edition as (का) <note>The letters "Ka," signifying the Kashi (Banaras) edition</note>.
