# SUMMARIES OF THE IMPORTANT TEXTS, WITH SOME CRITICAL NOTES.

## 1. THE FOOTPRINTS OF THE DOCTRINES OF THE PERFECTED MASTERS <note>original: "SIDDHA-SIDDHĀNTA-PADDHATI"</note>.

This manuscript bears the name of the venerable **Gorakhnāth** in the concluding statement of each chapter, which are referred to as <term>Instructions</term> <note>original: "Upadeśa"</note>. The work is composed in rhythmic couplets and consists of six such Instructions. It contains citations from various foundational texts of the **Nātha Yogīs** <note>a medieval order of yogis who sought spiritual liberation through the perfection of the physical body</note>, and addresses the following subjects:

Instruction I. — The Origin of the Body <note>original: "पिण्डोत्पत्तिः" (piṇḍotpattiḥ)</note>.
Instruction II. — Examination of the Body <note>original: "पिण्डविचारः" (piṇḍavichāraḥ)</note>.
Instruction III. — Conscious Perception of the Body <note>original: "पिण्डसंवित्तिः" (piṇḍasaṃvittiḥ)</note>.
Instruction IV. — The Foundation of the Body <note>original: "पिण्डाधारः" (piṇḍādhāraḥ)</note>.
Instruction V. — The Integration of the Body and the Supreme State <note>original: "पिण्डपदयोः समरसकरणम्" (piṇḍapadayoḥ samarasakaraṇam)</note>.
Instruction VI. — The Characteristics of a Liberated Yogi: — 
->The definition of a <term>Fully Liberated Yogi</term> <note>original: "अवधूतयोगिलक्षणम्" (avadhūtayogilakṣaṇam); an Avadhuta is one who has "shaken off" all worldly attachments and dualities</note>.<-

->*Summary of Instruction I.*<-

The **Nātha Yogīs** maintain that, in ultimate truth, there is no such thing as "Creation"; however, to accommodate human understanding, this work describes it. The Supreme Reality manifests itself through six distinct stages into a physical organism, a process known as **Creation** <note>original: "सृष्टिः" (sṛṣṭiḥ)</note>. However, the Supreme Reality, which is termed the **Nameless One** <note>original: "Anāmā"</note>, possesses no inherent power of Creation on its own. It is **The Prime Power** <note>original: "Śakti"</note> that serves as the foundation of Creation, operating through its five developmental forms: **The Innate** <note>original: "निजा" (nijā)</note>, **The Transcendent** <note>original: "परा" (parā)</note>, **The Non-transcendent** <note>original: "अपरा" (aparā)</note>, **The Subtle** <note>original: "सूक्ष्मा" (sūkṣmā)</note>, and **The Coiled Power** <note>original: "कुण्डली" (kuṇḍalī)</note>. The **Nameless One** represents the hidden, essential aspect of existence, while the **Prime Power** represents the visible, active aspect of the world. Neither is capable of creating without the assistance of the other. (This **Nameless One**, also known as the **Supreme State** <note>original: "Parampada"</note>, remains forever **Whole** <note>original: "पूर्ण" (pūrṇa)</note> despite the creation that occurs through its influence. The **Nameless One** is, in other words, the Infinite; it is like subtracting infinity from infinity—the result remains infinity <note>a reference to the mathematical logic found in the Upanishadic invocation: "Taking fullness from fullness, only fullness remains"</note>).

Now, each of the six <term>Individual Bodies</term> <note>original: "pinḍas"</note> through which the **Nameless One** manifests itself contains five factors, and each factor possesses five qualities. Thus, each **Individual Body** is comprised of twenty-five qualities, known as **Essential Realities** <note>original: "Tattvas"</note>.

The six stages of development through which one must pass are known as the **Transcendent** <note>original: "Parā"</note>, **Beginningless** <note>original: "Anādi"</note>, **Primordial** <note>original: "Ādi"</note>, **Great Form** <note>original: "Mahāśākāra"</note>, **Primal Matter** <note>original: "Prakṛti"</note>, and **Embryonic** <note>original: "Garbha"</note> bodies.

<summary>This page introduces the Siddha-Siddhānta-Paddhati, a key text of the Nath tradition attributed to Gorakhnath, which outlines the philosophical evolution of the physical body from the Nameless Supreme Reality.</summary>
<keywords>Siddha-Siddhānta-Paddhati, Gorakhnath, Nath Yoga, Pinda, Anama, Shakti, Creation, Tattvas, Avadhuta, yogic anatomy</keywords>

--- Page 58 ---
<meta>This page continues the summary of "Instruction I: The Origin of the Body" from the Siddha-Siddhānta-Paddhati, elaborating on the transition from the divine states to the physical embryonic form.</meta>

The physical body, with which we are familiar, represents the last stage, namely the <term>Embryonic Body</term> <note>original: "Garbha piṇḍa"</note>; the other five <term>bodies</term> <note>original: "piṇḍas"</note> are progressively subtler states leading up to this physical form. All these stages are described, and the various phases of embryonic growth within the mother's womb are also detailed.

The human body also consists of twenty-five qualities, and as such, it is known as the <term>Individual Soul</term> <note>original: "Jīva"</note> (1.54). Liberation is sought by this soul only when it realizes it is an integral part of <term>the Supreme Divine</term> <note>original: "Śiva"</note>. It is then that the soul seeks the Supreme Reality, or the <term>Highest State</term> <note>original: "Parampada"</note> of the Nātha tradition, and becomes eager for union with Him. Therefore, the individual soul must understand its own origin and nature. With this objective in view, the master **Gorakhanāth** described how the six stages of the body are created, how the individual soul is formed, and the nature of the Supreme. The <term>Great Manifest Body</term> <note>original: "Mahāsākāra piṇḍa"</note> is the Supreme Divine, and from Him we have the figures of Bhairava, Śrīkaṇṭha, Sadāśiva, Īśvara, Rudra, Viṣṇu, and Brahmā, known together as the <term>Eightfold Form</term> <note>original: "mūrt yaṣṭakam"</note> or eight incarnate beings (1.36).

From the final aspect of the Divine, or from the <term>Gaze</term> <note>original: "avalokana"</note> of **Brahmā** <note>the creator deity</note>, we have the union which produces the individual soul within the embryonic body. Thus, from the Supreme Divine, we arrive at the individual soul through six stages. (Consequently, the soul can attain liberation from human birth if it so desires and achieve union with the Divine. This constitutes the core philosophy of the **Nātha Yogīs**.)

In this chapter, the gross and subtle bodies of man are also described. These include the five qualities of speech—namely <term>Transcendent</term> <note>original: "parā"</note>, <term>Visualized</term> <note>original: "paśyantī"</note>, <term>Intermediate</term> <note>original: "madhyamā"</note>, <term>Articulated</term> <note>original: "Vaikharī"</note>, and the <term>Mother-letters</term> <note>original: "mātṛkā"</note>—as well as the seventeen <term>digits</term> <note>original: "Kalās"</note> of the moon, the twelve digits of the sun, the ten nerves, and the ten <term>vital airs</term> <note>original: "vāyus"</note> within the body. These details are highly significant.

*Notes:* In the Hardwar manuscript, an additional couplet on **Haṭha Yoga** <note>the yoga of physical discipline</note> is provided (see footnote No. 26 on section 1.68 of the *Siddha Siddhānta Paddhati*). While nine vital airs are mentioned in the Hardwar copy, the additional name of the <term>Upward-moving Air</term> <note>original: "Udāna Vāyu"</note> is found in the Jodhpur copy, which is the version printed here. The phrase stating that <term>The Supreme Shiva</term> <note>original: "Paraśivaḥ"</note> is <term>born</term> <note>original: "jātaḥ"</note> from the <term>Wheel of Power</term> <note>original: "Śaktichakra"</note> appears in different variations across the manuscripts as follows:
— "Arisen through the sequence of the wheel of power" in the Jodhpur and Tanjore copies. (1.14)
— "Arisen through the sequence of the wheel of power" in the Hardwar copy. (1.16)
— "Indeed born through the sequence of the wheel of power" in the Banaras copy.
— "Born from that through the sequence of the wheel" is perhaps the superior reading.

<summary>This page outlines the Nath Yogic theory of embryology and the soul's descent from the Divine (Shiva) through six stages into the physical body. It also lists the subtle components of the human body, such as levels of speech and vital breaths, noting variations across different historical manuscripts.</summary>
<keywords>Garbha pinda, Jiva, Shiva, Gorakhanath, Nath Yoga, subtle body, vital airs, vayu, levels of speech, Sanskrit manuscripts, Siddha Siddhanta Paddhati</keywords>

--- Page 59 ---
<meta>This page transitions from the formation of the physical body to the internal mystical anatomy described in the second instruction of the Siddha-Siddhānta-Paddhati, moving from the external shell to the inner spiritual centers.</meta>

## Summary of the <term>Second Instruction</term> <note>original: "Upadeśa II"</note>

The real <term>Practitioner of Yoga</term> <note>original: "Yogī"</note> must know the inner environments of the body. This chapter, therefore, deals with the nine nerve-centers, sixteen <term>supports</term> <note>original: "ādhāras"</note>, the three <term>points of concentration</term> <note>original: "laksyas"</note> and the five <term>firmaments</term> <note>original: "vyomans"</note> (inner spaces), which are all located in the body. Concentration on the nerve-centers known as <term>Wheels of Energy</term> <note>original: "Cakras"</note>, confers on the practitioner superhuman powers. The various <term>sacred seats</term> <note>original: "pīṭhas"</note>, <term>internal locks</term> <note>original: "bandhas"</note> and some <term>ritual seals</term> <note>original: "mudrās"</note> mentioned in this chapter may suggest some connection with the Buddhist <term>Thunderbolt Vehicle</term> <note>original: "Vajrayāna"</note> School, especially the <term>Thunderbolt Seal</term> <note>original: "Vajrolī Mudrā"</note> (2.13). In the Banaras copy of the *Compendium of the Teachings of the Adepts* <note>original: "Siddha Siddhanta Sangraha"</note>, the <term>Thunderbolt Seal</term> is mentioned as the <term>Little Thunderbolt</term> <note>original: "Vajrolikā"</note> (2.18) and the <term>earth-center</term> <note>original: "bhūcakra"</note>, <term>liberation-center</term> <note>original: "nirvāṇacakra"</note> and <term>sky-center</term> <note>original: "ākāśacakra"</note> are called the <term>uvula</term> <note>original: "ghaṇtikā"</note>, <term>mental dissolution</term> <note>original: "monolaya"</note> and <term>divine center</term> <note>original: "brahmacakra"</note>.

The <term>Bee Cave</term> <note>original: "Bhramara Guhā"</note> which is mentioned in connection with the <term>Thunderbolt Seal</term> is an important factor, as it is from this place in the lower region that <term>Deep Absorption</term> <note>original: "Samādhi"</note> starts. The Saint poets, Kabir and others, also speak of the <term>Bee Cave</term>, but the location is different; they describe it to be beyond the <term>Thousand-Petaled Lotus</term> <note>original: "Sahasrāra"</note> or <term>Opening of the Divine</term> <note>original: "Brahmaraṇdhra"</note> which is in the highest cerebral region.

*Additional Note:* The <term>Six Nerve-Plexuses</term> <note>original: "Ṣatcakra"</note> well known in Yogic practices, are the <term>root-support</term> <note>original: "mūlādhāra"</note>, <term>one's own base</term> <note>original: "svādhiṣṭhāna"</note>, <term>city of jewels</term> <note>original: "maṇipūra"</note>, <term>unstruck sound</term> <note>original: "anāhata"</note>, <term>pure center</term> <note>original: "viśuddha"</note> and <term>command center</term> <note>original: "ājñā"</note>, but the Nātha Practitioners have added to this list three more: the <term>palate</term> <note>original: "tālu"</note>, <term>liberation</term> <note>original: "nirvāṇa"</note> and <term>sky/ether</term> <note>original: "ākāśa"</note>. This <term>Palate Center</term> <note>original: "Tālucakra"</note> is important, as at this place there is the <term>tip of the uvula</term> <note>original: "Ghaṇtikā-liṅgamukha"</note> and the <term>Royal Tooth</term> <note>original: "Rājadanta"</note> or the tenth door of the <term>Conch-like nerve</term> <note>original: "Śaṅkhinī"</note>. Here the practitioner meditates on the ‘<term>Sun</term>’ <note>original: "Surya"</note> (representing the void or emptiness) and his mind merges in it. It is again at this point that the nectar flows from the moon and the practitioner turns his tongue backwards (known as the <term>Sky-Walking Seal</term> <note>original: "Khecarī mudrā"</note>) and saves it from falling into the destructive fire, situated in the navel. By this <term>seal</term>, the practitioner attains immortality and <term>Oneness with the Divine</term> <note>original: "Śivahood"</note>. The physical body is made up of five factors as has been described before (1.61), namely: <term>Activity</term> <note>original: "Karma"</note>, <term>Desire</term> <note>original: "Kāma"</note>, <term>The Moon</term> <note>original: "Candra"</note>, <term>The Sun</term> <note>original: "Sūrya"</note>, and <term>Fire</term> <note>original: "Agni"</note>. Of these, the primary elements are the Sun and the Moon. The Sun and fire are generally held to be the same. The moon represents the quintessence in the form of juice and possesses the <term>digit of cessation</term> <note>original: "nivṛtti kalā"</note> which is the digit of nectar (1.64). Hence the attempt to save the nectar from the moon falling into the fire or sun.

<summary>This page outlines the internal mystical anatomy of the body, detailing nine energy centers and the practice of preserving "vital nectar" to achieve immortality and spiritual union.</summary>
<keywords>Natha Yoga, Chakras, Khechari Mudra, Vital Nectar, Internal Anatomy, Bee Cave, Spiritual Immortality</keywords>

--- Page 60 ---
<meta>This page continues the technical comparison of the subtle body's internal anatomy, specifically focusing on the differences in the names and characteristics of the energy centers between two primary manuscript versions of the Siddha-Siddhānta texts.</meta>

The <term>Sky-Wheel</term> <note>original: "Ākāśa-cakra"</note> is called the <term>Divine Wheel</term> <note>original: "Brahmacakra"</note> in the Banaras copy. It is the place where the <term>Void</term> <note>original: "Śunya," referring to the state of pure potentiality or emptiness</note> is meditated upon while reciting the <term>Swan-Soul mantra</term> <note>original: "Haṁsa mantra," the natural sound of the breath, often translated as "I am He"</note>. There is some variation regarding the number of petals in the lotuses of these <term>energy centers</term> <note>original: "cakras"</note>. The system described in the *Footsteps of the Doctrines of the Adepts* <note>original: "Siddha-Siddhānta-Paddhati"</note> is not exactly the same as the one commonly used in standard yoga practice. Therefore, I believe a comparative table is appropriate here.

### THE NERVE PLEXUSES

| Common names in Yoga texts | Names found in the *Footsteps* <note>Siddha-Siddhānta-Paddhati</note> | Names found in the *Compendium* <note>S.S. Sangraha, Banaras copy</note> |
|:---|:---|:---|
| **1. Root Support** <note>original: "Mūlādhāra"</note> | <term>Divine Wheel</term> (triangular) (<term>Sacred Seat of Desire-Form</term> <note>original: "Kāmarūpa Pīṭha"</note>) | <term>Divine Wheel</term> (<term>Sacred Seat of Desire-Form</term>) |
| **2. Self-Standing Center** <note>original: "Svādhiṣṭhāna"</note> | <term>Self-Standing Center</term> (six petalled) (<term>Sacred Seat of Udiyana</term> <note>original: "Uḍḍiyāna Pīṭha"</note>) | <term>Self-Standing Center</term> (four petalled) (<term>Sacred Seat of Udiyana</term>) |
| **3. City of Jewels** <note>original: "Maṇipūra"</note> | <term>Navel Wheel</term> (<term>Coiled Energy</term> <note>original: "Kuṇḍalinī"</note> with 5 coils) | <term>Navel Wheel</term> |
| **4. Unstruck Sound** <note>original: "Anāhata"</note> | <term>Heart Support</term> (eight petalled) | <term>Heart Wheel</term> |
| **5. Pure Center** <note>original: "Viśuddha"</note> | <term>Throat Wheel</term><br><term>Palate Wheel</term> | <term>Throat Wheel</term><br><term>Palate Wheel</term> |
| **6. Command Center** <note>original: "Ājñā"</note> | <term>Brow Wheel</term> | <term>Little Bell</term> <note>original: "Ghaṇṭikā," referring to the uvula</note> |
| **7. Thousand-Petalled** <note>original: "Sahasrāra"</note> | <term>Liberation Wheel</term><br>(<term>Sacred Seat of Jalandhara</term>)<br><term>Sky Wheel</term><br>(16 petalled)<br>(<term>Sacred Seat of the Full Mountain</term> <note>original: "Pūrṇagiri Pīṭha"</note>) | <term>Mental Dissolution</term><br><term>Divine Wheel</term> |

---

*Notes:—* It is fascinating to observe that in the *Compendium*, both the first and the last energy centers are referred to as the <term>Divine Wheel</term> <note>original: "Brahmacakra"</note>. Beneath the first Divine Wheel—which is a three-fold triangular region—lies a <term>nerve-root</term> <note>original: "kāṇḍa"</note> where the <term>Divine Energy</term> <note>original: "Śakti"</note> resides. The <term>Sacred Seat of Desire-Form</term> is located here. Other sacred seats are indicated in the table above. The common <term>Command Center</term> or Brow Wheel is not mentioned in this specific manuscript copy; instead, we find the <term>Little Bell</term>.

<summary>This page provides a comparative analysis of the body's energy centers (chakras) between the Siddha-Siddhānta-Paddhati and the Banaras copy of the Siddha-Siddhānta-Sangraha, highlighting variations in terminology and petal counts.</summary>
<keywords>nerve plexuses, chakras, Brahmacakra, Kundalini, Pithas, yogic anatomy, Sanskrit manuscripts, Siddha-Siddhānta-Paddhati</keywords>

--- Page 61 ---
<meta>This page continues the overview of the subtle body's internal structure as described in the Siddha-Siddhānta texts, transitioning from a list of energy centers to the philosophical concept of the body as a mirror of the universe.</meta>

The <term>Thousand-Petaled Lotus</term> <note>original: "Sahasrāra"</note>, <term>Liberation</term> <note>original: "Nirvāṇa"</note>, and the <term>Dissolution of the Mind</term> <note>original: "Manolaya"</note> in the table mentioned previously carry more or less the same meaning, as it is here that the <term>Yoga practitioner</term> <note>original: "Yogī"</note> attains freedom and the mind is absorbed into the <term>Supreme State</term> <note>original: "Parampada"</note>.

Another point of interest is that the <term>Coiled Energy</term> <note>original: "Kuṇḍalinī"</note> within the <term>Navel Wheel</term> <note>original: "Nābhi-cakra"</note> is said to have five coils instead of the usual eight.

The text describes sixteen <term>supports</term> <note>original: "ādhāras"</note>—internal focal points—upon which the mind must be fixed to achieve different spiritual results. Three <term>targets</term> <note>original: "lakṣyas"</note> or points of concentration and five <term>firmaments</term> <note>original: "vyomans"</note> (inner spaces) lead the practitioner to various stages of purity and perfection. The <term>postures</term> <note>original: "āsanas"</note> and the <term>eight limbs of Yoga</term> <note>original: "Yogāṅgas," the sequential stages of practice</note> all assist the practitioner in attaining <term>Meditative Absorption</term> <note>original: "Samādhi," which is the fixation of the mind on the Supreme Spirit</note>.

In this Chapter, there is a definition of a true Yoga practitioner (Verse 2. 61).

->**The nine wheels, the sixteen supports, the three targets, and the five firmaments;**<-
->**He who does not know these properly is a practitioner in name only. (Verse 31)**<-
<note>original Sanskrit: "navacakraṃ kalādhāraṃ trilakṣyaṃ vyomapañcakam | samyagetanna jānāti sa yogī nāmādhārakaḥ || 31 ||"</note>

This means that one must be properly familiar with the nine <term>energy centers</term> <note>original: "cakras"</note>, sixteen supports, three targets, and five firmaments to be considered a practitioner in the true sense.

### *Summary of Teaching III* <note>original: "Upadeśa III"</note>

This chapter deals with the knowledge of the body and demonstrates the identity of the <term>individual body</term> <note>microcosm</note> with the <term>entire universe</term> <note>macrocosm</note>; in other words, whatever exists in the outside world is also present within the human body. Thus, the seven nether worlds are located in the portion of the body below the base of the spinal column, and the twenty-one celestial regions are in the upper region of the body. The dwelling place of the god <term>Śiva</term> is in the <term>Gate of the Palate</term> <note>original: "Tāludvāra," the uvula area</note> (3.5). The <term>Root of the Soft Palate</term> <note>original: "Lambikāmūla"</note>, the <term>Peak of Union</term> <note>original: "Śṛṇgāra"</note>, and the <term>Three Peaks</term> <note>original: "Trikūṭa"</note> are all described in this chapter. In verse 2.27, we find "<term>Gollāṭa</term>" (in the Hardwar manuscript copy) or "<term>Kollāṭa</term>" (in the Jodhpur copy) mentioned. The nine <term>regions</term> <note>original: "khaṇḍas," such as India and Kashmir</note> are located in the nine "avenues" or openings of the body. The hills, rivers, planets, gods, perfected beings, the sun and the moon (which are the two eyes), clouds (which are the tears), vines, plants, and so on, all have their counterparts in the human body. Our happiness is Heaven, our sorrow is Hell, and our <term>Actions</term> <note>original: "Karma"</note> are what bind us to this world—without which we might attain <term>Liberation</term> <note>original: "Mukti"</note>. When one’s mind does not

<summary>This page defines the requirements of a true Yogi through the mastery of internal anatomy and introduces the core concept of 'microcosm and macrocosm,' where the entire universe is mirrored within the human body.</summary>
<keywords>Sahasrara, Kundalini, Nine Cakras, Sixteen Adharas, Microcosm, Macrocosm, Shiva, Karma, Liberation, Subtle Body</keywords>

--- Page 62 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion on spiritual attainment, moving from the technical details of the subtle body's internal focal points to the philosophical union with the Divine and the nature of the power that supports life.</meta>

register any sense-impression, it is then only that one is united with <term>Śiva</term> <note>The Supreme Being or ultimate consciousness</note>. Be such a person awake or asleep, he is always enlightened about himself. Liberation and peace are achieved by such a person *i.e.*, he who has realized the identity of the world with himself. Moreover, the <term>Supreme Soul</term> <note>original: "Paramātmā"</note> is manifest in every human soul. This is how the knowledge of the body is gained.

*Note*: The nine <term>energy centers</term> <note>original: "chakras"</note> are also mentioned in the *Yoga-Viṣaya* <note>The "Subject of Yoga," an ancient Sanskrit text</note>, which is given later on. In this we find the mention of *gollāṭa* and not *kollāṭa* <note>These are technical terms for specific anatomical or mystical points within the head according to yogic traditions</note>.

### Summary of Teaching IV <note>original: "Upadeśa"</note>.

The fourth chapter treats of the support of the body which is <term>Śakti</term> <note>The Divine Feminine Power or Energy of the universe</note>. Thus, <term>Śakti</term> is called the <term>support of the physical form</term> <note>original: "piṇḍādhāra"</note> and is all-pervading. The different aspects of <term>Śakti</term> are described. <term>Śakti</term> is both <term>manifested form</term> <note>original: "kula"</note> and <term>the formless unmanifest</term> <note>original: "akula"</note>. <term>Manifested form</term> is the <term>effect</term> <note>original: "kārya"</note> and the <term>unmanifest</term> is the <term>cause</term> <note>original: "kāraṇa"</note> or <term>instrument</term> <note>original: "nimitta"</note>. When <term>Śakti</term> is unruffled and quiet, it is the same as the Divine <term>Śiva</term> or the <term>unmanifest</term>. In her revealed form, <term>Śakti</term> is fivefold: <term>supreme</term> <note>original: "parā"</note>, <term>appearance</term> <note>original: "bhāsa"</note>, <term>existence</term> <note>original: "sattā"</note>, <term>I-ness</term> <note>original: "ahantā"</note>, and <term>fragmented aspects</term> <note>original: "kalā"</note>.

The <term>unmanifest</term> <note>original: "Akula"</note> is unique in the sense that it assumes <term>manifested form</term> <note>original: "kula"</note>, thence descends into <term>worldly conduct</term> <note>original: "vyavahāra"</note> *i.e.*, service (4.12). But the Divine <term>Śiva</term> without <term>Śakti</term> is incapable of creation (4.13). This couplet is quoted from the *Vāmakeśvara Tantra*, as mentioned in the Jodhpur copy. From the Divine <term>Śiva</term> we have <term>Śakti</term>, who manifests herself in various forms and then again <term>Śakti</term> rests in the Divine (4.12).

*Additional note*: In the *Kaula-jñāna-nirṇaya* <note>The "Determination of Secret Knowledge" attributed to the yogi Matsyendranath</note> by <term>Matsyendranath</term> <note>The legendary founder of the Nath lineage of yogis</note> in Chapter II, we find that at the time of destruction the <term>Power</term> <note>original: "Śakti"</note> merges into the <term>Divine</term> <note>original: "Śiva"</note>, who Himself is then merged into the <term>Power of Action</term> <note>original: "Kriyāśakti"</note>. The two aspects of <term>Power</term>, which are <term>Action</term> <note>original: "Kriyā"</note> and <term>Will</term> <note>original: "Icchā"</note>, merge into the <term>Power of Knowledge</term> <note>original: "Jñāna Śakti"</note>. In this state it is the ultimate <term>Supreme Śiva</term> <note>original: "Paraḥśiva"</note> who alone remains. The world then ceases to exist. We may note here that this is the reverse of Creation, which has been described in our text, the *Way of the Settled Conclusions of the Perfected Ones* <note>original: "Siddha-Siddhanta Paddhati"</note> Teaching I.

What is then the <term>Supreme State</term> <note>original: "Parampada"</note> and who is the Divine <term>Śiva</term>? The <term>Supreme State</term> which we have described as <term>Nameless</term> <note>original: "anāmā"</note> before has no <term>causal nature</term> <note>original: "kāraṇa bhāva"</note> in it, but the Divine who is <term>unmanifest</term> <note>original: "akula"</note> has the <term>cause</term>...

<summary>This page summarizes the fourth chapter of the Siddha-Siddhānta Paddhati, focusing on the relationship between Śiva (consciousness) and Śakti (power). It explains that the physical body is supported by Śakti, who exists in both manifest and unmanifest forms, and highlights the philosophical concept that creation and dissolution are the flow of this divine energy.</summary>
<keywords>Śakti, Śiva, manifestation, unmanifest, causality, Matsyendranath, Kaula tradition, Supreme State, energy centers, spiritual liberation</keywords>

--- Page 63 ---
<meta>This page continues the detailed exploration of the body's spiritual anatomy, moving from the realization of the Supreme Soul to the specific mechanics of the Divine Power (Shakti) as it moves through the internal energy centers.</meta>

The <term>state of being</term> <note>original: "bhāva"</note> underlies it; thus, the Divine can manifest Itself as the <term>embodied form</term> <note>original: "kula"; refers to the manifest universe and the physical body</note>. This is the difference between the <term>Supreme State</term> <note>original: "Parampada" or "anāmā" (the nameless)</note> and <term>Śiva</term> <note>original: "akula"; the formless source</note>. There is a quotation from the *Lalitāsvacchanda* (4.11) which says that the formless and the embodied are essentially one; so, after manifestation, the embodied merges back into the formless (4.12).

The embodied power is both static and kinetic, or <term>unawakened</term> and <term>awakened</term> <note>original: "aprabuddha" and "prabuddha"</note> (4.14). As the support of the body, the <term>Divine Power</term> <note>original: "Śakti"</note> is known as <term>Kuṇḍalinī</term> <note>The "coiled" energy residing at the base of the spine</note>; she is either asleep in a coil in her unawakened state, or awake in her awakened state. The practitioner of Yoga who aims at the purity and stability of his body awakens the <term>Kuṇḍalinī</term>, and in this role, the Power is called <term>the one who effects the perfection of the physical body</term> <note>original: "piṇḍasansiddhikāriṇī"</note> (1) in its kinetic or conscious aspect.

The Primal Power is one, but she becomes manifest as minor powers in the different nerve centers. Although these powers are truly one (4.17), those located at the <term>Root Center</term> <note>original: "Mūlādhāra"</note>, the <term>Heart Center</term> <note>original: "Hṛdaya"</note>, and the <term>Crown Center</term> <note>original: "Sahasrāra"</note> are respectively called the **lower** <note>original: "adhaḥ"</note>, **middle** <note>original: "madhya"</note>, and **upper** <note>original: "ūrdhva"</note> powers or <term>kuṇḍalinīs</term>. The first, or lower power, keeps one attached to the outer world. The second, or middle power, is twofold: one being lower or gross and the other higher or subtle. Then, the third or upper power leads one to the attainment of the <term>Supreme State</term> (4.16).

Owing to the upward tension effected by the so-called <term>root lock</term> <note>original: "mūlādhāra bandha"; a physical yoga technique</note>, the Power rises upwards (4.18). The creation and dissolution of the world follow from the **expansion** <note>original: "prasara"</note> and **contraction** <note>original: "saṃkoca"</note> of this Power, who is known as the **Root of the Universe** (4.19).

**Note**: In this chapter, there is one important factor which should be noted: namely, that the **Middle Power** is twofold, one being lower and gross, while the other is higher and subtle. The phrase <term>through the skill of its own expansion</term> <note>original: "svaprasāracāturyatayā"</note> (4.21) in connection with the gross aspect signifies the sound "U" <note>original: "ukāra"</note> of Tantricism. That "U" sound is the manifestation or <term>blossoming state</term> <note>original: "unmeṣabhāva"</note> of the **Knowledge** <note>original: "Jñāna"</note> of Śiva. Because in its gross form it is the support of the world and <term>possesses form</term> <note>original: "sākāra"</note>, it has the "skill of its own expansion." In her subtle form, she is <term>without form</term> <note>original: "nirākāra"</note>. The <term>perfected masters</term> <note>original: "siddhas"</note> rouse this power to attain salvation. The stability and purity of the body—<term>bodily perfection</term> <note>original: "dehasiddhi"</note>—is possible only when she is roused.

Such are the different aspects of the Divine Power, although she is one with Śiva.

<meta>This page transitions from the final ontological verse of Chapter 4—emphasizing the non-duality of Shiva and Shakti—to a summary of Chapters 5 and 6, which focus on the practical attainment of spiritual equilibrium and the traits of a master.</meta>

Within <term>Shiva</term> <note>the formless, masculine aspect of the Divine</note> resides <term>Power</term> <note>original: "Śakti"; the manifest, feminine energy</note>, and within <term>Power</term> resides <term>Shiva</term>. One should perceive no distinction between them, just as one sees no difference between the moon and the moonlight. (4.26)

The difference between <term>Shiva</term> and <term>Power</term> is unreal and is due to our ignorance.

### Summary of Instruction V

This chapter deals with the manner in which the <term>microcosmic body</term> <note>original: "piṇḍa"</note> may be equilibrated with the <term>Supreme State</term> <note>original: "Parampada"</note>. This equilibrium is known as <term>spiritual integration</term> <note>original: "samarasa"; literally "having the same taste or flavor"</note>, but one has to know all about the different <term>bodily aspects</term> <note>original: "piṇḍas"</note> before one can attain this stage. How is this knowledge to be attained? Discussions and reasonings will lead one nowhere; it is only through the Grace of the <term>Teacher</term> <note>original: "Guru"</note> that knowledge of the Self and the <term>Supreme State</term> is obtained (5.3, 4) and equilibrium attained. The Grace of the <term>Teacher</term>, which is something beyond words, enables the seeker to shake off all the <term>supernatural powers</term> <note>original: "siddhis"</note> he has attained and realize the <term>state of permanent absorption</term> <note>original: "nirutthāna"; literally "no-arising," a state where the mind is fully stilled</note> and obtain union of the body with the <term>Supreme State</term> (5.7). When the self is revealed, there is supreme delight, and there arises a consciousness of pure and eternal joy (5.8). This is the highest state in which the <term>practitioner</term> <note>original: "Yogī"</note> realizes the non-dual state of the Self and the <term>Supreme State</term>.

A <term>practitioner</term> who aspires for such a state should wear the <term>conch shell</term> <note>original: "śankha"</note> and <term>ritual seals</term> <note>original: "mudrā"; likely referring to the earrings or insignia worn by the Nath sect</note> and stay in a secluded place, but he should not attach too much importance to the outer symbols.

After the Union, the <term>practitioner</term> conquers death and decay, has the knowledge of everything, and all <term>supernatural powers</term> come to him automatically (5.33 etc.). But all this is unattainable without the aid of a <term>True Teacher</term> <note>original: "Sadguru"</note>, who is held in high esteem by the <term>Nātha practitioners</term> <note>original: "Nātha Yogīs"</note>. There is no one greater than the Teacher (5.63). The last verse, which is important, says that the <term>True Teacher</term> can show in an instant the <term>Supreme State</term> which is otherwise unattainable by the seeker (5.66, 80).

### Summary of Instruction VI

This chapter gives the distinguishing characteristics of a <term>liberated mystic</term> <note>original: "Avadhūta Yogī"; one who has "shaken off" worldly attachments</note> who alone can be deemed to be a <term>perfected being</term> <note>original: "siddha"</note>. Such a <term>practitioner</term> is free from all bondages and has complete control over all the senses, the mind, and the body.

In order to give the reader an idea of the comparative superiority of a <term>liberated mystic</term> to the <term>perfected beings</term> of the other...

<summary>This page concludes the fourth chapter of the text by asserting the inseparable unity of Shiva and Shakti, and provides summaries of chapters five and six concerning the spiritual integration of the body and the role of the Guru.</summary>
<keywords>Shiva, Shakti, spiritual equilibrium (samarasa), microcosmic body (pinda), Supreme State (Parampada), Guru's Grace, supernatural powers (siddhis), Avadhuta Yogi, Nath tradition</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the discussion on the qualities of a Master from the Siddha-Siddhānta-Paddhati and introduces a new manuscript, the Yoga Viṣaya, attributed to the sage Matsyendra.</meta>

sects, the author says that he combines in himself the attainments of the <term>Perfected Masters</term> <note>original: "Siddhas"</note> of all the sects known by different technical names (VI 34-71). It is a <term>liberated ascetic</term> <note>original: "Avadhūta Yogī"; literally one who has "shaken off" all worldly attachments and dualities</note> who initiates a pupil into the doctrine of the <term>Natha Yogis</term> <note>a medieval tradition of yoga in India focusing on Hatha Yoga and spiritual liberation</note>. That doctrine combines in itself the good points in the doctrines of all the systems of philosophy, and it is such a liberated ascetic who is praised as a <term>True Teacher</term> <note>original: "Sadguru"</note> (VI. 72).

*Additional Note*: In the Banaras edition of the *Compendium of the Teachings of the Perfected* <note>original: "Siddha-Siddhānta-Sangraha"</note> there is an additional chapter which we do not find in the manuscripts of the *Guide to the Teachings of the Perfected* <note>original: "Siddha-Siddhānta-Paddhati"</note>. This seventh chapter deals with the <term>supernatural powers</term> <note>original: "siddhis"</note> which a practitioner can attain but which he should disregard if he wants to attain the <term>supreme state</term> <note>original: "parampada"</note>.

## 2. DISCOURSE ON YOGA <note>original: "YOGA VIṢAYA"</note>.

This is a manuscript of a work by Matsyendra (also known as <term>Lord of the Fish</term> <note>original: "Mīnanātha"</note>) as gathered from its concluding notes. The Teacher who is held in high esteem is described therein as <term>beyond all mental conception</term> <note>original: "bhāvanātīta"</note>. He alone can show the right path to his disciple. The disciple must have faith in his Teacher, and must have good qualities. When the difference between the Teacher and his <term>student</term> <note>original: "Śiṣya"</note> disappears, then the relationship between the two is that of <term>equal attainment</term> <note>original: "sama-siddhi"</note>, meaning equality (I.6).

The author, referring to the nerve centers of the body, says that a <term>practitioner of the Kula tradition</term> <note>original: "Kaula"</note> knows the six centers which are the <term>root</term> <note>original: "ādhāra"</note>, <term>sacral</term> <note>original: "svādhiṣṭhāna"</note>, <term>navel</term> <note>original: "maṇipūra"</note>, <term>heart</term> <note>original: "anāhata"</note>, <term>throat</term> <note>original: "viśuddhi"</note>, and <term>third eye</term> <note>original: "ājñā"</note> (1.8). The place of the <term>Stainless One</term> <note>original: "Niranjana"; referring to the pure, unconditioned state of the Divine</note> is beyond these centers—that is, <term>beyond the wheel-centers</term> <note>original: "cakrātīta"</note> (1.10).

The author also describes the main nerves, and having given the locations of the sounds 'A', 'U', and 'M' of the <term>sacred sound Om</term> <note>original: "omkāra"</note>, says that by pronouncing them, realization comes and the three <term>spiritual knots</term> <note>original: "granthis"</note> of the Creator, the Preserver, and the Destroyer <note>original: "Bramhā, Viṣṇu and Rudra"</note> are broken.

N. B.: The most important feature of this manuscript is the names of the nine <term>energy centers</term> <note>original: "cakras"</note> beyond the third eye, namely: the <term>Three Peaks</term> <note>original: "Trikūṭa"</note>, the <term>Three Strikes</term> <note>original: "trihaṭa"</note>, the <term>Whirling Circle</term> <note>original: "gollāṭa"</note>, the <term>Summit</term> <note>original: "śikhara"</note>, the <term>Thunderbolt</term> <note>original: "vajra"</note>, the <term>Sacred Sound</term> <note>original: "omkāra"</note>, the <term>Upward-Facing</term> <note>original: "ūrdhvanākha"</note>, and the <term>Face between the Brows</term> <note>original: "bhruvormukham"</note> (1.20). Either by contracting the <term>sun</term> <note>original: "ravi"</note> or by piercing these nine centers, the 'moon' <note>the source of spiritual nectar</note> can be seen (1.20-21).

The eight names given to the eight coils of the <term>Serpent Power</term> <note>original: "Kuṇḍalinī"</note> are mentioned in 1.22. By awakening this power, the nine doors <note>the nine openings of the body</note> are closed and the <term>attainment of the thunderbolt-cluster</term> <note>original: "vajra-gumphā siddhi"</note> is attained (1.23).

<summary>This page concludes the description of the ideal Natha Guru as a universal master and introduces the Yoga Visaya by Matsyendra, detailing a system of chakras and the spiritual importance of the Guru-disciple relationship.</summary>
<keywords>Avadhuta, Natha Yoga, Matsyendra, Chakras, Kundalini, Guru-Shishya, Omkara, spiritual knots</keywords>

--- Page 66 ---
<meta>This page continues the "Discourse on Yoga" attributed to Matsyendra, specifically detailing the internal sensory experiences of the practitioner, before transitioning into a description of the "Awakening to the Immortal Current" by Gorakhnāth.</meta>

When the sound, that is, <term>Divine Resonance</term> <note>original: "nāda"</note> is heard in the <term>Seat of Brahma</term> <note>original: "Brahmasthāna"; a point at the crown of the head or within the subtle body</note>, the <term>Conch-like nerve</term> <note>original: "śaṅkhinī"</note> showers nectar; thereby the six <term>energy centers</term> <note>original: "cakras"</note> are purified (liberated) and ‘knowledge’ is manifested. By this ‘lamp of knowledge’ one can know God, and on knowing Him one should offer to Him the ‘flower’ which is his mind and thus worship Him. God should be known as the Self, because the formless is manifest everywhere in form. Also a man by breathing 1600 times <note>Traditional Hatha Yoga texts usually cite 21,600 breaths per day; the 1600 here may be a specific manuscript variant or shorthand for a specific practice session</note> a day, utters the <term>Swan mantra</term> <note>original: "haṁsa" mantra, representing the sound of the breath: "ham" (out) and "sa" (in)</note>, thereby saying “I am He” (1.29). Śiva resides in the space between the eyebrows in the form of <term>Divine Light</term> <note>original: "jyoti"</note> and the <term>Seat of Brahma</term> (mentioned above) is free from either heat or cold.

### 3. AWAKENING TO THE IMMORTAL CURRENT <note>original: "AMARAUGHA PROBODHA"</note>

This manuscript is that of a work by Gorakhnāth <note>The legendary 11th-century yogi and founder of the Kanphata monastic order</note>. It mentions the names of Ādināth, Mīnanāth and Chauraṅgināth as <term>Perfected Masters</term> <note>original: "siddhas"</note>.

The distinctive features of the four yogas, <term>Absorption</term> <note>original: "laya"</note>, <term>Forceful</term> <note>original: "haṭha"</note>, <term>Incantation</term> <note>original: "mantra"</note>, and <term>Royal</term> <note>original: "rāja"</note> are mentioned. The <term>Royal Yoga</term> <note>original: "rājayoga"</note> is divided into two sections --- <term>medicinal</term> <note>original: "oṣadhi"</note> and <term>spiritual</term> <note>original: "ādhyātmaka"</note>. <term>Forceful Yoga</term> <note>original: "haṭha"</note> is also of two kinds. <term>Perfection and stability of the body</term> <note>original: "dehasiddhi"</note> cannot be attained without <term>Royal Yoga</term> (1.8). The <term>fearless ascetic</term> <note>original: "aghorī"</note> who performs various acts, is unable to obtain a perfect body. The mind should be controlled to enable one to control the vital air (1.9).

Note: This couplet is also found in the *Lamp of Forceful Yoga* <note>original: "Haṭhayoga Pradīpikā"</note> (4.14) with a slight variation in the second line, where the <term>spontaneous practice</term> <note>original: "sahajoli"</note> is mentioned.

Next is mentioned a book, ‘The Sacred Casket,’ <note>original: "Śri Sampuṭa"</note> in which Śiva tells Pārvatī, his consort, that ‘time’ <note>implying death or decay</note> is not greater than the ‘soul.’ Medicines cannot save one; the great physicians, Caraka <note>Ancient Indian physician and author of the medical treatise Caraka Samhita</note> and others have all died. <term>Vital Point</term> <note>original: "Bindu"</note> and <term>Divine Resonance</term> <note>original: "Nāda"</note> are the two great medicines which are present in every human being; those who have no Teacher <note>original: "Guru"</note> and are unaware of these, naturally face death (1.12, 13).

There is only one <term>Immortal Current</term> <note>original: "Amaraugha"</note> which is known as <term>Royal Yoga</term> (1.17).

The four kinds of <term>disciples</term> <note>original: "śiṣyas"</note> and the four yogas are then described. How <term>Body-Perfection</term> <note>original: "Kāyasiddhi"</note> is obtained (1.28), what are the <term>Great Seal</term> <note>original: "mahāmudrā"</note> and <term>Great Lock</term> <note>original: "mahābandha"</note>, where is the <term>field of liberation</term> <note>original: "kedār"</note>, and what is the <term>confluence of three channels</term> <note>original: "tri-veṇī"</note> (1.35), how the four <term>sacred seats</term> <note>original: "pīṭhas"</note> are penetrated by the vital air (1.39) etc., are all given.

<summary>This page concludes a description of the Yoga Viṣaya regarding internal nectar and light, then introduces the Amaraugha Probodha by Gorakhnāth, which outlines the four types of Yoga and emphasizes internal spiritual "medicine" over external physical remedies.</summary>
<keywords>Nāda, Brahmasthāna, Cakras, Gorakhnāth, Amaraugha Probodha, Haṭhayoga, Rājayoga, Bindu, Dehasiddhi, Siddhas, Caraka</keywords>

--- Page 67 ---
<meta>This page continues the summary of manuscripts attributed to the Natha tradition, specifically concluding the "Awakening to the Immortal Current" (Amaraugha Probodha) and beginning the description of the "Sun of Yoga" (Yoga Mārtaṇḍa).</meta>

The four conditions while practicing <term>union</term> <note>original: "yoga"</note> are as follows:

->The beginning, the vessel, and then the stage of acquaintance is the third;<-
->The stage of completion is the fourth; in all practices of union, the stages of yoga are fourfold.<- <note>original Sanskrit: "ārambhaśca ghaṭaścaiva pratyayaśca [usually paricayaśca] tṛtīyakaḥ / niṣpattiḥ sarvayogeṣu yogāvasthā caturvidhā"</note>

These are described in detail (1.45). Then <term>Lord Gorakhnāth</term> <note>The 11th-century founding master of the Kanphata yogis</note> tells us how one can avoid death and conquer ‘time’ and be like <term>Shiva</term> <note>The Hindu deity of transformation and the ultimate yogi</note>. This <term>supernatural power</term> <note>original: "siddhi"</note> is known as the <term>Attainment of the Immortal Current</term> <note>original: "Amaraugha-Siddhi"</note> and can be attained in ten months (1.64, 65). Then the <term>yogi</term> <note>A practitioner of yoga</note> knows the non-dual state and there is equilibrium of <term>consciousness</term> <note>original: "citta"</note>, which is life, and <term>non-consciousness</term> <note>original: "acitta"</note>, which is death. Such a <term>yogi</term> is <term>one liberated while living</term> <note>original: "jīvanmukta"</note>, and he can do what he pleases. 

One should practice the four <term>yogas</term> <note>Traditionally Mantra, Laya, Hatha, and Raja yoga</note> and find out the results for himself. As no medicinal books can cure a patient, so no theoretical knowledge can make one a <term>yogi</term>; such is the advice of <term>Lord Gorakhnāth</term>.

### 4. THE SUN OF YOGA <note>original: "YOGA MĀRTAṆḌA"</note>

This manuscript is by <term>Gorakhnāth</term>, and it begins with salutations to the <term>spiritual teacher</term> <note>original: "guru"</note>. Then it goes on to define <term>union</term> <note>original: "yoga"</note>, which is the fruit of <term>revealed scripture</term> <note>original: "śruti"</note>, and says that <term>yoga</term> should be practiced by those who are pious and desire to dispel the agonies of the world. 

The six parts of <term>yoga</term> are mentioned. The <term>physical postures</term> <note>original: "āsanas"</note>, of which the <term>Accomplished Pose</term> <note>original: "siddhāsana"</note> and the <term>Lotus Pose</term> <note>original: "padmāsana"</note> are the best, are fully described, as they destroy disease and the six <term>impurities</term> <note>original: "vikāras"; usually referring to the six "poisons" or changes of the mind/body such as desire and anger</note>. Then the <term>vital foundations</term> <note>original: "ādhāras"; internal focal points for meditation</note> are mentioned with the different <term>seats of power</term> <note>original: "pīṭhas"</note> (2.10). 

Of the 72,000 nerves, 72 are considered important, of which ten are the most important. Of these, the <term>Conch-like nerve</term> <note>original: "śaṅkhinī"</note> is the tenth nerve. The <term>yogi</term> should have knowledge of all these <term>energy channels</term> <note>original: "nāḍīs"</note> or nerves (17-19). 

Then the <term>unuttered prayer</term> <note>original: "ajapā-gāyatrī"; the natural sound of breathing</note> and the eight kinds of <term>coiled energy</term> <note>original: "kuṇḍalī śakti"</note> are described (33). 

The <term>root lock</term> <note>original: "mūlabandha"</note>, <term>upward flying lock</term> <note>original: "oḍḍyāna"</note>, <term>throat lock</term> <note>original: "jālandhara"</note>, <term>sky-walking seal</term> <note>original: "khecarī mudrā"</note>, <term>great seal</term> <note>original: "mahāmudrā"</note>, the peculiarities of the <term>sacred sound Om</term> <note>original: "omkāra"</note>, etc., all find a place in this manuscript (47-76). 

How <term>breath control</term> <note>original: "prāṇāyāma"</note> should be performed by the <term>yogi</term> is further described (77-113). 

Then <term>withdrawal of the senses</term> <note>original: "pratyāhāra"</note>, the <term>heart energy center</term> <note>original: "anāhatacakra"</note>, the <term>throat energy center</term> <note>original: "viśuddha cakra"</note>, the eight qualities of the <term>nectar of immortality</term> <note>original: "amṛta"</note>, and the benefit obtained from this <term>nectar</term> are given. 

The different kinds of <term>concentration</term> <note>original: "dhāraṇā"</note> for a <term>yogi</term> are <term>Earth-stability</term> <note>original: "haristhāyinī"</note>, <term>Water-stability</term> <note>original: "vāruṇī"</note>, <term>Fire-stability</term> <note>original: "vaiśvānari"</note>, <term>Air-stability</term> <note>original: "vāyavī"</note> and <term>Space-stability</term> <note>original: "nabho"</note>, and for the ordinary man—

<summary>This page concludes the Amaraugha Probodha by describing the four stages of yoga and the state of living liberation, before introducing the Yoga Mārtaṇḍa, which details yogic anatomy, postures, and breath control techniques.</summary>
<keywords>stages of yoga, Gorakhnāth, jīvanmukta, Yoga Mārtaṇḍa, nāḍīs, mudrās, prāṇāyāma, cakra</keywords>

--- Page 68 ---
<meta>This page continues the summary of the "Sun of Yoga" (Yoga Mārtaṇḍa) by Gorakhnāth, focusing on the higher stages of practice including concentration, meditation, and pure absorption. It then begins the description of the "Secret Doctrine of Gorakh" (Gorakh Upaniṣad), which outlines the metaphysical origins of the individual and the universe within the Natha tradition.</meta>

The "arrestor," "sleeper," "burner," "whirler," and "dryer" are the five <term>fixed concentrations</term> <note>original: "dhāraṇās," referring to the internal focus on the five elements: earth, water, fire, air, and ether</note> (143-154).

<term>Meditation</term> <note>original: "Dhyāna"</note> is classified as either "with form" or "formless" <note>original: "saguṇa" and "nirguṇa"</note>. The text then describes how to perform these practices with the help of different <term>yogic gestures</term> <note>original: "mudrās"</note> (157-170).

The <term>yogi</term> <note>A practitioner of yoga</note> exists without any <term>limiting conditions</term> <note>original: "upādhi"</note>. By "limiting condition," the text means a lack of proper knowledge regarding the true nature of things (173). Then follows <term>pure absorption</term> <note>original: "samādhi"</note>, which is the union of the soul and the mind (176).

**Note:** Found in this manuscript are many verses which are similar to the verses found in the *Light on Forceful Yoga* <note>original: "Haṭhayoga Pradīpikā," a foundational 15th-century manual</note>. Although we find much more detail regarding <term>postures</term> <note>original: "āsanas"</note> and other techniques in the *Light on Forceful Yoga*, this *Sun of Yoga* <note>original: "Yoga-Mārtaṇḍa"</note> by Gorakhnāth deals concisely with all the important aspects of yoga. Hence, we might consider this an important manuscript belonging to the <term>Natha Yogis</term> <note>A medieval religious movement in India that combined yoga, alchemy, and Shaivism</note>.

### 5. THE SECRET DOCTRINE OF GORAKH <note>original: "GORAKH UPANIṢAD"</note>

This manuscript is written in a mixture of Hindustani and Rajasthani.

It explains the meaning of the *Secret Doctrine of Gorakh*, as this book is well known among the <term>perfected masters</term> <note>original: "siddhas"</note>. This book, which brings liberation to the devotee, describes how the <term>Lord</term> <note>original: "Īśvara"</note> and the <term>Individual Soul</term> <note>original: "Jīva"</note> were products of the <term>Great Elements</term> <note>original: "Mahābhuta"</note>, which in turn were the product of an <term>indescribable substance</term> <note>original Sanskrit: "anirvacanīya padārtha," an undefinable object</note>.

This entity is beyond both form and the formless. It is <term>free from the distinction of duality and non-duality</term> <note>original: "dvaitādvaitā rahita"</note>, its <term>essence is eternal bliss</term> <note>original: "sadananda svarūpa"</note>, and it is the Natha <note>The "Lord" or "Master" of the tradition</note>.

In the Natha cult, the "god" or deity is beyond even <term>non-duality</term> <note>original: "advaita"</note>, but possesses the powers of <term>will, knowledge, and action</term> <note>original: "icchā, jñāna, and kriyā śaktis"</note> through which the <term>physical body</term> <note>original: "piṇḍa"</note> and the <term>universe</term> <note>original: "brahmāṇḍa"</note> are created.

The "one" manifests itself into nine forms, who are Satyanath, Santoshnath, Kurnmanath, Adinath, Sarvanath, Matsyendranath, his son, and his disciple, the venerable Gorakhnath.

**Note:** The name of Matsyendranath's son is omitted and the name of Dandanath is not mentioned in these lines. Later on, we find the name of Dandanath.

How the <term>Eternal Lord</term> <note>original: "Nirantarnath"</note> is attainable is explained here as follows: there are two ways—the "family" and the "beyond-family" <note>original: "kula" and "akula"</note>. The <term>path of the family</term> <note>original: "kulamārga"</note> is the <term>Way of Power</term> <note>original: "Śaktipatha"</note>, while the <term>beyond-family path</term> <note>original: "akulamārga"</note> is the <term>Way of Consciousness</term> <note>original: "Caitanyapatha"</note>.

The Natha, who is beyond duality and non-duality, becomes non-dual, then dual, and then again the nine Nathas for all practical purposes. The meanings of the names of the nine Nathas are given.

<summary>This page concludes the summary of the Yoga Martanda, focusing on advanced meditation, and begins an analysis of the Gorakh Upanisad's cosmogony and the nine founding masters of the Natha tradition.</summary>
<keywords>Gorakh Upanisad, Yoga Martanda, Natha Yogis, samadhi, dhyana, dharana, Nine Nathas, Matsyendranath, Gorakhnath, kula and akula, Shakti, consciousness</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the summary of the "Secret Doctrine of Gorakh" (Gorakh Upaniṣad) and related Natha literature, transitioning from the description of meditation and absorption to a list of specific texts and songs attributed to the legendary masters of the lineage.</meta>

*Note* : In this manuscript, the names of the "Manual on the Doctrines of the Perfected Masters" <note>original: "Siddha-Siddhanta-Paddhati," a central text of the Natha tradition</note> and of the "Manual for the Disciple" <note>original: "Śiṣya-Paddhati"</note> are mentioned as important reference books.

The special qualities of the <term>teacher</term> <note>original: "guru"</note> and the <term>student</term> <note>original: "śiṣya"</note> are given. The eight parts of yoga, and the meaning of the <term>liberated one</term> <note>original: "avadhuta," literally one who has "shaken off" all worldly attachments and dualities</note> are explained.

### 6. SONG OF THE RESPECTED MATSYENDRA <note>original: "MATSYENDRAJI KĀ PADA"</note>

The glories of the name Matsyendranāth <note>The legendary founder of the Natha tradition and teacher of Gorakhnāth</note> have been revealed. He is manifest in all holy places, so why seek for others? We should only worship Matsyendranāth; such is the strain of this small <term>song</term> <note>original: "pada," a short devotional poem intended to be sung</note>.

There is another song also giving some indications of the name Macchendranāth <note>An alternate spelling of Matsyendranāth</note>.

### 7-10. THE SAYINGS OF BHARTHARI, CHIRPAT, GOPICHAND AND JALANDHARI PĀO <note>original: "SABDIS"</note>

These are the eulogies of the different yogis, who had forsaken worldly pleasures to gain the <term>Supreme State</term> <note>original: "Parampada"</note> or the <term>Nature of the Natha Masters</term> <note>original: "Nāthasvarūpa"</note>. The language is a mixture of the colloquial and pure Rājasthānī <note>A language from the Rajasthan region of western India</note>.

<summary>This page describes the qualities of teachers and students within the Natha tradition and summarizes devotional songs and sayings attributed to foundational yogis like Matsyendranāth and Bharthari.</summary>

<meta>This page serves as the formal title page for the manuscript, marking the transition from the previous summary of Natha literature to the specific treatise attributed to the master Gorakshanath. It establishes the authority of the text within the Siddha tradition.</meta>

## Composed by the Respected Gorakshanath <note>original: "Śrī-Gorakṣanātha-kṛta"</note>

# THE MANUAL ON THE DOCTRINES OF THE PERFECTED MASTERS <note>original: "Siddha-Siddhānta-Paddhati"</note>

This foundational text, attributed to the 11th-century master **Gorakshanath**, is a cornerstone of the <term>Natha Tradition</term> <note>original: "Nātha Sampradāya," a medieval Indian movement that synthesized Hatha Yoga, Tantra, and asceticism</note>. The title itself reveals the scope of the work: 

*   **Siddha**: Refers to a <term>Perfected Master</term> <note>An enlightened yogi who has attained spiritual and physical perfection</note>.
*   **Siddhanta**: Means <term>Established Doctrine</term> <note>The final philosophical conclusions or "proven truths" of a school of thought</note>.
*   **Paddhati**: Refers to a <term>Manual</term> or "path" <note>A systematic guide or compendium of practices</note>.

Together, the title signifies a complete system of the philosophy and practices held by the lineage of perfected yogis. This manuscript focuses on the relationship between the <term>Microcosm</term> <note>the individual human body</note> and the <term>Macrocosm</term> <note>the universe or the divine body</note>, teaching that the truth of the entire cosmos is contained within one's own physical form.

<summary>This page is the formal title page of the Siddhasiddhantapaddhati, attributing the work to the legendary yogi Gorakshanath. It introduces the central manual of the Natha lineage's philosophical and yogic doctrines.</summary>

<keywords>Gorakshanath, Siddhasiddhantapaddhati, Hatha Yoga, Natha Sampradaya, Siddha, philosophy, title page</keywords>

--- Page 71 ---
<meta>This page transitions from the general introduction of the Natha tradition to the specific opening verses and philosophical foundation of the Siddha-Siddhanta-Paddhati. It establishes the lineage and the cosmogony—the theory of how the universe and the human body emerge from the Nameless absolute.</meta>

->॥ Propitiousness ॥<-

# # Composed by the Respected Gorakshanath—
# THE MANUAL ON THE DOCTRINES OF THE PERFECTED MASTERS

## The First Instruction

### ॥ Salutations to the Respected Ganesha ॥ <note>The elephant-headed deity invoked at the start of Hindu texts to remove obstacles.</note>

> Having bowed to the Primal Lord <note>original: "Ādinātha," referring here to Shiva, the first guru of the lineage</note>, who is joined with Power <note>original: "Śakti"</note> and is the Guru of the world, I, Gorakshanath, shall speak the Manual on the Doctrines of the Perfected Masters. ॥ 1 ॥

> <gloss>2</gloss> In this contemplation of the Truth, there is no [real] origin of the Universe <note>original: "Aṇḍa," literally "the egg"</note> or the Body <note>original: "Piṇḍa," the individual form</note>. Nevertheless, for the sake of worldly understanding, I shall speak according to the true tradition. ॥ 2 ॥

> This teaching, beginning with the origin of the body, is thoroughly well-known in the Siddha school. [The topics are:] the origin of the body, the investigation of the body, the consciousness of the body, the support of the body, the state of perfect equilibrium between the body and the ultimate state, and <gloss>1</gloss> the Respected Eternal Body of the <term>Avadhuta</term> <note>original: "Avadhūta," a liberated soul who has "shaken off" all worldly attachments and dualities</note>. ॥ 3 ॥

> When there is no self-creator, no cause, no manifested power <note>original: "Kula"</note> or pure consciousness <note>original: "Akula"</note>, then there exists only the Unmanifest, the Supreme <term>Brahman</term> <note>The ultimate, infinite reality</note>, the Nameless. ॥ 4 ॥

> That "Nameless" is itself beginningless, perfected, one alone, without birth or death, renowned in the doctrines of the perfected masters. Its own Power <note>original: "Śakti"</note>, which possesses the quality of will alone, is well-known. ॥ 5 ॥

> <gloss>4</gloss> By its <gloss>3</gloss> merely turning towards manifestation, the Supreme Power <note>original: "Parā Śakti"</note> arose. By its mere vibration <note>original: "Spandana"</note>, the Lower Power <note>original: "Aparā Śakti"</note> arose. ॥ 6 ॥

> From that, through the mere sense of "I-ness" <note>original: "Ahantā"</note>, the Subtle Power arose. From that, the <term>Kundalini</term> <note>The "coiled" spiritual energy that resides in the body</note> power, characterized by perception, emerged. ॥ 7 ॥

---

1 - Respected Eternal Avadhuta. 2 - No. 3 - The state of turning towards. 4 - Of that. 5 - The sense of "I-ness."

<summary>Gorakshanath opens the text by explaining that while the physical body and universe are ultimately illusions, he will describe their origin from the "Nameless" absolute through various stages of Power (Shakti), culminating in the Kundalini.</summary>

<keywords>Gorakshanath, Adinatha, Pinda, Shakti, Brahman, Kundalini, Avadhuta, cosmogony, Natha tradition</keywords>

--- Page 72 ---
<meta>This page continues the cosmogony of the Siddha-Siddhanta-Paddhati, detailing the five-fold qualities of the various stages of Power (Shakti) and how they combine to form the Supreme Body (Para-pinda). It then transitions into the stages of the absolute reality—the Non-Successive, the Supreme State, the Void, and others.</meta>

Eternity, stainlessness, motionlessness, lack of appearance <note>original: "nirābhāsatā," referring to a state where no objects are perceived separately from consciousness</note>, and non-arising—these five qualities belong to the **Innate Power** <note>original: "nijā śakti"</note>. ॥ 8 ॥

Existence, immeasurability, undividedness, immediacy, and unmanifested-ness—these five qualities belong to the **Supreme Power** <note>original: "parā śakti"</note>. ॥ 9 ॥

Vibration, clarity, expansion, bursting forth, and radiance—these five qualities belong to the **Immanent Power** <note>original: "aparā śakti"</note>. ॥ 10 ॥

Partlessness, continuity, immovability, certitude, and freedom from conceptualization—these five qualities belong to the **Subtle Power** <note>original: "sūkṣmā śakti"</note>. ॥ 11 ॥

Fullness, reflectivity, intensity, rising upward, and inward-facing—these five qualities belong to the **Coiled Power** <note>original: "kuṇḍalinī śakti," the fundamental energy often visualized as a sleeping serpent at the base of the spine</note>. ॥ 12 ॥

Thus, in the state of Power, through the combination of these five-fold qualities, the **Supreme Body** <note>original: "para-piṇḍa," the cosmic or archetypal body of the absolute</note> is produced. ॥ 13 ॥

->And it is said:<-

The Innate, the Supreme, the Immanent, the Subtle, and the Coiled—in these five-fold ways, arising through the progression of the wheel of powers, the Supreme Shiva is born as the Body. ॥ 14 ॥

The Non-Successive <note>original: "aparamparam," a state where time and sequence do not exist</note>, the Supreme State, the Void, the Stainless, and the Supreme Self. From the Non-Successive, mere vibration <note>original: "sphurattā"</note> is produced; from the Supreme State, mere contemplation <note>original: "bhāvanā"</note> is produced; from the Void, mere self-existence is produced; from the Stainless, self-perception <note>original: "svasākṣātkāra"</note> is produced; and from the Supreme Self, the Supreme Self is produced. ॥ 15 ॥

Spotlessness, incomparability, boundlessness, formlessness, and non-arising—these are the five qualities of the **Non-Successive**. ॥ 16 ॥

Partlessness, extreme subtlety, immovability, innumerability, and boundlessness—these are the five qualities of the **Supreme State**. ॥ 17 ॥

---

<gloss>6</gloss>—In the "Kuṇḍalinī" [variant spelling] (H.), regarding the "Kuṇḍalinī" (K.). <gloss>7</gloss>—Arisen through the progression (H.), by the progression indeed (K.). <gloss>8</gloss>—In Shiva (K.). <gloss>9</gloss>—Mere self-perception is produced (H.). <gloss>10</gloss>—Support-ness (H.).

<summary>This page defines the twenty-five qualities of the five stages of Power (Shakti) and describes the emanation of the Supreme Body (Para-pinda) from the absolute through stages like the Void and the Supreme Self.</summary>
<keywords>Nija Shakti, Para Shakti, Apara Shakti, Sukshma Shakti, Kundalini Shakti, Para-pinda, Shiva, Aparamparam, Parama-pada, cosmogony, five qualities</keywords>

--- Page 73 ---
<meta>This page continues the catalog of the "bodies" (pindas) within the Siddha tradition's cosmology. Having previously discussed the Supreme Body (Para-pinda), it now concludes the description of the Primordial Body (Anadya-pinda) and describes the First Body (Adya-pinda). Each "body" or stage of manifestation is defined by five principles, each having five qualities, totaling twenty-five qualities per stage.</meta>

Absorption <note>original: "līnatā," also read as playfulness or blueness in some manuscripts</note>, fullness, the state beyond mind <note>original: "unmanī," a meditative state where the thinking mind is transcended</note>, flickering, and deep immersion—these five qualities belong to the **Void** <note>original: "śūnyam"</note>. ॥ 18 ॥

Truthfulness, innateness <note>original: "sahajatva," the natural or spontaneous state of being</note>, equilibrium of flavors <note>original: "samarasatva," the state where all experiences are integrated into one "taste" of consciousness</note>, attentiveness, and all-pervasiveness—these five qualities belong to the **Stainless** <note>original: "nirañjanam"</note>. ॥ 19 ॥

Imperishability, impenetrability, the quality of being uncuttable, the quality of being unburnable, and indestructibility—these are the five qualities of the **Supreme Self** <note>original: "paramātmā"</note>. Thus, these are the five principles and twenty-five qualities of the **Primordial Body** <note>original: "anādya-piṇḍa"</note>. ॥ 20 ॥

->And it is said:<-

Through these five qualities—the Non-Successive, the Supreme State, the Void, the Stainless, and the Supreme Self—the **Primordial Body** is produced. ॥ 21 ॥

From the Primordial Body comes Supreme Bliss; from Supreme Bliss comes Awakening; from Awakening comes the Arising of Consciousness; from the Arising of Consciousness comes Illumination; and from Illumination comes the "I am He" Consciousness <note>original: "so’ham-bhāva," the realization of identity between the individual and the Divine</note>. ॥ 22 ॥

Vibration, joy, enthusiasm, stillness, and eternal happiness—these five qualities belong to **Supreme Bliss** <note>original: "paramānanda"</note>. ॥ 23 ॥

Arising, exultation, manifestation, expansion, and radiance—these five qualities belong to **Awakening** <note>original: "prabodha"</note>. ॥ 24 ॥

True being, discrimination, agency <note>original: "kartṛtva," the power to act</note>, knighthood <note>original: "jñātṛtva," the power to know</note>, and autonomy—these five qualities belong to the 
->**Arising of Consciousness** <note>original: "cid-udaya"</note>. ॥ 25 ॥<-

Changelessness, partlessness, freedom from conceptualization, equanimity, and rest—these five qualities belong to **Illumination** <note>original: "prakāśa"</note>. ॥ 26 ॥

I-ness <note>original: "ahantā"</note>, unbroken sovereignty, self-hood, the capacity for universal experience, and omniscience—these five qualities belong to the **"I am He" Consciousness**. ॥ 27 ॥

Thus, these are the five principles and twenty-five qualities of the **First Body** <note>original: "ādya-piṇḍa"</note>. ॥ 28 ॥

->And it is said:<-

Supreme Bliss, Awakening, the Arising of Consciousness, the varied Illumination, and ending with the "I am He" Consciousness—the **First Body** arises, endowed with these great principles and qualities. ॥ 29 ॥

<meta>This page transitions from the abstract realization of consciousness ("I am He") described on the previous page into the manifestation of the physical universe and the human form. It outlines how the "First Body" (Adya-pinda) differentiates into the five great elements and how these elements constitute both the macrocosm (the Great Formed Body) and the microcosm (the individual human body).</meta>

From the First Body comes the **Great Ether**; from the Great Ether, the **Great Air**; from the Great Air, the **Great Fire**; from the Great Fire, the **Great Water**; and from the Great Water, the **Great Earth**. ॥ 30 ॥

Space, the absence of gaps, the quality of being untouchable, a blue color, and the possession of sound—these are the five qualities of the **Great Ether** <note>original: "Mahākāśa," the most subtle element that provides the room for all other things to exist</note>. ॥ 31 ॥

Movement, circulation, touch, drying, and a smoke-like color—these are the five qualities of the **Great Air** <note>original: "Mahāvāyu," representing the principle of motion and kinetics</note>. ॥ 32 ॥

The power of burning, the power of cooking <gloss>13: also read as "purifying power"</gloss>, heat, light, and a red color—these are the five qualities of the **Great Fire** <note>original: "Mahātejas," representing energy, digestion, and transformation</note>. ॥ 33 ॥

Flow, nourishing <note>original: "āpyāyanam," the act of satiating or refreshing</note>, liquidity, taste, and a white color—these are the five qualities of the **Great Water** <note>original: "Mahāsalila"</note>. ॥ 34 ॥

Grossness <note>physical density</note>, variety of forms, hardness, smell, and a yellow color—these are the five qualities of the **Great Earth** <note>original: "Mahāpṛthvī"</note>. Thus, these five elements and their twenty-five qualities constitute the **Great Formed Body** <note>original: "Mahāsākāra-piṇḍa," the macrocosm or the universe as a visible, structured entity</note>. ॥ 35 ॥

That very body is **Shiva**; from Shiva comes **Bhairava**; from Bhairava, **Shrikantha**; from Shrikantha, **Sadashiva**; from Sadashiva, **Ishvara**; from Ishvara, **Rudra**; from Rudra, **Vishnu**; and from Vishnu, **Brahma**. This is the **Eightfold Form** <note>original: "mūrtyaṣṭakam"</note> of the Great Formed Body. ॥ 36 ॥

From the gaze of that Brahma, the **Nature-Body** <note>original: "Prakṛti-piṇḍa," the individual physical body</note> was produced in the forms of man and woman. That body consists of five sets of five components. ॥ 37 ॥

Bone, flesh, skin, vessels <note>nerves or channels</note>, and hair—these five qualities represent the **Earth** element within the body. ॥ 38 ॥

Saliva, urine, semen, blood, and sweat—these five qualities represent the **Water** element. ॥ 39 ॥

Hunger, thirst, sleep, radiance <note>original: "krānti," can also mean luster or beauty</note>, and laziness—these five qualities represent the **Fire** element. ॥ 40 ॥

Running, leaping <gloss>14: also read as "wandering" or "wavering"</gloss>, stretching, contracting, and restraining—these five qualities represent the **Air** element. ॥ 41 ॥

<meta>This page continues the detailed categorization of the "Formed Body" (Sākāra-piṇḍa). Having established the five physical elements in the macrocosm on the previous page, the text now transitions into the psychological and energetic components of the microcosm—the internal organs, the qualities of nature (gunas), and the powers of manifestation. This systematic breakdown is characteristic of Hatha Yoga and Siddha traditions, mapping the entire human experience onto a structured philosophical grid.</meta>

Attachment, aversion, fear, shame, and delusion—these are the five qualities of **Ether** <note>original: "Akasha"; here describing the psychological qualities associated with the most subtle element within the human constitution</note>.
Thus, the collective nature-body consists of these twenty-five qualities of the elements. ॥ 42 ॥

Mind, intellect, ego, memory-consciousness, and pure awareness—these constitute the **five-fold internal organ** <note>original: "Antahkarana," the inner psychological complex that processes experience</note>. ॥ 43 ॥

Intention, doubt, stupor, dullness, and reflection—these are the five qualities of the **Mind** <note>original: "Manas," the faculty of desire and sensory processing</note>. ॥ 44 ॥

Discrimination, detachment, peace, contentment, and forgiveness—these are the five qualities of the **Intellect** <note>original: "Buddhi," the faculty of judgment and wisdom</note>. ॥ 45 ॥

Pride, "mine-ness," my happiness, my sorrow, and "this is mine"—these are the five qualities of the **Ego** <note>original: "Ahankara," the "I-maker" or sense of self-identity</note>. ॥ 46 ॥

Understanding, fortitude, memory, renunciation, and acceptance—these are the five qualities of **Memory-Consciousness** <note>original: "Chitta," the storehouse of impressions</note>. ॥ 47 ॥

Inquiry, repeated practice <gloss>15: also read as "refined conduct"</gloss>, courage, contemplation, and desirelessness—these are the five qualities of **Pure Awareness** <note>original: "Chaitanya"</note>. Thus are the qualities of the internal organ. ॥ 48 ॥

Purity, passion, darkness, time, and the individual soul—this is the **five-fold group** <note>original: "Kula-pancakam"</note>. ॥ 49 ॥

Mercy, duty, compassion, devotion, and faith—these are the five qualities of **Purity** <note>original: "Sattva," the quality of light and harmony</note>. ॥ 50 ॥

Charity, enjoyment, eroticism, the grasping of objects, and self-interest <gloss>17: variants include "hoarding for oneself" or "taking for oneself"</gloss>—these are the five qualities of **Passion** <note>original: "Rajas," the quality of activity and motion</note>. ॥ 51 ॥

Dispute, grief, quarreling, bondage, and deceit—these are the five qualities of **Darkness** <note>original: "Tamas," the quality of inertia and ignorance</note>. ॥ 52 ॥

Calculation, imagination, delusion, negligence, and misfortune—these are the five qualities of **Time** <note>original: "Kala"</note>. ॥ 53 ॥

Waking, dreaming, deep sleep, the fourth state, and the state beyond the fourth—these are the five state-qualities of the **Individual Soul** <note>original: "Jiva"</note>. ॥ 54 ॥

Desire, action <gloss>16: also identified simply as "Action"</gloss>, illusion, nature, and speech—these are the **five powers of manifestation**. ॥ 55 ॥

Madness, latent tendencies, longing, anxiety, and effort—these are the five qualities of **Desire** <note>original: "Iccha," the primal will or impulse</note>. ॥ 56 ॥

Remembering, industry, work, resolve, and following the traditions of one's lineage—these are the five qualities of **Action** <note>original: "Kriya"</note>. ॥ 57 ॥

Intoxication, envy, hypocrisy, artificiality, and falsehood—these are the five qualities of **Illusion** <note>original: "Maya," the power that obscures reality</note>. ॥ 58 ॥

Hope, thirst, yearning, craving, and falsity—these are the five qualities of **Nature** <note>original: "Prakriti," referring here to the inherent disposition toward worldly lack</note>. ॥ 59 ॥
