<meta>This page continues the systematic mapping of the microcosm, moving from the internal psychological organs (Antahkarana) to the energetic expressions of the body. It categorizes the stages of speech, the powers of action and desire, and the mystical "phases" (Kalās) of the Moon, Sun, and Fire within the practitioner. Finally, it begins the anatomical mapping of the subtle channels (Nadis) and their corresponding "gates" or openings in the physical body.</meta>

The Supreme, the Visionary, the Intermediate, the Articulate, and the Primordial Mothers—these are the five qualities of **Speech** <note>original: "Vāk"; in this system, speech is not just sound but a cosmic force that descends from a silent, supreme state (Parā) down to audible words (Vaikharī)</note>.
Thus, the power of manifestation has five qualities; the entire collective consists of twenty-five qualities. ॥ 60 ॥

Action, desire, the moon, the sun, and fire—these constitute the **five-fold group of direct instruments** <note>original: "Pratyaksha-karana"; the primary drivers of human experience and energetic metabolism</note>. ॥ 61 ॥

Auspicious deeds, inauspicious deeds, fame, infamy, and the pursuit of unseen results <note>original: "Adrishta-phala-sadhana"; referring to rituals or actions performed to gain spiritual merit or future karmic rewards</note>—these are the five qualities of **Action** <note>original: "Karma"</note>. ॥ 62 ॥

Pleasure, affection, playfulness, longing, and yearning—these are the five qualities of **Desire** <note>original: "Kāma"</note>. ॥ 63 ॥

Surging <gloss>18: also read as "swelling"</gloss>, billowing, radiant, intoxicating, rippling, drying, free from greed <gloss>19: also read as "greedy"</gloss>, active, wave-like, quivering, flowing, spreading, streaming, gentle, serene, and floating—these are the sixteen phases of the **Moon**. The seventeenth phase is **Withdrawal** <note>original: "Nivṛtti"</note>; it is the Nectar-Phase. ॥ 64 ॥

Heating, devouring, fierce, contracting, drying, awakening, amorous, attracting, increasing contentment, wave-streaked, full of rays <gloss>20: also read as "beaming"</gloss>, and luminous—these are the twelve phases of the **Sun**. The thirteenth phase is **Self-luminosity** <note>original: "Svaprakashata"</note>; it is the Innate Phase. ॥ 65 ॥

Illuminating, pleasing <gloss>21: also read as "coloring"</gloss>, flaming <gloss>22</gloss>, sparking, fierce, digesting, terrible, burning <gloss>23: also read as "carrier" or "heat"</gloss>, passionate, and crested with fire—these are the ten phases of **Fire**.
The eleventh phase is **Splendor** <note>original: "Jyoti"</note>. This is the collective group of direct qualities and phases. ॥ 66 ॥

### Now, the Ten Gates of the Channels

The **Idā** and **Pingalā** channels flow through the two openings of the nose <note>these are the left and right conduits for breath and vital energy</note>.
The **Sushumnā** flows through the path of the palate up to the aperture of the crown <note>original: "Brahmarandhra"; the highest energy center at the top of the head</note>.
The **Sarasvatī** flows through the opening of the mouth.
The **Pūshā** and **Alambushā** flow through the two openings of the eyes.
The **Gāndhāri** and **Hastijihvikā** flow through the two openings of the ears.
The **Kuhū** flows through the opening of the anus.

<meta>This page continues the anatomical and energetic mapping of the human body. Having previously discussed the subtle channels (Nadis) and the phases of the moon and sun within, the text now transitions into the "Ten Vital Breaths" (Vayus) that animate the physical form. It concludes by detailing the traditional Indian understanding of embryology—the month-by-month development of the fetus and the spiritual loss of memory that occurs at the moment of birth.</meta>

...it flows through the spinal path up to the opening of Brahman <term>Brahmarandhra: the fontanelle or 'Gate of God' at the crown of the head, considered the exit point for the soul</term>. In this way, they flow through the ten gates. (All other channels flow through the pores of the skin). ॥ 67 ॥

### Now, the Ten Vital Breaths

In the heart resides the **breath of life** <term>Prana-vayu</term>, which causes inhalation and exhalation and is composed of the sounds *Ha* and *Sa* <note>original: "hakāra-sakārātmaka"; these two syllables form the natural mantra of breathing, "Soham," meaning "I am That"</note>. In the anus resides the **downward breath** <term>Apana-vayu</term>, governing excretion <note>original: "rechaka"</note> and retention <note>original: "kumbhaka"</note>. In the navel is the **balancing breath** <term>Samana-vayu</term>, the illuminator and the digestive fire. In the throat is the **pervasive breath** <term>Vyana-vayu</term>, which causes drying and nourishment. In the palate is the **upward breath** <term>Udana-vayu</term>, which governs swallowing, vomiting, and speech. 

The **Naga breath** <note>the first of the five "minor" breaths</note> pervades the whole body; it is the releaser and the mover. The **Kurma breath** causes the opening of the eyes. The **Krikala breath** causes belching and sneezing <note>original: "kshūtkāra"</note>. The **Devadatta breath** in the mouth causes yawning. The **Dhananjaya breath** produces sound and resonance. Through the observation of these ten breaths, the formation of the body <term>Pinda: the microcosm or "lump" of matter</term> occurs in either male or female form. ॥ 68 ॥

### Now, the Formation of the Embryo and the Body

Through the union of man and woman during the fertile period. ॥ 69 ॥

Through the union of the female seed and the male drop <note>original: "rajo-bindu"</note>, that soul <term>Jiva</term> on the first day becomes a **jelly-like mass** <term>kalala</term>. In seven nights, it takes the form of a **bubble**. 

In half a month, it becomes **spherical**. In exactly one month, it becomes **firm**. 

In two months, the **head** forms. In the third month, the **hands, feet, and limbs** appear. 

In the fourth month, the **eyes, ears, nose, mouth, and genitals** form. 

In the fifth month, the **back and belly** form. In the sixth month, the **nails and hair** appear. 

In the seventh month, it becomes endowed with **all consciousness**. In the eighth month, it is endowed with **all physical characteristics**. 

In the ninth month, it is endowed with the **inner knowledge of truth** <note>it is believed that in the womb, the fetus remembers its past lives and perceives the truth of existence</note>. 

In the tenth month, upon contact with the birth canal, the infant becomes **ignorant** <note>the trauma of birth and the "wind of Maya" causes the child to forget its divine nature and previous incarnations</note>. ॥ 70 ॥

<meta>This page concludes the first chapter (Upadesha) of Gorakshanath’s Siddha Siddhanta Paddhati. Having detailed the spiritual and energetic formation of the fetus in the previous sections, the text now focuses on the physical determinants of gender and the quantitative composition of the human body according to traditional Ayurvedic and Yogic science.</meta>

If the father's seed <term>Shukra: semen</term> is more abundant, a male child is born. If the mother's fluid <term>Rakta: literally "red," referring here to the ovum</term> is more abundant, a female child is born. If both are equal, a person of neutral gender <term>Napumsaka: neuter or eunuch</term> is born. If the man and woman are disturbed by mutual worries or anxious thoughts during the moment of conception, the child may be born blind, hunchbacked, a dwarf, lame, or missing limbs. ॥ 71 ॥

If, during the time of sexual union, the semen is released two or three times due to the quality of the pressure and movement of the limbs, a second child <note>referring to twins or multiple births</note> is born. ॥ 72 ॥

The body consists of three and a half *palas* <note>a traditional unit of weight, approximately 40-50 grams</note> of semen, twenty *palas* of blood, twelve *palas* of fat <term>Medas</term>, ten *palas* of marrow <term>Majja</term>, one hundred *palas* of flesh <term>Mamsa</term>, and twenty *palas* of phlegm <term>Shleshma</term>. There is an equivalent amount of wind <term>Vata</term>. There are three hundred and sixty bones, and an equal number of joints. There are thirty-five million hair follicles. All of this constitutes the combined essence of the father and mother. This description of the womb <gloss>5</gloss> "The Origin of the Individual Body" teaches that the body is composed of ten primary substances alongside the three humors of wind, bile, and phlegm <term>Vata, Pitta, and Shleshma/Kapha</term>. ॥ 73 ॥

<meta>This page transitions from the first chapter, which discussed the physical formation of the body in the womb, into the second chapter (Upadesha) of the Siddha Siddhanta Paddhati. Here, Gorakshanath shifts focus from the biological "pinda" (body) to the "subtle" anatomy—specifically the internal energy centers known as chakras that a yogi must contemplate.</meta>

# Second Lesson

->Now, the examination of the body is told.<-

Within the body <term>Pinda: the microcosm or individual body</term> there are nine chakras <term>Chakra: "wheel" or energy center</term>. The first is the Foundation, the Brahma-chakra. It is thrice-revolved and shaped like the circle of the female creative organ <note>original: "bhaga-mandala-akaram"</note>. There lies the root-bulb <term>Mula-kanda: the energetic source located between the anus and genitals</term>. One should meditate there on the Power <term>Shakti</term> in the form of a drinking vessel <gloss>1</gloss> <note>The footnote suggests a variant reading: "in the form of fire" (pavaka-karam), which is common in Yogic texts to describe the spark of Kundalini.</note>. In that very place is the Kamarupa Seat <term>Pitha: a sacred site or "throne" of power within the body</term>, which grants all desires <gloss>2</gloss> <note>The variant reading in the footnote says "grants the fruits of all desires."</note>. ॥ 1 ॥

The second is the Svadhisthana chakra. In its center, one should meditate on a lingam <term>Lingam: a pillar-like symbol of divine generative power</term> facing backward and resembling a sprout of coral. In that very place is the Uddyana Seat, which possesses the power of attracting the entire world. ॥ 2 ॥

The third is the Navel chakra <term>Nabhi-chakra</term>, which has five coils like a serpent. In its center, one should meditate on the Kundalini power, which shines like ten million rising suns. She is the central Power who grants all spiritual perfections <term>Siddhi</term>. ॥ 3 ॥

The fourth is the Heart-foundation <term>Hridaya-adhara</term>, an eight-petaled lotus facing downward. In its center, within the pericarp, one should meditate on a light in the form of a lingam. That is the Swan-digit <term>Hamsa-kala: representing the soul or the rhythm of the breath</term>, which brings all the senses under control <gloss>3</gloss> <note>The variant reading says "all the senses are mastered."</note>. ॥ 4 ॥

The fifth is the Throat chakra <term>Kantha-chakra</term>, measuring four finger-breadths. To the left is the Ida <note>the lunar energy channel</note>, to the right is the Pingala <note>the solar energy channel</note>, and in the middle, one should meditate on the Sushumna <note>the central channel of spiritual ascent</note>. That is the Unstruck-digit <term>Anahata-kala</term>, where the perfection of the Unstruck sound is attained. ॥ 5 ॥

The sixth is the Palate chakra <term>Talu-chakra</term>. There, the stream of the flow of nectar <term>Amrita: the "deathless" fluid of immortality</term> is found. It is the site of the bell-shaped lingam <note>the uvula</note>, the root-opening, the "royal tooth," the Shakhini-opening, and the Tenth Door <note>the spiritual gateway to the crown</note>. One should meditate on the Void <term>Shunya</term> there; through this, the dissolution of the mind occurs. ॥ 6 ॥

The seventh is the Brow chakra <term>Bhru-chakra</term>, the size of the middle joint of a thumb. One should meditate there on the Eye of Knowledge, shaped like the flame of a lamp. Through this, the perfection of speech is attained. ॥ 7 ॥

The eighth is the Liberation chakra at the Brahma-opening <term>Brahmarandhra-nirvana-chakra</term>, which is as fine as the point of a needle. One should meditate on it in the form of a trail of smoke. There is the Jalandhara Seat, which grants final liberation <term>Moksha</term>. ॥ 8 ॥

1 - in the form of fire (Manuscripts H. and T.). 2 - granting the fruits of desires (Manuscripts H. and T.). 3 - all the senses are mastered (Manuscript H.).

<summary>This page describes the first eight of the nine internal chakras (energy centers) and their associated sacred seats (pithas), detailing the specific visualizations and spiritual benefits for each.</summary>
<keywords>Chakra, Pinda, Kundalini, Nadi, Sushumna, Pitha, Kamarupa, Siddhi, Moksha, Gorakshanath</keywords>

--- Page 80 ---
<meta>Continuing from the previous page, which described the first eight energy centers (chakras), the text now concludes the section on the nine chakras and transitions to the sixteen "foundations" or "bases" (adharas). These are specific physical and energetic points in the body used for concentration and breath control in Hatha Yoga practice.</meta>

The ninth is the Sky Chakra <term>Akasha-chakra: the center of space or ether, located at the top of the head</term>, which is a sixteen-petaled lotus facing upward. In its center, within the pericarp, is the shape of three peaks <term>Trikuta</term>. One should meditate on the Power <term>Shakti</term> above that, which is the Supreme Void <term>Parama-shunya: the state of absolute stillness or emptiness</term>. In that very place is the Purnagiri Seat <term>Pitha: a sacred site of power within the subtle body</term>; there, the fulfillment of all desires occurs. ॥ 9 ॥

->Thus ends the investigation of the nine chakras.<-

## Now, the sixteen foundations are described.

There, the first is the Foundation of the Big Toe <term>Padangustha-adhara</term>. One should meditate on the tip of it as being composed of light; the gaze then becomes steady. ॥ 10 ॥

The second is the Root Foundation <term>Mula-adhara</term>. One should remain pressing the perineum <note>original: "mutram," though contextually referring to the area between the genitals and anus</note> with the left heel; therein occurs the kindling of the internal fire. ॥ 11 ॥

The third is the Anus Foundation <term>Guda-adhara</term>. One should repeatedly expand and contract it; the downward-moving breath <term>Apana-vayu</term> then becomes steady. ॥ 12 ॥

The fourth is the Foundation of the Genitals <term>Medhra-adhara</term>. By contracting the sexual organ, piercing the three "Knots of Brahma" <note>The Brahma-granthi are energetic blockages along the central channel</note>, and resting in the "Cave of the Bee" <note>a metaphorical name for a space in the upper brain/palate</note>, the seminal drop <term>Bindu: the vital essence or seed</term> is thereafter retained in the upward direction. This is famous as the Vajroli technique <term>Vajroli-mudra: a powerful practice for preserving vital energy</term>. ॥ 13 ॥

The fifth is the Oddyana Foundation <term>Oddyana-adhara</term>. Through its binding <note>referring to the abdominal lock or "flying upward" gesture</note>, there is a contraction of the stool and urine. ॥ 14 ॥

In the sixth, the Navel Foundation <term>Nabhi-adhara</term>, when the syllable OM is chanted with a focused mind, the dissolution into the inner sound <term>Nada-laya</term> <gloss>4</gloss> <note>original: "nado-dayam-upaiti" — the rising of the sound occurs</note> takes place. ॥ 15 ॥

In the seventh, the Heart Foundation <term>Hridaya-adhara</term>, one should restrain the breath; the blossoming of the lotus <note>the opening of the heart center</note> occurs. ॥ 16 ॥

In the eighth, the Throat Foundation <term>Kantha-adhara</term>, one should block the root of the throat with the chin <note>this is the Jalandhara Bandha or Chin Lock</note>; the breath in the Ida and Pingala <term>the two primary energy channels flanking the spine</term> becomes steady. ॥ 17 ॥

In the ninth, the Uvula Foundation <term>Ghantika-adhara</term>, one should place the tip of the tongue; the essence of nectar <term>Amrita-kala: the "digit of immortality" believed to drip from the moon in the head</term> flows. ॥ 18 ॥

In the tenth, the Palate Foundation <term>Talva-adhara</term>, within the cavity of the palate, having lengthened the small tongue <note>referring to the uvula or the soft palate area</note> through the practices of moving and "milking" it, one should insert it in a reversed position; one then becomes firm like a log <note>This describes the Khechari Mudra, where the tongue is turned back into the nasal cavity</note>. ॥ 19 ॥

Now, the eleventh is the Tongue Foundation <term>Jihva-adhara</term>. One should fix the tip of the tongue there; the destruction of all diseases occurs. ॥ 20 ॥

<summary>This page concludes the description of the nine chakras and begins a detailed list of the sixteen 'foundations' (adharas), providing specific yogic instructions for meditation and physical locks at each point.</summary>
<keywords>Akasha-chakra, Purnagiri Pitha, Shodasha-adhara, Vajroli, Bindu, Amrita, Khechari Mudra, subtle body, Hatha Yoga</keywords>

--- Page 81 ---
<meta>This page continues the technical instructions for Hatha Yoga practice, moving from the internal energetic centers (chakras) to the sixteen physical "foundations" (adharas) and the three "targets" or types of meditative focus (lakshyas). The previous page ended while describing the fourth of the sixteen foundations.</meta>

The twelfth is the Foundation of the Eyebrow Center <term>Bhru-madhya-adhara</term>. There, one should meditate on the orb of the moon; the practitioner then attains a state of coolness. ॥ 21 ॥

The thirteenth is the Foundation of the Nose <term>Nasa-adhara</term>. One should focus on the tip of the nose; the mind then becomes steady. ॥ 22 ॥

In the fourteenth, at the Root of the Nose <term>Nasa-mula</term>, is the "Gate Foundation" <term>Kavata-adhara</term>. One should fix <gloss>5</gloss> <note>original: "dharayet"</note> the gaze there; after six months, one sees a mass of light. ॥ 23 ॥

In the fifteenth, at the Foundation of the Forehead <term>Lalata-adhara</term>, one should focus <gloss>6</gloss> <note>original: "dhyayet," meaning "should meditate"</note> on the mass of light; one becomes radiant. ॥ 24 ॥

In the remaining sixteenth, at the Opening of Brahman <term>Brahma-randhra: the soft spot at the crown of the head</term>, is the Sky Chakra <term>Akasha-chakra</term>. There, one should always behold the pair of the Holy Guru’s lotus feet; one becomes full and all-encompassing, like space itself. Thus ends the description of the sixteen foundations. ॥ 25 ॥

### Now, the Three Targets.

One should remember the Channel of Brahman <term>Brahma-nadi: the central energetic channel of the spine</term>, which is white in color, attached to the spinal column, and extends from the root bulb <term>Mula-kanda: the energetic source located between the anus and genitals</term> to the Opening of Brahman at the crown. Within that channel, one should focus the mind on a form as fine as a lotus fiber, brilliant as ten million lightning bolts, and moving upward. There, it bestows all spiritual powers. ॥ 26 ॥

Alternatively, above the forehead in the "Pavilion of the Skull" <gloss>7</gloss> <note>original: "Gollata," a technical term for a specific region in the upper head</note>, one should focus on a vibrating or flickering form. Or, within the "Cave of the Immortal" <term>Amara-guha: a metaphorical space in the center of the head</term>, one should focus on the form of a reddish bee. Or, one should block both ears <gloss>8</gloss> <note>original: "with both [fingers], the reddish fire element"</note> with the forefingers; then, one hears the internal sound "dhum dhum" in the middle of the head. Or, in the middle of the eyes, one should focus on a blue light in the shape of a small doll. This is called the Internal Target. ॥ 27 ॥

The External Target is now described.

One should focus on a blue light four finger-widths outside the tip of the nose. Or, focus on the Air element, which is smoke-colored, six finger-widths from the tip of the nose. Or, focus on the Fire element, which is reddish, at eight finger-widths. Or, focus on the Water element, which is like a surging wave, at ten finger-widths. Or, focus on the Earth element, which is yellow, at twelve finger-widths from the tip of the nose. Or, one should look up and gaze at the face of the sky.

5 - Hold or fix (from the H. manuscript). 6 - Meditate (from the H. manuscript). 7 - Gollata (variant reading from the H. manuscript). 8 - Both reddish fire element (variant reading from the H. and T. manuscripts).
</note>

<meta>This page continues the technical instructions for Hatha Yoga practice, specifically concluding the "Three Targets" (tri-lakshya) by describing the External and Intermediate focal points, followed by a detailed meditation on the Five Voids (vyoma-panchakam), before introducing the Eight Limbs of Yoga (ashtanga-yoga).</meta>

...one sees [the sky] filled with rays; this is the process of purification. Or, by looking upwards into the intervening space, one sees faces of light. Or, wherever one looks, one should focus on the space <term>akasha: the ether or sky</term>; the mind then becomes like space, which grants liberation. Or, with the inner gaze, one should focus on the ground appearing like molten gold <note>original: "tapta-kanchana-sannibham"</note>; the vision then becomes steady. These are the various kinds of External Focus <term>Bahir-lakshya</term>. ॥ 28 ॥

Now, the Intermediate Focus <term>Madhyama-lakshya</term> is described. 
One should focus the mind on a form that is white, red, or black; or shaped like a flame, or as a form of light, or shaped like lightning, or the orb of the sun, or a half-moon. This form should be the size of one’s own body as desired, without being fixed to any specific location. These are the various kinds of Intermediate Focus. ॥ 29 ॥

Now, one should focus on the Five Voids <term>Vyoma-panchakam</term>. The Five Voids are: Ether, Supreme Ether, Great Ether, Reality Ether, and Sun Ether. One should focus on the Ether <term>Akasha</term> as being extremely pure and formless, both outside and inside. Or, one should behold the Supreme Ether <term>Parakasha</term>, which resembles total darkness both inside and out. One should behold the Great Ether <term>Mahakasha</term>, which is like the fire of time <note>original: "kalanala," the fire that consumes the universe at the end of an age</note> inside and out. One should behold the Reality Ether <term>Tattvakasha</term>, which is the nature of one's own essence <note>original: "nija-tattva-svarupam"</note>, inside and out. Or, one should behold the Sun Ether <term>Suryakasha</term>, which is like ten million suns, inside and out. Thus, by beholding the Five Voids, the practitioner becomes like space itself. ॥ 30 ॥

->And it is said:<-

The nine chakras, the sixteen foundations, the three targets, and the five voids—the yogi who does not know these correctly is a yogi in name only. ॥ 31 ॥

### Now, the Eight-Limbed Yoga.

Restraint, observance, posture, breath control, withdrawal of the senses, concentration, meditation, and absorption <term>Yama, Niyama, Asana, Pranayama, Pratyahara, Dharana, Dhyana, and Samadhi</term> are the eight limbs. Restraint <term>Yama</term> is the calming <note>original: "upashamah," referring to the quiescence of the mind and senses</note>—

---

<margin>Variant readings: "filled with rays there" (MS T). 10-"there" (MS H).</margin>

<summary>This page describes methods for external and intermediate meditative focus, details the five stages of space-meditation (The Five Voids), and begins the section on the traditional Eight Limbs of Yoga.</summary>
<keywords>External Focus, Intermediate Focus, Five Voids, Akasha, Eight-Limbed Yoga, Ashtanga Yoga, Yama, meditation techniques</keywords>

--- Page 83 ---
<meta>This page concludes the "Consideration of the Body" (Pinda-vichara) by detailing the Eight Limbs of Yoga (Ashtanga Yoga). While these terms are familiar from Patanjali's Yoga Sutras, the author Gorakshanatha provides a unique interpretation focused on internal realization and the union of the self with supreme reality.</meta>

Victory over all senses, as well as victory over food, sleep, cold, wind, and heat, should be achieved slowly and gradually. ॥ 32 ॥

**Observance** <term>niyama: the second limb of yoga, usually meaning ethical rules</term> is defined as the restraint of the fluctuations of the mind. Its characteristics include: living in solitude, detachment, indifference to worldly concerns, contentment with whatever is obtained, a lack of interest in sensory pleasures <note>original: "vairasyam"</note>, and being firmly established at the feet of the Guru. ॥ 33 ॥

**Posture** <term>asana: the third limb, referring to physical seating</term> is the state of being near to one's own true nature <note>The text makes a pun here: "asana" (posture) is defined as "sam-asannata" (nearness)</note>. Among the various postures such as the **Auspicious Posture** <term>Swastikasana</term>, the **Lotus Posture** <term>Padmasana</term>, and the **Perfected Posture** <term>Siddhasana</term>, one should adopt whichever one is desired and remain alert. This is the definition of Posture. ॥ 34 ॥

**Breath Control** <term>pranayama: the fourth limb, regulation of the life-force</term> is the stability of the vital breath <term>prana</term>. The four actions that define Breath Control are: **Exhalation** <term>rechaka</term>, **Inhalation** <term>puraka</term>, **Retention** <term>kumbhaka</term>, and their **Union** <term>sanghatta</term>. ॥ 35 ॥

**Withdrawal** <term>pratyahara: the fifth limb, pulling senses away from objects</term> is the pulling back of the waves <gloss>Alternative reading: "horses" <note>original: "turanganam"</note></gloss> of consciousness. It is characterized by the cessation of all disturbances, even those arising from the many different transformations of the mind. ॥ 36 ॥

**Concentration** <term>dharana: the sixth limb, fixing the mind</term> means that one should train the internal instrument of the mind to realize that the inner and outer are a single essence of one's own reality. Whatever arises should be held within the formless. One should hold oneself steady, like a lamp in a windless place. This is the definition of Concentration. ॥ 37 ॥

Now, **Meditation** <term>dhyana: the seventh limb, sustained focus</term> is described. There is a certain state of supreme non-duality; that alone is the Self. One should contemplate that whatever manifests or shines forth is that very nature. Equal vision toward all beings is the definition of Meditation. ॥ 38 ॥

Now, the definition of **Absorption** <term>samadhi: the eighth limb, total immersion</term>. It is the state of equilibrium of all principles of existence <term>tattvas</term>, a state of effortlessness, and the quality of remaining in a condition of natural ease. This concludes the definition of the Eight Limbs of Yoga.

->।। Thus ends the Second Instruction, called the Consideration of the Body,<-
->in the *Method of the Proven Teachings* <term>Siddha Siddhanta Paddhati</term> composed by the venerable Gorakshanatha. ।। 2 ।।<-

<meta>This page marks the beginning of the Third Instruction, transitioning from the previous discussion on the Eight Limbs of Yoga to a detailed mapping of the cosmos within the human body. The previous page concluded with the definition of Withdrawal (pratyahara), and this section shifts focus to "Body-Perception," the realization that the entire universe—from the underworlds to the highest heavens—exists within the individual form.</meta>

# Third Instruction

Now, **Body-Perception** <term>pinda-samvitti: the direct realization of the body as a microcosm</term> is described.
The Yogi who knows the moving and the unmoving <note>original: "characharau"</note> within the body becomes one who possesses Body-Perception. ॥ 1 ॥

The **Tortoise** <note>In mythology, the tortoise Kurma supports the world; here, it supports the body</note> dwells at the soles of the feet. The underworld of **Patala** is in the big toes; **Talatala** is at the tips of the toes; **Mahatala** is on the top of the feet <margin>Variant: Mahatala is at the back of the foot</margin>; **Rasatala** is in the ankles; **Sutala** is in the shanks; **Vitala** is in the knees; and **Atala** is in the thighs. Thus, the seven underworlds exist under the lordship of the deity **Rudra**. Within the body, the state of being in the form of anger is none other than **Kala-Agni-Rudra** <term>the Fire of Time/Destruction</term>. ॥ 2 ॥

The **Earthly Realm** <term>Bhurloka</term> is in the private region <note>specifically the anus or perineum</note>; the **Atmospheric Realm** <term>Bhuvarloka</term> is in the genitals; the **Heavenly Realm** <term>Svarloka</term> is in the navel. In these three worlds, **Indra** is the deity. Within the body, he who regulates all the senses is indeed Indra. ॥ 3 ॥

In the sprout of the spinal column <term>danda-ankura</term> is the **Realm of Greatness** <term>Maharloka</term>; in the hollow of the spine is the **Realm of Creation** <term>Janaloka</term>; in the spinal channel is the **Realm of Penance** <term>Tapoloka</term>; in the root-lotus <note>referring here to the highest center</note> is the **Realm of Truth** <term>Satyaloka</term>. In these four worlds, deities such as **Brahma** reside. Within the body, he exists in the form of various measures and self-identities. ॥ 4 ॥

The **Realm of Vishnu** resides in the abdomen; there, **Vishnu** is the deity. Within the body, he is the performer of many activities. In the heart is the **Realm of Rudra**; there, **Rudra** is the deity. Within the body, he dwells in a fierce form. In the chest area is the **Realm of Ishvara**; there, **Ishvara** is the deity. Within the body, he dwells in the form of contentment. In the middle of the throat is the **Realm of the Blue-Throated One** <term>Nilakantha-loka</term>; there, **Nilakantha** is the deity who dwells eternally within the body. At the gateway of the palate is the **Realm of Shiva**; there, **Shiva** is the deity. Within the body, he dwells in an incomparable form. At the root of the uvula <note>original: "lambika-mule"</note> is the **Realm of Bhairava**; there, **Bhairava** is the deity. Within the body, he dwells in the most excellent form. In the middle of the forehead is the **Beginningless Realm**; there, the **Beginningless Deity** dwells within the body in a state of supreme bliss—

<summary>This page introduces the concept of the body as a microcosm, systematically mapping the seven underworlds and various heavenly realms (lokas) onto specific anatomical locations from the feet to the forehead. It identifies the governing deities of these internal regions, equating cosmic forces with internal physical and psychological functions.</summary>

<keywords>Pinda-samvitti, Microcosm, Underworlds, Patala, Lokas, Rudra, Indra, Vishnu, Shiva, Internal Anatomy, Natha Yoga</keywords>

--- Page 85 ---
<meta>This page continues the mapping of the macrocosm (the universe) onto the microcosm (the human body), specifically moving from the spine into the higher centers of the head and then detailing how the physical tissues and fluids correspond to the legendary islands, oceans, and regions of the world.</meta>

...stands in the form of the slayer <note>original: "hanta-svarupi"; likely referring to the destructive aspect of the deity mentioned at the end of the previous page</note>. ॥ In the crest <note>original: "shringara"; the top of the head or a specific energy center</note> is the **Clan-Realm** <term>Kula-loka</term>. There, the **Lord of the Clan** <term>Kuleshvara</term> is the deity. Within the body <term>pinda-madhye</term>, he exists in the **form of Bliss**. In the center of the conch-bone <note>the temporal bone or a region in the skull</note>, at the place of the Lotus-Fiber <term>Nalini</term>, the **Lord beyond Clan** <term>Akuleshvara</term> is the deity. Within the body, the state of **egolessness** <note>nirabhimana-avastha</note> resides there. In the **Opening of Brahma** <term>Brahmarandhra: the spiritual fontanelle at the crown of the head</term> is the **Realm of the Supreme Absolute** <term>Parabrahma-loka</term>. There, the **Supreme Absolute** is the deity. Within the body, the **state of fullness** <note>paripurna-dasha</note> resides there. In the **Upper Lotus**, there is the **Higher-Lower Realm** <term>Parapara-loka</term>. There, the **Supreme Lord** <term>Parameshvara</term> is the deity. Within the body, the **Higher-Lower disposition** resides there. In the place of the **Triple-Peak** <term>Trikuta: often identified with the space between the eyebrows or the medulla</term> is the **Realm of Power** <term>Shakti-loka</term>. There, the **Supreme Power** <term>Parashakti</term> is the deity. The state of being the **universal doer** for all exists there. Thus is the contemplation of the locations of the twenty-one universes, including the seven underworlds, within the body. ॥ 5 ॥

In the principle of right conduct dwell the **Priests** <term>Brahmans</term>; in heroism, the **Warriors** <term>Kshatriyas</term>; in commerce, the **Merchants** <term>Vaishyas</term>; and in the spirit of service, the **Laborers** <term>Shudras</term>. Even in the sixty-four arts, there are sixty-four castes. ॥ 6 ॥

Now, the **seven oceans** and **seven islands** <note>traditional Indian cosmology describes the world as seven concentric rings of land and sea</note> are described:
In the marrow is **Jambu Island** <gloss>Variant 2: Shaka Island, Shalmali Island; or in the brain is Shaka Island</gloss>.
In the bones is **Shakti Island**.
In the veins is the **Subtle Island** <term>Sukshma-dvipa</term>.
In the skin is **Krauncha Island**.
In the hair is **Gomaya Island**.
In the nails is the **White Island** <term>Shveta-dvipa</term>.
In the flesh (or bones) is **Plaksha Island**.
Thus are the seven islands. ॥ 7 ॥

In the urine is the **Salt Ocean**.
In the saliva is the **Milk Ocean**.
In the phlegm is the **Curd Ocean**.
In the fat is the **Clarified Butter Ocean**.
In the muscle-fat is the **Honey Ocean**.
In the blood is the **Sugarcane Ocean**.
In the reproductive fluid is the **Nectar Ocean**.
Thus are the seven oceans. ॥ 8 ॥

The **Nine Regions** <term>Nava-khandas</term> dwell in the **nine gates** <note>the nine physical openings of the body: eyes, ears, nostrils, mouth, anus, and genitals</note>.
<gloss>Variant 3</gloss> **Bharata Region**, **Kashmir Region**,
<gloss>Variant 4: Bhai Region</gloss> **Karpara Region**, **Shri Region**, **Shankha Region**, **Ekapada Region**, **Gandhara Region**,
<gloss>Variant 5: Kharva Region or Karpara Region</gloss> **Kaivartaka Region**, and the **Great Meru Region**.
Thus are the nine regions. ॥ 9 ॥

<meta>This page continues the mapping of the macrocosm (the universe) onto the microcosm (the human body). The previous page concluded the journey through the twenty-one celestial realms and energy centers in the head; this page shifts to mapping the Earth's geography—mountains, rivers, and celestial bodies—onto the physical anatomy.</meta>

### Page 16

**Mount Meru** <note>the cosmic axis</note> resides in the **Meru-segment** <gloss>6: alternative reading: "in the staff," referring to the spinal column</gloss>. **Mount Kailasa** resides in the **Gate of Brahma** <note>Brahma-kapata: the fontanelle at the crown of the head</note>.
The **Himalayas** are in the back. **Mount Malaya** is in the left shoulder. **Mount Mandara** is in the right shoulder.
**Mount Vindhya** is in the right ear. **Mount Mainaka** is in the left ear. **Shri-parvata** <note>the "Sacred Mountain"</note> is in the forehead.
These are the eight **Clan-Mountains** <term>Kula-parvata: the primary mountain ranges of classical Indian geography</term>. Other minor mountains reside in all the fingers. ॥ 10 ॥

<gloss>7: alternative reading: Pitasā</gloss> The **Ganges**, **Yamuna**, **Chandrabhaga**, **Sarasvati**, <gloss>8: alternative reading: Vitasta</gloss> **Vipasha**, **Shatadru**, **Shriratri**, and the **Narmada**—these nine rivers reside in the **nine channels** <term>nadis: subtle energetic pathways</term>. ॥ 11 ॥

Other tributary rivers and minor streams reside in the **seventy-two thousand channels**. ॥ 12 ॥

The twenty-seven **lunar mansions** <term>nakshatras</term>, the twelve **zodiac signs**, the nine **planets**, and the fifteen **lunar days**—these reside in the inner circle within the seventy-two thousand hand-measured enclosures of the body. Numerous **constellations** reside in the waves <note>likely referring to the pulses or the undulations of the body's fluids</note>. The **thirty-three million deities** reside in the hair follicles of the arms. Numerous **sacred seats and secondary seats** <term>pithas and upapithas: geographical locations sacred to the Goddess</term> reside in the hair follicles. Gods, demons, nature spirits, titans, flesh-eaters, spirits, and ghosts reside in the joints of the bones. The **Clan-Serpents** <term>Kula-nagas: divine serpents that support the earth</term> reside in the chest.
Other groups of sages, such as **Sanaka** and his brothers, reside in the hair follicles of the armpits.
Other mountains reside in the hairs of the abdomen. The **celestial musicians**, **horse-headed beings**, **heavenly spirits**, and **nymphs** reside in the belly. Other sky-walking playful **Mothers**, **Powers** <term>Shaktis</term>, and **fierce deities** reside in the speed of the breath. Numerous **clouds** reside in the falling of tears. Numerous **pilgrimage sites** <term>tirthas</term> reside in the vital points <term>marmas</term>. Infinite **perfected masters** <term>siddhas</term> reside in the light of the intellect. The **Moon and Sun** reside in the two eyes. Numerous **trees, vines, shrubs, and grasses** reside in the hair follicles of the legs. Numerous **worms, insects, and moths** reside in the excrement. ॥ 13 ॥

What is **happiness**, that is **heaven**. What is **suffering**, that is **hell**. What is **action** <term>karma</term>, that is **bondage**. What is **free of mental construction** <term>nirvikalpa: a state of pure consciousness</term>, that is **liberation** <term>mukti</term>. In the state of one’s own true nature, such as in the transition from sleep to wakefulness, the awakening of the Self is **Peace**. Thus, within all bodies, the **Supreme Lord** exists in the form of the **Universe**.

<meta>This page concludes the third chapter of the text. Having mapped the mountains, rivers, planets, and deities onto the physical anatomy in the preceding verses, the author now summarizes the spiritual principle underlying this mapping: the presence of the universal consciousness within every individual form.</meta>

->The Supreme Soul, through its indivisible nature, resides in every vessel <note>ghata: a common yogic metaphor where the human body is compared to a clay pot containing the space of consciousness</note> in the form of pure consciousness.<-

->In this manner, the **realization of the microcosm** <term>Pinda-samvitti: the profound experiential understanding that the individual body (pinda) and the universe are one</term> is attained. ॥ 14 ॥<-

->॥ Thus ends the third lesson, entitled **Realization of the Microcosm**,<-
->in the *System of the Adepts' Doctrines* <term>Siddha-Siddhanta-Paddhati</term> composed by the venerable **Gorakhnath**. ॥ 3 ॥<-

<summary>This page concludes the third chapter by explaining that the Supreme Soul dwells within every human body as consciousness and signifies the completion of the "Realization of the Microcosm." It serves as the formal colophon for this section of Gorakhnath's influential yoga treatise.</summary>

<keywords>Supreme Soul (Paramatma), Consciousness (Chit), Microcosm (Pinda), Gorakhnath, Siddha Siddhanta Paddhati, realization, non-duality</keywords>

--- Page 88 ---
<meta>This page begins the Fourth Lesson (Upadesha) of the Siddha-Siddhanta-Paddhati. Following the Third Lesson, which mapped the external universe onto the human body (the microcosm), Gorakhnath now moves to a more metaphysical discussion. He explains the fundamental "Support of the Body" (Pindadhara), identifying it not as a physical structure, but as the primordial, conscious Power (Shakti) that underlies all existence.</meta>

# Fourth Lesson

->**Now, the Support of the Body is described.**<-

There exists a certain transcendental, innate Power <term>Nija-shakti: the inherent, primordial energy of the divine</term>, whose very nature is pure Consciousness <gloss>variation: Consciousness</gloss> <gloss>4</gloss>. Ever-awakened, she serves as the support for all physical forms <term>pinda: the individual body or "vessel"</term>. She is well-known for bringing forth the "agent" <note>the sense of being an individual doer</note> through the unfolding of the first sprouts of the state of cause and effect <gloss>1</gloss>; for this reason, she is called the **Supportive Power** <term>Adhara-shakti</term>. She is the witness to the direct realization of both worldly knowledge and sacred scripture, reachable only through the experience of one’s own extremely subtle internal light and self-awareness. She is sung of as the **Supreme Consciousness-Form** <gloss>variation: Supreme Consciousness-Form</gloss> <gloss>2</gloss>. When that same Power remains in her natural state within herself, in a condition of perfect stillness where no fluctuations arise, she then becomes one with **Shiva** <note>the Absolute or Pure Being</note>. ॥ 1 ॥

Therefore, this Power is said to be the "Innate Foundation of Equanimity," possessing the nature of both the Manifest and the Unmanifest <term>Kula-Akula: 'Kula' refers to the manifested power or form, while 'Akula' refers to the formless Absolute</term>. ॥ 2 ॥

Regarding the Manifest <term>Kula</term>: Through her aspects <note>Kala</note> of Supreme Existence, I-ness, and Vibrant Radiance <gloss>3</gloss>, she herself remains as the five-fold support of the universe. ॥ 3 ॥

Therefore, that which is the Supreme-and-Non-Supreme, the light-filled nature that illuminates even that which has no appearance, is called **The Supreme** <term>Para</term>. ॥ 4 ॥

That which accepts that there is only one primordial, established, supreme non-duality is called **Existence** <term>Satta</term>. ॥ 5 ॥

That which has the characteristic of signaling the "I"—the feeling of "I am the joy of the rays of light," whose nature is beginningless, endless, and immeasurable—is called **Supreme I-ness** <term>Para-ahanta</term>. ॥ 6 ॥

That which makes manifest the state of mental wonder and absolute stillness born of one's own experience is called **Vibrant Radiance** <term>Sphuratta</term>. ॥ 7 ॥

That which perceives the self-luminosity of the eternally pure and awakened nature is called the **Supreme Aspect** <term>Para-kala</term>. ॥ 8 ॥

---

1—The foundational support <gloss>original: anantara-adhara</gloss> (Manuscript H). 2—The Supreme Power whose form is consciousness <gloss>original: para-chid-rupini</gloss> (Manuscript H). 3—According to the Yoga tradition: "Through the supreme appearance, I-ness, existence, and radiant vibration, she is the Manifest" <gloss>original: parabhasatvad-ahanta-satta-sphuratta-kula</gloss> (Yoga-paddhati). 4—Consciousness <gloss>original: samvit</gloss> (Tantra-paddhati).

<summary>This page introduces the Fourth Lesson, defining the "Support of the Body" as a primordial conscious Power (Shakti). It describes her transition from a state of stillness (Shiva) to a five-fold manifestation comprising Existence, I-ness, and Radiance.</summary>

<keywords>Shakti, Shiva, Pinda (Body), Consciousness (Samvit), Ahanta (I-ness), Sphuratta (Radiance), Kula-Akula, Gorakhnath, Siddha Siddhanta Paddhati</keywords>

--- Page 89 ---
<meta>This page continues the Fourth Lesson of the Siddha-Siddhanta-Paddhati. In the previous section, Gorakhnath defined the "Support of the Body" and introduced the concepts of Kula (the manifest) and Akula (the formless). Here, he delves deeper into the non-dual nature of these two states and the role of the Supreme Power (Shakti) in bridging them.</meta>

# Fourth Lesson (Continued)

Regarding the **Formless** <term>Akula: the unmanifest Absolute, beyond all categories</term>: It is well known as being the one [reality], acting as the singular cause for all distinctions such as caste, social class, and lineage. 

As it is stated in the *Dialogue between Uma and Maheshvara* <note>original: Umamaheshvara-samvada</note>:
> In the Peerless state, because it is non-different, indivisible, non-dual, and without external support <gloss>5: variations: "without hunger" or "without refuge"</gloss>, because it is without specific attributes and without name—the Formless is said to be the Peerless <gloss>6: variation: "the continuous"</gloss>. ॥ 9 ॥

Thus, in clarifying the stage where the light of the harmony <term>Samarasya: the "equal flavor" or perfect union where two things become one</term> between the Manifest and the Formless is revealed, there remains only that one **Transcendent Power** <term>Aparampara-shakti: a power that is "beyond succession" or "beyond the beyond"</term> which is capable of this manifestation. This "Beyond-Succession" Power is famously known as the "One Full of Desire" <note>Ashavati: suggesting the divine will to manifest</note>; she brings about the entire web of the cosmic expansion and unites it with the Supreme Truth <gloss>7: variation: "the lower power"</gloss>. ॥ 10 ॥

->**It is said in the *Lalita-Svacchanda* Tantra:**<-

> The Formless assumes the nature of the Manifest, and the Manifest desires the Formless. Following the logic of water and its bubbles, the Supreme Shiva is of a single, unified form. ॥ 11 ॥

Therefore, the One who is of a single form, who possesses infinite power, although established in his own innate bliss, plays through various forms and is said to enjoy his own foundation by himself.

->**It is said in the *Pratyabhijna*:**<-
<note>Pratyabhijna refers to the "Recognition" school of Shaivism, which teaches that liberation is recognizing one's own nature as Shiva.</note>

> He whose power is never diminished, eternally manifesting as the totality of all forms, remains at the end as the One, in his own true nature. ॥ 12 ॥

Thus, the Supreme Cause, the Supreme Lord, the Shiva who is beyond the beyond, is "all-faced" <note>omnipresent and omniscient</note> in his essential nature; because he has the capacity to manifest as all forms, he is the **Possessor of Power** <term>Shaktiman</term>.

->**It is said in the *Vamakeshara-Tantra*:**<-

> Even Shiva, if devoid of Power, is unable to do anything. But joined with his own Power, he becomes the one who illuminates everything. ॥ 13 ॥

Thus, it is well known to the Perfected Masters <term>Siddhas</term> that the Supreme Lord, possessing infinite power and being the very form of Universal Consciousness, becomes the universe itself. And the **Kundalini**, which is the nature of both the higher and the lower...

<summary>This page explains the non-dual relationship between the formless Absolute (Akula/Shiva) and the manifest world (Kula/Shakti), asserting that they are as inseparable as water and its bubbles. It introduces the Transcendent Power (Aparampara-shakti) as the bridge that unites the cosmic expansion with the Supreme Truth.</summary>

<keywords>Akula, Kula, Shiva, Shakti, Samarasya, Shaktiman, Aparampara-shakti, Kundalini, Lalita-Svacchanda, Vamakeshara-Tantra, Pratyabhijna</keywords>

--- Page 90 ---
<meta>This page continues the Fourth Lesson of the Siddha-Siddhanta-Paddhati. The previous page concluded with a discussion of the Transcendent Power (Aparampara-shakti) that bridges the manifest and the formless. This page shifts focus to how this power manifests within the human body as Kundalini and the three functional aspects of Shakti (Lower, Middle, and Upper).</meta>

# Fourth Lesson (Continued)

...exists. Therefore, they are famously known as the **Perfected Beings of the Body** <term>Pinda-siddhas: masters who have realized the identity of their physical body with the cosmic body</term>. That **Coiled Power** <term>Kundalini: the latent spiritual energy at the base of the spine</term> is of two types: awakened and unawakened. 

The "unawakened" state is known as the form of consciousness within each individual body—which is, by its very nature, a web of various thoughts, activities, and worldly efforts—and is characterized by a "coiled" or crooked disposition. For yogis, this same power becomes famously known as the "Upward-Moving Kundalini" through the intentional effort of restraining those various manifest mental fluctuations. ॥ 14 ॥

Regarding the term "Upward": Since all the cosmic principles <term>Tattvas: the constituent elements of reality</term> exist in the highest state as one’s own essential nature, she is well-known as the **Reflective Consciousness** <term>Vimarsha-rupini: the power of self-awareness or divine mirror-like reflection</term> through whom yogis come to understand their own true nature. ॥ 15 ॥

->**And so it is said in the *Evaratnaka*:**<-

> By the awakening of the **Middle Power**, by the contraction of the **Lower Power**, and by the descent of the **Upper Power**, the Supreme State is attained. ॥ 16 ॥

That single Power is described as being divided into three parts based on these distinctions of Middle, Upper, and Lower. ॥ 17 ॥

That which consists of the activities of the external senses and a multitude of various thoughts is called the **Lower Power** <note>original: adhah-shakti</note>. Therefore, for those yogis, the contraction <gloss>restraint</gloss> of this power is achieved through the binding of the **Root-Support** <term>Muladhara: the lowest energy center or chakra</term>. It is from this root that the entire world of moving and unmoving things—composed of both consciousness and matter—arises. This very Root-Support is famously known as the **Expansion of Pure Consciousness** <note>original: samvit-prasara</note>. ॥ 18 ॥

->**It was said by the Teacher Shivananda:**<-
<note>Shivanandacharya was a prominent historical figure in the Kaula and Natha traditions, known for synthesizing tantric philosophy.</note>

> "Through the expansion and contraction of all powers, the creation and withdrawal of the world occurs; of this there is no doubt. Therefore, it is called the **Root**." 

Consequently, almost all perfected beings are devoted to the Root-Support. ॥ 19 ॥

That which is capable of always holding the individual soul—which is restless by nature like a wave and wanders in vain—within the center of its own self-effulgent light as its true nature, is celebrated as the **Middle Power** <note>original: madhya-shakti</note>, the Kundalini.

It is certain that the Great Doctrine <note>original: maha-siddhanta</note> is perceived through these gross and subtle forms. ॥ 20 ॥

---

<margin>8. A variant reading from manuscript 'H' suggests "devoted to contraction" instead of "of whose contraction."</margin>

<summary>This page explains the functional aspects of Kundalini energy as Lower, Middle, and Upper powers, emphasizing that spiritual perfection is achieved by redirecting energy from external sensory thoughts (Lower Power) back to the Root-Support and the Middle Power.</summary>
<keywords>Kundalini, Pinda-siddha, Muladhara, Shivanandacharya, Madhya-shakti, Samvit-prasara, Vimarsha-rupini, Root-Support, Middle Power</keywords>

--- Page 91 ---
<meta>This page continues the Fourth Lesson of the *Siddha-Siddhanta-Paddhati*. Having introduced the three divisions of power—Lower, Middle, and Upper—the text now elaborates on the distinction between the "Gross" and "Subtle" forms of the Coiled Power (Kundalini) and explains the "Descent of Power" (Shakti-nipata) that leads to the realization of non-duality between Shiva and Shakti.</meta>

That which is called "Gross" <term>Sthula</term> is the foundation of everything that can be perceived; though it is the very nature of all perceivable things, it appears to wander among various objects as if it were those objects themselves. This is the **Coiled Power with Form** <term>Sakara-sthula-kundalini: the energy manifested as the physical and objective world</term>. 

Furthermore, when this same power exists through its skillful mastery of expansion, it is known to yogis as the **Coiled Power of Supreme Bliss**. That which exists as the state of absolute certainty is the **Subtle Power** <term>Sukshma-kundalini</term>; she is formless, awakened, and famously known in the teachings of the Great Adepts <term>Mahasiddhas</term>. ॥ 21 ॥

->It is said in the **Essence of Reality** <note>original: *Tattvasara*</note>:—<-

The Coiled Power is known as Creation itself, and she exists in two states. In one way, she is the **Gross Form**, which constitutes the individual consciousness <note>original: *pratyag-atmika*</note> of worldly people. ॥ 22 ॥

The other is the **Subtle**, all-pervading power, which is beyond the distinction of "pervaded" and "pervader." A person who is deluded by mere sensory perception does not understand the difference between these two. ॥ 23 ॥

Therefore, the **Subtle Power** is the nature of Supreme Consciousness <note>original: *Para-samvit-svarupa*</note> and is known as the **Middle Power** <term>Madhya-shakti</term>. For the purpose of perfecting the body <term>Deha-siddhi</term>, yogis should learn of her from the mouth of a true teacher and awaken her within the state of their own essential nature. ॥ 24 ॥

### Now, the Descent of the Upper Power is described.

Because it exists above all cosmic principles <term>Tattvas</term>, the nameless **Supreme Station** <note>original: *Paramam padam*</note> is famously known as the "Upper." The power that indicates various direct realizations of one’s own self-awareness is called the **Upper Power** <term>Urdhva-shakti</term>. Her "descent" <term>Nipatana: often translated as the descent of grace</term> means the removal of the appearance of duality within one's own nature; instead, one exists in the undivided wholeness of their true self. ॥ 25 ॥

->And it is said:—<-

Power <term>Shakti</term> is inside the Auspicious One <term>Shiva</term>, and the Auspicious One is inside Power. One should perceive no difference between them, just as there is no difference between the **moon and its moonlight**. ॥ 26 ॥

Thus, it is established that through the **Descent of the Upper Power**, the Great Adept Yogis attain the Supreme Station. ॥ 27 ॥

---

<margin>Note 9: *Vidrupa* (knowing form).</margin> <margin>Note 10: *Vyapya* (pervaded).</margin>

<summary>This page defines the transition from Gross Kundalini (the physical world) to Subtle Kundalini (Supreme Consciousness), identifying the latter as the "Middle Power." It concludes by explaining the "Descent of the Upper Power" as the realization of the absolute unity between Shiva and Shakti, likened to the moon and its light.</summary>

<keywords>Kundalini, Tattvasara, Shaktinipata, Shiva, Shakti, Samvit-svarupa, Mahasiddha, Paramapada, non-duality, Middle Power, Upper Power</keywords>

--- Page 92 ---
<meta>This page concludes the Fourth Lesson of the *Siddha-Siddhanta-Paddhati*. It synthesizes the previous discussions on the Coiled Power (Kundalini) by identifying it with universal Consciousness (Samvit), which serves as the ultimate support for all existence, both physical and spiritual.</meta>

->And it is said:—<-

In every living being, the entire design of creation shines as a single **Pure Consciousness** <term>Samvit: the fundamental, non-dual awareness that underlies all of reality</term>. In every fundamental principle <note>original: *tattva*, referring to the elemental building blocks of the universe</note>, it is Consciousness alone that manifests as supreme majesty. ॥ 28 ॥

In every state of being and every emotion, this Consciousness is restless and intensely engaged <note>original: *lampata*, literally "lustful" or "greedy," here used metaphorically to describe how consciousness "clings" to and immerses itself in every possible experience</note>. In every appearance of light and every perception, Consciousness is expansive and skillful in its devotion to its own expression. ॥ 29 ॥

What is the meaning of this? The established conclusion <term>Siddhanta</term> is this: **Pure Consciousness**, which consists of the power of reflection between the highest and the lowest <note>original: *para-apara-vimarsha-rupini*</note>, exists as the foundation of the entire microcosm <term>Pinda: the individual body, which the yogi views as a complete mirror of the entire universe</term> through its various manifestations of power. ॥ 30 ॥

->**Thus, in the *Track of the Doctrines of the Adepts* <note>original: *Siddha-Siddhanta-Paddhati*</note> composed by the venerable Gorakshanatha,**<-
->**this is the fourth lesson, titled "The Foundation of the Microcosm."** <term>Pindadhara</term> ॥ 4 ॥<-

<summary>This page concludes the fourth chapter by defining Samvit (Pure Consciousness) as the singular, all-pervading force that supports the entire body and the cosmos. It emphasizes that every aspect of creation and perception is simply a manifestation of this one underlying awareness.</summary>
<keywords>Samvit, Pure Consciousness, Pinda, Microcosm, Gorakshanatha, Siddha-Siddhanta-Paddhati, Pindadhara, Foundation of the Body</keywords>

--- Page 93 ---
<meta>This page begins the Fifth Lesson of the *Siddha-Siddhanta-Paddhati*. Having established the nature of the microcosm (the body) in the previous lesson, the text now transitions into the practical and experiential "unification" of that body with the ultimate reality. It shifts from anatomical and metaphysical description to the spiritual methodology of the Great Adepts.</meta>

# The Fifth Lesson

Now, the unification of the individual body and the supreme state is described:
The Great Perfected Yogis <note>original: *Mahasiddhayogins*</note>, having understood the sequence previously explained—from the Supreme Microcosm down to one's own individual body—should bring them into a state of "one-flavor" <term>Samarasa: a state of absolute equilibrium or "equal taste" where the individual soul and the universal spirit are merged</term> within the Supreme State. ॥ 1 ॥

The "Supreme State" is that which is known only through self-experience <term>Svasamvedya: knowledge that cannot be taught but must be felt directly within one's own consciousness</term>, and it consists of the ultimate manifestation of light. ॥ 2 ॥

->It is said in the *Compendium of Truth* <note>original: *Tattva-samhita*</note>:—<-

> Where neither the intellect nor the mind exists, there is the supreme power <note>original: *kala*</note> that knows the Truth. Neither reasoning nor doubt should be practiced there; what can speech accomplish in that place?
> How can that state be reached even by an eloquent teacher? Therefore, Shiva himself has said that the Supreme State is known only through one's own experience. ॥ 3 ॥

Therefore, even after being struck with wonder<gloss>1: discussion</gloss> by the brilliance<gloss>2: the essential form</gloss> of various clever philosophical arguments, the established conclusion <term>Siddhanta</term> remains that the Supreme State is indeed known only through direct self-experience, determined through devotion to the Guru's feet<gloss>3: through the mere support of his grace, or the state of being unattached and homeless</gloss>. ॥ 4 ॥

In this process, the Guru is the one who consistently shows the true path.
The true path is the path of Yoga; any other path is a heretical path <note>original: *pashanda-marga*</note>.

->As was said by the Primordial Lord <note>original: *Adinatha*, a name for Shiva as the first teacher of Yoga</note>:—<-

> Those who are initiated into the Yogic paths and the Tantras yet revile them are called heretics, as are those who associate with such people. ॥ 5 ॥

When this path is shown, the direct realization of self-experience arises at that very moment. Therefore, the Guru alone is said to be the cause of this realization. ॥ 6 ॥

Thus, through the downward glance of the Guru's eyes <note>an act of spiritual transmission or initiation</note> and through self-experience, the Great Perfected Yogis unify their own individual bodies <note>original: *pinda*</note> with the "one-flavor" state through an experience from which there is no return <note>original: *nirutthana*, a deep state of absorption where the practitioner does not "rise" back into ordinary, dualistic thinking</note>. This is the final conclusion. ॥ 7 ॥

---

<note>The following are variants found in different manuscript traditions:</note>
1 - Discussion (H.). 2 - Essential limb/form (T.). 3 - Through the mere support of grace (H.); Because of the Guru's state, being without attributes and being without a fixed home (T.). 4 - By the Master Teacher (T.).

<summary>This page introduces the concept of Samarasakarana (unification), arguing that the highest spiritual state cannot be reached through intellect or speech, but only through the direct experience granted by a Guru's grace.</summary>
<keywords>Samarasa, Guru, self-experience, Yoga path, heretical paths, Adinatha, Pinda, spiritual unification</keywords>

--- Page 94 ---
<meta>This page continues the Fifth Lesson of the *Siddha-Siddhanta-Paddhati*. The previous page concluded that the "Supreme State" can only be known through direct personal experience and devotion to a Guru. Page 24 now moves into the specific methodology for attaining this state and the outward lifestyle and symbols associated with a perfected Yogi.</meta>

For example—the means to attain the state of "non-arising" <term>Nirutthana: a high state of yogic absorption where mental fluctuations and worldly attachments no longer arise</term> is described:

Through the contemplation of one's own true nature, a "personal immersion" <note>original: *nijavesha*</note> occurs for the Great Perfected Yogis <note>original: *Mahasiddhayogins*</note>. From this personal immersion, a certain magnificent dawning of the state of non-arising is produced. 

Then, from the wonder of "Existence-Consciousness-Bliss" <term>Sachidananda: the fundamental nature of reality in Hindu philosophy</term>, the awakening of the light of the non-dual form occurs. 

From this awakening, this entire world—manifested as both dual and non-dual—is clearly revealed as the Supreme State itself, which illuminates the appearance of consciousness. This is the truth. ॥ 8 ॥

Therefore, by the Great Perfected Yogis—having obtained the grace of the Guru and possessing the unified power of focused attention—the Supreme State is experienced in that very moment. ॥ 9 ॥

By the strength of that experience, having thoroughly understood one's own perfected individual body <term>Pinda: literally "lump" or "ball," referring here to the microcosmic physical and energetic body</term>, and having unified it with the Supreme State, one experiences a sense of greatness within, as if returning to and ascending into that state for the sake of the perfection of one's own body. ॥ 10 ॥

The "individual body" is merely the dawning of the bliss of the rays of one's true nature; the withdrawal of this dawning is what constitutes "unification" <term>Samarasa: the state of "one-flavor" where all distinctions melt away</term>. ॥ 11 ॥

Therefore, it is well known that Great Perfected Yogis remain for the sake of the perfection of the body, accepting their own body—a mass of great rays appearing in its own form—within themselves through self-contemplation. ॥ 12 ॥

Now, the outward appearance <note>or insignia</note> for the perfection of the body is described:

> The wearing of the conch and the seal <term>Mudra: here referring to the ritual earrings worn by Kanphata Yogis</term>, the maintenance of the hair and body hair, the pure drinking of the nectar <term>Amari-pana: a tantric practice involving the recycling of internal fluids</term>, and vigorous massage. ॥ 13 ॥

> Living in solitude, initiation, the twilight rituals, silent recitation <note>original: *japa*</note>, and the worship of the form of Bhairava-of-Knowledge <note>Bhairava is the fierce manifestation of Shiva</note> with wine and other offerings. ॥ 14 ॥

> The blowing of the conch, the "lion's roar" <note>a specific yogic sound or shout</note>, the loincloth, and wooden sandals; the body-cloth, the outer garment, the blanket, and a wondrous umbrella. ॥ 15 ॥

<summary>This page describes the internal process of attaining the state of non-arising (Nirutthana) through the grace of a Guru and outlines the specific physical items and ritual practices—such as wearing earrings and carrying a conch—that define the lifestyle of a perfected Yogi.</summary>

--- Page 95 ---
<meta>This page continues the Fifth Lesson of the *Siddha-Siddhanta-Paddhati*. The previous page discussed the internal realization of the "Supreme State" (Parama-pada) and how it is revealed through the Guru's grace. Page 25 now bridges the gap between the Yogi's outward appearance—their physical symbols—and the internal "Innate" (Sahaja) state of non-dual consciousness.</meta>

The staff, the water pot, and the three horizontal lines of ash <note>original: *tripundrakam*; a sacred mark of Shiva devotees</note>—one should adopt these symbols with diligent effort, preceded by bowing to the Guru. When the perfection of the body <term>Pinda-siddhi: the spiritualization and total mastery of the physical and energetic body</term> is achieved through these practices, all supernatural powers are within reach. ॥ 17 ॥

->It has been said—<-

By knowing which the entire world is mastered effortlessly as if in play, and the spiritual perfections <note>original: *siddhis*</note> arrive of their own accord; therefore, one should meditate upon the Supreme State. ॥ 18 ॥

The Supreme State is not truly attained through outward appearance <note>original: *vesha*</note>. Appearance is rooted in the physical body and serves as a means for public recognition <note>to show one's status or path to the world</note>. ॥ 19 ॥

In the world, what is "low" and "high" is distinguished and adopted; this is called "one's own duty" <note>original: *svadharma*</note>, particularly within the path of Yoga. ॥ 20 ॥

There is no path higher than the path of Yoga—not in the revealed Vedas <note>original: *shruti*</note> nor in the traditional codes of law <note>original: *smriti*</note>. ॥ 21 ॥

In ancient times, Lord Shiva declared in all the scriptures that Yoga is the method of preparation <note>original: *sannahana*; literally "girding on armor"</note> for the union and application of knowledge. ॥ 22 ॥

In the world, whatever base actions people constantly perform, they eventually abandon those actions through the power of worldly wisdom. ॥ 23 ॥

For all human beings, through the influence of past impressions <term>Samskara: the mental imprints or latent tendencies left by previous thoughts and actions</term>, the truth of scripture, logic, and proper conduct becomes gradually clear. ॥ 24 ॥

Thus, when the body is perfected, in order to attain knowledge and having fully understood the view of the Great Perfected Ones <note>the *Mahasiddhas*</note> regarding that Supreme State, the individual soul <term>Jivatma: the individual spark of consciousness</term> is perceived within that sense of "I-ness" through the non-dual progression of innate restraint. ॥ 25 ॥

Regarding the term "innate" <note>original: *sahaja*</note>: 
That knowledge which exists as one's own true nature—perceiving the Supreme Lord who transcends the universe yet appears as the universe itself as one and the same—is known as the "Innate." ॥ 26 ॥

10 - Meditation <note>Tamil script: *tam.*</note>

<vocab>Perfection of the body, The path of Yoga, The Supreme State, Innate/Spontaneous, Individual soul, Three-lined ash mark, Non-duality</vocab>

<summary>This page explains that while outward yogic symbols (like the staff and ash) are useful for social recognition and discipline, the true goal is the inner "Innate" (Sahaja) state where the individual soul recognizes its non-dual identity with the Supreme Lord. It emphasizes that Yoga is the highest path for refining one's nature and attaining spiritual perfection.</summary>
<keywords>Yoga path, Pinda-siddhi, Sahaja, Parama-pada, Jivatma, Tripundrakam, Shiva, Svadharma, Samskara</keywords>

--- Page 96 ---
<meta>This page continues the exposition of the internal states of a realized Yogi. While the previous page contrasted outward religious symbols with internal mastery, page 26 defines the specific psychological and spiritual states—Restraint, the Method, and Non-duality—that lead to the "Innate" (Sahaja) state. It emphasizes that this realization comes only through the Guru, not through scholarly study or intellectual Vedic mantras.</meta>

**Restraint** <note>original: *Samyama*</note> is so called because, after restraining one's own mental functions which are active and flickering, one is fixed within the Self. ॥ 27 ॥

**The Method** <note>original: *Sopaya*</note> is this: one must always remain established in Reality, having unified oneself with the Self through that which is inherently self-luminous. ॥ 28 ॥

**Non-duality** <note>original: *Advaita*</note> means that the Yogi, being a "non-doer" <note>one who acts without the ego of agency</note>, is eternally satisfied and free from conceptual constructs <note>original: *nirvikalpa*</note>; he remains forever in a state where no worldly thoughts arise. ॥ 29 ॥

->It has been said:<-

> The innate state <term>Sahaja: the natural, spontaneous state of enlightenment</term> is the consciousness of one's own Self; restraint <gloss>11</gloss> is the mastery over oneself; the "Method" is resting in one's own nature; and Non-duality is the Supreme State. ॥ 30 ॥
>
> That state is to be known <gloss>14</gloss> from the mouth of a **True Guru**; it cannot be found otherwise, even through millions of scriptures, nor through logic, verbal knowledge, rituals, or the chanting of the Vedas. ॥ 31 ॥
>
> Nor is it found through listening to the *Vedanta* <note>the philosophical end of the Vedas</note>, nor from the realization of "Thou Art That" <note>original: *Tat Tvam Asi*; a famous Upanishadic 'Great Saying'</note>, nor from the repetition of the "Hamsa" breath-mantra, nor from the intellectual contemplation of the unity of the individual soul and the Absolute. ॥ 32 ॥
>
> Neither through meditation nor <gloss>13</gloss> through absorption <note>original: *laya*</note> is one dissolved; the Yogi who has crossed to the further shore of perfection <gloss>12</gloss> is known by the All-knowing ones. Such a Yogi is a self-made master of his own will, ageless and immortal through his spiritual play <note>original: *lila*</note>. ॥ 33 ॥
>
> Unslayable by gods or demons, he sports like **Bhairava** <note>a fierce, liberated form of Lord Shiva</note>. Thus, he who is unshakeable attains this state through the natural progression of his spiritual play. ॥ 34 ॥
>
> All "impossible" perfections <note>original: *siddhis*</note> are made manifest by the Lord. In the first stage, the perfection of health is achieved, and the Yogi becomes beloved by all people; everyone constantly yearns for a sight of him who is established in the Self. ॥ 35 ॥
>
> <gloss>15</gloss> In the second stage, he becomes successful and masters all forms of speech <gloss>16</gloss>. In the third stage, he possesses a divine body and is no longer bothered by serpents or tigers. ॥ 36 ॥

---

11 - Self-restraint <note>variant reading</note>. 12 - From one who has crossed over. 13 - From the absorption of the body, a Yogi is formed by relying on the practice of silence. 14 - Without the Guru's compassion. 15 - Poetry. 16 - Speech.

<summary>This page defines the core internal states of Yoga—Samyama, Sopaya, and Advaita—and asserts that true realization (Sahaja) is a gift of the Guru that transcends scriptural study. It also outlines the first three stages of physical and social perfections (siddhis) attained by the Yogi.</summary>

--- Page 97 ---
<meta>This page continues the description of the Yogi's progression. Having previously established the necessity of the Guru and the definition of the "Innate" state, the text now details a chronological timeline—spanning twelve years—of the specific supernatural powers and spiritual transformations a practitioner undergoes.</meta>

In the fourth year, the practitioner becomes free from hunger, thirst, sleep, cold, and heat. He is born as a divine **Lord of Yogis** <note>original: *divyayogīśo*</note> and, without a doubt, gains the power of **remote hearing** <note>the ability to hear sounds from great distances</note>. ॥ 37 ॥

Mastery of speech <note>the power for one's words to come true</note> comes in the fifth year, as well as the ability to **enter the bodies of others**. In the sixth year, he cannot be cut by weapons, nor is he afflicted by lightning strikes. ॥ 38 ॥

In the seventh year, he becomes as swift as the wind, gains the power to **leave the earth** <note>levitation</note>, and develops remote vision. By the eighth year, he becomes endowed with the eight perfections, such as **Minuteness** <term>Anima: the yogic power to become as small as an atom</term>. ॥ 39 ॥

In the ninth year, he gains an **Adamantine Body** <term>Vajrakaya: a body as indestructible and radiant as a diamond</term>; he becomes a "sky-walker" and can travel in any direction. In the tenth year, he is swifter than the breeze and runs wherever he desires. <gloss>variant: he runs wherever he wishes (from the Yoga-treatise)</gloss> ॥ 40 ॥

Perfectly in the eleventh year, he becomes all-knowing and a possessor of **Perfection** <note>original: *Siddhi*</note>. In the twelfth year, he becomes **equal to Shiva**; he himself becomes the creator and the destroyer. ॥ 41 ॥

This **Perfected One** <term>Siddha: an accomplished master</term> is worshipped in the three worlds, truly like the glorious **Bhairava** <note>a fierce and powerful manifestation of the god Shiva</note>. Thus, in twelve years, a **Master Yogi** of great power is born through the influence of the **True Guru’s** feet; of this, there is no doubt. ॥ 42 ॥

->The lineage of the Guru’s family is said to be five-fold:<-
The **Ai** lineage, the **Lord of the Cave** <note>original: *Vileśvara*</note> lineage, the **Sacred Ash** <note>original: *Vibhūti*</note> lineage, the **Master** <note>original: *Nātha*</note> lineage, and the **Lord of Yogis** <note>original: *Yogīśvara*</note> lineage. Each of these lineages possesses its own distinct specialty. ॥ 43 ॥

In the ultimate sense, everything is composed of the **five elements**. Humans are born from that. However, **Shiva** <note>the Supreme Consciousness</note> is of the sole nature of pure **Self-awakening**. Everything other than that is ignorance and unmanifest, whereas Shiva is Knowledge itself. ॥ 44 ॥

Even among these lineages, some turn their faces away from the True Form. Possessing **only the outward dress** <note>wearing the robes of a monk without the internal realization</note>, they engage in buying and selling, disparage one another’s lineages, and show hatred toward the path of Yoga. ॥ 45 ॥

<margin>17 — Runs wherever he desires.</margin> <margin>18 — Not born (variant reading).</margin>

<summary>This page outlines a twelve-year timeline of spiritual and supernatural milestones for a Yogi, culminating in becoming equal to the god Shiva. It also lists five specific Guru lineages and warns against hypocrites who wear the clothes of a Yogi but seek only profit.</summary>

--- Page 98 ---
<meta>This page transitions from describing the chronological powers (Siddhis) of a twelve-year practice to contrasting the enlightened state of a Yogi with the delusions of the worldly, while detailing the elemental and spiritual structure of the practitioner's body.</meta>

### Page 28

Driven by **passion** <term>Rajas: the quality of energy, attachment, and restless activity</term>, those of terrible resolve are lustful and extremely irritable <gloss>alternate reading: arrogant</gloss>. ॥ 19 ॥ Such hypocritical, proud, and sinful men mock those who are dear to the Lord. ॥ 46 ॥

They mock those characterized by the company of the holy, the study of sacred scriptures, and supreme bliss—those who possess the knowledge and realization of moving through the world according to their own free will. ॥ 47 ॥

"You are wicked, we are virtuous; you are ruined, and so are we"—thus they speak constantly, steeped in total delusion. ॥ 48 ॥

Earth, water, fire, wind, and space—these are well-known as the **continuums** <term>Santana: here referring to the elemental emanations or lineages of the physical world</term>. ॥ 49 ॥

Hardness, fluidity, heat, motion, and stability—these are indeed the five qualities of the continuums, respectively. ॥ 50 ॥

Brahma, Vishnu, Rudra, Ishvara, and Sadashiva are the deities assigned to these continuums in order. ॥ 51 ॥

The **gross**, the **subtle**, the **causal**, the **fourth**, and **that which is beyond the fourth**—these are the five states of consciousness <note>original: *pancavasthah*</note> recognized in order. He who knows all of these is a **Yogi**, an **Accomplished Master** <term>Siddha: one who has attained spiritual perfection</term>, and the **Lord of the Lords of Yogis**; thus, the supreme secret is revealed. ॥ 52 ॥

Therefore, by serving the **True Guru** <term>Sadguru</term> who brings about one's own spiritual rest, and by carefully attaining the supreme state, one should remain steady in the **Essence of All-Bliss** through total stillness, having brought one's own body <note>original: *pinda*, literally "the lump" or individual form</note> into the state of **Essential Oneness** <term>Samarasa: the state where all dualities are perceived as having the same "flavor" or essence</term>. Then, it is the truth that he becomes a **Great Accomplished Master**. ॥ 53 ॥

There is no ritual rule, no social class <gloss>alternate reading: no prohibition</gloss>, and no concept of what is or is not forbidden <gloss>20</gloss>. There is no distinction, no death, no impurity, and no need for funeral rites. ॥ 54 ॥

Such is the state for the Lord of the Lords of Yogis, the practitioner who is eternally satisfied, having attained the state of resting in the happiness of the consciousness of the Self through his merit. ॥ 55 ॥
