<meta>This page continues the transition from the previous page's discussion of yogic practices and elemental knowledge to a critique of physical rituals, emphasizing that the "Supreme State" is attained solely through the grace of the Guru rather than external efforts or austerities.</meta>

### Page 29

->Not from the correct understanding of one's own inherent nature, nor from sequential practice, nor from physical postures;<-
->Not from dispassion <term>Vairagya: the state of being free from worldly desires</term>, nor from despair, nor from the regulation of food or breath; ॥ 56 ॥<-

->Not from the mastery of ritual gestures <term>Mudra: symbolic or energetic hand positions and body seals</term>, nor from reliance on pious deeds;<-
->Not from the vain efforts of those who have renounced the world, nor from the endurance of bodily suffering; ॥ 57 ॥<-

->Not from chanting, nor from austerities or meditation, nor from sacrifices or visiting holy sites;<-
->Not from relying on the worship of deities with devotion, nor from the observance of the rules of the stages of life; ॥ 58 ॥<-

->Not from the study of the **six philosophical schools** <term>Shad-darshana: the classical systems of Indian philosophy</term> or the wearing of matted hair, nor from shaving the head;<-
->The **Supreme State** <note>original: Param Padam</note> is not obtained through an infinite number of external methods and efforts. ॥ 59 ॥<-

->Having abandoned all these physical means, the **Accomplished Masters** <term>Siddha: an enlightened being who has attained spiritual perfection</term> dwell in the non-physical Supreme State. ॥ 60 ॥<-

->How then is it reached? For those who are firm and truthful, that state usually arises through the **Guru’s glance** <term>Guru-drk-pata: the transformative look of a spiritual teacher</term>. ॥ 61 ॥<-

->Whether through spoken instruction, the **descent of power** <term>Shaktipata: the transmission of spiritual energy from teacher to student</term>, or by looking at the Guru’s feet—<-
->The Supreme State is fully attained through the grace of one’s own Guru. ॥ 62 ॥<-

Therefore, it was said by the Lord Shiva:

> There is nothing greater than the Guru, nothing greater than the Guru, nothing greater than the Guru, nothing greater than the Guru.
> This is the command of Shiva, the command of Shiva, the command of Shiva, the command of Shiva. ॥ 63 ॥

->Whether through a mere word or a glance, he who instantly makes known<-
->The clear, self-realized Supreme State of Shiva <note>original: Shambhavam</note>. ॥ 64 ॥<-

->Cutting the **eight bonds** <term>Pasha-ashtaka: the eight fetters of the individual soul, such as shame, doubt, fear, and social status</term> of the bound soul with the sword of compassion,<-
->He who produces perfect bliss is called the **True Guru** <term>Sadguru</term>. ॥ 65 ॥<-

->He who, through a glance lasting only half a blink, or by looking at his feet,<-
->Establishes the Self in stillness—to that Holy Guru, I bow. ॥ 66 ॥

<summary>This page argues that the "Supreme State" cannot be reached through external rituals, austerities, or philosophies, but is instead attained instantly through the grace, glance, and word of a True Guru. It concludes with a powerful exaltation of the Guru as the supreme authority in the Shaiva tradition.</summary>
<keywords>Guru, Shaktipata, Supreme State, liberation from rituals, Shiva's command, Sadguru, eight bonds</keywords>

--- Page 100 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion on the nature of the Supreme State, moving from the critique of external rituals to the defining characteristics of a True Guru. It emphasizes that realization is not a product of intellectual labor but of the "inner rest" triggered by a qualified teacher.</meta>

### Page 30

He who, through his discourse, brings about the **rest of various mental fluctuations** <term>Vishranti: a state of meditative repose or "resting" where the mind's divided activity ceases</term> is to be known as the **True Guru** <note>original: Sadguru</note>, not a false pretender. ॥ 67 ॥

Therefore, to attain the Supreme State, that True Guru is always to be honored. The title "Guru" is one who increases peace <gloss>21: variant: "excellence of qualities" or "beyond qualities"</gloss> and correctly teaches the **resting in consciousness** <term>Chaitanya-vishranti: the soul's repose in pure awareness</term>. By this resting, the **Supreme State** <note>original: Paramapada</note>, which is higher than the highest, manifests of its own accord. In that moment, one becomes a **Direct Perceiver** <term>Sakshatkari: one who experiences the truth firsthand rather than through books or logic</term>. ॥ 68 ॥

For this reason, it is declared in the opinion of the **Great Adepts** <term>Mahasiddha: perfected masters of yoga and tantra</term>: He who, through mere words, or a **transformative glance** <term>Drkpata: the transmission of spiritual power through the teacher's gaze</term>, or a proper look, leads one to a state of repeated rest in an instant (leading one to be liberated in rest), is the True Guru. Otherwise, the **Established Doctrine** <term>Siddhanta: a finalized philosophical conclusion</term> is that without one's own internal rest, the **harmonization of the individual and the Supreme** <term>Samarasa: literally "equal flavor"; the non-dual state where the practitioner and the absolute are experienced as one</term> does not occur. Therefore, only he who provides this inner rest is called a True Guru, and no other. Furthermore, a guru who is merely **wearisome** <gloss>22: variant: shramaka; implying one who labors over logic without spiritual results</gloss> through scriptural views, inference, and the tricks of logic, should be abandoned. ॥ 69 ॥

->And it is said:<-

A guru lacking in knowledge is to be abandoned; he is a liar and a pretender. If he does not know his own inner rest, what can he do for others? ॥ 70 ॥

Can one reach the "other shore" by means of a stone? A heap of stones only causes one to sink <note>This classic metaphor suggests that an un-enlightened teacher is like a heavy rock that drags the student down into the ocean of worldly existence rather than acting as a boat</note>. Only he who has himself crossed over is truly capable of ferrying others across. ॥ 71 ॥

From the terrible ocean of **dualistic thoughts** <term>Vikalpa: the mental constructs, doubts, and choices that divide reality</term>, which is difficult to cross because of the waves of anxiety, and which is infested with the nets of "crocodiles" that are the corrupt **latent impressions** <term>Vasana: the deep-seated subconscious habits and desires that drive human behavior</term> of the phenomenal world... ॥ 72 ॥

...from the force of the waves of these impressions, he who is able to ferry one across <gloss>23: variant: "unable to be destroyed"</gloss> by his own stable and fresh instruction, which brings an immediate cessation of all further mental disturbances—he is the True Guru. ॥ 73 ॥

21-Excellence of qualities (Yoga), beyond qualities (Ta.). 22-A laborer/wearisome (Ha.). 23-Variant reading suggesting inability to cross/destroy.

<summary>This page defines the "True Guru" not by their ritual status or logic, but by their ability to grant the student "inner rest" (Vishranti). It warns against false teachers who are like stones that sink their followers in the ocean of mental anxiety and latent desires.</summary>
<keywords>Sadguru, Vishranti, Paramapada, Mahasiddha, Samarasa, Vikalpa, Vasana, True Guru vs. False Guru</keywords>

--- Page 101 ---
<meta>This page concludes the fifth chapter, continuing the description of the True Guru's power to grant immediate liberation. It transitions from the definition of a teacher to the internal results of their grace—specifically the dissolution of the individual ego into the supreme state.</meta>

### Page 31

He truly delivers the disciple through a **transformative glance** <term>Drkpata: the transmission of spiritual energy through the eyes</term>, through his spoken word, or even a simple look. Once delivered, the disciple is established in his own true state and becomes steady within his own innermost self. ॥ 74 ॥

Then, that disciple is released from the **bonds of birth and worldly existence** <note>original: Janma-samsara-bandhana; the cycle of reincarnation and suffering</note>. Becoming filled with supreme bliss and **undivided** <term>Nishkala: literally "without parts"; referring to the absolute, partless nature of ultimate reality</term>, he attains the state of the Divine <note>original: Shivatam; the nature of Shiva</note>. ॥ 75 ॥

In that very moment, the Guru delivers tens of millions of the disciple's ancestral lineages. Therefore, one should directly honor that **True Guru** <note>original: Sadguru</note> at the three transitions of the day <note>dawn, noon, and sunset</note>. ॥ 76 ॥

One should serve the Guru constantly, offering praise and a **prostration of the whole body** <note>original: Sarvanga-pranipata; a bow where all limbs touch the ground as a sign of total surrender</note> <gloss>24: a virtuous man</gloss>. Through the "roasting" <note>the metaphorical burning away of past karmas and mental impurities</note> of these bonds, he attains stability and becomes the very essence of his own true nature. ॥ 77 ॥

What use is there in speaking much here, or in studying hundreds of millions of scriptures? The **rest of the mind** <term>Chitta-vishranti: the cessation of mental agitation</term> is difficult to attain without the supreme grace of the Guru. ॥ 78 ॥

For those yogis who have attained this mental rest, whose consciousness is firm, and who are immersed within their own selves, there is a special state of **permanent absorption** <term>Nirutthanam: literally "not rising again"; a state where the mind does not return to its previous distracted or worldly condition</term>. ॥ 79 ॥

In the blink of an eye, the difficult-to-attain **Supreme State** <note>original: Paramam Padam</note> shines forth clearly. In that state, the **individual body** <term>Pinda: the physical and subtle body of the practitioner</term> becomes dissolved suddenly; of this, there is no doubt. ॥ 80 ॥

For those who devote themselves to this very "rest"—which arises through the play of consciousness <gloss>25: the manifestation of the rays of one's own true nature and the play of consciousness</gloss>, through the activity of pure awareness, and the unfolding of spiritual powers—the light shines of its own accord. When the momentum of one's own impulses is swallowed up, the **unity of the State and the Body** <note>original: Pada-pinda-aikyam</note> truly becomes a **single harmonious flavor** <term>Samarasa: the non-dual state where all distinctions between the individual and the absolute vanish</term> for those who are beloved of the Guru. ॥ 81 ॥

-> ॥ Thus ends the fifth lesson, titled <-
-> **The Harmonization of the Body and the State** <-
-> in the *Method of the Proven Teachings* composed by the illustrious Gorakshanatha ॥ 5 ॥ <-

--- Page 102 ---
<meta>This page begins the Sixth Chapter, moving from the role of the Guru to a detailed definition of the "Avadhuta," the perfected ascetic who has realized the teachings. It provides etymological and symbolic explanations for the lifestyle and tools of the yogi.</meta>

# Chapter Six

Now, the characteristics of the **Avadhuta Yogi** <term>Avadhuta: literally "the shaken-off one"; a high-level adept who has "shaken off" all worldly attachments, social obligations, and egoic notions</term> are described:

In response to the question, "Who is an Avadhuta Yogi?" it is said:

He who "shakes off" (*avadhū*) all the **transformations of nature** <term>Prakriti-vikara: the changes and evolutions of the physical and mental world</term> is an Avadhuta. He who possesses Union (Yoga) is a Yogi. The root *dhūñ* signifies "to shake" or "to agitate." Here, "shaking" refers to taking the mind—which is usually attached to the body, bodily concerns, and the external world—withdrawing it from those objects, and dissolving the consciousness into the majesty of one's own divine abode. Such a person is empty of the worldly illusion and free from the distinctions of beginning, middle, end, or death. ॥ 1 ॥

->The syllable *Ya* is the **seed-syllable of Air** <term>Vayu-bija: a sacred sound-vibration associated with the element of air and the breath</term>; the syllable *Ra* is the **seed-syllable of Fire** <term>Vahni-bija</term>.<-
->The non-dual union of these two is the sound *Om*, which is proclaimed as the very form of Consciousness. ॥ 2 ॥<-

This is stated clearly:
> Through the cutting of the waves of **afflictions and bonds** <note>original: klesha-pasha; kleshas are mental poisons like ignorance and ego; pashas are the tethers of worldly existence</note>, one attains a true "shaving" <note>original: vimundanam; refers to the ritual of shaving the head for monks, but here used metaphorically for the total removal of mental attachments</note>. He who is liberated from all states of being is called an Avadhuta. ॥ 3 ॥

> The Yogi who is adorned on his own body with the **sacred ash** <term>Vibhuti</term> of his own **innate radiance** <note>original: nija-sphara; the footnote suggests "sphara" (expansion/radiance) rather than "smara" (memory/desire)</note>, and who is firmly established in the **foundation** <note>original: Adhara; referring to the foundational chakra or the absolute base of reality</note>, is called an Avadhuta. ॥ 4 ॥

> Sitting firmly in the midst of the world, yet abandoned of all **mental fabrications** <note>original: kalana; the mental process of projecting labels and distinctions onto reality</note>; possessing only a loincloth and a begging bowl, yet free from wretchedness—he is called an Avadhuta. ॥ 5 ॥

> *Shaṃ* signifies bliss; *Khaṃ* signifies the **Supreme Brahman** <note>the ultimate, infinite reality</note>. Through their union (*shang-kha*), the **Conch** <note>Shankha; the yogi's ritual conch shell is here a symbol for the meeting of individual joy and universal consciousness</note> is born. He who holds this **established truth** <term>Siddhanta: the final conclusion of spiritual inquiry</term> is called an Avadhuta. ॥ 6 ॥

> His "sandals" are the awareness of the Supreme State; his "deer-skin" <gloss>original variant: the "unstruck" sound</gloss> is the **great repository** <note>original: maha-hrada; literally "the great lake," a metaphor for the deep, still pool of pure consciousness</note>. He whose only boundary is **Supreme Consciousness** is called an Avadhuta. ॥ 7 ॥

> His "waist-band" is eternal **renunciation** <term>Nivritti: the path of inward withdrawal from worldly action</term>; his "straw mat" is his own true nature. He who has withdrawn from the **six transformations** <note>the six stages of physical life: birth, existence, growth, maturity, decay, and death</note> is called an Avadhuta. ॥ 8 ॥

<summary>This page defines the Avadhuta Yogi through etymological play and symbolic reinterpretations of a yogi's traditional possessions (ash, conch, sandals). It emphasizes that true renunciation is an internal state of consciousness rather than mere external appearance.</summary>
<keywords>Avadhuta, Yoga, seed-syllables (Beeja), seed-syllable of Air (Ya), seed-syllable of Fire (Ra), Om, renunciation (Nivritti), six transformations (Shad-vikara), sacred ash (Vibhuti)</keywords>

--- Page 103 ---
<meta>This page continues the sixth chapter of the manuscript, providing a series of metaphorical definitions for the Avadhuta. It translates the traditional external symbols of a wandering ascetic (earrings, staff, begging bowl) into internal, spiritual realizations.</meta>

# Chapter Six (Continued)

He whose two earrings are **manifold light** <term>Chitra-prakasha: the variegated or multifaceted light of consciousness</term> and **supreme bliss**, and whose rest for the senses is his rosary, is called an Avadhuta. ॥ 9 ॥

He whose staff is made of **fortitude**, whose begging bowl is the **supreme space** <term>Parakasha: the highest ether or transcendental void</term>, and whose meditation band <note>original: yogapatta; a cloth strap used by yogis to support the knees during long periods of seated meditation</note> is his own innate power, is called an Avadhuta. ॥ 10 ॥

He who takes "difference and non-difference" <note>original: bhedabheda; the philosophical problem of whether the soul is different from or identical to God</note> as his alms, who delights in tasting the **six flavors of existence**, and whose total absorption is his process of "digestion" <term>Jarana: literally "digestion" or "calcination" in alchemy; here referring to the spiritual processing of worldly experience into wisdom</term>, is called an Avadhuta. ॥ 11 ॥

He who enters his own interior within the **inconceivable direction and space** of the Self, and who dwells in that singular, transcendent place, is called an Avadhuta. ॥ 12 ॥

He who, in order to make his own physical body immortal, drinks the infinite **nectar of immortality** <term>Amari: a yogic term for the "nectar" or "ambrosia" (Soma) that flows within the body during deep states of meditation</term> by himself, is called an Avadhuta. ॥ 13 ॥

The inconceivable **Great Thunderbolt** <note>original: ma vajri; likely referring to the power of the central channel or a specific yogic state</note> is firm and often crowded with the impurities of **latent tendencies** <term>Vasana: the deep-seated subconscious inclinations and habits formed over lifetimes</term>. He by whom this "Thunderbolt" has been consumed—leaving him free of those tendencies—is called an Avadhuta. ॥ 14 ॥

He who always revolves correctly within his own center and knows the entire world through the lens of **equanimity**, is called an Avadhuta. ॥ 15 ॥

He who understands his own Self and dwells only in that Self, perfectly established in a state of **non-arising** <term>Anutthana: a state where worldly thoughts and disturbances no longer "rise up" in the mind</term>, is called an Avadhuta. ॥ 16 ॥

He who is endowed with the support of this non-arising state, who has reached the further shore of **supreme rest**, and who knows the reality consisting of fortitude and **pure consciousness**, is called an Avadhuta. ॥ 17 ॥

He who brings the **unmanifest** into the **manifest**, and then completely devours the entire manifest world back into himself—remaining as Truth within—is called an Avadhuta. ॥ 18 ॥

He who is the very light of illumination, well-established in his own **self-luminosity**, and who delights in the world as if it were a game or a play, is called an Avadhuta. ॥ 19 ॥

Sometimes appearing as one who enjoys worldly things, sometimes as a renunciant; sometimes appearing disheveled like a **ghost** <term>Pishacha: a spirit or ghoul; referring to the "madman" style of some high-level saints who disregard social norms</term>, sometimes like a king, and sometimes following traditional conduct—he is called an Avadhuta. ॥ 20 ॥

<summary>This page defines the Avadhuta through esoteric metaphors, reframing the physical tools of a monk—like the staff, earrings, and begging bowl—as internal qualities like fortitude, bliss, and supreme space. It describes the perfected yogi as one who has "swallowed" the world and lives in a state of effortless, self-luminous play.</summary>

<keywords>Avadhuta, spiritual metaphors, immortality, equanimity, self-luminosity, non-duality, internal alchemy, Yoga-patta, Vasana, Cinmaya</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the sixth chapter of the manuscript, transitioning from the internal symbols of the Avadhuta to his identity as the universal essence of all spiritual and philosophical systems. It argues that the Avadhuta is the common thread connecting various Indian traditions, from materialism to high Vedanta.</meta>

# Chapter Six (Continued)

In this way, within the **eternally shining reality** which is indicated by manifold symbols, he who is the true awakener of the self-nature of all philosophical systems is called an **Avadhuta Yogi**; he is the **True Guru** <term>Sadguru: a "true" or "perfect" teacher who provides the ultimate spiritual realization</term>. Because he brings harmony to the vision of the self-nature of all philosophies, he is the Avadhuta Yogi.

He is the one beyond the stages of life <note>original: Atyashrami; one who has transcended the four traditional stages of Hindu life—student, householder, forest-dweller, and monk</note>, the Yogi, the Knower, the Perfected One, and the one of good vows.
He is the Lord, the Master, the Blessed One, and the holy Saint. ॥ 21 ॥

He is the conqueror of the senses, the Auspicious One, the wise, the expert, and the enlightened.
He is the Materialist <note>original: Charvaka; an ancient school of Indian skepticism/materialism</note>, the Jain <note>original: Arhata</note>, the Buddhist, and the Knower of Light. ॥ 22 ॥

The Logician, the follower of Sankhya <note>Sankhya is one of the oldest systematic philosophies of India, focusing on the dualism of consciousness and matter</note>, and the Mimamsaka <note>a school focused on the ritual and hermeneutic interpretation of the Vedas</note> know him as such.
He is celebrated by scholars in millions of scriptures as the Divine, and so on. ॥ 23 ॥

He is the Soul, the Supreme Soul, the Individual Soul, and the Self.
He is the Supreme Reality of Existence, directly named as **Shiva** and **Rudra** <note>Shiva in his fierce aspect</note>. ॥ 24 ॥

That which is situated within the **lotus-cavern of the body** <term>Sharira-padma-kuhara: a poetic and yogic term for the heart center or the subtle space within the human frame where the divine resides</term> of all beings—
That must certainly be known by seekers of liberation through its Great Meaning. ॥ 25 ॥

He is Brahma, Vishnu, and Rudra; he is the Imperishable, the Supreme, the Self-Ruling Sovereign.
He is Indra, he is the Life-Breath, he is the Fire of Time, and he is the Lord of the Senses <note>original: aindrakah; relating to Indra, or as noted in variants, the Moon and Sun</note>. ॥ 26 ॥

He is the Sun, he is Shiva, he is the Supreme Shiva.
He is attainable through Yoga by those devoted to the Sankhya scriptures. ॥ 27 ॥

He is called "Action" <note>original: Karma</note> by all those who investigate the nature of ritual action.
He is called "Truth and Bliss" by the followers of the Vedas everywhere. ॥ 28 ॥

These distinctions exist only in worldly convention; in truth, there is no difference for the One.
In the joy of delight, there is the bliss of giving/receiving <gloss>receiving</gloss> <note>original: ra-dane; the text provides a folk etymology for the word Mudra, where 'Mud' means joy and 'Ra' refers to the act of taking or bestowing</note> between the Individual Soul and the Supreme Soul. ॥ 29 ॥

The consciousness of the **unity of both** is celebrated as the **Mudra** <term>Mudra: while often meaning a hand gesture, here it refers to the "seal" or "final impression" of spiritual realization where the soul and God are seen as one</term>. ॥ 30 ॥

<meta>This page continues the sixth chapter, further defining the spiritual identity of the Avadhuta. It shifts from identifying the Yogi with philosophical schools to providing esoteric, internal definitions of traditional social and religious stages (Ashramas), reinterpreting them as states of consciousness rather than external lifestyles.</meta>

The hosts of gods rejoice and the masses of demons are put to flight; therefore, she is called a **Mudra** <term>Mudra: usually a ritual hand gesture, but here derived from the roots "mud" (to rejoice) and "dra" (to fly/dissolve)</term>, for she directly bestows the gift of eternal auspiciousness. ॥ 31 ॥

Those who are uninitiated into this path and remain forever attached to the passions of the worldly cycle <note>Samsara: the cycle of birth, death, and rebirth</note> are called **heretics** <term>Pashandin: a term often used for those outside the Vedic fold or, in this context, those lacking internal realization</term>; they are weakened and made trivial by the world. ॥ 32 ॥

The Yogi whose very body is the **Avadhuta** remains established in the formless state; within him, the true essential nature of all philosophical systems is revealed. ॥ 33 ॥

### Here, he describes the stage of the Celibate Student among the Brahmins:

He who moves within the knowledge of the Absolute <note>original: Brahma-vidya</note>, which is filled with the form of the All and expanded by his own inner awakening, is called a **Brahmachari** <term>Brahmachari: traditionally a celibate student; here re-defined as one whose conduct (charati) is rooted in the Absolute (Brahman)</term>. ॥ 34 ॥

Eternal Fullness is his housewife, and the unmoving sky of consciousness is his home. He who dwells within these two is designated a **Householder** <note>original: Grihastha; the author is internalizing the second stage of life, where the "home" and "wife" are spiritual qualities rather than physical realities</note>. ॥ 35 ॥

He who has journeyed forever inward into the forest of self-effulgent light is to be known as a **Forest-dweller** <term>Vanaprastha: the third stage of life, traditionally involving retirement to the woods</term>; he is not a forest-dweller simply by wandering in the woods like a deer. ॥ 36 ॥

The Supreme Soul and the individual soul flash forth abundantly within the Self alone. He who has forever cast all things into that realization is designated a **Renunciant** <term>Sannyasi: literally "one who has cast down" or "laid aside" everything</term>. ॥ 37 ॥

He by whom the net of illusion, karma, and its fragmented parts is constantly subdued is the **Triple-Staff Monk** <term>Tridandi: a monk who carries three joined staves, symbolizing control over thought, speech, and action</term>; he shines as unmoving as a great mountain. ॥ 38 ॥

The mind is one, yet it takes many forms and is forever unstable and flickering. He by whom that mind is subdued is called a **Single-Staff Monk** <term>Ekadandi: a monk who carries a single staff, representing the realization of non-duality</term>. ॥ 39 ॥

Pure, peaceful, formless, and supreme bliss, eternally rising—he who knows that **Shiva** in such a way becomes a pure and enlightened follower of Shiva. ॥ 40 ॥

He who constantly "heats" his own blazing senses <note>The text plays on the root "tap" (to heat), which is the basis of the word for asceticism</note> is to be known as an **Ascetic** <term>Tapasa</term>; he is not an ascetic simply by wearing the ash of burnt cow-dung. ॥ 41 ॥

<meta>This page continues the sixth chapter of the manuscript, which provides esoteric, internal definitions of various religious identities. On the previous page, the author redefined the four traditional stages of Hindu life (Ashramas). Here, the text moves to redefine the specific Shaiva and Shakta sects—such as the Pashupatas, Kapalikas, and Kaulas—interpreting their names not as social labels, but as specific states of internal yogic realization.</meta>

### Redefining the Shaiva Sects

Having slain the "beast" <note>Pashu: literally 'animal' or 'tethered soul'</note> of the web of ritual actions and having attained the state of lordship and fullness, he who remains in that pure state <note>original: asubha-vena, likely a scribal error for shubha-bhavena, 'auspicious state'</note> is truly a **Pashupata** <term>Pashupata: historically a member of the oldest Shaiva sect; here redefined as one who has conquered the 'beast' of ritualism to become a 'Lord' (Pati)</term>. ॥ 42 ॥

The **Linga** <term>Linga: an iconic pillar representing Shiva; here, the internal sign of consciousness</term> consists of supreme bliss and is set upon the eternal, immovable seat of one's own self. He who worships that Linga is truly a **Kalamukha** <term>Kalamukha: literally 'Black-faced', a medieval Shaiva sect; here redefined as one whose 'face' is turned toward the sign of Eternal Time (Kala)</term>. ॥ 43 ॥

Having caused the dissolution of all objective principles <note>Tattvas: the building blocks of reality</note> and holding them steady, that hero who is forever firm becomes a **Lingadhari** <term>Lingadhari: 'Wearer of the Sign'; here, one who 'bears' the dissolved universe within their own consciousness</term>. ॥ 44 ॥

Abandoning the elements of death and time, naked and clad only in the four directions <note>original: Digambara; 'sky-clad'</note>, he who is merged in the state of extinction is a **Nirvanapara** <note>Nirvanapara: one devoted to the state of ultimate liberation or 'blowing out' of the ego</term>. ॥ 45 ॥

The knowledge that is the nature of one's own self, protected on all sides by the sacred word <note>Mantra</note>—he who always maintains this non-duality is truly a **Kapalika** <term>Kapalika: literally 'Skull-bearer'; here re-interpreted as one who 'protects' (palayati) the 'cup' (kapala) of self-knowledge</term>. ॥ 46 ॥

The principle of "Great Pervasiveness" is beyond the duality of the container and the contained. He who maintains that vow is truly a **Mahavrata** <term>Mahavrata: the 'Great Vow', a rigorous ascetic path; here, it is the vow to remain in a state of all-pervasive awareness</term>. ॥ 47 ॥

### Redefining the Shakta and Kaula Paths

**Kula** <term>Kula: literally 'family' or 'totality', referring to the manifest world/body</term> is the totality of the physical body, while **Akula** <term>Akula: 'beyond Kula', referring to the unmanifest Absolute</term> is the all-pervading form. The state of unity between these two is Power <note>Shakti</note>. He who knows this is a **Possessor of Power** <note>Shaktibhak</note>. ॥ 48 ॥

The **Kaula** path is the consumption of all limited parts and arts. He who understands that power which performs this constant consumption is called a **Knower of Power** <note>Shaktijnani</note>. ॥ 49 ॥

Having understood the principles of Kula and Akula step by step, through the great self-luminous power, one then attains the **Station of Power** <note>Shaktipada</note>. ॥ 50 ॥

Intoxication, Wine, Intellect, Seal, Illusion, Fish, Mind, Meat, Swooning, and Union <note>original: Mada, Madya, Mati, Mudra, Maya, Mina, Manas, Pala, Murchana, Maithuna; these are internal re-readings of the 'Five Ms' of Tantric ritual</note>—he whose life consists of these [internalized] qualities is called a **Shakta** <term>Shakta: a worshiper of Shakti, the Divine Power</term>. ॥ 51 ॥

He who recognizes that Power by which the radiant, shimmering form of reality is clearly and forcefully manifested—he is designated here as a **Shakta**. ॥ 52 ॥

He who brings about the cessation of worldly rising, while the consciousness of the "Doer" forever expands—and who finds rest through that Power—he is designated here as a **Shakta**. ॥ 53 ॥

<summary>This page provides esoteric etymologies for various medieval Indian religious sects (Pashupata, Kapalika, Shakta, etc.), arguing that a true practitioner is defined by internal states of consciousness rather than external sectarian affiliation.</summary>
<keywords>Pashupata, Kalamukha, Linga, Kapalika, Mahavrata, Kula, Akula, Shakti, Shakta, Tantra, consciousness, liberation</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the sixth chapter's esoteric redefinition of religious identities. While the previous page focused on Shaiva and Shakta sects like the Pashupatas and Kapalikas, this section transitions into the internal meanings of Vaishnava (devotees of Vishnu), Sattvika (pure ones), and Jain (Kshapanaka) identities. The author continues to argue that these titles describe internal states of consciousness rather than external social affiliations.</meta>

### Redefining the Vaishnava and Ascetic Identities

Knowing that supreme essence of Vishnu <note>original: Vishno, the 'Pervader'</note> which is all-pervasive, primeval, and imperishable, and which provides a place of rest within the body—one who knows this becomes a **Vaishnava** <term>Vaishnava: traditionally a worshiper of Vishnu; here defined as one who experiences the all-pervading essence of the divine within their own physical form</term>. ॥ 54 ॥

He whose own nature is luminous, who is free from the dualities of difference and non-difference <note>original: bhedabheda, a major philosophical debate regarding the relationship between the individual soul and God</note>, and in whose body this light always shines—he indeed becomes a **Bhagavata** <term>Bhagavata: a 'devotee of the Lord' (Bhagavan); here defined by the internal light of non-dual consciousness</term>. ॥ 55 ॥

He who knows the "Vaishnava distinction" <note>original: bheda</note> as his own nature, consisting of everything and nothing, awakened and residing within all bodies—such a one is a **Bhedavadi** <term>Bhedavadi: literally 'one who speaks of difference' or a dualist; the author paradoxically redefines this as one who recognizes the 'difference' that the divine resides in every unique form</term>. ॥ 56 ॥

The "night" is said to be the state of "fiveness" <note>Panchatva: a common Sanskrit euphemism for death or dissolution into the five elements</note>—the imperishable loss of the five senses. He who understands that night becomes a **Pancharatrika** <term>Pancharatrika: a follower of the Pancharatra scriptures; here the name is treated as a pun on 'pancha' (five) and 'ratri' (night), signifying the internal dissolution of the senses</term>. ॥ 57 ॥

That Life <note>Jiva</note> by which all living beings truly live and through which they attain liberation in an instant—he by whom that Life is known is called a **Sadajivi** <term>Sadajivi: 'One who lives forever' or 'Ever-living'</term>. ॥ 58 ॥

He who always offers his love to the gracious Supreme Person <note>Purusha</note> and whose senses are fully mastered is designated as a **Sattvika** <term>Sattvika: one characterized by 'Sattva' (purity/goodness)</term>. ॥ 59 ॥

He who knows the "Subtle Swan" <note>Sukshma-Hamsa; the Swan is a symbol of the soul or the breath 'Ham-Sa'</term>—which possesses all forms yet is formless, without cause, and stainless—becomes a **Sukshma-Sattvika** <term>Sukshma-Sattvika: a 'Subtly Pure' being</term>. ॥ 60 ॥

Knowing the Truth to be one, unborn, eternal, infinite, imperishable, and fixed, the wise one who speaks of it is called a **Satyavadi** <term>Satyavadi: 'Speaker of Truth'</term>. ॥ 61 ॥

Through the union of knowledge and the object of knowledge, by the very nature of a Yogi, the pervasive **Purushottama** <term>Purushottama: 'Supreme Person,' a title of Vishnu</term> is to be known even as the "Stained One" <note>Kalanki: perhaps a reference to the Kalki avatar or a paradoxical statement that the Supreme Person encompasses even the 'stains' of the world</note>. ॥ 62 ॥

He who possesses the knowing intellect—that self-luminous and pervasive mind which is intent on the path of liberation—is known as a **Sattvika**. ॥ 63 ॥

One who performs the destruction <note>original: kshapanam</note> of the fluctuations of the mind and the plundering of attachment and aversion, and who is as humble as the vast sky, is a **Kshapanaka** <term>Kshapanaka: usually a term for a Jain monk; here redefined as one who 'destroys' (kshapana) mental impurities</term>. ॥ 64 ॥

Power <note>Shakti</note> appears as expansion; Shiva appears as contraction. He who brings about the union of these two is a **Siddhayogirat** <term>Siddhayogirat: 'King of the Perfected Yogis'</term>. ॥ 65 ॥

<vocab>वैष्णव (Vaishnava), भागवत (Bhagavata), पांचरात्रिक (Pancharatrika), सात्त्विक (Sattvika), सूक्ष्मसात्त्विक (Sukshma-Sattvika), सत्यवादी (Satyavadi), पुरुषोत्तम (Purushottama), क्षपणक (Kshapanaka), सिद्धयोगिराट् (Siddhayogirat)</vocab>

It concludes by defining the highest yogi as one who balances the cosmic forces of expansion (Shakti) and contraction (Shiva).</summary>

--- Page 108 ---
<meta>This page concludes the redefinition of various religious identities by shifting the focus toward the "Siddha Yogi" and the "Avadhuta." While the previous page interpreted Vaishnava and sectarian labels as internal states, these verses describe the ultimate realization of the practitioner who has harmonized all these paths into a single, non-dual experience of reality. The text transitions from defining specific sects to describing the universal qualities of a perfected master (Siddha).</meta>

### The Definition of a Perfected Yogi

Just as the world and that which transcends the world <note>original: vishvātītaṃ, the transcendent absolute</note> appear as one and the same, he who remains in constant union with this reality is a **Siddha Yogi** <term>Siddha Yogi: a "perfected practitioner" who has achieved the goal of yoga; one who sees no difference between the physical world and the divine spirit</term>. ॥ 66 ॥

He in whom the expansion of all personal mental fluctuations <note>original: vṛttīnāṃ, the "whirlpools" or tendencies of the mind</note> finds its dissolution—he is known in the **Siddha Siddhanta** <term>Siddha Siddhanta: the "Established Doctrine of the Perfected Ones," the philosophical system of the Nath yogis</term> as a powerful Siddha Yogi. ॥ 67 ॥

Always equanimous, peaceful, self-abiding, and revealing his own inner light; filled with supreme bliss and wisdom—he is a **King of Siddha Yogis**. ॥ 68 ॥

Perfectly complete, a joyful soul, risen above the states of both "everything" and "nothing," pure and filled with absolute bliss—he is a **King of Siddha Yogis**. ॥ 69 ॥

Complete, joyful, bringing happiness to all, wise, and possessing a mind full of grace toward everyone—he is a **King of Siddha Yogis**. ॥ 70 ॥

The Yogi does not grieve for what is lost, nor does he crave wealth; he does not rejoice in gain. Filled with bliss and absorbed in self-knowledge, he is never bound by the path of time <note>original: kālapatha, a reference to fate, death, or the passage of linear time</note>. ॥ 71 ॥

In this way, even though the various philosophical perspectives <note>original: siddhānta-darśanānāṃ</note> appear distinct from one another, the **Sadguru** <term>Sadguru: the "True Guru" or ultimate teacher</term> is praised as the **Avadhuta** <term>Avadhuta: literally "the shaken-off one"; a radical ascetic who has shaken off all worldly attachments and dualistic concepts</term>—the teacher who demonstrates the synthesis of all paths within the Absolute. That state where these individual teachings find their rest is called "Repose" <note>original: viśrānti, the final state of stillness and spiritual satisfaction</note>. ॥ 72 ॥

He who spontaneously dissolves into a state of wonder where no further impulse to "rise" occurs <note>original: nirutthana, a technical yogic state where the mind no longer returns to worldly distraction after meditation</note>, and who remains in that stillness during meditation—he is the **King of Avadhutas**. ॥ 73 ॥

Therefore, one should always bow to and worship that True Guru directly. Through him, one instantly attains the state of a Siddha, which reveals the light of the Self. ॥ 74 ॥

Those who are rigid like wood <note>original: kāṣṭhaiḥ; some manuscripts read "kaṣṭaiḥ" meaning "with difficulty/pain"</note> and cause confusion in one's vision are not to be saluted. A wise person following the Siddha path should avoid such "gurus" from a distance. ॥ 75 ॥

<summary>This page defines the "Siddha Yogi" and "Avadhuta" as individuals who have transcended worldly dualities and personal mental fluctuations. It emphasizes the "Sadguru" as a master of synthesis who harmonizes all conflicting philosophies into a single state of spiritual repose.</summary>

--- Page 109 ---
<meta>Continuing from the previous page, where the qualities of a "King of Siddha Yogis" were extolled, the text now shifts to a "refutation of opposing views" (parapakṣa-nirāsa). Having defined what a true Master is, the author critiques various religious sects, philosophical schools, and even specific ascetic practices, arguing that they fall short of the spontaneous, natural realization found in the Siddha tradition.</meta>

### He Refutes the Opposing Views :-

The **Vedantin** <term>Vedantin: a follower of the Vedanta school, which focuses on the philosophical study of the Upanishads</term> has a mind hardened by excessive logic and is gripped by illusion <note>original: māyayā, the power that veils reality</note>. The **Bhattas** <term>Bhattas: followers of the Mimamsa school of ritual and hermeneutics</term> are agitated by the desire for the fruits of their ritual actions; the **Vaisheshikas** <term>Vaisheshikas: an ancient school of Indian logic and physics</term> have their intellects destroyed by a belief in duality. Others are delighted only in differentiation and are distressed by debate; they are all cheated of the Truth <note>original: tattvato-vañcitāḥ, deceived regarding the ultimate reality</note> (or "cheated of their very essence").
Therefore, a wise person should always seek refuge in the **Established Doctrine of the Perfected** (*Siddha-mata*) and the **Path of Natural Being** (*Svabhāva-samaya*). ॥ 76 ॥

The followers of **Samkhya**, the **Vaishnavas**, the **Vedic ritualists**, the **Renunciates**, and the **Ascetics**; the Sun-worshippers, the Hero-worshippers, those focused on the material world, the **Buddhists**, the **Jains**, and the **Shravakas** <term>Shravakas: "listeners," often referring to lay disciples in the Jain or Buddhist traditions</term>—all these are devoted to hardship and have wandered onto a useless path; they are cheated of their very essence.
Therefore, a wise person should always seek refuge in the **Established Doctrine of the Perfected** and the **Path of Natural Being**. ॥ 77 ॥

Teachers who have received many initiations, those devoted to fire-offerings, the naked-vow ascetics, and those who practice austerities; those who visit various holy pilgrimage sites, those devoted to chanting, and those who remain in perpetual silence—these people are truly devoted to a heavy burden of sorrow; they are cheated of their very essence.
Therefore, a wise person should always seek refuge in the **Established Doctrine of the Perfected** and the **Path of Natural Being**. ॥ 78 ॥

Those who first practice the methods of **Rechaka** (exhalation), **Puraka** (inhalation), and **Kumbhaka** (breath retention) to purify the path of the channels <note>original: nāḍī-pathā, the subtle energetic veins of the body</note>; then, within the "lotus of the heart," they suddenly cause the mind to become "faint" <note>original: mahā-mūrcchitam, a state of deep meditative absorption or "mental swoon"</note>; then, with a concentrated mind, they visualize the imperishable, undying syllable **Om** as a sprout of light in the "supreme family" <note>original: parakule, a technical term for the highest state of consciousness</note>—even for them, the Eternal Abode is not found.
Therefore, a wise person should always seek refuge in the **Established Doctrine of the Perfected** and the **Path of Natural Being**. ॥ 79 ॥

The **Charvakas** <term>Charvakas: ancient Indian materialists who believed only in what can be perceived by the senses</term> are clever and skilled in logic, yet they are devoted only to the doctrine that the body is the soul. All those who are purely virtuous may cross over the most difficult of sorrows; [but] all those foreigners <note>original: yavanāḥ, historically referring to Greeks or those outside the Indo-Aryan social order</note> who are devoted to sin and are merciless—their worldly life yields only meager fruit; it is not the Truth, nor is it the place of liberation <note>original: mokṣa-padam</note>. ॥ 80 ॥

These are all devoted to artificial mantras and tantras; they are cheated of the Truth. 18—By passion (variant). 19—The fruit of liberation (variant). <-

The author claims these other paths are burdensome or deceptive, leading away from the true, effortless essence of reality.</summary>

--- Page 110 ---
<meta>The previous page established the author's critique of mainstream philosophical and religious schools like Vedanta, Samkhya, and Buddhism. This page continues the "refutation of opposing views," but shifts the focus toward internal Yogic practices. The author argues that even practitioners of complex Hatha Yoga—those focused on specific energy centers (chakras), breath control (pranayama), and physical seals (mudras)—remain trapped in the cycle of suffering if they lack the "natural" realization of the Siddha path.</meta>

At the Śrīhaṭṭa <gloss>20: a variant text reads "at the triangular crossing"</gloss>, at the end of the head, in the cavity of the Threefold Fold, in the **Opening of Brahma** <term>Brahmanandhra: the fontanelle or "soft spot" on the skull, considered the exit point for the soul</term>, and in the forehead;
In the eyes and eyebrows, at the tip of the nose, in the path of the internal sound in the ears, at the uvula, and the "Royal Tooth" <note>the soft palate or a point behind the upper teeth used in specific yogic concentrations</note>;
In the throat, the center of the heart and navel, in the hollow of the "Three Lotuses," in the **Oḍḍiyāna** center, and at the base center <gloss>21: variant reads "at the seat"</gloss>;
Behold! Those who are merely stuck on these physical locations find no awakening into the Supreme State. ॥ 81 ॥

At the Kollāṭa <note>a mystical center in the head</note>, in the mass of brilliance like the fire of the final dissolution, or at the "Seat of Jālandhara";
At the triangular crossing, a single light flickering like lightning within the **Central Channel** <term>Brahma-nadi: the innermost subtle channel of the spine through which energy rises</term>;
At the edge of the forehead, a lightning-like glow, and above that at the summit, as fierce as ten million suns;
Behold! Those who meditate on these daily find no awakening into the Supreme State. ॥ 82 ॥

From the genitals to the "sprout of the staff" <note>the base of the spine</note>, following the movement of the mind and breath, piercing the channels starting with the Central Channel;
Leading the **Bindu** <term>Bindu: the vital essence or "drop" of consciousness often equated with semen in hatha yoga</term> to the "Cave of the Supreme State" above the hollow of the conch;
There, producing the internal sound of the sky-quality <note>the element of space or ether</note> through the sequence of the "Diamond Staff" <note>likely referring to the Vajroli mudra or the central nadi</note>;
Behold! Those who do this here with such great effort find no awakening into the Supreme State. ॥ 83 ॥

Having made the tongue long through constant stretching and "milking" <note>the Khechari practice of lengthening the tongue to reach the nasal cavity</note>;
Inserting it into the inner palate, even crossing into the "Tenth Door" <gloss>22: variant reads "junction in the belly of the door" or "the Shankhini channel"</gloss>;
Leading it from the head, having reached it <gloss>23</gloss> through the union of the middle junction;
Drinking the "sixfold nectar" and remaining long intoxicated by that delusion <gloss>24: variant reads "those deluded ones who desire such worship"</gloss>; ॥ 84 ॥

Blocking both the rear and front paths <note>the anus and genitals</note> from the secret place, leading the breath into the middle channel;
Through meditation, absorption, and the practice of various physical postures;
By the constant union of the upward and downward breaths <gloss>25: variant reads "the coming and going of breath"</gloss> in the "Belly of the Swan" <gloss>26: variant reads "from the collision/union of breaths"</gloss>;
Behold! Even those who worship in this way sink into the waters of worldly existence, remaining miserable. ॥ 85 ॥

By contracting the energy <note>the practice of Mula Bandha or root-lock</note>, kindling the internal fire by pressing the "Base Center" (*Adhara*);
Having awakened the **Kundalini** <term>Kundalini: the primordial energy visualized as a coiled serpent at the base of the spine</term> from her place, then [leading] her to the head...

<gloss>20—In the triangular crossing. 21—In the seat. 22—The junction in the belly of the door; the Shankhini channel. 23—Reached. 24—Those deluded ones who desire such worship. 25—By the coming and going of breath. 26—From the union/collision.</gloss>

--- Page 111 ---
<meta>Continuing from the previous page, the author further dissects the physical and meditative practices of Hatha Yoga. Having previously dismissed the focus on specific anatomical points or "stations," the text now turns to the manipulation of internal energies and the complex physical purifications (kriyas) that many yogis believe are the key to liberation.</meta>

Those who lead the breath to the **Sacred Peak** <term>Purnagiri: one of the four principal centers of power (pithas) in the subtle body, often associated with the head</term> and then cast it downward; those who delight in such fragmented knowledge are far from their own true state. ॥ 86 ॥

Those who practice internal locks, piercings, and seals; those who direct the "fire" through the path of the throat-cavity and chin; who attempt to harmonize the **Sun and Moon** <note>original: "chandra-arkau," referring to the Ida and Pingala subtle channels</note>; who strive for equanimity through self-restraint and rules, focusing on the "point" at the end of the moon's digit—even if they are joined to the "mindless" yoga through an experienced intellect, they merely wander through the worlds. Turned away from their own true nature, they remain travelers on the path of suffering born of karma. ॥ 87 ॥

The **Eight-limbed path of Yoga** <term>Ashtanga Yoga: the classical system consisting of restraints, observances, postures, breath control, withdrawal, concentration, meditation, and absorption</term>, the doctrines of the Primordial Man, the piercing of the **Six-faced Center** <note>the Shanmukhi-chakra</note>; the path that pervades everything, resembling sunbeams in the space between the upper and lower breaths—those who gaze upon this as a liquid light or the blue color of the sky, or who meditate in this way with a fixed mind; alas, they too are only sharers in hardship. ॥ 88 ॥

First practicing concentration, then the "conch" concentration, and then the **Great Concentration** <note>Maha-dharana</note>; the full counter-concentration performed according to rule; the pure gaze; the techniques of the "inner essence" <gloss>27: variant reading "the seat/posture"</gloss>, the "multiple," the "churning," and the "vomiting" practice <note>various Hatha Yoga cleansing techniques like Vajroli, Amaroli, and Vamana</note>—those who do these things or cause them to be done always wander and are weary. ॥ 89 ॥

The **internal cleansing of the conch** <term>Shankhaprakshalana: a technique for cleaning the entire digestive tract</term>, the movement of the tongue through the palate, lips, and nose; the "vomiting" technique and the "door" technique; the drinking of the **immortal nectar** <note>Amari-pana: a practice involving the consumption of internal fluids, believed by some to grant longevity</note> and the "skull" practice; those who consume their own essence or apply it as an ointment—those fools do not reach the fruit produced by the True Doctrine. ॥ 90 ॥

But when one hears the sound of bells, the rhythm of drums, and the boom of the great kettledrum—the perfect **Unstruck Sound** <term>Anahata-dhvani: the mystical sound heard within the heart center during deep meditation, not caused by any physical striking</term>—pervading the individual body and the entire cosmos continuously; then the state of the Perfected Ones is attained, and the appropriate Supreme Reality is found. ॥ 91 ॥

Through detachment, those who eat only grass, leafy greens, water, bulbs, fruits, and roots, and devote themselves to living in the forest or wandering to other lands...

--- Page 112 ---
<meta>Continuing from the previous page, the author continues to critique yogis who focus on external displays and intellectual gymnastics rather than the true internal realization provided by a Guru. The text shifts from critiquing specific physical "locks" and "breaths" to critiquing the outward behavior and scholarly pretenses of practitioners who lack genuine spiritual experience.</meta>

Acting like a child, a madman, a ghost, a mute, or a fool, they perform various behaviors. Those who do this without reaching the [true] state, relying only on the strength of their intellect, fall away and are deluded. ॥ 92 ॥

Telling stories, witnessing various wonderful visions, wandering for alms, and performing theatrical displays; smearing the body with ash and making the limbs very rough <gloss>31: An alternate reading suggests "speedily"</gloss>, they spend a year in these practices. They wander from sacred site to sacred site through very difficult terrain, having restrained all their senses. Yet, they do not find the supreme state from the mouth of a Guru; they are simply tormented by fate. ॥ 93 ॥

And those who use the **four-fold speech** <term>Vaikhari (audible), Madhyama (mental), Pashyanti (visualized), and Para (transcendent)</term>, whether composed by themselves or created by the Perfected Masters; those who are clever with the Gayatri mantra, devoted to recitation, and fond of debating scriptures—they do not find the true meaning that is identical to the Self. They suffer through the force of delusion. These frauds, seeking refuge in the wicked, are [punished] with rods, shears, spears, wheels, and clubs. ॥ 91 ॥

Thus, they talk about the **void within the void** <gloss>32: The absolute void</gloss>, the state beyond the high and low, the void of the five voids; that which is beyond space, without beginning, the manifest and the unmanifest, the wonderful form of the universe; the unmanifest, the intermediate space, the effortless, the nameless unity of light. While they describe this in many ways with mere words, their minds are agitated and they wander in confusion. ॥ 94 ॥

That state which always fulfills the master’s command, which is eternally appropriate, full, and illuminating; the pure method present within the **microcosm of the body** <gloss>33: An alternate reading suggests "that which destroys death"</gloss>, the excellent essence of established truth; the internal removal of obstacles that gives the greatest happiness, the end of time, and the eternal. That is the permanent state, **shaken free from time** <gloss>34: An alternate reading suggests "triumphant over time"</gloss>, full of the Guru's grace, the state beyond arising that must be known. ॥ 95 ॥

In the investigation of the individual self, the supreme self, and the life-soul; the arising of the unity of these three is proclaimed as **The Command** <term>Adesha: the traditional greeting and technical term among Nath yogis, signifying the realization of the unity of the soul and the divine</term>. ॥ 96 ॥

The one who speaks this holy word—the "Command" which destroys the suffering of all dualities—to a yogi, that person attains the divine Self. ॥ 97 ॥

The burning of desires is the sacred ash; the pair of earrings is the satisfaction found in contemplation. A steady mind is the loincloth; the vast sky is the begging bowl; and the worship is the adoration of the Self. ॥ 98 ॥

<meta>Continuing from the previous page, the text transitions from a critique of false practitioners to a formal declaration of the scripture's sanctity. Having warned against those who merely perform external rituals or engage in dry intellectualism, the author now establishes the conditions under which this secret knowledge—the Siddhanta—should be shared and the specific character required of a true student.</meta>

This scripture is supremely divine, a secret belonging to the Supreme Lord. It is the established doctrine <term>Siddhanta: The final, proven conclusion of a school of thought</term>, the essence of all wisdom, and the definitive explanation of various symbolic signs. ॥ 99 ॥

Revealed by the Perfected Masters <term>Siddhas: Enlightened beings who have attained physical and spiritual perfection</term>, it is itself perfected and provides immediate conviction. It brings eternal bliss to the self and destroys all doubt. ॥ 100 ॥

It must not be given to the disciples of others, nor should it be recited in the presence of others. It should not be shared out of friendship, nor under compulsion, nor out of greed, nor through delusion, nor through falsehood or deceit. ॥ 101 ॥

It should not be given because of a feeling of camaraderie, nor in exchange for gifts, nor due to someone’s beauty, nor because of shared status. It should not be given even to one's own son, except through the proper succession from teacher to student <term>Guru-shishya krama: The traditional lineage of oral transmission</term>. ॥ 102 ॥

<note>The following verses describe the 'Adhikari' or the qualified seeker worthy of receiving these secrets.</note>

Those who are truthful, compassionate in heart, firm in their devotion, and always steady; those who are "without waves" <note>meaning their minds are undisturbed by shifting emotions</note>, deeply peaceful, and always awakening to true knowledge; ॥ 103 ॥

Those who are free from fear, wretchedness, disgust, shame, craving, hope, and grief; who have cast aside laziness, intoxication, envy, hypocrisy, illusion, and deceit; ॥ 104 ॥

Those who have turned away from ego, great delusion, attachment, and aversion; who are free from anger, selfish desire, lust, jealousy, confusion, and greed; ॥ 105 ॥

Those who are without worldly desire, pure, courageous, and always delighting in the state of non-duality <term>Advaita: The realization that the individual self and the supreme reality are one</term>—to them, this should be given with great effort. One must always hide it from the wicked. ॥ 106 ॥

Those who are critics, those of bad conduct, sycophants <note>original: "cumbakah," literally "kissers" or parasites</note>, and those who violate the sanctity of the teacher's home; those who are atheists, rogues, cruel, or those who delight only in intellectual debates; ॥ 107 ॥

Those who have fallen away from the practice of Yoga, who are fond of sleep and quarreling, who are zealous in their own selfish interests but indifferent to the work of the teacher; ॥ 108 ॥

One should avoid these people from a distance, even if they have come seeking to be disciples. ॥ 109 ॥

If one desires a long life, this true scripture, the path of the Perfected, and the **System of the Teachings of the Perfected** <term>Siddha Siddhanta Paddhati</term> must never be given to such people. ॥ 110 ॥

<meta>Continuing from the previous page, the text completes the description of the qualified seeker (Adhikari) and moves into the final warnings regarding the secrecy of these teachings. The previous page ended with a list of psychological qualities—such as being free from fear and craving—that a student must possess before being initiated into the higher mysteries of Yoga.</meta>

This knowledge must be protected with great effort, just as one hides wealth from thieves. He who, out of delusion, reveals it to someone who has not been tested <gloss>36: Other versions read 'the aforementioned'</gloss> or who is dull-witted— ॥ 111 ॥

Liberation shall never be his; instead, he will perpetually sink into misery. The Sky-wanderers, the Earth-dwellers, the Yogis, the female spirits <term>Shakini: A type of supernatural female being or guardian spirit often associated with the esoteric path</term>, and the Night-striders— ॥ 112 ॥

The terrible curse of these Perfected Masters <term>Siddhas: Enlightened beings who have attained physical and spiritual perfection</term> and of the Lord Bhairava <term>Bhairava: A fierce manifestation of Shiva, often regarded as the protector of secret tantric and yogic wisdom</term> falls upon his head. Therefore, one should protect this teaching with the utmost care. ॥ 113 ॥

One should always speak only after testing the student who stays at the teacher’s lotus feet <gloss>37: Other versions read 'who has taken refuge'</gloss>. For the great-souled knower of Truth, how can there be any sorrow or fear? ॥ 114 ॥

This knowledge should be bestowed only out of compassion, for the sake of continuing the tradition <term>Sampradaya: A living lineage of oral transmission from master to disciple</term>. Indeed, the continuation of the tradition is accepted and approved by all authorities. ॥ 115 ॥

Bowing to Mayashankaranatha <note>A name for the Supreme Lord or perhaps the author's specific lineage deity</note>, he who writes down this **System of the Proven Truth** <term>Siddhanta-paddhati: The title of this manuscript, meaning a manual or path of established conclusions</term> or reads it with devotion, attains the supreme state. ॥ 116 ॥

May that Light named Ganapati <note>original: 'Ganapatyabhidham mahah'. Referring to the elephant-headed deity Ganesha as a form of divine splendor</note>—the embodiment of grace toward his devotees, whose mind is fixed on the supreme bliss of divine love—bestow an absolute and unwavering refuge. ॥ 117 ॥

<meta>Continuing from the previous page, which concluded the requirements for a spiritual seeker and the warnings against sharing secret knowledge with the unworthy, this page begins a new section titled "The Subject of Yoga." It transitions from the ethical preparations to the specific lineage of the teachings and the internal maps of the yogic body.</meta>

# ॥ The Subject of Yoga ॥

The teacher, the teacher’s son, and whoever else are the teacher’s kinsmen — ॥ 1 ॥

I bow at their feet, for they are the elders in the line of succession. I bow to that Teacher who transcends all thought and feeling. The world wanders about in delusion, but he who is free from delusion is firm in his resolve. ॥ 2 ॥

Success is attained by those men who are established in the Primal Lord <term>Adinatha: The "First Lord," usually identified as Shiva, the original source of all yogic wisdom</term>. One who comes from a good family, who is endowed with good character and virtues, — ॥ 3 ॥

And who is filled with devotion to the teacher and possesses wisdom—such a person is called a disciple. Through the grace of the Primal Lord, the inhabitant of Kejapupina <note>original: "Kejapupina-vasina"; this likely refers to a specific sacred geographic location or a spiritual dwelling place of the deity</note>, — ॥ 4 ॥

The Supreme Truth was understood by Minanatha <term>Minanatha: A legendary yogi and mahasiddha, often identified as Matsyendranatha, the founder of the Nath lineage</term>. Minanatha, in turn, spoke to his virtuous disciple with a composed mind: ॥ 5 ॥

“You are the teacher and you are also the disciple; you are both the disciple and the teacher. In this state, there is no longer any difference between the two, and a balanced perfection arises.” ॥ 6 ॥

I am Minanatha, the lord of sages and the son of Umashankara <note>A name representing the union of the Goddess Uma and Lord Shiva</note>. I shall now speak of the Supreme Truth, which brings realization of the manifest and the transcendent <term>Kulakulavibodhakam: Knowledge that awakens the understanding of Kula (the manifest/Shakti) and Akula (the unmanifest/Shiva)</term>. ॥ 7 ॥

The Root, the Self-Abode, the City of Jewels, the Unstruck, the Pure, and the Command—these are the six auspicious centers <term>Chakras: Internal "wheels" or focal points of energy used in meditative practice</term>. ॥ 8 ॥

The Root <note>Adhara/Muladhara</note> is located at the anus, and the Self-Abode <note>Svadhisthana</note> is at the genitals. The City of Jewels <note>Manipura</note> is situated at the navel, and the Unstruck <note>Anahata</note> is in the heart. ॥ 9 ॥

The Pure <note>Vishuddhi</note> is in the region of the throat, and the Command Center <note>Ajna</note> is at the face of the eyebrows. By knowing these divisions of the centers, one comes to know the "Untainted One" <note>Niranjana</note> who exists beyond the centers. ॥ 10 ॥

The channel of *Ida* flows on the left, and the channel of *Pingala* flows on the right. In the middle of *Ida* and *Pingala* is *Sushumna*, the nature of which is pure bliss. ॥ 11 ॥

<meta>This page continues the technical exposition of Hatha Yoga started by Minanatha on the previous page. Having established the lineage and the sanctity of the teaching, the text now moves into a detailed map of the "subtle body," including the energy centers (chakras), the vital breaths (pranas), and the psychic knots (granthis).</meta>

At the base, the genitals and navel, the heart-lotus, the root of the palate, and the forehead; ॥
The petals are two, sixteen, twelve, ten, six <note>original: "dwadashardhe" meaning "half of twelve"</note>, and four.
At the end of the nose and the middle of the brow, including the letters from *da* to *pha* and *ka* to *tha* <note>These represent specific Sanskrit phonemes associated with the petals of the chakras</note>, in the region of the throat, and the vowels— ॥
I bow to the form of the letters, endowed with the meaning of the essential Truth, residing in every petal. ॥ 12 ॥

The inward breath, the downward breath, the balancing breath, the upward breath, and the diffused breath <term>Prana, Apana, Samana, Udana, Vyana: The five primary "Vayus" or vital airs that govern physiological and energetic functions</term>; ॥
These are joined with the five organs of action <term>Karmendriyas: The faculties of speech, grasping, locomotion, excretion, and procreation</term> and are ready for the power of action. ॥ 13 ॥

The serpent, the tortoise, the partridge, the gift-of-the-gods, and the conqueror-of-wealth <term>Naga, Kurma, Krikara, Devadatta, Dhananjaya: The five subsidiary vital airs (Upapranas) responsible for functions like belching, blinking, sneezing, yawning, and nourishment</term>; ॥
These are joined with the five organs of perception <term>Jnanendriyas: The faculties of hearing, touch, sight, taste, and smell</term> and are endowed with the power of the intellect. ॥ 14 ॥

The Sun <note>Representing the solar energy or "Pingala" channel</note> is situated in the navel-wheel, in the midst of the power of fire. ॥
When all the locks and seals <note>original: "Bandha-mudra"; physical internal locks used to direct energy</note> are performed, the eyes should be well-fixed on the tip of the nose. ॥ 15 ॥

The sound **A** is in the region of fire <note>the base/navel</note>, the sound **U** is situated in the heart, ॥
And the sound **M** is in the middle of the eyebrows. One should understand this through the teaching. ॥ 16 ॥

The Knot of Brahma is in the lower region <note>at the sound 'A'</note>, the Knot of Vishnu is situated in the heart; ॥
The Knot of Rudra is in the middle of the eyebrows. Thus, these three knots are released. <note>The "Granthis" are psychic blockages that the yogi must pierce to allow energy to rise to the crown</note> ॥ 17 ॥

They say that the sound **A** is Brahma, the sound **U** is called Vishnu, ॥
And in the sound **M** is Shiva himself—the supreme, which is peace and even beyond peace. ॥ 18 ॥

By contracting the throat <note>the Jalandhara Bandha</note>, one should firmly restrain the two channels <note>the Ida and Pingala nadis</note>. ॥
The tongue is then pressed upward <note>the Khechari Mudra</note> toward the "sixteen" <note>likely referring to the sixteen-petaled throat chakra or the "void" of the upper palate</note>. ॥ 19 ॥

The three peaks, the triple-force, the hole of the skull, the summit, ॥
The triple-spear, the thunderbolt, the sound **Om**, the root of the nose, the "face" between the eyebrows <note>These are esoteric names for the various stations and goals of the ascending energy</note>. ॥ 20 ॥

One should contract the Sun <note>the solar center at the navel</note>, and then the posterior channel <note>the Sushumna, or central spinal column</note> begins to flow. ॥
By piercing the intersection of the triple-force, one sees the Moon <note>the cooling nectar of enlightenment</note> in both. ॥ 21 ॥

The Sacred Syllable, the rectal-tube, the lotus-like, the female serpent, ॥
The curved-pipe, the destroyer, the heroic, the coiled one, the earring-wearer—these are the names remembered for her <term>Kundalini: The primordial energy depicted as a sleeping serpent at the base of the spine</term>. ॥ 22 ॥
