--- Page 117 ---
<meta>This page concludes the technical instructions on Hatha Yoga and internal alchemy attributed to Matsyendranath. It bridges the anatomical descriptions of the previous page with the spiritual realization of the self and the "Hamsa" mantra, finally providing a traditional colophon naming the author.</meta>

One should stir the Coiled Power <term>Kundalini: The dormant spiritual energy at the base of the spine</term> with the vital breaths once the lunar circle <note>original: "shashimandala," referring to a center of cooling, nectar-like energy in the head</note> has been pierced. The diamond-like bindings are perfected; one should close the nine gates <note>The nine openings of the body: eyes, ears, nostrils, mouth, and the lower apertures</note>. ॥ 23 ॥

The mind, mounted on the breath, becomes steady and free of physical attributes. Through the resonance in the Seat of the Absolute <note>original: "brahmasthana"</note>, the "Conch-like" channel <term>Shankhini: One of the primary subtle energy channels (nadis) in the body</term> rains down nectar. ॥ 24 ॥

One should reveal the lamp of knowledge, the rising of the circles of the six centers <term>Shatcakra: The six major energy centers located along the spinal column</term>. A ritual bathing of all the inner deities is performed within the body as an act of worship. ॥ 25 ॥

Having bathed the Lord of Consciousness with the nectar of the moon with devotion, one should offer the "flower of the mind" and worship the Supreme Auspicious One <note>original: "Paramam Shivam"</note>. ॥ 26 ॥

Having perceived the form of the Self, which is pure knowledge and free from ailment, the all-pervading and stainless Reality is seen within the form of the body. ॥ 27 ॥

The phrase "I am He, I am He" <term>Hamsa: A mantra representing the sound of the breath; it literally translates to "Swan," a symbol of the soul's purity and discrimination</term> resides in the bodies of all living beings. It is called the knot of the union of the inward and downward breaths... ॥ 28 ॥

Twenty-one thousand and six hundred times <note>The traditional number of breaths taken by a human in a twenty-four-hour period</note> every day, the breath is uttered; from the sound of its movement, the "Swan" mantra is also called "I am That" <term>So'ham: A fundamental mantra signifying the unity of the individual soul and the universal spirit</term>. ॥ 29 ॥

In the front part is the pillar <note>original: "lingam," a symbol of divine energy</note>, and to the west/back is the "Conch-like" channel. Between the brows is the Pillar of Light, the Auspicious One composed of both red and white <note>These colors symbolize the union of the female (red/ovum) and male (white/semen) principles of the universe</note>. ॥ 30 ॥

Situated in the eastern and western directions within the Diamond Staff <term>Vajradanda: A yogic name for the spinal column</term>. These two are the place of the sixty-two-fold serpent energy <note>original: "bhogini"</note>; let the rear pillar be revealed. ॥ 31 ॥

The supreme place is neither cold nor hot, filled with the vital essences of fat and marrow. It flows from the Seat of the Absolute and drenches the three worlds. ॥ 32 ॥

This practice for all diseases, [balancing] the humors of wind and bile <term>Vata and Pitta: Two of the three "doshas" or constitutional humors in Ayurvedic medicine</term>, and free from eighteen defects, is told by the Lord of the Fish <note>original: "Minanatha," another name for Matsyendranath</note>. ॥ 33 ॥

->|| Thus ends the [treatise on] Devotion <unclear>composed</unclear> by Matsyendranath. ||<-

<summary>This page describes the culminating stages of Hatha Yoga, where the practitioner activates the Kundalini, balances the vital breaths, and achieves a state of non-dual consciousness symbolized by the "Hamsa" and "So'ham" mantras. It concludes with a signature (colophon) attributing the work to the legendary sage Matsyendranath.</summary>
<keywords>Kundalini, Matsyendranath, Hamsa mantra, So'ham, Shatcakra, subtle anatomy, nectar, Shankhini nadi, Ayurveda, Vata-Pitta balance</keywords>

--- Page 118 ---
<meta>This page marks the beginning of a new treatise, the Amaraughaprabodha, attributed to Gorakshanatha. While the previous page concluded a section on Hatha Yoga practices attributed to his teacher Matsyendranatha (Minanatha), this text seeks to categorize and harmonize the four major systems of Yoga: Laya, Hatha, Mantra, and Raja Yoga.</meta>

->॥ Auspicious Invocation ॥<-

# Composed by the Illustrious Gorakshanatha
## “The Awakening to the Immortal Stream” <note>original: "Amaraughaprabodha"</note>

<image-desc>Decorative flourish/ornament with symmetrical scrolls and a central floral motif.</image-desc>

Salutations to the Primal Lord <term>Adinatha: Traditionally identified as the god Shiva, the first teacher of Yoga</term>, and salutations indeed to Minanatha <note>Also known as Matsyendranatha, the legendary founder of the Natha lineage</note>. Salutations to the wise and enlightened Charinginatha, who possesses great intellect. ॥ 1 ॥

For the benefit of those who have undertaken practices like Laya Yoga <term>Laya Yoga: The Yoga of mental dissolution or absorption</term> but whose minds are still eager for debate and controversy, Goraksha has spoken this [teaching], which consists of direct experience and awakening. ॥ 2 ॥

There is Laya Yoga, Hatha Yoga, and Mantra Yoga as the third; the fourth is Raja Yoga <term>Raja Yoga: "The Royal Path," often considered the highest state of meditative stillness</term>, which is free from the state of duality. ॥ 3 ॥

That in which the mind dissolves internally is declared to be Laya. That which is devoted to the methods of the vital breath <note>original: "prabhanjana-vidhana," referring to the techniques of breath control</note> is Hatha. That which is under the power of the form of the sacred sound is Mantra Yoga. And that which is devoid of all mental fluctuations is Raja Yoga. ॥ 4 ॥

Raja Yoga is sometimes said to be of two kinds: medicinal <note>referring to the use of alchemical elixirs or herbs to aid meditation</note> and spiritual. Hatha is also twofold, categorized by the cultivation of the vital breath and the vital seed <term>Bindu: The essential creative energy of the body</term>. ॥ 5 ॥

One does not see that Mantra [Yoga] which is the cause for the accomplishment of the six cleansing acts <term>Shatkarma: Six physical purification techniques used to balance the bodily humors</term>. Like a worm moving between the eyebrows and the nose, the mind may somehow become <insert> (submerged) </insert> [in the object of focus]. However, without the glorious Raja Yoga, which is the dwelling place of the contemplation of the eternal and blissful Self, the vital breath does not enter the Foundation <term>Adhara: The Muladhara or root center at the base of the spine</term> despite the various practices of yogis. ॥ 6 ॥

By constantly chanting a mantra, one does not attain sovereignty, nor does one attain lordship. Even the daily union with divine women <note>This likely refers to mystical experiences of bliss or specific Tantric attainments</note> is not attained merely by meditating. Can a horse be born from a female elephant? Can a young camel come from a donkey, or fine rice from coarse grain? Tell me, O Lord, how can such results be produced by force <note>original: "hathatah," a play on words suggesting that Hatha Yoga alone, when practiced as "force," cannot yield the highest results</note> without the presence of one's own innate power? ॥ 7 ॥

<summary>This opening page of Gorakshanatha's treatise introduces the four systems of Yoga—Laya, Hatha, Mantra, and Raja—defining each and arguing that physical or ritualistic practices are ineffective without the spiritual realization of Raja Yoga.</summary>
<keywords>Gorakshanatha, Amaraughaprabodha, Laya Yoga, Hatha Yoga, Mantra Yoga, Raja Yoga, Adinatha, spiritual awakening, breath control, internal dissolution</keywords>

--- Page 119 ---
<meta>This page continues the Amaraughaprabodha's exploration of the four yogas, moving from the definitions provided on the previous page to a critique of purely physical or alchemical practices. It emphasizes that physical techniques (Hatha) and alchemical elixirs are insufficient for immortality without the mental stillness of Raja Yoga.</meta>

Some drink their own urine <note>A practice known as Amaroli</note> out of a desire for results; some consume saliva. Others, having entered a woman, attempt to draw the "drop" <note>original: "bindu," referring to seminal fluid or vital energy</note> upward. Some consume processed metals and minerals, thinking themselves experts in circulating the breath through all the veins of the body. But the perfection of the body <term>Deha-siddhi: The attainment of a "diamond body" that is immortal and free from disease</term> is not achieved by these people without Raja Yoga, which is characterized by the cessation of the individual mind. ॥ 8 ॥

When the mind attains equanimity and the breath enters the middle channel <note>The Sushumna, or central energetic pathway along the spine</note>, then the practices of Amaroli, Vajroli, and Sahajoli <term>Vajroli, Sahajoli, and Amaroli: A triad of physical Hatha Yoga techniques designed to preserve and redirect the body's vital essence</term> are said to truly manifest. ॥ 9 ॥

->And as stated in the *Shri Samputa* <note>A significant Tantric text often cited in Natha literature</note>:<-

> Holding the breath through Hatha, there was a "morsel" <note>Referring to the legendary sage Minanatha</note> inside the belly of a fish. Until the Lord Shiva suddenly released him, he did not realize his own liberation. After Shiva spoke this [teaching], saying "There is no time other than the mind," he and Parvati spoke to Minanatha, saying: "Thus may we live forever." ॥ 10 ॥
> 
> If one could live in this world free from trouble simply by using medicinal herbs, then why would doctors—who are themselves gripped by disease and old age—die? ॥ 11 ॥
> 
> Those who are clever in following Charaka and those eager to hear the teachings of Sushruta <term>Charaka and Sushruta: The two primary ancestral sages of Indian Ayurvedic medicine</term>—if they lack the medicine of the "No-Mind" state <note>original: "Amanaska," the state of Raja Yoga where mental fluctuations cease</note>, how can the destruction of all worldly existence occur? ॥ 12 ॥
> 
> Bindu and Nada <term>Bindu and Nada: The "seed" (vital energy/light) and "sound" (vibration), the two fundamental principles of the cosmos within the body</term> are the two "great medicines" existing within all creatures. Without knowing these two, and lacking the guidance of a Guru, everyone meets death. ॥ 13 ॥
> 
> One should move the "enclosed breath" <note>Breath held in the lungs or belly</note>, which kindles the internal sun and fire. When this fire blazes, it moves; from this, O Parvati, the "seed" <note>The vital Bindu</note> moves. ॥ 14 ॥
> 
> For one who knows the essence of these two through the instructions of a true Guru, where is the fear of bodily pain, old age, disease, sin, or death? ॥ 15 ॥
> 
> When the King of the Mind departs and the "Sun" <note>The solar breath or Pingala</note> travels through the path of Meru <note>The spinal column, compared to the cosmic mountain</note> in every direction; when the fire blazes and the "Moon" <note>The lunar nectar or Ida</note> flows, quickly filling the body—then a harvest of bliss arises. The darkness of delusion, such as "mine" and "thine," is cast aside. When the Crown Center <term>Brahmarandhra: The "Cavitity of Brahman," the energetic gateway at the top of the skull</term> is pierced, there is a victory: an extraordinary union of Shiva and Shakti. ॥ 16 ॥

<summary>This page argues that physical Hatha practices and medicinal alchemy are useless for achieving immortality without Raja Yoga (the state of "No-Mind"). It describes the internal process where breath, sun, and moon energies unite at the crown of the head to produce a state of supreme bliss and liberation.</summary>
<keywords>Raja Yoga, Deha-siddhi, Amanaska, Bindu, Nada, Minanatha, Charaka, Sushruta, Brahmarandhra, Shiva and Shakti</keywords>

--- Page 120 ---
<meta>This page continues the Amaraughaprabodha's discussion on the supremacy of Raja Yoga. Having established that physical and alchemical methods are secondary, the text now addresses the classification of practitioners and the specific yogic paths suited to each level of ability, leading back to the "Flood of Immortality" (Amaraugha).</meta>

Indeed, the Flood of Immortality <note>original: "Amaraugha," the title of the work and a state of spiritual perfection</note> is essentially one, known by the name Raja Yoga. How then can a "fourth" be given when it is combined with the teachings of Matsyendra and others? ॥ 17 ॥

A practitioner <term>Sadhaka: A person dedicated to a spiritual practice or "sadhana"</term> should be understood as fourfold: mild, moderate, intense, and the most intense. The staircase <note>referring to the gradual progression of stages</note> for these levels is described here: ॥ 18 ॥

One who lacks enthusiasm, possesses little passion, is dependent on others, is a critic <note>or "jester"</note>, is sickly, has a weak will, and is a householder—such a person is known as a **mild** practitioner. ॥ 19 ॥

One with a balanced intellect, who is consistent in practice, has a balanced physique, seeks stable refuge, and remains neutral in all dealings is called a **moderate** practitioner. ॥ 20 ॥

One possessed of patience, heroism, and wisdom, who is youthful and strong-willed, independent, and a bold hero, is said to be an **intense** practitioner. ॥ 21 ॥

One who has mastered all the scriptures, is charming, heroic and courageous, free from delusion, perfect in every way, changeless, and a great refuge to others— ॥ 22 ॥

—such a person is the **most intense** practitioner, manifesting a "great body" <note>likely referring to the perfected "divine body" (divya-deha)</note>. They cross the ocean of worldly existence and enable others to cross it as well. ॥ 23 ॥

The **Mantra** path is given to the mild; **Laya** <term>Laya Yoga: The yoga of dissolution, where the mind dissolves into the object of meditation</term> is taught to the moderate. One should give **Hatha** to the intense practitioner, and the **Flood of Immortality** <note>Raja Yoga</note> is for the Great Lord <note>the most intense practitioner</note>. ॥ 24 ॥

### Regarding Mantra:—

The mantra is the single-syllable "Aha" <gloss>Om</gloss> <note>The gloss corrects "Aha" to "Om," the primordial sound</note>, preceded by the sacred syllable *Pranava* <note>another name for Om</note>. Having meditated on the auspicious Lord Shiva in the heart, within the twelve-petaled mandala <note>likely the heart chakra</note>, ॥ 25 ॥

One should destroy even the most terrible death through one hundred thousand recitations. One should then perform a fire sacrifice <note>homa</note> of one-tenth that count, using honey, milk, clarified butter <note>ghee</note>, and lotuses. ॥ 26 ॥

### Now, Laya:—

In the "Seat of Desire" <note>original: "Kamarupa," a sacred energy center in the subtle body</note>, one should meditate within their own body on the god Shiva, who appears in the form of a *Linga* <term>Linga: An abstract, pillar-like representation of Shiva's infinite energy</term> shining like a jewel, dripping with the essence of nectar. ॥ 27 ॥

<summary>This page classifies yoga practitioners into four grades—mild, moderate, intense, and most intense—and assigns a specific yoga (Mantra, Laya, Hatha, or Raja Yoga) to each. It begins detailing the practices for Mantra Yoga and Laya Yoga, focusing on the syllable Om and internal meditation on Shiva.</summary>
<keywords>Sadhaka, Mantra Yoga, Laya Yoga, Hatha Yoga, Raja Yoga, practitioner types, Shiva, Kamarupa, Om</keywords>

--- Page 121 ---
<meta>This page continues the Amaraughaprabodha's classification of yoga practices. Following the discussion on Mantra and Laya Yoga, the text now transitions into the technical instructions for Hatha Yoga, focusing on the three "Great" seals or locks (mudras) intended to awaken the vital energy (Kundalini).</meta>

Through constant practice, within six months, one becomes a possessor of success <note>original: "Siddhi," referring to supernatural powers or spiritual perfection</note>. Free from wrinkles and grey hair, such a person lives for three hundred years. ॥ 28 ॥

->**Now, Hatha Yoga —**<-

Pressing the perineum <term>Yonim: in this anatomical context, the perineum or the "space between the genitals and the anus"</term> with the left heel, and extending the right leg, one should hold the foot firmly with both hands and fill the lungs by inhaling through the mouth. ॥ 29 ॥

Applying the throat lock <note>the Jalandhara Bandha, where the chin is pressed against the chest</note>, one draws the air upward from the base. Just as a snake struck by a staff becomes straight as a rod— ॥ 30 ॥

—so the Serpent Power <term>Kundalini: the latent spiritual energy at the base of the spine</term> suddenly becomes straight. Then, the state of "death to the world" <note>a state of deep meditative suspension where ordinary breath and sensory awareness cease</note> occurs, resting in the two channels <note>the Ida and Pingala channels on either side of the spine</note>. ॥ 31 ॥

Great diseases, great afflictions, and even death itself are overcome. Therefore, the best of the wise call this practice the **Great Seal**. <term>Mahamudra: the "Great Seal," a physical posture used to direct vital energy into the central channel (Sushumna)</term> ॥ 32 ॥

When the Great Seal is mastered, success comes to the practitioner. Next is described the **Great Bond**, which destroys birth and death. <term>Mahabandha: the "Great Bond," a lock used to redirect internal energy</term> ॥ 33 ॥

Having filled the mouth with air, and placing the chin on the heart, one should firmly contract the perineum and focus the mind in the center <note>likely referring to the central energy channel of the spine</note>. ॥ 34 ॥

This practice purifies the upward movement of all the energy channels. Having reached the confluence of the three rivers, one should bring the mind to the sacred field. <note>The "three rivers" (Triveni) refers to the junction of the main energy channels at the brow; the "sacred field" (Kedara) refers to the energy center in the head.</note> ॥ 35 ॥

Just as a woman endowed with beauty and grace is fruitless without a man, the Great Seal and Great Bond are fruitless without the **Piercing**. <term>Mahavedha: the "Great Piercing," a technique that forces energy through the base of the spine into the central channel</term> ॥ 36 ॥

One should hold the extended left leg firmly with both hands. Then, one should cause the body to vibrate along the three paths <note>the three primary energy channels</note>. ॥ 37 ॥

Again, one should strike the ground with the hips while remaining steady with the throat lock. Having gathered the movement of the air, one performs inhalation and breath retention. <term>Puraka and Kumbhaka: the technical terms for the inhalation and holding of the breath</term> ॥ 38 ॥

<summary>This page details the first three major physical techniques of Hatha Yoga—Mahamudra, Mahabandha, and Mahavedha—explaining how they manipulate the body's energy to awaken the Kundalini and achieve longevity.</summary>
<keywords>Hatha Yoga, Kundalini, Mahamudra, Mahabandha, Mahavedha, breath control, longevity, energy channels</keywords>

--- Page 122 ---
<meta>This page continues the technical instructions for Hatha Yoga practices in the Amaraughaprabodha. It concludes the description of the "Great Piercing" (Mahavedha) and begins a detailed explanation of the four stages of yogic progress—Beginning, Vessel, Practice, and Perfection—along with the internal sounds and physical signs that accompany the awakening of energy through the psychic knots (granthis).</meta>

With both hands held together and the feet positioned likewise, one should pierce the "four seats" <note>referring to specific energy centers or postures that support the body's vital force</note> sequentially by means of the breath. ॥ 39 ॥

One should strike the "Great Mountain" of the spine <term>Mahameru: the spinal column, envisioned as the central axis of the body like the mythological Mount Meru is to the universe</term> with the breath, as if with a thunderbolt or fire. By occupying both side-channels <note>the Ida and Pingala nerves</note>, the breath then pulsates rapidly. ॥ 40 ॥

One should recognize the union of the moon, sun, and fire <note>symbolizing the three main energy channels: the left, the right, and the central Sushumna</note> as the path to immortality. Once the "state of death" <note>a state of deep meditative suspension where ordinary breathing stops</note> is attained, how can there be any fear of death itself? ॥ 41 ॥

These three practices <note>the Great Seal, Great Bond, and Great Piercing</note> are extremely secret and destroy old age and death. They should be performed eight times, during every three-hour watch <term>Yama: a traditional unit of time lasting about three hours</term>, day after day. ॥ 42 ॥

These practices increase the internal digestive fire and bestow powers such as becoming as small as an atom <term>Animadi: the eight supernatural powers or 'Siddhis,' starting with Anima, the ability to become infinitesimally small</term>. They accumulate merit and shatter the vast flood of sins. ॥ 43 ॥

For those who are well-instructed, only a little effort is needed in the initial practice. However, in the beginning, one must avoid proximity to fire, the company of women, and long journeys. ॥ 44 ॥

In all systems of Yoga, there are four stages of progress: the **Beginning**, the **Vessel**, the **Practice**, and the **Perfection**. ॥ 45 ॥ <note>original: "Arambha, Ghata, Pratyaya (usually Parichaya), and Nishpatti"</note>

When the "Knot of Brahma" <term>Brahmagranthi: the first major energetic blockage located at the base of the spine</term> is pierced, bliss arises from the void. A variety of tinkling sounds—the "unstruck" resonance—can be heard within the body. ॥ 46 ॥

In this **Beginning** stage, the yogi attains a divine, radiant body, a celestial fragrance, and freedom from disease. He becomes courageous, with a heart full of contentment. ॥ 47 ॥

In the second stage, the **Vessel**, the breath enters the central channel. The yogi becomes firm in his posture and shines with the light of wisdom. ॥ 48 ॥

By piercing the "Knot of Vishnu" <term>Vishnugranthi: the second energetic blockage, located at the heart center</term>, supreme bliss is signaled. Within the "Great Void" of the inner space, a sound like a kettle-drum <note>original: "Bheri," a large resonant drum</note> resounds. ॥ 49 ॥

In the third stage, having broken through <insert>the knots</insert>, the sound of a double-headed drum <note>original: "Mardala"</note> is heard. Then the "Greatest Void" <term>Mahashunya: a state of profound meditative emptiness or pure consciousness</term> is reached, which is the refuge of all truth. ॥ 50 ॥

<summary>This page describes the final stages of the Great Piercing technique and introduces the four evolutionary stages of Yoga, detailing the physical purification and internal mystical sounds (Anahata Nada) that occur as the breath moves through the psychic knots.</summary>
<keywords>Mahavedha, Mahameru, Animadi Siddhis, Four Stages of Yoga, Arambha, Ghata, Brahmagranthi, Vishnugranthi, Anahata Nada, Mahashunya</keywords>

--- Page 123 ---
<meta>This page continues the description of the advanced stages of yogic practice. It transitions from the physical and energetic "piercing" of the vital centers to the attainment of Raja Yoga (the "Royal Yoga") and concludes with a breakdown of the elemental composition of the human body and the signs of physical decay.</meta>

### Page 53

Having then conquered the **bliss of the mind** <note>original: "cittānanda"</note>, the **innate bliss** <note>original: "sahajānanda"; the natural state of joy that arises without external cause</note> is born. The yogi is then abandoned by faults, death, old age, sorrow, hunger, and sleep. ॥ 51 ॥

Then, having pierced the **Knot of Rudra** <term>Rudragranthi: the third and final psychic knot located at the center between the eyebrows, which, when broken, allows the energy to reach the highest state</term>, the breath reaches the **seat of purity** <note>original: "sattvapīṭha"</note>. In the stage of perfection, a sound like a flute <note>original: "vaiṇava-śabda"</note> arises, tinkling <gloss>as if</gloss> with a ringing tone. ॥ 52 ॥

At that time, the mind becomes unified; this is what is called **Royal Yoga** <term>Raja Yoga: the 'King of Yogas,' representing the highest state of meditative union where the mind is completely still</term>. This Lord of Yogis becomes equal to the creator and destroyer of the universe. ॥ 53 ॥

There is no sound, no point <note>original: "bindu"; a concentrated point of consciousness or energy</note>, no mind, and no lack of consciousness. Nothing is difficult to achieve through practice; this is declared to be **Royal Yoga**. ॥ 54 ॥

That in which the moving and unmoving world is absorbed through dissolution is called the **Sign** <term>Linga: here used not just as a symbol of Shiva, but as the 'mark' or 'dissolution point' of the entire cosmos</term>. That Power <note>Shakti</note> is the one that illuminates the three worlds from the depths of her inconceivable form. That is meditation where the mind is free from the activity of all worldly objects. That is the sovereign state which effortlessly takes its seat upon the veil of infinite time. ॥ 55 ॥

The body is composed of the **five elements** <term>Panchabhuta: Earth, Water, Fire, Air, and Space (Ether)</term> and is filled with five spheres. Earth is to be known by its hardness, and water by its fluid form. ॥ 56 ॥

Fire is that which illuminates, and air is characterized by the sense of touch. One who desires Yoga should know that in space, everything is consciousness. ॥ 57 ॥

Twenty-one thousand and six hundred times <note>the traditional number of breaths a human takes in a 24-hour cycle</note> the breath flows during a day and night, through the expulsion of the atmospheric sphere. ॥ 58 ॥

When the **earth-sphere** is depleted, the body's strength fails. When the **water** element is depleted, the hair gradually turns white like bleached grass. ॥ 59 ॥

When **fire** is depleted, hunger and the luster of the skin vanish. When **air** becomes slack, there is constant trembling, and the person lives only by sheer force of will. ॥ 60 ॥

Death results from this kind of elemental decay, while life is maintained by holding the elements in balance. If <unclear>...</unclear> focus is lost, death occurs; otherwise, it is avoided. ॥ 61 ॥

**Vocabulary:** Bliss of the mind, Innate bliss, Knot of Rudra, Royal Yoga, Absorbed, Dissolution, Five elements, Earth, Atmospheric sphere, Death.

<summary>This page describes the transition from physical breath control to the supreme state of Raja Yoga, marked by flute-like internal sounds and the conquest of mortality. It also outlines the Ayurvedic and Yogic theory of the five elements (Mahabhutas) within the body and how their depletion leads to aging and death.</summary>
<keywords>Raja Yoga, Rudragranthi, Sahajananda, Panchabhuta, elemental decay, internal sounds, immortality, yogic physiology</keywords>

--- Page 124 ---
<meta>This page continues the technical instructions on Raja Yoga, following the descriptions of the psychic knots (granthis) and internal sounds. It moves into the results of prolonged practice and introduces the authorial voice of Goraksha, the legendary master of this lineage.</meta>

### Page 54

Through the practice of eight watches <note>a "watch" or *yama* is a period of three hours; eight watches signify a full twenty-four-hour cycle of continuous practice</note>, the yogi swallows all breaths. Endowed with sixteen parts <note>original: "kalā"; these are phases or portions, often compared to the sixteen phases of the moon</note>, the lunar energy remains during the inhalations. ॥ 62 ॥

When the mind is steady and without waves, the **vital breath** <term>Vayu: the "wind" or life-force that moves through the body's channels</term> moves through the middle path. The "sun" <note>representing solar, heating energy in the body</note> reaches the highest station, and through the **concentrated point** <term>Bindu: the seed of consciousness or vital essence, often associated with the preservation of life and youth</term>, mastery is achieved. ॥ 63 ॥

Filled with bliss, the yogi becomes similar to **Shiva** <note>the supreme deity of transformation and the archetypal yogi</note>. Then, from the tenth month of practice, all the qualities of the Lord are seen within him. ॥ 64 ॥

Thus, in the **Perfection of the Flood of Immortals** <term>Amaraughasamsiddhi: the title of this treatise, referring to the "flood" or "stream" of the immortal masters</term>, this was revealed by **Goraksha** <note>Gorakshanatha, the famous medieval master and founding father of Hatha Yoga</note>. It consists of the awakening of the conviction in the skillful methods of **absorption** <term>Laya: the meditative state where the individual mind dissolves into the universal consciousness</term> and other techniques. ॥ 65 ॥

Abandoning all worries, let this be tested for just one day. If that conviction does not arise, then my words are false. ॥ 66 ॥

Smoke, mirages, fireflies, lamp-flames, the moon, and the sun—these internal lights shine forth as the Great Image, the Universal Form. <note>These are specific "signs" or photisms that appear in the practitioner's inner vision as they progress in meditation.</note> ॥ 67 ॥

He who does not know this awakening does not find the **Flood of Immortality**. When equanimity arises and the mind is freed from duality... ॥ 68 ॥

...and after saying "I" and "mine" <gloss>meaning the ego and sense of possession have been transcended</gloss>, he should contemplate the **Flood of Immortality**. They say that the mind is life, and the absence of mind is death. ॥ 69 ॥

When mind and non-mind become equalized, **Living Liberation** <term>Jivanmukti: the state of being spiritually free and enlightened while still living in a physical body</term> is declared here. In this state, the true nature of existence cannot even be described in words. ॥ 70 ॥

Then, no one truly "lives" and no one will "die" <note>the yogi has transcended the cycle of reincarnation</note>. Having reached the state of **Royal Yoga** <term>Raja Yoga: the 'King of Yogas,' the highest state of meditative stillness</term>, which subdues all living beings... ॥ 71 ॥

...He may do everything or do nothing, acting according to his own desire. One [yogi] might be naked in caves, while another wears divine garments or a simple loincloth. One might be joined in union with divine women, yet elsewhere he remains a celibate. <note>This describes the "crazy wisdom" or "avadhuta" state where the enlightened master is no longer bound by social conventions or external appearances.</note>

<summary>This page describes the final stages of yogic attainment, where the practitioner achieves "Living Liberation" (Jivanmukti) and divine qualities after ten months of intense practice. It identifies Goraksha as the author/revealer of these teachings and lists the visionary signs of success.</summary>
<keywords>Goraksha, Amaraugha, Jivanmukti, Raja Yoga, Bindu, Laya, Shiva-likeness, internal lights</keywords>

--- Page 125 ---
<meta>This page concludes the Amaraughaprabodha. It transitions from the description of the perfected yogi’s internal state to a final exhortation by the author, Gorakshanatha, emphasizing that practical application is superior to theoretical knowledge.</meta>

### Page 55

->Sometimes content with food received from begging, and sometimes attaining a place of worldly enjoyment,<-
->the King of Yogis moves everywhere with unimpeded action, destroying all afflictions. ॥ 72 ॥<-

->The venerable Lord **Goraksha** <note>original: "Goraksanatha"; the semi-legendary founding master of the Hatha Yoga tradition</note>, who dwells forever within the **stream of the immortals** <note>original: "amaraugha"; referring to the lineage of perfected masters who have transcended death</note>,<-
->taught the systems of **Absorption** <term>Laya-yoga: the process of dissolving the individual ego into universal consciousness</term>, **Chant** <term>Mantra-yoga: the use of sacred sounds to focus the mind</term>, and **Force** <term>Hatha-yoga: the physical discipline of breath and posture</term> solely for the sake of reaching the **Royal Union** <term>Raja-yoga: the ultimate state of "kingly" meditation and self-realization to which all other yogas lead</term>. ॥ 73 ॥<-

->One should put this to the test with the body itself; what can a mere master of words achieve?<-
->Can a sick person be cured simply by reading a medical treatise? ॥ 74 ॥<-

->॥ Thus concludes the **Awakening to the Flood of Immortals** —<-
->composed by the venerable Lord Goraksha. ॥<-

<summary>This final page of the manuscript identifies Gorakshanatha as the author and asserts that all forms of yoga (Hatha, Laya, and Mantra) are merely subordinates to the ultimate goal of Raja Yoga. It ends with a famous analogy emphasizing that yoga must be practiced physically to be effective, just as medicine must be taken rather than just studied.</summary>

<keywords>Gorakshanatha, Amaraughaprabodha, Raja Yoga, Hatha Yoga, Laya Yoga, Mantra Yoga, practice vs theory, King of Yogis, spiritual liberation</keywords>

--- Page 126 ---
<meta>This page marks the beginning of a new treatise titled the Yoga Martanda (The Sun of Yoga), following the conclusion of the Amaraughaprabodha on the previous page. While the previous text ended by emphasizing practice over theory, this new work begins by outlining the specific physical and internal disciplines required for that practice, shifting from general exhortation to technical instruction.</meta>

# ॥ Now begins the Book of the Sun of Yoga ॥

->The fruit of the **tree of wishes** <note>original: "shrutikalpataro"; the Vedas, envisioned as a divine tree that grants all desires</note> which was brought forth by the **twice-born** <note>original: "dvija"; a reference to the high-born or the spiritually initiated</note>,<-
->O virtuous ones, serve this Yoga which calms the heat of worldly suffering. ॥ 1 ॥<-

**Posture** <term>Asana: the physical positions of the body</term>, **breath-restraint** <term>Pranasamrodha: also known as Pranayama, the regulation of the vital life-force</term>, **withdrawal of the senses** <term>Pratyahara: drawing the focus inward away from external distractions</term>, and **concentration** <term>Dharana: fixing the mind on a single point</term>,
**meditation** <term>Dhyana: the continuous flow of thought toward the object of concentration</term>, and **absorption** <term>Samadhi: the state of total meditative union</term>—these are the **six limbs of Yoga**. ॥ 2 ॥ <note>While the famous Yoga Sutras of Patanjali list eight limbs, many Hatha Yoga traditions focus on these six specific stages.</note>

There are as many postures as there are species of living beings.
Only the **Great Lord** <note>original: "Maheshvara"; a title for the god Shiva, who is considered the first teacher of Yoga</note> knows all their various characteristics and distinctions. ॥ 3 ॥

Out of the eighty-four hundred thousand types, one has been described for each [lakh/hundred thousand].
From those, Shiva selected a hundred and sixteen seats. ॥ 4 ॥

Out of all these various postures, two in particular are distinguished.
The first is called the **Auspicious Seat** <note>original: "Siddhasana"; also meaning the Pose of the Accomplished</note>, and the second is the **Lotus Seat**. ॥ 5 ॥

One should firmly place one heel against the **source-place** <note>original: "yoni-sthana"; the perineum</note> and then place the other foot above the genitals, keeping the body straight and steady. ॥ 6 ॥

Remaining motionless, with the senses restrained and the eyes steady, one should gaze at the **space between the eyebrows** <note>original: "bhruvorantaram"; the location of the "third eye" or Ajna Chakra</note>. This is called the **Accomplished Pose** <term>Siddhasana</term>, which acts as the key to the gate of liberation. ॥ 7 ॥

Having placed the right foot upon the left thigh, and likewise the left foot upon the right thigh;
Having firmly grasped the toes with the hands crossed from behind <note>The text implies the "Bound Lotus" variation (Baddha Padmasana) where the arms cross behind the back to reach the feet.</note>, ॥ 8 ॥

One should place the chin upon the chest and gaze at the tip of the nose.
This is called the **Lotus Pose** <term>Padmasana</term>, which destroys all diseases and imbalances for the self-controlled practitioner. ॥ 9 ॥

The first **energy center** <term>Chakra: literally a "wheel"; a focal point in the subtle body</term> is the **Foundation** <term>Adhara: also known as Muladhara</term>, and the second is the **Self-Base** <term>Svadhisthana</term>.
Between these two, at the location of the perineum, lies the place known as **Kamarupa** <note>original: "Kamarupa"; literally "the form of desire," associated with the awakening of vital energy</note>. ॥ 10 ॥

At the location of the anus, in the "Foundation" center, is a four-petaled lotus.
In the center of that is said to be the **Source** <note>original: "yoni"</note>, known as Kamakhya, which is worshipped by the perfected masters. ॥ 11 ॥

<summary>This opening page of the Yoga Martanda introduces the "Six-Limbed Yoga" and describes the two primary seated postures, Siddhasana and Padmasana, before beginning an explanation of the body's internal energy centers (chakras).</summary>

<keywords>Yoga Martanda, Six-limbed Yoga, Siddhasana, Padmasana, Muladhara Chakra, Svadhisthana Chakra, Shiva, Gorakshanatha</keywords>

# Page 97

In the center of the **source** <term>Yoni: here referring to the perineum or the space between the anus and the genitals</term> stands the **Great Lingam** <term>Mahalinga: a symbolic pillar representing the creative power of Shiva</term> facing toward the back <note>original: "pashchimabhimukham"; literally "facing West," but in yogic anatomy, West refers to the posterior or the back of the body</note>. He who knows the jewel-like disc on its head is a **knower of Yoga**. ॥ 12 ॥

Shining with the luster of molten gold, flashing like a streak of lightning, is that triangular "City of Fire." Below the region of the genitals— ॥ 13 ॥

By the word *sva* is meant the **vital breath** <term>Prana: the life-force or energy carried on the breath</term>; thus this center is remembered as the **Self-Base** <term>Svadhisthana: the second chakra, located at the root of the sexual organs</term>. ॥ 13 (cont.) ॥

This Svadhisthana circle is said to be at the genitals. Like a jewel threaded on a string, where the body is filled with the wind—that circle in the region of the navel is called the **City of Jewels** <term>Manipura: the third chakra, located at the solar plexus/navel</term>. ॥ 14 ॥

In the great twelve-spoked circle <note>Though usually associated with the Heart center, the text here mentions it following the navel center</note>, which is free from the duality of merit and sin, a person wanders only as long as they do not find the **Essential Truth** <term>Tattva: the ultimate reality or principle</term>. ॥ 15 ॥

Above the genitals and below the navel is the **root-bulb** <term>Kanda: a foundational node of energy in the lower abdomen</term> which is shaped like a bird’s egg. From there, seventy-two thousand **channels** <term>Nadis: the subtle conduits through which energy and breath flow</term> originate. ॥ 16 ॥

Among those thousands of channels, seventy-two have been specified. Out of those, ten are remembered as the primary carriers of the vital breath. ॥ 17 ॥

**Ida**, **Pingala**, and the third, **Sushumna**; **Gandhari**, **Hastajihva**, **Pusha**, and **Payasvini**; ॥ 18 ॥

**Alambusha**, **Kuhu**, and the tenth is remembered as **Shankhini**. Thus, this circle of channels must always be known by yogis. ॥ 19 ॥

The channels constantly carry the vital breath and are presided over by the deities of the **Moon, Sun, and Fire**. The three channels called Ida, Pingala, and Sushumna are the most renowned. ॥ 20 ॥

Ida is located on the left side, Pingala on the right, and Sushumna is in the middle. These are remembered as the three paths of the vital breath. ॥ 21 ॥

[The ten vital breaths are:] **Prana**, **Apana**, **Samana**, **Udana**, and **Vyana**; then **Naga**, **Kurma**, **Krikara**, **Devadatta**, and **Dhananjaya**. ॥ 22 ॥

<summary>This page details the internal geography of the yogic body, specifically the Svadhisthana and Manipura chakras, the central energy "bulb" (Kanda), and the network of 72,000 energy channels (Nadis), highlighting the ten most important channels and ten types of vital breath (Vayus).</summary>

# Page 58

In the heart dwells the **Life-breath** <term>Prana: the primary life-force associated with inhalation</term>, always controlled; the **Downward-breath** <term>Apana</term> is in the region of the anus; the **Equalizing-breath** <term>Samana</term> is in the navel; and the **Upward-breath** <term>Udana</term> moves within the center of the throat. ॥ 23 ॥

The **Pervasive-breath** <term>Vyana</term> pervades the entire body. These five are the principal winds. There are another five famous winds, beginning with **Naga**... ॥ 24 ॥

**Naga** <note>traditionally associated with belching or the movement of speech</note> is the first of these five; **Kurma** is situated in the opening of the eyes. **Krikara** causes hunger, and **Devadatta** acts in the process of yawning. ॥ 25 ॥

**Dhananjaya** produces sound; it does not leave the body even for a moment <note>original: "kshanamatram na nishrayet"; Dhananjaya is traditionally said to remain in the body even after death until decomposition begins</note>. In this way, these winds, taking the form of the **individual soul** <term>Jiva: the living spark of consciousness</term>, dwell within the thousands of energy channels. ॥ 26 ॥

Driven by the force of the **Life-breath** <term>Prana</term> and the **Downward-breath** <term>Apana</term>, the breath runs upward and downward. Because of its flickering nature, it is not seen as it moves through the left and right paths <note>referring to the Ida and Pingala, the two main nostrils or energy channels flanking the spine</note>. ॥ 27 ॥

Just as a ball struck by a wooden staff <note>original: "bhujadandena"; literally "arm-staff," referring to the force of a strike</note> moves along the ground, the soul—tossed about by the opposing forces of Prana and Apana—does not find rest. ॥ 28 ॥

Just as a hawk <note>original: "shyeno"</note> tied to a rope is pulled back even after flying away, so the soul, bound by its qualities <term>Gunas: the fundamental tendencies of nature that keep the soul tethered to material existence</term>, is pulled to and fro by the breaths. ॥ 29 ॥

The Downward-breath pulls the Life-breath, and the Life-breath pulls the Downward-breath. These two are situated above and below; he who understands their relationship is a **knower of Yoga**. ॥ 30 ॥

With the sound of *Ham*, the breath goes out; with the sound of *Sa*, it enters again. The soul always chants this mantra: *Hamsa, Hamsa* <note>The natural sound of breathing; "Hamsa" also means "Swan," a symbol for the soul that migrates between worlds</note>. ॥ 31 ॥

Twenty-one thousand and six hundred times in a single day and night, the soul chants this mantra. ॥ 32 ॥

This is known as the **Unuttered Prayer** <term>Ajapa Gayatri: a prayer performed naturally by the body without conscious effort</term>, which grants liberation to yogis. By the mere intention of this, a person is freed from all sins. ॥ 33 ॥

# Page 57

There is no knowledge equal to this, no recitation equal to this; there is no merit equal to this, neither in the past nor in the future. ॥ 34 ॥

Born from the **Coiled Power** <term>Kundalini</term>, the **Gayatri** <term>the personification of the most sacred Vedic hymn</term> is the sustainer of life. This "Science of the Breath" is the greatest knowledge; one who knows it truly knows the ancient wisdom of the **Vedas**. ॥ 35 ॥

Above the **root-bulb** <term>Kanda: a physical and energetic center located below the navel</term>, the **Kundalini** power is coiled eight times. She remains there constantly, covering the mouth of the **Path to Brahman** <term>Brahmadvara: the entrance to the central spiritual channel of the spine</term> with her own face. ॥ 36 ॥

The stainless **Abode of Brahman** is reached by the path of Yoga. The Goddess lies dormant there, <insert>covering that door with her mouth</insert>, 
->the Supreme Lady. ॥ 37 ॥<-

Shaped like a vibrating serpent, beautiful and fine as a lotus fiber, she remains asleep along with the **internal wind**. She is awakened by the internal **fire**. ॥ 38 ॥

Once awakened by the union of fire, the mind, and the wind, she moves upward through the **central channel** <term>Sushumna</term> like a needle pulling a thread <note>original: "suciiva gunamadaya"; the thread here refers to the subtle energy following the path of the "needle" or the Kundalini</note>. ॥ 39 ॥

Just as one might force open a door with a key <note>original: "kunchikaya"</note>, the practitioner of **Hatha Yoga** <term>the yoga of physical effort</term> should break open the door to the path of liberation using the **Kundalini**. ॥ 40 ॥

Joining the hands together firmly, assuming the **Lotus Posture** <term>Padmasana</term> with great stability, and placing the chin tightly against the chest, one should meditate within the mind. ॥ 41 ॥

Again and again, one should drive the **Downward-breath** <term>Apana</term> upward after filling the body with breath. By slowly releasing and restraining the **Life-breath** <term>Prana</term>, a person awakens the Power <note>original: "shaktiprabodhannarah"</note>. ॥ 42 ॥

One should massage the limbs to remove the moisture of sweat. By this practice, the body attains both firmness and lightness. ॥ 43 ॥

The yogi, devoted to his practice, should abandon pungent, sour, and salty foods, and instead take a diet of milk. He should remain celibate and be a **moderate eater** <term>Mitahari</term>. ॥ 44 ॥

Above the **root-bulb**, the **Kundalini** power is coiled eight times. To the foolish, she is a cause of bondage, but to practitioners of Yoga, she is the giver of liberation. ॥ 45 ॥

# Page 60

The **Great Seal** <term>Mahamudra</term>, the **Sky Seal** <term>Nabhomudra</term>, the **Upward Lock** <term>Oddiyana</term>, the **Throat Lock** <term>Jalandhara</term>, and the **Root Lock** <term>Mulabandha</term>—the yogi who knows these shall certainly attain success <term>Siddhi: supernatural powers or spiritual perfection</term>. ॥ 46 ॥

Through the constant practice of the **Root Lock**, the **downward wind** <term>Apana</term> and the **vital breath** <term>Prana</term> become unified, urine and excrement are diminished, and even an old man becomes young. ॥ 47 ॥

By pressing the perineum with the heel, one should contract the anus. By drawing the **downward wind** upward, this is called the **Root Lock** <term>Mulabandha</term>. ॥ 48 ॥

Because the "Great Bird" <note>original: "Mahakhagah"; a metaphor for the breath or the Kundalini energy</note> flies upward through it without resting, it is called the **Upward Lock** <term>Oddiyana</term>, and it is performed in that bound manner. ॥ 49 ॥

->The Upward Lock is the Lion to the Elephant of Death.<-

When the region behind the stomach and below the navel is pulled back and up, it is called the **Upward Lock** <term>Oddiyana</term>. It is a natural defense that acts as a lion against the elephant of death. ॥ 50 ॥

Because it binds the network of vessels <note>original: "sirajalam"</note> and causes the "water of the sky" to stop below the navel, it is called the **Throat Lock** <term>Jalandhara</term>, which destroys a multitude of throat-related ailments. ॥ 51 ॥

When the **Throat Lock** is performed, characterized by the constriction of the throat, the **nectar** <term>Piyusha: the subtle fluid of immortality secreted from the head</term> does not fall into the digestive fire, nor does the breath become agitated. ॥ 52 ॥

When the mind wanders in the "sky" <note>original: "khe"; refers to the inner spiritual space</note> and the tongue reaches the hollow of the skull, then the tongue, moving backward, has entered the cavity of the palate. ॥ 53 ॥

With the gaze fixed between the eyebrows, this becomes the **Sky-Moving Seal** <term>Khechari Mudra</term>. ॥ 54 ॥

Neither disease, nor death, nor sleep, nor hunger, nor thirst, nor fainting affects him who knows the **Sky-Moving Seal**. ॥ 55 ॥

Therefore, this **Sky-Moving Seal** is revered by all those who have attained mastery. The head is established there, <gloss>The vital seed is the root of the body</gloss> at the root of the body's vital essence <term>Bindu</term>. ॥ 56 ॥

Practitioners contemplate the body <gloss>he who [knows] the body</gloss> from the soles of the feet to the head. He who has sealed the opening above the uvula with the **Sky-Moving Seal**... ॥ 57 ॥

...his **vital essence** <term>Bindu</term> does not waste away, even if he is embraced by a beautiful woman. As long as the **vital essence** remains in the body, why should there be any fear of death? ॥ 58 ॥

<meta>This page continues the technical instructions on Hatha Yoga, moving from the definitions of the locks (bandhas) to the internal alchemy of the vital fluids and the specific practice of the Great Seal (Mahamudra).</meta>

# Page 61

As long as the **Sky Seal** <term>Nabhomudra: a practice involving the tongue and the upper palate</term> is held, the **vital drop** <term>Bindu: the masculine creative essence or "seed" of consciousness</term> does not fall. Even if the **vital drop** has already begun to move toward the digestive fire <note>original: "Hutashanam"; the internal fire of the stomach which usually consumes this essence</note>, ॥ 59 ॥

it is forced upward by the power of the **Womb Seal** <term>Yonimudra: a seal used to redirect energy from the pelvic floor</term>. The **vital drop** is constantly said to be of two types: white and red. ॥ 60 ॥

The white is called the **semen** <term>Shukra</term>, and the red is known as the **great fluid** <term>Maharajas</term>. This **generative fluid** <term>Rajas</term>, which resembles the color of vermilion, resides in the "Place of the Sun" <note>original: "Ravisthana"; typically the solar plexus or navel region</note>. ॥ 61 ॥

The **vital drop** <term>Bindu</term> resides in the "Place of the Goddess" <term>Shaktisthana</term>. Their union is very difficult to achieve. The **drop** is the god Shiva and the **fluid** is the goddess Shakti; the **drop** is the Moon and the **fluid** is the Sun. <note>The text here identifies the internal fluids with the primary masculine and feminine cosmic principles</note> ॥ 62 ॥

When the **fluid** and the **drop** achieve oneness, the body becomes divine. Through this luminous union, the supreme state is attained. One should move the **vital energy** <term>Shakti</term> with the breath—the Moon/Drop and the Sun/Fluid. ॥ 63 ॥

The **semen** is joined with the Moon and the **fluid** is joined with the Sun. He who knows the unified, single essence <note>original: "samarasa"; the state of balanced immersion</note> of these two is a **knower of Yoga** <term>Yogavit</term>. ॥ 64 ॥

The purification of the network of energy channels <term>Nadis</term>, the movement of the solar and lunar breaths, and the drying of the tongue—these are achieved by one who practices the **Great Seal** <term>Mahamudra</term>. ॥ 65 ॥

One should place the chin upon the chest <note>original: "hansa/vaksa"; referring to the chin-lock posture</note> and firmly press the **perineum** <note>original: "yonim"</note> with the left heel. With both hands, one should hold the extended right leg. ॥ 66 ॥

Filling the abdominal cavity with breath, one should bind it <note>hold the breath</note> and then exhale slowly. This is called the **Great Seal** <term>Mahamudra</term>, which is said to destroy all diseases in people. ॥ 67 ॥

After practicing on the "Sun side" <note>the right side or right nostril</note>, one should practice again on the "Moon side" <note>the left side or left nostril</note>. When the number of breaths is equal on both sides, one may release the seal. ॥ 68 ॥

For such a person, there is no distinction between wholesome and unwholesome food; even things that are completely tasteless become flavorful. Even lethal poison, if consumed, is digested as easily as **nectar** <term>Piyusha</term>. ॥ 69 ॥

Consumption <term>Kshaya: a wasting disease similar to tuberculosis</term>, leprosy <term>Kushtha</term>, constipation, abdominal tumors <term>Gulma</term>, colic, and the suffering of fever—all these diseases are destroyed for the one who practices the **Great Seal**. ॥ 70 ॥

<meta>This page continues the technical instructions on Hatha Yoga, moving from the internal alchemy of the Sun and Moon (the generative fluids) to the practice of the Great Seal and the mystical significance of the syllable Om.</meta>

# Page 62

This **Great Seal** <term>Mahamudra: a foundational yoga posture involving a seated forward fold and energy locks</term> has been described; it brings **great success** <term>Mahasiddhi</term> to men. It must be guarded with great effort and not given to just anyone. ॥ 71 ॥

Having assumed the **Lotus Posture** <term>Padmasana: the classic cross-legged meditation pose</term>, keeping the body, head, and stomach in a straight line, with the gaze fixed solely on the **tip of the nose** <note>original: "nasagre drishtir"</note>, one should chant the eternal **syllable Om** <term>Omkara</term>. ॥ 72 ॥ <note>The OCR reads "doṅkāram," a likely transcription error for "oṅkāram."</note>

The worlds of Earth, Atmosphere, and Heaven; the deities of the Moon, Sun, and Fire; where these three "colors" <note>original: "varna"; here referring to the three phonemes of Om which represent different qualities of reality</note> are situated, that is the **Supreme Light** <term>Param Jyoti</term> called Om. ॥ 73 ॥

The letter A, the letter U, and the letter M, along with the **Dot** <term>Bindu: the nasal vibration that concludes the syllable</term>; where these three sounds reside, that is the **Supreme Light** called Om. ॥ 74 ॥

The three divisions of time <note>Past, present, and future</note>, the three worlds, the three Vedas, and the three musical notes; where the three gods <note>The trinity of Brahma the creator, Vishnu the sustainer, and Shiva the destroyer</note> reside, that is the **Supreme Light** called Om. ॥ 75 ॥

That **seed-syllable** <term>Bija: a "root" sound used in mantra practice</term> which one chants with the voice, practices with the body, and remembers daily with the mind—that is the **Supreme Light**. ॥ 76 ॥

Whether pure or impure, whoever chants the eternal **syllable Om** is not smeared by sin, just as a **lotus leaf** is not wetted by water. ॥ 77 ॥

When the **breath** <term>Vata</term> moves, everything <note>meaning the mind and the senses</note> moves; when it is still, all is still. One attains the "place of the origin" <note>original: "yonisthanam"; the source of spiritual power in the pelvic floor</note>, therefore one should restrain the **vital breath** <term>Vayu</term>. ॥ 78 ॥

As long as the **breath** remains in the body, life does not depart. Death is the departure of the breath; therefore, one should restrain the **breath**. ॥ 79 ॥

As long as the **breath** <term>Marut: another name for the vital wind</term> is bound within the body, the **mind** <term>Chitta</term> remains free from disease. As long as the gaze is held between the eyebrows, how can there be any fear of death? ॥ 80 ॥

Therefore, out of fear of **death** <note>The OCR "mūkālabhayād" is likely "kālabhayād," meaning fear of Time or Death</note>, even the creator-god **Brahma** became devoted to **breath control** <term>Pranayama</term>. The best of yogis should therefore restrain the **breath**. ॥ 81 ॥

The **Swan** <term>Hansa: a metaphor for the breath, which makes the sound "ham" when exiting and "sa" when entering</term> travels thirty-six finger-widths outward through the left and right nostrils; there the **vital energy** <term>Prana</term> is established. ॥ 82 ॥

<meta>This page continues the technical instructions on Hatha Yoga. Having previously described the Lotus Posture and the significance of the syllable Om, the text now moves into the specific mechanics of Nadi Shodhana (channel purification) and the various stages of Pranayama (breath control).</meta>

# Page 63

One is purified <gloss>original: "drumeti," likely a variant or error for a word meaning "through this process"</gloss> when the entire network of channels <term>Nadis: the subtle pathways through which life-force flows</term> is cleared of impurities <gloss>original: "malakulam"</gloss>. Only then does the yogi become capable of fully restraining the breath <gloss>original: "pranasamgrahane kshamah"</gloss>.
->॥ 83 ॥<-

Firmly seated in the **Lotus Posture** <term>Padmasana</term>, the yogi should inhale the breath through the **Moon** <note>The left nostril, associated with cooling, lunar energy</note>. Having held it according to his capacity, he should then exhale it through the **Sun** <note>The right nostril, associated with heating, solar energy</note>. ॥ 84 ॥

Meditating on the **Lunar Disk**, which resembles liquid nectar and is as white as cow’s milk, the practitioner of **breath control** <term>Pranayama</term> becomes filled with joy. ॥ 85 ॥

Drawing the breath in through the **Sun** [right nostril], one should slowly fill the belly. After performing **retention** <term>Kumbhaka: the practice of holding the breath</term> according to the prescribed method, one should exhale again through the **Moon** [left nostril]. ॥ 86 ॥

Meditating on the **Solar Circle** situated at the navel—a mass of blazing, flickering flames—the yogi becomes filled with joy in his breath control. ॥ 87 ॥

If one draws the breath through the **Ida** <note>original: "ila," the left energy channel</note> and exhales it through the other, and then, after drinking in a mass of air through the **Pingala** <note>The right energy channel</note> and holding it, releases it through the left... ॥ 88 ॥

By this method, for those who meditate on the two disks of the **Sun and Moon**, the entire system of channels becomes purified after three months of practice. ॥ 89 ॥

From the **purification of the channels** <term>Nadi Shodhana</term>, there arises the ability to hold the breath as long as desired, the kindling of the internal fire <note>The digestive and spiritual fire known as Agni</note>, the manifestation of the **inner sound** <term>Nada: the mystical resonance heard in deep meditation</term>, and perfect health. ॥ 90 ॥

The **Prana** <term>Life-force or breath</term> is the air residing in the body; its movement is tied to the **Apana** <term>The downward-moving energy responsible for elimination</term>. The flow of breath moves through the opening of a single respiratory passage. ॥ 91 ॥

**Exhalation** <term>Rechaka</term>, **inhalation** <term>Puraka</term>, and **retention** <term>Kumbhaka</term> are the three parts of breath control, and they are essentially composed of the **syllable Om** <term>Pranava</term>. This breath control is three-fold, refined by twelve **measures** <term>Matra: a unit of time, roughly the time it takes to blink or touch one's knee</term>. ॥ 92 ॥

The Sun and Moon <note>The right and left breaths</note>, when combined with the twelve measures, destroy the web of defects. This must be understood by yogis at all times. ॥ 93 ॥

The classification of breath control is as follows: twelve measures is called **low**; twice that <note>24 measures</note> is recorded as **middle**; and three times that <note>36 measures</note> is called **high**. ॥ 94 ॥

<vocab>Pranayama (breath control), Nadi Shodhana (channel purification), Padmasana (lotus pose), Kumbhaka (retention), Rechaka (exhalation), Puraka (inhalation), Yogi (practitioner)</vocab>

<summary>This page details the practice of Nadi Shodhana (alternate nostril breathing) combined with visualizations of the Sun and Moon to purify the body's energy channels. It also defines the three parts of breath (inhalation, retention, exhalation) and categorizes the intensity of the practice based on the duration of the breath held.</summary>
<keywords>Nadi Shodhana, Pranayama, Sun and Moon energy, breath retention, Matra measures, internal fire, Nada sound</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the technical instructions on Hatha Yoga. Having previously described the mechanics of channel purification (Nadi Shodhana) through alternate nostril breathing, the text now details the physical symptoms of success in breath control and defines the higher "limbs" of yoga—concentration, meditation, and absorption—through a specific numerical hierarchy.</meta>

# Page 64

The lowest level of practice [produces perspiration], the middle level produces trembling, and the highest level attains **steadiness** <note>original: "sthanu," literally a pillar or post. In yoga manuals, this refers to a state of physical immobility or levitation</note>. Therefore, one should master the restraint of the breath.
->॥ 95 ॥<-

Firmly seated in the **Lotus Posture** <term>Padmasana</term>, the yogi—having bowed to the Guru and to Shiva—should fix the gaze upon the tip of the nose and practice **breath control** <term>Pranayama</term>.
->॥ 96 ॥<-

Closing the nine gates <note>The nine openings of the body: eyes, ears, nostrils, mouth, and the two lower excretory ports</note> and having conquered the breath, one should hold it firmly. Guided by the **Internal Power** <note>original: "shaktya," referring to the Kundalini energy</note>, the breath should be moved toward the inner space along with the "downward-moving air" <term>Apana</term> and the internal fire.
->॥ 97 ॥<-

By drawing the **Apana** upward and uniting it with the **Prana** <note>The "upward-moving" vital life force</note>, the yogi who holds the breath through this union is liberated from all sins.
->॥ 98 ॥<-

Through this method of self-meditation and steady practice, one attains a state of upward-moving bliss. As long as the yogi remains in this state, they are praised by the great **Sankhya** <term>A school of philosophy focused on the discrimination between the eternal self and the material world</term>.
->॥ 99 ॥<-

In the practice of breath control, a fire is born that consumes the dense forest of sins. It is always described by the yogis as a great bridge over the ocean of disease.
->॥ 100 ॥<-

Through **posture** <term>Asana</term>, one conquers the vital winds <gloss>original: "maruto"</gloss>. Through **breath control** <term>Pranayama</term>, one destroys sin. Through **withdrawal of the senses** <term>Pratyahara</term>, the knower of yoga destroys mental distortions.
->॥ 101 ॥<-

Through **concentration** <term>Dharana</term>, one gains mental fortitude. Through **meditation** <term>Dhyana</term>, one attains wondrous powers. Through **absorption** <term>Samadhi</term>, one achieves liberation, having abandoned both good and bad karma.
->॥ 102 ॥<-

A period of twelve **breath control** exercises is called **withdrawal** <term>Pratyahara</term>. Twelve such withdrawals constitute an auspicious **concentration** <term>Dharana</term>.
->॥ 103 ॥<-

Those skilled in meditation state that twelve concentrations make one **meditation** <term>Dhyana</term>. Twelve such meditations are called **absorption** <term>Samadhi</term>.
->॥ 104 ॥<-

This absorption is the supreme light, present in every limb and facing every direction. Therefore, one must strive to see that light once the coming and going of the breath has been stilled.
->॥ 105 ॥<-

Firmly establishing the posture at the base, and closing the gates of the ears, eyes, and nostrils with the fingers, one should hold the breath—whether it was filled through the mouth [or held otherwise].
->॥ 106 ॥<-

<summary>This page describes the physical milestones of breath control and outlines the mathematical relationship between the stages of yoga, where each higher stage (from withdrawal to absorption) is defined as twelve units of the preceding stage.</summary>
<keywords>Pranayama, Pratyahara, Dharana, Dhyana, Samadhi, Nine Gates, Apana, Sankhya philosophy, Yoga milestones</keywords>

--- Page 135 ---
<meta>This page continues the technical instructions on the results of breath control (Pranayama) and transitions into the definition and practice of the next stage of yoga: Sensory Withdrawal (Pratyahara). It continues the theme of internal alchemy—combining fire and air within the body—to achieve longevity and spiritual union.</meta>

# Page 65

Holding the breath in the chest along with the internal fire and the air, the practitioner should maintain it while it is in a state of swirling. In this way, the **yogi** <term>A practitioner of yoga</term> attains equality with Shiva and lives for a very long time.
->॥ 107 ॥<-

When the breath reaches the **Inner Space** <note>original: "gaganam," literally "the sky." In this context, it refers to the etheric space within the crown of the head</note>, a great sound arises. When a deep sound like that of a bell is heard, success is not far off for him.
->॥ 108 ॥<-

Through the proper practice of **breath control** <term>Pranayama</term>, all sins are destroyed. But through improper or haphazard practice, every kind of disease may arise.
->॥ 109 ॥<-

Hiccups, labored breathing, coughs, and pains in the head, ears, and eyes—these afflictions occur through incorrect methods and from the mismanagement of the breath.
->॥ 110 ॥<-

Just as a lion, an elephant, or a tiger is tamed slowly and gradually—otherwise it kills the trainer—so too must the breath be mastered with great care.
->॥ 111 ॥<-

One should exhale properly, inhale properly, and hold the breath properly. In this way, one attains **perfection** <term>Siddhi</term>.
->॥ 112 ॥<-

The eyes and other senses naturally wander toward their respective objects in sequence. Their systematic withdrawal from those objects is called **Sensory Withdrawal** <term>Pratyahara</term>.
->॥ 113 ॥<-

Just as the sun in the late afternoon withdraws its rays, the yogi established in this third limb of yoga withdraws all mental fluctuations.
->॥ 114 ॥<-

Just as a **tortoise** <term>Kurma</term> retracts its limbs into its shell, the yogi should withdraw the senses into the Self.
->॥ 115 ॥<-

Whatever one hears with the ears, whether pleasant or unpleasant, seeing it all as the Self is called **Sensory Withdrawal**.
->॥ 116 ॥<-

Whether experiencing pleasure, heat, or pain, and whatever is encountered through the faculty of speech <note>original: "vacha." While the word means "speech," in this list of senses, it likely represents the faculty of touch or the general experience of the physical world. The instruction is to see the witness-self behind the sensation.</note>, seeing that as the Self is called **Sensory Withdrawal**.
->॥ 117 ॥<-

Whatever fragrance or lack of fragrance one smells with the nose, seeing that as the Self is called **Sensory Withdrawal**.
->॥ 118 ॥<-

<summary>This page warns of the physical dangers of improper breath control while detailing the signs of success, such as hearing internal sounds. It then defines Pratyahara (Sensory Withdrawal) as the process of seeing the divine Self within all sensory perceptions.</summary>

<keywords>Pranayama, Pratyahara, Siddhi, sensory withdrawal, tortoise metaphor, internal sound, breath control dangers, Shiva</keywords>

--- Page 136 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion on the stages of yoga, specifically focusing on Sensory Withdrawal (Pratyahara) and the internal alchemy of the Sun and Moon. It follows the previous page's metaphor of the tortoise withdrawing its limbs to explain how a practitioner internalizes their energy.</meta>

# Page 66

The **Sun** <note>original: "Bhaskara," the sun, symbolizing the internal fire located in the navel</note> pulls back the stream of nectar consisting of the Moon's essence. That very act of pulling back is called **Sensory Withdrawal** <term>Pratyahara</term>.
->॥ 119 ॥<-

When the breath is joined with those two [channels] coming from the **Lunar Sphere** <term>Somamandala</term>, and when the third [stage] is attained through them, one becomes free from old age and death.
->॥ 120 ॥<-

In the center of the **navel** <term>Nabhi</term> dwells the Sun, which is of the nature of fire. The **Moon** <term>Candrama</term>, consisting of the essence of nectar, is eternally situated in the center of the **palate** <term>Talu</term>.
->॥ 121 ॥<-

The Moon rains downwards, and the Sun (facing upwards) consumes it. There is a practice known [to the masters] by which the **Nectar of Immortality** <term>Piyusha</term> is obtained.
->॥ 122 ॥<-

The navel is placed above, and the palate below; the Sun is above and the Moon is below. This is known as the **Inverted Action** <term>Viparita Karani</term>, and it is learned only through the words of a Guru.
->॥ 123 ॥<-

Where the Great Bull <note>A metaphor for the primordial sound or the breath bound by the three "locks" or Bandhas</note> roars loudly after being bound in three ways—that is the **Unstruck Center** <term>Anahata Chakra</term>, which shatters the intense suffering of the yogi.
->॥ 124 ॥<-

Having transcended the Unstruck Center and drawing [the breath] up from the **City of Jewels** <term>Manipura Chakra</term>, the breath of the living being, and likewise the lotus, becomes like nectar for the yogis.
->॥ 125 ॥<-

From above, the nectar trickles from the **Sixteen-Petaled Lotus** <note>The throat center, or Vishuddha Chakra</note>, obtained through the forceful practice of breath. By restraining the tongue in the upper opening (of the palate) and meditating on the Supreme Power...
->॥ 126 ॥<-

...the yogi who drinks the pure, surging water of the lunar nectar with his tongue becomes free from all defects. With a body as delicate as a lotus stalk, he lives for a very long time.
->॥ 127 ॥<-

One should drink the cool "water" <note>referring to the cool air or internal nectar</note> through the mouth shaped like a **crow’s beak** <note>original: "Kaka-cancu," a technique where the tongue is pursed to inhale cooling air</note>. Within half a month, one is freed from all diseases and delusion.
->॥ 128 ॥<-

When the **Purity Center** <term>Vishuddha Chakra</term>, which is of the nature of the moon's rays, becomes purified, the nectar flows through the upward path, bypassing and "deceiving" the mouth of the Sun <note>The Sun/Navel-fire normally consumes the nectar; this practice diverts it to rejuvenate the body instead</note>.
->॥ 129 ॥<-

Through the regulation of the **inward and outward breaths** <term>Prana and Apana</term>, the yogi becomes ageless. He should constantly drink the breath through the tongue and the root of the palate.
->॥ 130 ॥<-

<summary>This page describes internal yogic alchemy, specifically the practice of "Inverted Action" (Viparita Karani) to prevent the "Sun" in the navel from consuming the "Moon's" nectar from the palate. It details how drinking this internal nectar through the throat and tongue leads to longevity, health, and mastery over the body's energy centers.</summary>
<keywords>Pratyahara, internal nectar, Sun and Moon energy, Viparita Karani, Anahata Chakra, Manipura Chakra, Vishuddha Chakra, breath control, immortality, Kaka-cancu mudra</keywords>

--- Page 137 ---
<meta>This page continues the instructions for the advanced practitioner of yoga, specifically detailing the internal alchemy of the "Lunar Crescent" (Somakla) and the practice of Khechari Mudra, where the tongue is turned back to catch the falling nectar of immortality.</meta>

# Page 67

Binding the crystal-clear water of the **Lunar Crescent** <term>Somakla; the cooling, nectar-like energy associated with the moon in the head</term> from above the ears, it moves through the nose to the gateway of breath from all sides.
->॥ 131 ॥<-

With the face turned upwards, the gaze fixed, one should drink [the nectar] so the limbs of the body are saturated. For one who has conquered the mind and senses in this way, there is no decay.
->॥ 132 ॥<-

Then, having turned the tongue upwards, he who performs the **Drinking of the Moon** <term>Somapana</term>—in half a month, of this there is no doubt, the **Knower of Yoga** <term>Yogavit</term> conquers death.
->॥ 133 ॥<-

Pressing the tip of the tongue against the great opening of the **Royal Staff** <note>original: "Rajadanda," referring here to the uvula or the channel in the roof of the mouth</note> and meditating on the Goddess made of nectar, in six months one becomes a wise seer.
->॥ 134 ॥<-

By this binding which yields fruit above, the life-force <note>original: "Vishnu," here used to represent the pervasive sustaining energy of the body</note> is restrained. The practitioner becomes ageless and immortal, just like the five-faced **Lord Shiva** <note>original: "Hara"</note>.
->॥ 135 ॥<-

Equipped with **Posture** <term>Asana</term> and joined with **Breath Control** <term>Pranayama</term>, accomplished in **Sensory Withdrawal** <term>Pratyahara</term>, one should practice **Concentration** <term>Dharana</term>.
->॥ 136 ॥<-

One seals all the "gates" <note>The physical openings of the body, such as eyes, ears, and nose</note> when that upper opening is held. The nectar never leaks or is lost when the five concentrations are accomplished.
->॥ 137 ॥<-

<unclear>cuṃ........tāyaddi</unclear> <note>The manuscript is damaged or the transcription is incomplete here</note> ...the tip of the uvula constantly, the tongue dripping with essence. It may taste like milk, or bitter, or like something burnt, or like a mixture of honey and clarified butter.
->॥ 138 ॥<-

It is the removal of diseases, the end of old age, and the very support of the sacred scriptures. To him belongs immortality, the eight-fold supernatural powers, and the attraction of perfected celestial beings.
->॥ 139 ॥<-

When the yogi's body is filled with nectar, after two or three years, there arises in him the power of **Miniaturization** <term>Anima; the ability to become as small as an atom</term> and other such qualities.
->॥ 140 ॥<-

The embodied soul, eternally filled with the **Lunar Crescent** <term>Somakla</term>, does not leave the body <note>meaning the yogi achieves a state of physical immortality or chooses his time of death</note>. Equipped with posture, joined with breath control, accomplished in sensory withdrawal, one should practice concentration.
->॥ 141 ॥<-

--- Page 138 ---
<meta>This page continues the technical instructions for advanced yoga practice, transitioning from the internal nectar-drinking techniques (Khechari Mudra) to the specific methods of Concentration (Dharana) on the five elements to achieve supernatural powers and liberation.</meta>

# Page 68

The **Concentration** <term>Dharana; the sixth limb of yoga involving intense mental focus</term> on the five elements, beginning with Earth, is performed individually. By achieving stillness of the mind, this concentration is successfully established.
->॥ 142 ॥<-

**Concentration on Earth** <term>Prithvi Dharana</term> is characterized by the square shape, shimmering like yellow orpiment, gold, and rubies. It is presided over by the god Brahma <note>original: "Kamalasana," the one who sits on a lotus</note> and is situated in the region of the heart.
->॥ 143 ॥<-

Drawing the breath and mind there to the nostrils, one should hold them for five **Ghatikas** <term>a unit of time equaling 24 minutes; five ghatikas is approximately two hours</term>. This concentration on Earth always brings steadiness to those who have conquered the element of earth.
->॥ 144 ॥<-

**Concentration on Water** <term>Varuni Dharana</term> resembles the shape of a half-moon and is as white as a jasmine flower. It is situated in the region of the ears <note>The text mentions "karne" (ear), though many traditions place the water element in the pelvic region</note>, associated with the "Va" **Seed-Syllable** <term>Bija; a sacred sound associated with an element</term> which is like nectar, and is ever-joined with Lord Vishnu.
->॥ 145 ॥<-

Absorbing the breath and mind there, one should hold them for five *ghatikas*. This Watery Concentration removes the misery caused by terrible poisons and fire.
->॥ 146 ॥<-

**Concentration on Fire** <term>Vaishvanari Dharana</term> is situated at the palate and is triangular in shape, resembling the deep red of a velvet mite <note>original: "indragopa," a bright red insect appearing in the rainy season</note>. It is composed of the seed-syllable "Ra," shines like coral, and is associated with Lord Rudra.
->॥ 147 ॥<-

Having dissolved the breath and mind there for five *ghatikas*, one practices this concentration.
->॥ 148 ॥<-

For the yogis, this concentration burns away the fear of death; it is the Fiery Concentration. **Concentration on Air** <term>Vayavi Dharana</term> is seen between the eyebrows, resembling a mass of black eye-salve <note>original: "anjan-punja," a dark collyrium used for the eyes</note>.
->॥ 149 ॥<-

This element is the essence of truth, associated with the seed-syllable "Ya," where the deity Ishvara presides. Drawing the breath and mind there, one should hold them for five *ghatikas*.
->॥ 150 ॥<-

This is the Airy Concentration which grants the power of movement through the air <note>original: "yoga-manam" or "yogam-langhanam," implying the ability to leap or fly</note> to the practitioners. **Concentration on Ether** <term>Nabha Dharana</term> is as pure as clear space and is situated at the **Crown of the Head** <term>Brahmarandhra; the "opening of Brahma" at the top of the skull</term>.
->॥ 151 ॥<-

Within it resides the letter "Ya" <note>Usually, the seed-syllable for Ether is "Ha"; the manuscript here reads "Ya," which may be a scribal variation or error</note>, joined with the Lord Sadashiva. Absorbing the breath and mind there, one should hold them for five *ghatikas*.
->॥ 152 ॥<-

This Ethereal Concentration is said to be the skillful breaker of the **Gate of Liberation** <note>original: "Mokshakavata-bhedana"</note>. These concentrations provide stability, conquer sleep, burn away impurities, and cause the mind to transcend the world.
->॥ 153 ॥<-

<summary>This page details the "Pancha Dharana," or the five elemental concentrations (Earth, Water, Fire, Air, and Ether), describing their geometric shapes, colors, seed-syllables, and presiding deities. These practices are used to master the physical body and gain spiritual powers like flight and immunity to toxins.</summary>
