--- Page 139 ---
<meta>This page concludes the specific instructions on the five elemental concentrations (Dharana) and transitions into the definition, types, and supreme value of Meditation (Dhyana), followed by a guide to meditating on the internal energy centers (Chakras).</meta>

# Page 69

This fivefold **Concentration** <term>Dharana; the practice of fixing the mind on a specific object or element</term> on the elements serves to wither away the physical impurities of all living beings.
->॥ 154 ॥<-

All these five concentrations are pleasing to the ears, yet they are very difficult to obtain. He who constantly understands them is liberated from all sins.
->॥ 155 ॥<-

Abandoning everything else, that which dwells in the heart of the yogi when the mind is unmoving in the ultimate reality—that is what is declared to be **Meditation** <term>Dhyana; a state of sustained, profound contemplation</term>.
->॥ 156 ॥<-

That meditation is of two kinds: **with attributes** <term>Saguna; meditation on a deity or form with qualities like color and shape</term> and **without attributes** <term>Nirguna; meditation on the formless, pure consciousness</term>. The wise know that the "with attributes" kind is distinguished by different colors/forms, while the "without attributes" is pure consciousness alone.
->॥ 157 ॥<-

A thousand **Horse-Sacrifices** <term>Ashvamedha; an ancient and elaborate Vedic ritual performed by kings to prove their sovereignty</term> and a hundred **Vajapeya** sacrifices <note>original: "Vajapeya," a high-ranking Vedic "Drink of Strength" ritual</note> are not worth even a sixteenth part of the merit gained from a single practice of meditation.
->॥ 158 ॥<-

With the consciousness turned inward and the eyes [partially open] looking outward, established in a **Comfortable Posture** <term>Sukhasana; a cross-legged seated position used for meditation</term> and maintaining the evenness of the body—this is celebrated as the **Meditation Gesture** <term>Dhyana Mudra</term>.
->॥ 159 ॥<-

Focusing on the first center, the **Root Base** <term>Adhara Chakra (Muladhara); the energy center at the base of the spine</term>, which resembles molten gold, and fixing the gaze on the tip of the nose, one meditates and is released from all wickedness.
->॥ 160 ॥<-

Meditating on the second center, the **Self-Base** <term>Svadhisthana; the sacral energy center</term>, which is like the crest of a beautiful ruby, while fixing the gaze on the tip of the nose, the entire world begins to shine with light.
->॥ 161 ॥<-

Meditating on the center situated in the **Space of the Heart** <term>Hrid-akasha; the Anahata chakra</term>, which possesses the brilliance of a fierce sun, while fixing the gaze on the tip of the nose, one becomes of the very nature of the Absolute <note>original: "Brahmamayo," meaning one with Brahman or universal consciousness</note>.
->॥ 162 ॥<-

The **Pure Center** <term>Vishuddha Chakra; the throat center</term> is said to be constantly located in the middle of the lotus-pericarp. By fixing the gaze on the tip of the nose and meditating there, one is liberated from all suffering.
->॥ 163 ॥<-

At the **Soft Palate** <term>Lambika; the area of the uvula</term> is the **Lunar Sphere** <term>Chandra-mandala</term> full of flowing nectar. By fixing the gaze on the tip of the nose and meditating there, one is released from death.
->॥ 164 ॥<-

Meditating on the Divine situated **between the eyebrows** <note>original: "dhruvormadhya," referring to the Ajna chakra or third eye</note>, which resembles a glistening pearl, while fixing the gaze on the tip of the nose, one becomes filled with supreme bliss.
->॥ 165 ॥<-

<summary>This page defines Meditation (Dhyana), distinguishes between its formal and formless types, and provides a systematic guide to meditating on the energy centers (Chakras) from the base of the spine to the third eye.</summary>
<keywords>Dhyana, Saguna, Nirguna, Ashvamedha, Sukhasana, Chakras, Adhara, Svadhisthana, Vishuddha, Nasagra-drishti, liberation</keywords>

--- Page 140 ---
<meta>This page continues the technical instructions on meditation (Dhyana), moving from the general theory of attributes to specific anatomical locations for practice. It concludes by defining Samadhi (absorption) and its relationship to the duration of practice.</meta>

# Page 70

By constantly **meditating** <term>Dhyana; a state of sustained contemplation</term> on the **Supreme Lord** <note>original: "Parameshvara," a title for the highest divinity, often Shiva</note> in the space between the eyebrows, a yogi whose vital breath is restrained within the Self attains the state of Yoga.
->॥ 166 ॥<-

Fixing the gaze upon the **center of the eyebrows** <note>original: "bhru-madhya," considered a major focal point for internal energy</note> and meditating on the attribute-less, peaceful, and all-pervading **Auspicious One** <note>original: "Shiva," referring here to the formless Absolute</note> within the vast sky of consciousness, one becomes one with the Absolute.
->॥ 167 ॥<-

Meditating on the **Self** <term>Atman; the eternal, innermost soul</term> as being pure, having the form of the infinite sky, shimmering like water in a mirage, and pervading all things, the yogi attains the goal of Yoga.
->॥ 168 ॥<-

The anus, the genitals, the navel, the heart, the energy center above, the uvula, the location of the palate, the center of the eyebrows, and the **hollow of the sky** <note>original: "nabho-bilam," the cranial void or "aperture of Brahma" at the top of the head</note>—
->॥ 169 ॥<-

These have been declared as the specific locations for meditation for yogis. When coupled with the true principles and the use of **attributes** <term>Upadhi; a limiting condition or external quality used as a focus</term>, they bring about the rise of the eightfold supernatural powers.
->॥ 170 ॥<-

Two things have been illustrated through this established practice: the "attribute" and the "reality." The attribute is said to be the **outward appearance** <note>original: "varna," literally color or form</note>, while the Self is called the reality.
->॥ 171 ॥<-

Knowledge gained through attributes is of one kind, while the state of reality is another. Yogis know that "reality" is that which is entirely free from all attributes and limiting conditions.
->॥ 172 ॥<-

Just as a clear **crystal** <note>original: "sphatika," a common metaphor in Indian philosophy for the pure mind reflecting its surroundings</note> appears to take on different colors based on what it is near, the Self appears to be shaped by the subtle elements of sound and the weight of past actions and attributes.
->॥ 173 ॥<-

Through various methods and the different expressions of **Power** <term>Shakti; the active, creative energy of the universe</term>, that eternal Self is revealed. Those who know reality understand that principle which the scriptures praise day and night—the stainless, supreme ether, the imperishable and undecaying.
->॥ 174 ॥<-

Thus is meditation established; following that is the supreme asceticism of **Absorption** <term>Samadhi; the final stage of yoga where the meditator's mind merges completely with the object of meditation</term>. Concentration is held for five units of time, while meditation lasts for six. <note>The original uses "nadika," a traditional unit of time approximately equal to 24 minutes.</note>
->॥ 175 ॥<-

By twelve of these cycles, Absorption is achieved through the restraint of the vital breath. Just as there is a complete blending of **water and salt** <note>original: "ambu-saindhavayo," a classic metaphor for non-duality</note> when they are joined, so too the oneness of the Self and the mind is called Absorption.
->॥ 176 ॥<-

<summary>This page details specific anatomical points for meditation and distinguishes between the "attribute" (external form) and the "reality" (the Self). It concludes by defining Samadhi as the complete merging of mind and spirit, using the metaphor of salt dissolving in water.</summary>
<keywords>meditation, eyebrow center, Shiva, Atman, energy centers, Samadhi, salt-water metaphor, spiritual attributes, yogic powers</keywords>

--- Page 141 ---
<meta>This page concludes the "Yoga Mārtāṇḍa," moving from the specific physical points of focus described on page 70 to the ultimate state of Samadhi (Absorption). It defines the characteristics of the enlightened yogi and the nature of the Absolute (Brahman), ending with a traditional benediction and a statement of the text's spiritual benefits.</meta>

# Page 71

Just as the **vital breath** <term>Prana; the life force</term> is completely exhausted and the mind dissolves, that state of **oneness of equal essence** <note>original: "samarasa-ekatvam," a state where individual consciousness and the divine merge seamlessly, like water poured into water</note> is called **Absorption** <term>Samadhi; the final stage of yoga where the practitioner becomes one with the object of meditation</term>.
->॥ 177 ॥<-

Through this direct experience, the senses and the activities of the mind [vanish]. When the individual soul attains **non-duality** <note>original: "advayatvam," the state of being "not two," where the distinction between the self and the universe disappears</note>, there is neither mind nor senses.
->॥ 178 ॥<-

The one joined in Yoga through Absorption perceives no smell, no taste, no form, no touch, nor sound; they know neither themselves nor the "other."
->॥ 179 ॥<-

Such a person cannot be pierced by any weapons or scriptures, cannot be harmed by any living beings, and is not afflicted by **Time** <note>original: "kala," which signifies both time and death</note> or by the weight of **past actions** <term>Karma; the moral law of cause and effect</term>.
->॥ 180 ॥<-

The yogi established in Absorption cannot be dominated by anyone, nor can they be grasped by the **science of spells** <note>original: "mantra-shastra," referring to the use of sacred sounds or ritual magic</note>.
->॥ 181 ॥<-

For one who is disciplined in food and recreation, disciplined in their efforts in work, and disciplined in sleep and wakefulness, Yoga becomes the **destroyer of all suffering** <note>This verse is a direct reference to the Bhagavad Gita (6.17), emphasizing that yoga is not just meditation but a balanced lifestyle</note>.
->॥ 182 ॥<-

Knowers of reality recognize the True Principle as being free from fear, without need of support, beyond the complexities of the material world, without a refuge, without disease, and without form.
->॥ 183 ॥<-

Knowers of the **Absolute** <term>Brahman; the ultimate, infinite, and unchanging reality</term> recognize the Absolute as pure, motionless, eternal, without qualities, and inactive; it is the vast space of consciousness and bliss.
->॥ 184 ॥<-

Beyond examples, freed from all attachments, and beyond the reach of the senses; the knowers of the Absolute recognize the Absolute as the space of consciousness and bliss.
->॥ 185 ॥<-

Just as milk poured into milk, clarified butter into clarified butter, or fire placed into fire becomes indistinguishable, the yogi who is **dissolved** <note>original: "lina," meaning merged or absorbed</note> into the Supreme reaches the state of imperishability.
->॥ 186 ॥<-

This book is the fire for the forest of worldly existence and the ladder leading to the path of liberation. It reveals the highest truth and is the secret of all secrets.
->॥ 187 ॥<-

Whoever reads or hears this even once with a focused mind becomes blessed on this earth—a vessel for both worldly enjoyment and final liberation.
->॥ 188 ॥<-

->॥ Thus ends the **Sun of Yoga** <note>original: "Yoga Martanda"</note> composed by the venerable **Lord Goraksha** <note>Gorakshanath, the legendary medieval master of Hatha Yoga</note>. ॥<-
->॥ May this be offered to the Eternal Shiva, who is with the Goddess. ॥<-

<vocab>Samadhi, Yoga, Brahman, Gorakshanath, Yoga Martanda, Mukti</vocab>

<summary>This final page defines Samadhi as the total merging of breath and mind into the Absolute, rendering the yogi immune to time, karma, and suffering. It concludes the Yoga Mārtāṇḍa with a praise of the text as a "ladder to liberation" and a dedication to Shiva.</summary>

--- Page 142 ---
<meta>This page marks the beginning of a new text, the "Gorakh Upanishad," following the conclusion of the "Yoga Mārtāṇḍa" on the previous page. It shifts from technical yogic instructions to a philosophical dialogue between the Great Adept Gorakshanath and the Goddess Vimala regarding the origins of the universe.</meta>

## ॥ Hail to Rama ॥

# ॥ The Gorakh Upanishad ॥

The Illustrious **Nath** <note>original: "Nath," referring to the lineage of masters in the Nath tradition</note> is Supreme Bliss, the Teacher of the World, the Stainless One, and the All-Pervading One. He is the ultimate goal of the Great Adepts. May our **greetings** <term>Adesh; the traditional salutation used by practitioners of the Nath sect</term> be unto Him.

**Now follows the introduction.**

At one time, the Great Goddess named **Vimala** became somewhat curious and perplexed. The illustrious and Great **Gorakshanath** questions her. His intent is to dispel her confusion and, out of compassion, to liberate all people by revealing the **Wisdom of Great Yoga** <term>Mahajoga Vidya; the supreme science of union</term>. Through his own mouth, the Illustrious Lord reveals this **Upanishad** <term>Upanishad; a sacred text conveying esoteric spiritual knowledge, literally "sitting down near" a teacher</term>.

The word **Go** refers to the senses; because He protects the inner self and all living beings through his indwelling presence, His name is **Gorakshanath** <note>original: "go" (senses) + "raksha" (protection)</note>. And because this text imparts the knowledge of Yoga, it is named an Upanishad. For this reason, the *Gorakshopanishad* is famous among the Great Adepts.

->Vimala spoke:<-

The meaning of her root question is: In that time when there was only the **Great Void** <term>Mahashunya; the primordial state of emptiness before creation</term>, and the five great elements <note>Space, Air, Fire, Water, and Earth</note> did not exist, and the Lord who is composed of those five elements did not exist, nor did the **individual soul** <term>Jiva; the living being or embodied spirit</term> or any other thing—at that time, who was the creator of this world?

The point of the question is this: Various kinds of creation exist, but the first creators are usually considered the Great Elements. By taking the pure essence of existence, they became the **Lord** <term>Ishwar; the personal God or Creator</term>, and by taking the clouded essence, they became the individual soul. Yet, these could not have been the original, direct creators. When these did not yet exist, there must have been some **indescribable substance** <note>original: "anirvachaniya padarth," something that cannot be defined by words or logic</note>. Who was that creator?

Upon this question, the Great Gorakshanath gives his answer.

->The Great Gorakshanath spoke:<-

"O Goddess, know that the primary creator is a certain primordial, eternal, blissful, and inconceivable entity, which is beyond both **form** <term>Sakar; having a physical or visible shape</term> and **formlessness** <term>Nirakar; without shape or attributes</term>. For if the creator were only formless, it would be a contradiction for Him to hold the desire to take on a form. If the creator were only a physical form, He would lack the quality of being all-pervading, which is also a contradiction. Therefore, the Creator is that **Indescribable Lord of Eternal Bliss** <note>original: "Sadananda Swarup"</note> who is free from the distinctions of both **duality and non-duality** <term>Dvaitadvaita; the philosophical debate over whether the individual soul and God are separate or one</term>. This is what will be explained in the following verses."

<summary>This page introduces the Gorakh Upanishad, establishing the etymology of Gorakshanath's name and beginning a philosophical dialogue where the Goddess Vimala asks about the nature of the Creator before the existence of the physical elements.</summary>
<keywords>Gorakh Upanishad, Gorakshanath, Vimala, Mahashunya, Creation, Duality, Non-duality, Nath Tradition, Senses, Elements</keywords>

--- Page 143 ---
<meta>This page continues the dialogue within the Gorakh Upanishad, transitioning from the Goddess Vimala’s question about the state before creation to a description of the Supreme Deity’s manifestation into various forms and powers.</meta>

To resolve the doubt regarding who the deity of this path is, it is said:

The deity is **Great Bliss**, who exists beyond **non-duality** <term>Advaita; the state of being one without a second</term>. Since He is beyond non-duality, He is naturally beyond **duality** <term>Dvaita; the perceived separation between self and God</term>. Know that in this manner, He is established as the Supreme Doer. This Creator first manifested His **Power of Will** <term>Iccha Shakti</term>. Afterward, He manifested the **Power of Knowledge** <term>Jnana Shakti</term>. Then, He manifested the **Power of Action** <term>Kriya Shakti</term>.

Following this, the **microcosm** <term>Pinda; the individual body</term> and the **macrocosm** <term>Brahmanda; the entire universe</term> were manifested. Within them, He became all-pervasive through His **unmanifest, attribute-less form** <term>Avyakta Nirguna; the divine in its pure, formless state</term>. He began to play in His manifest form of Bliss. Just as I [the Lord] became nine forms from one—you became **Satyanath** <note>Lord of Truth</note>, and then **Santoshnath** <note>Lord of Contentment</note>. Because He remains unattached to the varied qualities of this world, He is known as Santoshnath.

Next is **Kurmanath** <note>Lord of the Tortoise</note>, who is of the nature of Space and the Illustrious **Adinath** <note>the Primal Lord</note>. The word *Kurma* refers to the netherworld or the foundation below the earth; thus His name is Kurmanath. In the middle is **Sarvanath** <note>Lord of All</note>, the Lord of the earthly sphere. In this manner, the Seven Nathas came to be.

Then came the Illustrious **Matsyendranath** <note>Lord of the Fish</note>. For the sake of the world’s creation, He took on the beauty of **Maya** <term>creative illusion</term>, yet remained unattached through the practice of Yoga—observing all as the Self within every living being. His disciple is **Gorakshanath**. The syllable *Go* refers to the Word-Brahman <note>the sacred sound of creation</note>; the syllable *Ra* means He protects it; the syllable *Ksha* means He is free from decay. Thus, as the Imperishable Brahman, the Illustrious Gorakshanath became the fourth form, and in another sense, one of the nine forms.

Among them, how is the one **Nirantarnath** <note>the Eternal Lord</note> to be attained? The reason is explained now. Two paths have been manifested in the world: **Kula** and **Akula**. The *Kula* path is the **Path of Power** <note>Shakti</note>. The *Akula* path is the **Path of Consciousness** <note>Chaitanya</note> belonging to the Indivisible Lord. Within these, through elements of Tantra and Yoga, a small portion of the worldly expansion exists.

In this way, from the nature of the Lord who is free from both duality and non-duality, He became the **Non-dual Attribute-less Lord** for the purpose of worldly interaction. From Non-duality, He became the **Dual form of the Self as Bliss**. Within that, from Me as One, I became Nine Forms for the purpose of specific interaction. Now follows the description of those Nine Forms.

**Shri** signifies one endowed with indivisible splendor. **Guru** means the teacher of all. **Adi** refers to the first among these Nine Forms to be mentioned. In the word **Nath**, the syllable *Na* imparts the knowledge of the **Sound-Brahman** <term>Nada Brahman; the sacred vibration from which the universe arises</term>. The syllable *Th* refers to the one who has established the three worlds. Such is the nature of the Illustrious **Adinath**. Next is Matsyendranath. After him, his son...

<summary>This page describes how the supreme, non-dual divine manifests into the three creative powers (Will, Knowledge, and Action) and eventually into the nine forms of the Nath masters, including Adinath and Gorakshanath. It explains the etymology of "Gorakshanath" and "Nath" while defining the two spiritual paths of Kula (Power) and Akula (Consciousness).</summary>
<keywords>Nath lineage, Advaita, Dvaita, Shakti, Gorakshanath, Adinath, Kula, Akula, Nada Brahman, Nine Nathas</keywords>

--- Page 144 ---
<meta>This page continues the genealogy of the Nath Siddhas within the Gorakh Upanishad. Having previously established the manifestation of the first seven Naths, the text now details the specific attributes and symbolic meanings behind the names of Udaynath, Dandnath, Matsyanath, and Brahmanath, while explaining the transition from external religious rituals to internal yogic practice.</meta>

The disciple **Udaynath** revealed the science of Yoga from the Illustrious **Adinath** <note>the Primal Lord, Shiva</note>. Because the "rise" (*udaya*) of Yoga occurred through him and many Great Siddhas emerged, he became famous by the name **Udaynath** <note>Lord of the Rising</note>. Afterward, **Dandnath** used the teachings of that same Yoga to strike down ignorance. Because he is known as the staff (*danda*) of punishment for the God of Death (*Kala*) for all people, he became known as **Dandnath** <note>Lord of the Staff</note>.

Next, **Matsyanath** <note>Matsyendranath</note> shone with the Great Truth, having destroyed Time, which is composed of the nature of untruth and illusion (*Maya*). And because he knows **Brahmanath**, who is beyond all attributes (*nirguna*), he is the Primal Brahmin, the knower of the subtle Veda. 

Usually, a Brahmin is a reciter of the Vedas, such as the Rig, Yajur, and Sama; but for these Siddhas, there is what is called the **Subtle Veda**. The sacred syllable *Om* (*Pranava*) is that Veda; therefore, he is a "Knower of the Subtle." This is evidenced by the writings of the *Siddha Siddhanta Paddhati* <note>a core text of the Nath tradition</note>, which details the **Khechari Mudra** <term>the 'sky-walking' seal; a yogic practice of curling the tongue</term>, the internal Khechari, the external Veshari, and the Ear Mudra, all perfected through the power of the Seals.

Endless monasteries and temples belong to the union of Shiva and Shakti. The Nath is the "Desire of Shiva." The **sacred thread** (*yagnopavita*) <note>the physical thread worn by upper-caste Hindus</note> is here the thread of Shiva; that thread is the Self; that sacrifice is Yoga; and the sacred thread of this yogic sacrifice is the dark woolen cord. 

To walk in the state of the Absolute (*Brahman*) is true Celibacy (*Brahmacharya*); to gather inner peace is the Householder stage; to dwell in spirituality is the Forest-dweller stage; and the renunciation of all desires is the Sannyasa stage. By being called the "Primal Brahmin," he became the teacher of the four castes, and because all four stages of life are contained within him, he is known as the **Transcendent of Stages** (*Atyashrami*), the teacher even of the life-stages themselves. 

This has been specifically described in the *Lineage of Disciples*. The meaning is this: those beings who are still possessed by inconceivable worldly desires, even if they lack the sense of "me and mine," belong to the **Kula path** <term>the 'family' or manifest path associated with Shakti and physical lineage</term>. However, those whose inner selves have become entirely free of all desires come to the worship of Yoga; among all paths, this is the **Akula path** <term>the 'non-family' or unmanifest path associated with Shiva and pure consciousness</term>. 

Other scriptures teach through a web of words. My teaching is a "Sign" (*Sanketa*); that sign is the **Void** (*Shunya*), which is the Nath himself. To dispel the doubt of "Who is the deity in this path?", it is said: it is the **Lineage of Ishvara** [the Lord]. 

This lineage is of two kinds: the form of **Sound** (*Nada*) and the form of the **Drop** (*Bindu*); the Drop is the form of Sound. The disciple is the form of the Drop, and the son [spiritual successor] is the form of the Nath. They are created through the Sound-form of Power and the Drop-form of Sound. 

First, it is said to the disciple: the nature of the **Nine Naths** is the power in the form of the Drop. The Supreme Shiva, by the name of Ishvara, is my lineage. By that...

<summary>This page explains the symbolic origins of the names of key Nath Siddhas and reinterprets traditional Hindu social structures (castes and life-stages) as internal yogic states. It distinguishes between the Kula (manifest) and Akula (unmanifest/void) paths of spirituality.</summary>

<keywords>Udaynath, Dandnath, Matsyanath, Brahmanath, Siddha Siddhanta Paddhati, Kula and Akula, Nada and Bindu, Atyashrami, Subtle Veda, Shunya</keywords>

--- Page 145 ---
<meta>This page concludes the philosophical definitions of the Nath tradition started on the previous page. It transitions from the symbolic meaning of the "sacred thread" and "Brahmin" identity to the specific yogic practices (Ashtanga and Shadanga Yoga) and the nature of the Guru as the embodiment of bliss. It serves as a concluding exhortation for the text.</meta>

...[He] directs the manifestation of the universe. In the Path of Yoga <note>original: "Jog Mata"</note>, the Eightfold Path (**Ashtanga Yoga**) <term>the eight-limbed practice consisting of ethical restraints, observances, postures, breath control, sensory withdrawal, concentration, meditation, and absorption</term> is considered primary. Some also speak of the Sixfold Path (**Shadanga**) which is called "without lineage" (*Akula*); this is the Yoga of the Renunciant (**Avadhuta Yoga**) <term>the path of the "shaken-off" ones who have transcended social conventions and worldly attachments</term>. The essential practice within this Yoga path is the Eightfold Yoga.

In the beginning, the Earth was filled with the four classes: the **Brahmin** <note>priests/scholars</note>, **Kshatriya** <note>warriors/rulers</note>, **Vaishya** <note>merchants/farmers</note>, and **Shudra** <note>laborers</note>. Among these, the Brahmin class is held as primary. But who should truly be called a Brahmin? One who knows the **Nirguna Brahman** <term>the Absolute Reality without attributes or form</term> through the gateway of the **Saguna Brahman** <term>the Divine manifested with qualities and form</term>—he is a true Brahmin.

This Lord of Yogis (**Jogishwar**) is the Manifest Lord, the ultimate object of knowledge. He is the Instructor, the Lord whose very nature is the embodiment of Bliss (**Ananda**), and the form to be worshipped. He is the Guru who has transcended all social stages (**Atyashrami**) <note>literally "beyond the ashrams," referring to one who is no longer bound by the four stages of Hindu life</note>, the practitioner of the Renunciant's Yoga, and the qualified seeker of liberation (**Mumukshu**).

Within this book, a liberation (**Moksha**) is described that is beyond both bondage and liberation—a certain inexpressible (*anirvachaniya*) state of being. Therefore, whosoever reads this or causes it to be read shall receive infinite and inconceivable merit.

<summary>This page defines the true "Brahmin" as one who realizes the formless Absolute through manifested form and identifies the Avadhuta Yoga as the path to an inexpressible liberation beyond worldly categories.</summary>
<keywords>Ashtanga Yoga, Avadhuta, Brahmin, Saguna and Nirguna Brahman, Moksha, Jogishwar, Guru, liberation</keywords>
