<meta>This page follows the discussion of the true Brahmin and the Eightfold Yoga path. Having established the theoretical framework of the Nath tradition, the manuscript now presents two devotional songs (padas) attributed to the founding master, Matsyendranath. These songs shift the focus from systematic definitions to the experiential and emotional aspects of devotion, emphasizing internal realization over external ritual.</meta>

# The Verse of Matsyendranath

->[ Melody: Kalingda ]<-
<note>Kalingda is a classical musical framework (raga) traditionally performed in the early morning, often evoking a mood of deep yearning or devotion.</note>

I have developed a hunger for your Name. To me, it is dear, so dear—the Name of the Lord <note>original: "Bhagwant"</note>, my beloved. **Refrain**

Knowing that essence, I meet it with color; how wonderful is the worship of the Lord, my beloved. (1)
If He dwells within all seventy-two pilgrimage sites <note>original: "satrahi," likely referring to the numerous holy places or the internal energy centers</note>, then why does anyone perform external bathing, my beloved? (2)
They go about bathing to become pure; what kind of comparison is this to the truth, my beloved? (3)
In the pilgrimage of the body (**Kaya Teerth**), wisdom is the greatest thing; through spiritual practice, the vision (**Darshan**) <term>the auspicious sight of a deity or holy person</term> is attained, my beloved. (4)
Matsyendra says: this is the comparison—there is none other than the Lord, my beloved. (5)

->[ Melody: Ghanakshari ]<-
<note>Ghanakshari is a rhythmic poetic meter often used for bold, aphoristic, or spiritual declarations.</note>

The bird [of the soul] will fly away; the cry of time has come. As long as a man acts out of selfishness, no task of his is truly fulfilled. **Refrain**

The fish longs for the water, the peacock longs for the clouds, and the servant longs for God <note>original: "Ram"</note> just as the **Chakor bird** <note>a literary bird in Indian poetry said to live only on moonbeams</note> gazes at the moon. (1)
This selfish soul will not let go of its selfishness. But when the Lord (**Govinda**) showed mercy, my mind finally came to understand. (2)
Know him to be a true **Yogi** <term>a practitioner of yoga who seeks spiritual liberation</term> who remains detached from the world. He attains the Immaculate Essence (**Tat Niranjan**) <term>the pure, "un-tinted" reality of the divine</term>; so says Matsyendra Nath. (2) <note>The numbering repeats in the original manuscript.</note>

->|| Conclusion ||<-

<summary>This page contains two devotional hymns attributed to Matsyendranath, emphasizing that true pilgrimage is internal and that spiritual liberation comes through detachment and devotion to the divine name.</summary>
<keywords>Matsyendranath, Nath Sampradaya, internal pilgrimage, Kaya Teerth, Raga Kalingda, Raga Ghanakshari, detachment, Niranjan, spiritual hunger</keywords>

--- Page 147 ---
<meta>Following the devotional verses of Matsyendranath, this page transitions to the teachings of King Bharthari, one of the most famous disciples in the Nath tradition. While the previous page focused on the emotional longing for the Divine, these verses (Sabadi) emphasize the renunciation of worldly power and the paradoxical nature of internal yogic experience.</meta>

# The Verses of Bharthari Ji

Ego has worn away the earth. The brave have turned to dust. 
Yogi Bharthari speaks the ultimate truth: old age <note>original: "jura"</note> is the enemy of the physical body. (1)

The suffering weep and the happy laugh. The passionate woman engages in play. 
The deer fight and the wild boars flee. King Bharthari speaks the ultimate truth. (2)

The king is suffering and the subjects are suffering. The priest and the merchant are suffering. 
King Bharthari is happy, for he has recognized the Word <term>the spiritual vibration or 'Sabda' transmitted by a teacher</term> of the Guru. (3)

Why are you so full of pride, milkmaid? Look at your graying hair. 
I had sixteen hundred queens in my power; yet I left my household behind. (4)

In the forest, the trees were once laden with fruit; great elephants moved among them. 
I, the King, have seen those very trees wither away before my eyes. (5)

He who climbs will surely fall. Ponder the essence of why one might not fall. 
The wealthy people will eventually be diminished; but what of yours can be diminished, Bharthari the beggar? (6)

King Bharthari was lost in delusion; then he saw the fire below and the stove above <note>This refers to 'Sandhya Bhasha' or twilight language, describing the reversal of energy in Hatha Yoga where the internal fire (tapas) is redirected upward.</note>. 
By joining these two in skillful union, King Bharthari lives through the four ages <note>the Yugas, or the entire cycle of cosmic time</note>. (7)

Oh Ascetic! The lotus blooms without water, and the bee finds it without the lotus; the cuckoo sings without a throat. 
There is a forest without deer, a hunter without a deer; a single arrow pierces five people <note>The "five people" are the five senses, which are brought under control by a single point of meditative focus.</note>. (8)

Lock the shutters of the nine gates <note>The nine openings of the human body (eyes, ears, etc.) through which energy is normally lost.</note>; in the tenth gate <term>the 'Dasam Dwar' at the crown of the head</term>, Shiva holds his path. 
Two hundred thousand moons and one hundred thousand suns shine there; the deer of the soul is pierced in the station of the sky <note>The "sky" or 'Gagan' refers to the state of pure consciousness or the void (Sunya) reached in deep meditation.</note>. (9)

The soul, once pierced, does not leave the Master's side. Thus speaks Bharthari, the servant of Gorakh. 
If the body is without desire but the mind still dwells on illusion, then shaving the head is merely a mockery of the body. (10)

<summary>A collection of ten verses attributed to King Bharthari, emphasizing the inevitability of aging, the suffering inherent in worldly status, and the paradoxical internal experiences of Sahaja Yoga.</summary>
<keywords>Bharthari, Sabadi, Gorakhnath, Renunciation, Ninth Gate, Tenth Gate, Yoga, Twilight Language</keywords>

--- Page 148 ---
<meta>Continuing from the previous page, these verses continue the "Sabadi" (spoken wisdom) of King Bharthari. Having established the futility of worldly status, he now describes the internal state of a Yogi who has mastered his senses and the relationship between the physical body and the Formless Divine.</meta>

# The Wisdom of King Bharthari

The mind is detached, yet it tastes the essence of divine love. 
King Bharthari says: such a man is a true Yogi. 
The five elements <note>original: "panch pada," referring to the five senses or elements</note> are exceedingly powerful; 
Within the territory of the Mind-King, the elephant of ego roars. (11)

When the harsh wave of desire <note>original: "kandrap," referring to Kamadeva, the god of love</note> arises, 
The brave fight on while the cowards flee. 
A renunciant Yogi should not indulge in worldly passion; 
He must keep the mind and its desires under close guard. (12)

The seat of the Perfected One <term>Siddha</term> is inaccessible and beyond the senses. 
He has shattered all hope and craving. 
When hope is shattered and worldly desires are broken, 
The mind and the breath <note>original: "pavana"</note> become his royal ministers. (13)

King Bharthari speaks the ultimate truth: then the mind becomes steady. 
When a kingdom is lost, one grieves for the power; 
When the physician is gone, the patient suffers. (14)

When beauty fades, the passionate woman grieves; 
When the vital seed <term>bindu, the yogic essence of life-force</term> is lost, the Yogi grieves. 
In the ultimate state, there is no seed, no sprout, 
No form, no distinguishing marks, and no ego. (15)

In that state where "rising" and "setting" <note>metaphors for birth and death, or the passage of time</note> cannot be described, 
There Bharthari remains absorbed in contemplation. 
He has no fear of death, 
Nor does he cling to the hope of living. (16)

King Bharthari speaks the ultimate truth: 
Our delight and play are found in the natural, effortless state <term>Sahaja</term>. (17)

The patched garment <note>original: "kantha," the traditional rag-cloak of a Yogi</note> of this body is vast in its complexity; 
Speak only of the Formless <term>Niranjan</term> who remains beyond all shapes. 
Vikram <note>King Vikramaditya, the legendary brother of Bharthari</note> and the fifty-two heroes ask: 
"Who is the one who remains steady in this world?" (18)

Listen, O Vikram, to the knowledge of the Absolute <term>Brahm Gayan</term>. 
Meditate on that which is entirely separate from the physical body. 
Where rising and setting cannot be described, 
There Bharthari remains absorbed. (19)

With the fire in front and the sun behind, 
He meditates beneath the tree of focused awareness. 
He speaks of the Formless and remains detached, 
Yet even now, the lingering scent of hope does not leave the breath. (20)

Illusion <term>Maya</term> is an enemy that fosters pride. 
Neither cattle, nor youth, nor the king’s wealth will last. 
Gold, women, and the pleasures of the senses— 
Bharthari says these lead to the destruction of the body. (21)

If you wish to achieve this, begin by mastering the single breath—

->Abandon all impurities.<-

**Vocabulary:**
**Bharthari:** The King-Yogi; **Vikram:** His brother, King Vikramaditya; **Jogi:** An ascetic/Yogi; **Niranjan:** The "Spotless" or Formless Divine; **Vairagi:** A renunciant; **Surti:** Deep meditative awareness; **Man:** The mind/psyche; **Khandi:** To break or shatter; **Kanak:** Gold; **Kamani:** A beautiful or passionate woman.

<summary>King Bharthari instructs his brother Vikram on the nature of renunciation, explaining that true stability is found not in kingdoms, but in the "Sahaja" state where birth and death no longer apply. He emphasizes the need to control the "elephant" of the mind and the "wave" of desire to reach the Formless (Niranjan).</summary>
<keywords>Bharthari, Vikramaditya, renunciation, Sahaja, Niranjan, breath control, detachment, Nath tradition, Maya, ego</keywords>

--- Page 149 ---
<meta>Continuing from the previous page, King Bharthari further explores the distinction between the true ascetic path and the distractions of worldly attachment. Having discussed the internal state of the mind, he now turns to the necessity of serving the Formless Divine and warns against the "foolish yogis" who remain trapped in physical desires despite their outward renunciation.</meta>

# The Wisdom of King Bharthari

To dwell is to remain in the shadow of the Unspoken Word <note>original: "nih sabda," referring to the Anahata Nada or the silence of the absolute</note>—
->To serve is to serve the Unstained and Formless Divine. (22)<-

A girl of low lineage, naked and young; her deer-like beauty appears terrifying <note>to the spiritual seeker, worldly beauty is often described as a snare or a frightening distraction from the eternal</note>. The lotus-essence shines, but whose braid is it? From where has this movement of shame and sin come? (23)

You do not account for wood, nor do you account for stone. You do not account for the living creatures of the water. Even now, why is your body acting like a prideful lion? You are not yet a renowned Master of Yoga <term>Jogeshwara</term>. (24)

The Yogi is troubled by anxiety and mental distractions; in my view, he is consumed by them. How can one find Yoga through these worldly "yokes" <note>a play on the word "Yoga" (union) and "Jug" (age/attachment)</note>? For this reason, Yoga is not attained. (25)

This is not Yoga, and he is no Yogi; he is merely a consumer of all things. Lacking the Guru’s wisdom, he wanders as a "fool-yogi" <term>muth-jogi</term>. Blessed is the daughter of a noble family, and blessed is the faithful wife. (26)

I am the Goddess of this land, and I give instruction to the foolish yogis. For the forest-dweller, the bed is of grass, and the sky above is his only canopy. (27)

Bharthari’s mind is unwavering. Let Indra, the King of Heaven, pour down his torrential rains. He who has a mother has devotion; he who drinks the divine essence dies to the world <note>original: "mridanta," implying the death of the ego</note>. (28)

O, you wicked and immoral people! Why do you not become renunciants <term>vairagi</term>? (29)

He who possesses Illusion <term>maya</term> also possesses a wife. Why have you not abandoned the poison of the body? O, you wicked people! You have abandoned the True Essence <note>original: "nij tat"</note> and attached your hearts to mere flesh and blood. (30)

The art of lust has climbed into the mind. On the bed of the senses lies the meat of desire. The loss of the vital seed <note>original: "biraj," referring to bindu or life-force</note> is a spiritual murder <note>original: "brahma-hatya," literally the sin of killing a Brahmin, used here to describe the gravity of losing spiritual energy</note>. O, you wicked people! Where has your purity gone? (31)

Entering the female womb, the vital seed is lost, and millions of acts of worship are destroyed. Without knowledge, fasting, bathing, and penance are shattered; there is no true austerity. Every step is a spiritual murder. (32)

Gorakh <note>referring to Gorakhnath, the great master of the Natha tradition</note> says: The path is a difficult, narrow bridge. On one side stands the tigress <note>a common metaphor for worldly temptation or Maya</note>, and on the other side stands the Master <term>Natha</term>. (33)

<summary>This page contains verses attributed to King Bharthari and Gorakhnath, emphasizing the necessity of celibacy and mental discipline while critiquing "foolish yogis" who fail to master their desires.</summary>
<keywords>Niranjana, Nirakara, Vairagi, Gorakhnath, Bindu, Brahma-hatya, Renunciation, Asceticism, Maya</keywords>

--- Page 150 ---
<meta>Continuing from page 79, King Bharthari (a legendary king turned ascetic) continues his discourse on the nature of renunciation. In the previous section, he addressed the distractions of the senses; here, he lists the specific worldly attachments he has discarded and reflects on the impermanence of all things—including kings, scholars, and the celestial bodies themselves—contrasting them with the eternal nature of the soul attained through Yoga.</meta>

Skin <note>referring to physical beauty or attachment to the body</note>, wealth, and pride—these three things must be abandoned.
So speaks King Bharthari in truth. By distancing himself from these, he became a true renunciant <term>vairagi</term>. (34)

Lust for women, theft, and adultery—these three things must be abandoned.
Truly, truly speaks King Bharthari. By distancing himself from these, he became a true renunciant <term>vairagi</term>. (35)

Do not bind yourself with attachment <note>original: "moha," the delusion of worldly ties</note>; instead, awaken the mind. Contemplate the wisdom that dispels falsehood.
Work with the five elements <note>or the five senses</note>, but remain devoted to the One; thus shall you cross over the ocean of existence. (36)

He abandoned his elephants, his horses, his villages, and his forts. In his mind, there is no longer either joy or sorrow.
Therefore, King Bharthari has become stilled and steady in the ultimate state. (37)

Living in this false world only causes diseases to increase manifold.
Therefore, Bharthari departed from it, for the path of spiritual union <term>yoga</term> tasted sweet to him. (38)

The sky shall pass away, the celestial spheres shall pass away; the moon and the sun shall pass away.
But King Bharthari shall not pass away, for he is perfected by the Guru’s Sacred Word <note>original: "sabad," the divine sound or instruction of the master</note>. (39)

The kings who ruled have gone; the common folk who made merry have gone.
The women who wore many beautiful forms have gone; even Brahma <note>the creator god</note>, who read the Vedas, has gone. (40)

The trees that bore sweet fruit have gone; the parrots that chirped have gone.
Truly, truly speaks King Bharthari: I, too, would have passed away had I not become a Yogi. (41)

Those men who enjoyed worldly pleasures are no more; those women who delighted in their beauty are no more.
The elderly, who endured life from its very roots, are no more; even the wells that once held the nectar-water have vanished. (42)

King Bharthari does not pass away, for he is absorbed in the knowledge <term>gyan</term> received through the Guru’s grace <term>guruprasada</term>.
Even if all the great mountains and Mount Sumeru <note>the golden mountain at the center of the universe</note> were to fall, he has cast his soul into that which is equal to them in steadiness. (43)

Even though the spiritual practice is full of hardship, the Protector is the Stainless One <term>Niranjan</term>.
Though he were to cast his soul into the fire in the midst of the sea, he would remain steady.
Even though the practice is difficult, the Protector is the Stainless One. (44)

<summary>King Bharthari enumerates the worldly vices he has renounced and reflects on the universal inevitability of death for all beings, asserting that only the wisdom of Yoga and the Guru's grace offer immortality. He concludes by identifying the "Stainless One" (Niranjan) as the ultimate protector of the ascetic through the hardships of the path.</summary>

<keywords>King Bharthari, renunciation, Vairagi, impermanence, Yoga, Guru's grace, Niranjan, Mount Sumeru, immortality, asceticism</keywords>

--- Page 151 ---
<meta>Continuing from page 80, where King Bharthari reflected on the departure of past rulers and the transient nature of worldly existence, he now concludes his teachings. On this final page of the section, he describes his new "royal court" in the wilderness—where the elements of nature replace his former palace luxuries—and speaks of the immortal state he has achieved through spiritual steadying.</meta>

As long as the earth remains, so long shall Bharthari remain; though the earth itself shall eventually pass away, Bharthari shall not depart <note>original: "n gatvā," meaning he has achieved a state beyond death and rebirth</note>.
The trees are my royal canopy, and the grass is my bed. The wind fanning me serves as my whisk <note>original: "chamalicha," a fly-whisk used for royalty</note>.
In the courtyard of my house, the moon is my lamp. Thus, my kingly mind has become steady and unmoving. (45)

The peacocks dance for me and the cuckoo sings. The black bees play the flute of Shiva <note>original: "Rudra Beni," a stringed instrument or flute associated with the god Shiva</note>.
From the four directions, the maiden-like winds blow to cool me. In this way, Bharthari is immersed in the play of divine wisdom <term>gyan</term>. (46)

A stone is my throne, and garments of bark are my clothes. The sky is the royal umbrella that graces my head.
From the four directions, the maiden-like winds blow, while the jewel of wisdom charms my mind. (47)

Wandering through many lands, he is ever the renunciant <term>vairagi</term>. The scholars are renunciants through their scriptures;
the young maidens and the widows are renunciants in their own states. In this way, King Bharthari is a renunciant through spiritual wisdom. (48)

The wise one is he who is deep and profound, like a vessel filled with still, unmoving water.
Bharthari speaks of the Form of Truth: when the inner essence is contemplated, there is neither mark nor form <note>referring to the "Nirguna" or formless aspect of the Divine which has no physical attributes</note>. (49)

->॥ Thus the Sacred Verses <term>sabadi</term> of the venerable Bharthari are complete ॥<-

<summary>King Bharthari describes his ascetic life in nature as a replacement for his lost kingdom and concludes that true immortality is found in the formless state of spiritual wisdom.</summary>
<keywords>Bharthari, Vairagi, Renunciation, Gyan, Nature metaphors, Immortality, Sabadi, Yoga</keywords>

--- Page 152 ---
<meta>Following the conclusion of King Bharthari’s teachings on the previous page, where he described the steadying of the mind through the natural elements, this page transitions to the "Shabadis" (sacred utterances) of Charpatji. Charpatji is one of the renowned Nine Masters (Navnaths) of the Nath tradition, known for his sharp, satirical critiques of hypocrisy and outward displays of religiosity that mask a lack of inner discipline.</meta>

## ## Now Begin the Utterances of Charpatji

The body is a tree and the mind is a monkey. It wanders among the branches and leaves every day.
It laments, it burns with desire, and it rushes toward the ten directions <note>original: "dahdis," referring to the cardinal points, their intermediates, up, and down</note>. For this reason, brother, no one becomes a perfected master <term>Siddha</term>. (1)

With a loose loincloth and a fragmented mind, he wanders. He casts his eyes into every house he passes.
The body wastes away and his speech lacks truth <note>original: "baacha phurai," referring to the spiritual power where one's words become manifest</note>. For this reason, the fool withers and dies again and again. (2)

The mind is restless, the breath is restless, and restless is the flow of the wind.
Within this body, all three are restless. How then will you guard the gate of this leaking physical form? <note>refers to the "nine gates" or openings of the body through which vital energy is lost</note> (3)

How can one be a Master <term>Nath</term> if they cannot master themselves? They walk accompanied by five disciples <note>a metaphor for the five senses</note>.
They beg for alms and eat until they are full. Where are those "Masters"? They simply die over and over. (4)

Wearing the large earrings <term>darshan</term>, he calls himself a Master. From his mouth, he speaks clever words.
But just as a termite <note>original: "ghun"</note> consumes green wood, he is hollowed out from the branches to the roots. (5)

With a sectarian mark on the forehead and the sounding of a drum, he speaks with a sweet tongue.
Charpat says, "Listen, Naga Arjun <note>referring to a disciple or the famous alchemist-yogi associated with the tradition</note>, these are merely the signs of worldly people." (6)

They appear colorful and beautiful to the eye, like a counterfeit coin plated in gold.
Charpat says, "Listen, O people! There is outward behavior, but there is no union <term>yog</term> with the Divine." (7)

One wears white robes, another wears the robe of discipline. One wears silk, another has matted hair.
One abandons the path to follow the mind’s crooked ways. Charpat says, "These are all just performers for the sake of the belly." (8)

Wearing a red patched cloak and making a great noise, with sandals on the feet, he speaks boastfully.
They eat, they drink, and they indulge in pleasures. Charpat says, "They have ruined the path of Yoga." (9)

Wearing rings and bracelets on the hands, accompanied by fallen female yogis <note>original: "nakti buchi," literally 'nose-cut and ear-cut,' implying social or moral outcasts</note>.
There is the jingle of ornaments as they rise and sit. They could not abandon the snare of worldly illusion <term>Maya</term>. (10)

<summary>This page contains a series of verses by the Nath yogi Charpatji, critiquing the hypocrisy of false ascetics who focus on outward symbols like robes and earrings while failing to control their restless minds and senses.</summary>
<keywords>Charpatji, Nath Sampradaya, asceticism, hypocrisy, Maya, Naga Arjun, mind control, spiritual discipline</keywords>

--- Page 153 ---
<meta>The text continues the "Shabadis" (sacred utterances) of the Master Charpatji. While the previous page critiqued the restlessness of the mind and the vanity of religious attire, this page focuses on the "Janjal" (worldly snares) and provides a stark, visceral critique of the human body's impurity to discourage lust and hypocrisy among practitioners of Yoga.</meta>

Snares in front, snares behind. Why do you set up these traps? 
Having renounced such entanglements to become a yogi, why do you set the snare once again? (11)

With dogs and beggars as his companions, he speaks sweet, charming words to a young woman. 
Begging by day and indulging in pleasures by night—Charpat says such a man ruins the path of Yoga <term>Jog</term>. (12)

Fouler than waste or a pot of filth, the bones break as he lies down. 
He has not conquered that four-finger span <note>original: "aangulchyari," likely a reference to the genitals or the base of the spine where vital energy is lost</note>; Charpat says strike such a head. (13)

Walls of water, pillars of air—beholding this temple, one is struck with wonder. 
Yet inside and out, it is nothing but foul odors; why are you so blind, you fool? (14)

The fixed gaze of the eyes, the bridge of the nose, the covering of skin, and the cloth of beauty. 
Where sweat and filth reside, the consciousness sleeps. It is a bag for food, a pot of hell. (15)

The mind dwells where there is a lump of flesh; the gate of creation is a brush of hair. 
Where foul smells are pondered by the four <note>original: "char vichari," likely referring to the four inner faculties: mind, intellect, ego, and consciousness</note>, Charpat goes forth, bowing to the Mother <note>original: "mat juhari," an ironic or humble salutation to the physical nature of birth</note>. (16)

A bag of skin, a needle of skin—for the love of this, the whole world has died. 
Gods, celestial musicians <term>Gandharvas</term>, and humans alike; only the one who remains conscious through the Guru’s word <term>Gurumukh</term> is saved. (17)

Hollow, hollow is their talk of wisdom! They beat their skin and claim to meditate. 
They provoke the perfected masters <term>Siddhas</term>; Charpat says these are the hollow debaters of the Dark Age <term>Kaliyuga</term>. (18)

The perfected ones <term>Siddhas</term> say those who indulge in the flesh <note>original: "bhugtain bhag," referring to carnal pleasure</note> have black faces and yellow feet. 
They beat their skin and claim to meditate, but what wisdom is there in such a state? (19)

Charpat says: Listen, O Renunciant <term>Avadhuta</term>! Do not keep company with a tempting woman. 
She dries up the vital seed <term>Bindu</term> and the nine channels <term>Nadis</term>; day by day the body wastes away. (20)

With effort, the vital essence is preserved. Do not inflict a wound upon yourself by gazing at the flesh. 
One's life span increases by millions of years; thus truly, truly speaks the venerable Master Charpat. (21)

<summary>In these verses, Master Charpatji uses visceral and often harsh imagery to deconstruct the beauty of the physical body, warning yogis that lust and hypocrisy dry up spiritual energy and lead to rebirth.</summary>
<keywords>Charpatji, Nath tradition, Avadhuta, Kaliyuga, Bindu, renunciation, anatomy, hypocrisy, spiritual discipline</keywords>

--- Page 154 ---
<meta>This page continues the "Shabadis" (sacred utterances) of the Master Charpatji. While the previous page provided a visceral critique of the physical body's impurities to discourage worldly attachment, this page shifts toward the specific practices of Hatha Yoga and the internal discipline required to achieve immortality and spiritual perfection.</meta>

Charpat wears the patched garment of the mind and the tattered cloth of the spirit. He wears the skin of consciousness.
O seeker <term>Avdhut</term> <note>a wandering ascetic who has "shaken off" all worldly attachments</note>, act in such a way that you do not have to face death again. (22)

Make the mind firm and the consciousness steady. Daily cleanse the body and the breath <note>original: "pavan," literally 'wind', referring to the vital breath or Prana</note>.
When the body becomes as still as the unmoving, the swan-soul <note>original: "hansa," the spirit or vital breath</note> does not fly away; it is secured in the bond. (23)

O seeker, place a lock upon the root-gate <note>a reference to the "Muladhara" or the base of the spine, a key point in Hatha Yoga</note>. Push the breath toward the summit.
Then old age is reversed and disease is shattered; Charpat says, blessed, blessed is such a practice of Yoga <term>Jog</term>. (24)

Bind the connections and close off the objects of the senses. Place the Sun below and the Moon above <note>a yogic metaphor for reversing the internal energy flow to preserve the 'nectar' of immortality</note>.
Day and night, Charpat drinks this nectar; the oil in his lamp never runs dry. (25)

If the noble yogi <note>original: "raval," a title for a high-ranking yogi or prince</note> is so wise, why has he not bound the screen?
Twelve fingers’ breadth has entered, and sixteen fingers’ breadth is torn <note>these measurements refer to the length of the breath exiting the body; the yogi seeks to shorten the exhalation to preserve life force</note>. (26)

The breath is his patched garment and the fire is his dwelling; no one can come near such a mind.
The mind is the teacher of secret wisdom; Charpat says, blessed is this seeker <term>Avdhut</term>. (27)

Fearless and without doubt is the knower of reality <term>Tattvavetta</term>. Having controlled the mind and conquered the senses <term>Indriyajit</term>,
He is steeped in the knowledge of the white crystal-gem. Charpat says, this is the doctrine of the perfected ones <term>Siddha mata</term>. (28)

Conquer sleep within the crown of the head. Even in dreams, let the vital drop <note>original: "bind," referring to Bindu or the essence of life</note> not fall.
Then the body does not perish, nor does disease seize it. Charpat says, this is the Yoga of the Perfected Ones <term>Siddha Jog</term>. (29)

The masters Bharthari <term>Bharthar</term>, Charpat, and Gopichand are bound in perfect, supreme bliss <term>Pramanand</term>.
They abandoned the pleasures of fine food and luxury; they realized the Self and mastered Yoga. (30)

Both the copper vessel and the gourd have "died" <note>meaning they have undergone a transformation; the "death" of the gourd refers to it being dried and hollowed for a yogi's use</note>. Both the King and the Yogi are held in high esteem.
Yet, the copper sinks while the gourd floats; the Yogi lives while the King dies <note>the hollow gourd represents the detached yogi who stays afloat on the ocean of worldly existence</note>. (31)

A bright patched garment is ruined by the scent of worldly desire; he does not let a woman’s body come near him.
He keeps the five senses firm; Charpat calls him a Lord of Yogis. (32)

With alms in his hand and a dwelling beneath a tree, he does not let a woman’s body come near him.
Whether in the wild forest or the cremation ground, Charpat says, he is the true seeker <term>Avdhut</term>. (33)

<summary>This page outlines the esoteric yogic practices of the Nath tradition, focusing on breath control, the conservation of vital energy, and the attainment of a "deathless" state through detachment and sensory control. It cites legendary masters like Bharthari and Gopichand as exemplars of this path.</summary>
<keywords>Hatha Yoga, Charpatnath, breath control, detachment, immortality, Avdhut, Siddha, Bharthari, Gopichand, Bindu preservation</keywords>

--- Page 155 ---
<meta>This page continues the "Shabadis" (sacred utterances) of the Master Charpat Nath. While the previous page focused on the internal mechanics of Hatha Yoga—breath control, the stabilization of the 'Sun' and 'Moon' energies, and the pursuit of the nectar of immortality—this page pivots toward a sharp social and ethical critique. Charpatji here contrasts the true, internal transformation of a yogi with the superficial "hypocrisy" of those who merely wear the clothes or shave their heads without mastering their minds.</meta>

One assumes a certain form and wears the attire of mountain caves. Two such people do not let their bodies even come near one another <note>This likely refers to the extreme isolation or the avoidance of physical duality/company in certain ascetic practices</note>.
The physical form <term>Kaya</term> transforms and the diseases of the vessel vanish; Charpat says, blessed is such a practice of Yoga <term>Jog</term>. (34)

Charpat has kept himself detached from the patched garment <term>Kantha</term> <note>the traditional ragged cloak of a Nath yogi</note>. His path <term>Panth</term> is beyond both merit and sin.
His mouth is free from the desire for taste or tastelessness; he finds joy wherever the shaven-headed seekers <note>original: "mundai mund," referring to the community of monks</note> go. (35)

One shaves the hair, but does not shave the mind; what kind of instruction is it to merely shave the hair?
Unless the pride of a man’s mind is shorn, it is not true renunciation. Charpat speaks the essential knowledge <term>Tattagyan</term>. (36)

There is much pushing and shoving, fist-fighting, and the loud noise of the marketplace.
Such a person slanders their own Guru while praising the Gurus of others; they are merely quarrelsome and full of empty chatter. (37)

I have received the beggar’s bag and the begging bowl; I have understood the secret of the path.
I go out empty and return full <note>A metaphor for the spiritual fulfillment gained through the humble act of begging</note>. What more can the Divine Teacher do? (38)

Beg for alms <term>Bhikshya</term> with a natural, effortless disposition <note>original: "Sahaj Subhai," a state of spontaneous spiritual ease</note>, and engage in devotional song in the evening.
O seeker <term>Avdhut</term>, sit with your posture <term>Asan</term> firm; letting the mind and mouth wander is a kind of death. (39)

They come in vain and they go in vain; they speak in vain and they receive in vain.
They sit in vain and engage in disputes; Charpat says these are all nothing but worldly afflictions. (40)

With a water-pot in the left hand and a staff in the right, you worship a circle of shaven-headed hypocrisy.
You sit in the presence of women <note>original: "rand," a derogatory term here implying the yogi has abandoned celibacy for worldly company</note>; Charpat says all of this is mere hypocrisy <term>Pakhand</term>. (41)

One took the outward garb of a monk but did not find the inner secret. They left their home but could not abandon the illusion <note>original: "Maya," the deceptive nature of the material world</note>.
Forgetting the Lord <term>Nath</term>, they looked upon the world without the fear of God; they simply put on a costume and went to sleep contentedly. (42)

I have no wife and no religious vows to maintain; I do not chase after the spells of youth.
I have no son and no unwed daughter; therefore, sleep is dear to Charpat <note>meaning he sleeps soundly because he is free from the anxieties of a householder's responsibilities</note>. (43)

Rising each day, they make their rounds from house to house, yet they never turn inward to look upon the Soul.
Their begging bowl is filled and their belly is bloated; they simply beg for alms and swallow them down. (44)

<summary>This page contains verses by the Nath yogi Charpat, criticizing superficial religious practices like head-shaving and wearing ascetic robes while remaining mentally attached to ego and worldly desires. He emphasizes that true yoga requires internal discipline, "essential knowledge," and a mind free from hypocrisy.</summary>
<keywords>Charpat Nath, Renunciation, Hypocrisy, Shaven head, Maya, Avdhut, Alms, Internal vs External practice</keywords>

--- Page 156 ---
<meta>This page continues the "Shabadis" (sacred utterances) of Charpat Nath. While the previous page touched upon the physical transformation and detachment of a true seeker, this section intensifies the critique of "hypocritical" yogis—those who adopt the external symbols of the Nath tradition (earrings, ashes, cloaks) but remain tethered to worldly desires, ego, and sensory pleasures.</meta>

### Page 86

One found a morsel of food but did not keep to the truth. Such a person is like a city dog. 
He has no news of the secret of Yoga <term>Jog</term>; he is simply ruined by piercing his ears <note>The Kanphata ("split-ear") yogis wear large rings as a mark of initiation, but Charpat suggests that without inner discipline, the physical act is meaningless</note>. (45)

"I will not leave the path <term>Ai</term> <note>The 'Ai Panth' is one of the twelve traditional sects of the Nath yogis</note>, nor will I go out to seek it. That is why my name is Charpat." 
One must leave the attachment to the path and leave the desire to acquire it. Master Gorakh says: "O son, truth is found by reflecting on one’s own conduct." (46)

Do not let the mind wander... day and night, keep the vessel of the body deep and grave. 
Listen to the discipline of the senses and practice much forgiveness; Charpat bows to such a perfected ascetic <term>Avadhut</term>. (47)

A laughing yogi, a fighting bull, a man of ill-character, a prostitute's pulse, a shy poet, and a shameless woman—Charpat says, strike these on the head <note>This verse lists various social and spiritual contradictions where the behavior is opposite to the expected nature of the person</note>. (48)

Earrings <term>Mudra</term> in the ears and prayer beads <term>Rudraksha</term> around the neck, yet he begs again and again for the "fly's produce" <note>likely referring to honey or sweets, indicating gluttony</note>. 
Charpat says: "Listen! If there is true character, his conduct should be as pure as water." (49)

He asks for new things, he asks for raw things, he asks for the cotton of the dead. 
Charpat says: "Listen, O ascetic <term>Avadhut</term>, true home-life is found without conflict <note>original: "bin radi gharvasa"</note>." (50)

Leather shoes on the feet and a cap on the head; a fine robe on the body but charcoal in the mind. 
Seeing the illusion of the world <term>Maya</term>, he expands his reach. Charpat says: such a man dies without ever finding the path. (51)

He wears a tiny loincloth but enjoys the taste of betel leaves; he visits holy pilgrimages only to gather donations. 
He practices medicine to revive the sick; Charpat says: such a one is a ruined yogi <note>original: "viguta jogi"; implying that a yogi should seek spiritual liberation, not a career as a physician for profit</note>. (52)

Matted hair like a forest, limbs covered in ashes <term>Chhar</term>, and a thick patched cloak <term>Kantha</term>—let me tell you of them in detail. 
With strange speech and a healthy body, these travelers of the road take many deceptive forms. (53)

He sets his heart on wine and meat; he sings songs devoid of any true knowledge <term>Gyana</term>. 
This nightly indulgence in worldly pleasure, Charpat says, leads to the destruction of the body <note>original: "kanth-vinas," literally the destruction of the cloak or the throat/body</note>. (54)

I am weary of mere talk and speech; if you can bind anything, then bind the "wind" <note>original: "vav" or "pavan," referring to the breath or life-force (Prana)</note>. 
Charpat says: the breath is the string; while the "donkey" <note>a metaphor for the ignorant person or the ego</note> was braying, the thief came and stole the treasure. (55)

<summary>In this series of verses, Charpat Nath fiercely critiques the hypocrisy of ascetics who prioritize outward displays—such as earrings, ashes, and specific garments—over internal purity and breath control. He warns that true spiritual life is lost when a seeker indulges in sensory pleasures, greed, or the performance of piety for public donations.</summary>
<keywords>Charpat Nath, Avadhut, Maya, Kanphata, hypocrisy, breath control, Ai Panth, Gorakhnath, renunciation</keywords>

--- Page 157 ---
<meta>This page concludes the Shabadis (sacred utterances) of the Nath yogi Charpat. Following the previous page's critique of the external trappings of wandering ascetics, these final verses shift focus toward a critique of institutional religion and idol worship, emphasizing instead the "internal pilgrimage" and the divinity residing within the individual.</meta>

### Page 87

They do not know the high essence of righteousness <term>Dharma</term>, yet they chatter. They build high temples while performing false deeds. 
Charpat says: "Listen, O people! You have lost the precious jewel <term>Ratan Padarath</term> <note>Literally "gem-like substance," a common metaphor in Sant literature for the human life or the soul's potential</note> for something hollow." (56)

One stone is placed beneath the feet, while another stone is the object of devotion. 
Charpat reveals the secret of such people: Why is this one just a stone, and that one a god? <note>Charpat points out the logical inconsistency of worshipping one rock while walking upon another of the same material.</note> (57)

By worshipping and worshipping stones <term>bhatha</term>, the whole world has come to ruin. The true essence <term>nij-tattva</term> has been forgotten. 
The Form of Light <term>Jyoti-swarup</term> is right there with you; reflect upon that instead. (58)

If you must worship, then worship the God within the Self <term>Atma-dev</term>. If you must make an offering, let it be the leaf of the Beginningless <note>An "eternal leaf" suggests a mental or spiritual offering rather than a physical plant</note>. 
Charpat says: "Do not wander and die. Every single body is a place of pilgrimage <term>Tirath</term>." (59)

->॥ Thus conclude the Sacred Utterances of Charpatji ॥<-

<summary>This final section of Charpat Nath’s verses rejects external idol worship and temple rituals in favor of finding the divine "Light" within the human body, concluding that the true pilgrimage is internal.</summary>
<keywords>Charpat Nath, Nath Siddhas, idol worship critique, Atma-dev, internal pilgrimage, Dharma, Sant Bhasha</keywords>

--- Page 158 ---
<meta>Following the conclusion of Charpat Nath’s verses on the previous page, this section introduces the "Shabadis" (sacred utterances) of Gopichand. While the previous page focused on internalizing divinity, these verses present a dramatic dialogue between King Gopichand and his mother, Mainavati, who famously urged her son to renounce his kingdom and seek immortality through yoga to avoid the cycle of death.</meta>

# ॥ Now begin the Sacred Utterances of Gopichand ॥

Renounce the kingdom, my son; renounce the throne. Renounce the elephants and the horses.
Mother Mainavati speaks the absolute truth: "O son, life is short in this Dark Age <term>Kali Yuga</term> <note>original: "kali"; refers to the current age of spiritual decline where life is fleeting</note>." ॥ 1 ॥

"In the King's house, there were queens, Mother; you were my mother, dear.
You used to sit within the golden mansions <note>original: "savnai bhon"; likely refers to golden or radiant apartments</note>, Mother; from where did you bring this wisdom?" ॥ 2 ॥

"Our Guru is called Gorakh; Charpat is my brother-disciple <term>Gurubhai</term> <note>original: "gurubhai"; a fellow disciple of the same spiritual teacher</note>.
Guru Gorakhnath gave me a single Word <term>Sabda</term> <note>original: "sabad"; a sacred sound or teaching that initiates transformation</note>; that is what I have brought to you, Mother Mainavati." ॥ 3 ॥

"Among sixteen hundred queens, I enjoyed the gardens; I was a great enjoyer of the land of Bengal.
Let me rule for twelve years; after that, I will become a yogi <term>Jogi</term>." ॥ 4 ॥

"Thinking of 'today, today' and 'tomorrow, tomorrow,' O son, the body wastes away like a distiller's furnace.
Mother Mainavati speaks the absolute truth: "O son, this body will burn and become the soil of the cremation ground <term>Masan</term>." ॥ 5 ॥

"There will be no yoga, my son, and there will be no enjoyment; this body, like a bubble on the water <note>original: "jal vinba"; a common metaphor for the ephemeral nature of physical life</note>, will not endure.
Mother Mainavati speaks the absolute truth: "O son, do not be lost in the illusions of the earth." ॥ 6 ॥

"You will die; you will surely die. Then you will become the ashes of the cremation ground.
If you would only recognize the Supreme Essence <term>Param Tat</term> <note>original: "param tat"; the ultimate spiritual reality or truth</note>, my son, you would cross over the ocean of worldly existence <term>Sansar</term>." ॥ 7 ॥

<summary>This page records a dialogue between King Gopichand and his mother, Queen Mainavati, where she urges him to renounce his royal luxuries for the path of yoga, warning him of the inevitability of death. It establishes the spiritual lineage of Gorakhnath and Charpat as the source of her wisdom.</summary>
<keywords>Gopichand, Mainavati, Gorakhnath, Charpat, renunciation, Kali Yuga, immortality, Bengal, cremation ground, Supreme Essence</keywords>

--- Page 159 ---
<meta>Continuing from the previous page, where King Gopichand expressed his hesitation to leave the comforts of his palace and his sixteen hundred queens, this page captures the pivotal moment of his transformation. He questions the physical loss of his status, and his mother, Mainavati, counters with spiritual metaphors. The text then shifts from their dialogue to a narrative description of his initiation by the Guru Jalandhar Nath and his ultimate realization regarding the nature of the mind and the vital essence.</meta>

"Who will make me forget my hunger for rice? Who will wash my feet?
Where shall I find my multi-storied palaces? Where will you be, Mother Mainavati?" ॥ 8 ॥

"The Invisible Lord <term>Alakh</term> <note>original: "alash"; the Unseen or Formless Divine, a primary name for God in the Nath tradition</note> will make you forget your hunger; the holy Ganges will wash your feet.
The wild trees and bushes shall be your multi-storied palaces, and in every house where you beg, you shall find a Mother Mainavati." ॥ 9 ॥

Following his mother’s instruction, he abandoned the land of Bengal <note>In medieval Indian lore, Bengal was often depicted as a land of magic and powerful women where kings were easily distracted from their spiritual paths.</note>.
Gopichand took refuge at the Guru’s feet, and in that meeting, he turned away the time of death. ॥ 10 ॥

He left the throne and the kingdom; he even left the water <note>This likely refers to the ritual comforts or perhaps specific royal territories.</note>; he abandoned all pleasures and luxuries.
Gopichand settled his mind in the "Slow House" <note>original: "dhila ghar"; likely a metaphor for a state of deep meditative stillness or the inner body</note> through the Word <term>Sabda</term>, and leaving everything behind, he went to dwell in the forest. ॥ 11 ॥

The queens and all the young maidens broke into cries of "Alas! Alas!"
The chieftains, the horses, and the elephants were all in an uproar: "Where has King Gopichand gone?" ॥ 12 ॥

The master Jalandhari Pa <note>Jalandhar Nath, one of the nine immortal masters (Naths) and the guru of Gopichand.</note> placed a staff in his hand and initiated Gopichand into the path.
Within the very temple, palace, and gates where he once ruled, he now raised the cry of "Alakh" <note>The traditional salutation of Nath yogis, meaning "The Invisible."</note>. ॥ 13 ॥

Addressing them as "Mother" and "Sister," he begged for alms. He blew the stag-horn <term>Singi</term> and sounded the holy whistle <term>Nand</term>.
When the Queen met him, a great dialogue <term>Samvad</term> took place between them. ॥ 14 ॥

"The Mind is the King, the Mind is the Subject. The Mind is the bond that holds all together.
He who has understood the Mind has reached the further shore," thus says King Gopichand. ॥ 15 ॥

There is nothing to grasp and nothing to see; it is a state beyond the moon and the sun <note>In yoga, the "moon" and "sun" represent the two main energy channels (ida and pingala) in the body.</note>.
Like a reflection in water or a shadow in a mirror—such is the unthinkable state that Gopichand has attained. ॥ 16 ॥

"I have found it! Oh, I have truly found it!"—a state that does not depend on any external thing.
That which was formless began to be seen, and the body itself attained realization. ॥ 17 ॥

"When the mind is stilled, the breath is stilled. When the breath is stilled, the vital essence <term>Bindu</term> <note>original: "bind"; literally "drop" or "semen," referring to the vital spiritual energy that yogis seek to preserve for immortality.</note> is stilled.
When the essence is stilled, the living spirit is stilled," thus speaks Gopichand. ॥ 18 ॥

"When the mind wanders, the breath wanders. When the breath wanders, the essence wanders.
When the essence wanders, the body <term>Kandha</term> falls," thus speaks Gopichand. ॥ 19 ॥

<summary>This page concludes the dialogue between Gopichand and his mother, detailing his renunciation of the throne of Bengal and his initiation into the Nath yoga tradition. It concludes with profound philosophical verses on the interconnectedness of the mind, breath, and vital energy (Bindu) as the keys to immortality.</summary>
<keywords>Gopichand, Mainavati, Jalandhari Pa, Bengal, Yoga, Siddha, Renunciation, Forest-dwelling, Alakh, Dialogue, Bindu (Vital Essence)</keywords>

--- Page 160 ---
<meta>Continuing from the previous page, where King Gopichand left his palace and queens to follow the path of a wandering ascetic, this page transitions into the "Sabda" or spiritual sayings of his master, Jalandhari Nath. The dialogue shifts from Gopichand’s worldly anxieties to the specific yogic teachings required to overcome the physical and mental obstacles of the spiritual path.</meta>

# Now Begins the Spiritual Sayings of Jalandhari Pa

> In the circle of the Void <note>original: "Sunya Mandala"; a state of pure, thoughtless consciousness often described as a "circle" in the crown of the head</note>, the mind finds its dwelling. There, the supreme light of wisdom shines.
> The seeker asks of themselves and answers from within. When the True Guru is met, one attains the Supreme State <term>Paramapada</term> <note>The highest spiritual realization where the soul merges with the divine</note>. ॥ 1 ॥

> A strange thing has occurred: a well has been breathed into a pitcher <note>A classic metaphor in "twilight language" (Sandhya Bhasha) where the infinite divine (the well) is contained within the finite human body (the pitcher)</note>.
> But if one does not reach that source, the world remains thirsty and people continue to die in ignorance. ॥ 2 ॥

> Cast away the net of desires and attachments. Stop the mind from wandering outward.
> Keep the company of those who have achieved mastery and those who strive for it. Contemplate the wisdom of the Guru's words. ॥ 3 ॥

> Earth, sky, wind, and water—understand the Sun and Moon and the six systems of philosophy <note>The "Darshanas" or traditional schools of Indian thought</note>.
> One who understands the secret of the sacred sound *Om* <term>Om-kar</term> is a perfected one <term>Siddha</term>, becoming invisible and infinite. ॥ 4 ॥

> Gopichand says: "O Master! If I stay in the village, lust <term>Kandarpa</term> <note>named after the god of desire</note> takes hold of me.
> If I go to the jungle, hunger torments me. If I remain still, the illusion of the world <term>Maya</term> affects me.
> If I walk the path, my body wears away. Even sweet sugar brings disease. Tell me, how can I successfully practice Yoga?" ॥ 5 ॥

> "Listen, seeker <term>Avadhuta</term> <note>A term for a liberated soul who has 'shaken off' worldly ties</note>, to the essence of contemplation. This is the crown and core of all things:
> Control your diet, and lust will not affect you. Feed upon the vital breath <note>original: "bai ahar"; literally 'eating the air,' referring to advanced breath control</note>, and hunger will not torment you.
> Through the perfected posture <term>Siddha Asana</term>, the illusion of the world will not touch you. By focusing on the internal sound <term>Nada</term>, the body will not waste away.
> Do not indulge the tastes of the tongue; master the mind and the breath <term>Pavana</term> to practice Yoga." ॥ 6 ॥

> Control your senses with courage, O seeker! Restrain the breath and steady the body.
> Hold the soul firm in the battlefield of the heart. Through contemplation, abandon the illusions of the world. ॥ 7 ॥

> The truth of the perfected ones <term>Siddhas</term> is that they have crossed over. Such a Yogi never dies and never takes birth again.
> They are absorbed in the Void where no lute <note>original: "vina"</note> is played, yet music is heard. There, they are merged with the Invisible Lord <term>Alakh Purusha</term>. ॥ 8 ॥

> Those who neither practice Yoga nor enjoy the world are merely the "walking dead."
> Like donkeys in the village or pigs in the forest, they take birth over and over again in the cycle of suffering. ॥ 9 ॥

<summary>In this passage, Guru Jalandhari Nath provides King Gopichand with practical yogic instructions on how to overcome physical hunger, lust, and the decay of the body through breath control and internal focus. The text emphasizes that spiritual realization (Alakh) frees a person from the cycle of reincarnation, contrasting the enlightened Yogi with those who live aimlessly.</summary>

<keywords>Jalandhari Nath, Gopichand, Yoga, Maya, Alakh, Sunya, Nada, rebirth, breath control, asceticism</keywords>

--- Page 161 ---
<meta>Continuing from the previous page, Jalandhari Nath responds to King Gopichand’s concerns about the difficulties of the ascetic life. He moves from addressing the King's personal fears to explaining the universal law of Karma and the internal yogic practices required to achieve liberation.</meta>

In this existence, we have found a field to till. What is sown now is what will bear fruit in the future. This world is a garden of *Karma* <note>the law of cause and effect where every action determines future circumstances</note>. This cycle continues as long as the worldly soul remains bound by its own desires and worldly power. ॥ 10 ॥

What was committed in the past is what one must endure now. What is done in the present will be reaped in the days to come. As you give, so shall you receive; therefore, one should treat this physical body as the fertile soil for spiritual labor. ॥ 11 ॥

Chanting the *Ajapa* <term>Ajapa-japa</term> <note>the 'unspoken chant'; a meditative practice where one focuses on the natural sound of the breath (So-ham), which repeats itself without conscious effort</note>, performing penance without the need for external fire, capturing the inner resonance <term>Nada</term>, and holding firm in meditation. Upheld by Yoga and striking down the roots of sin—such is this wondrous, mystical knowledge <term>Gyan</term>. ॥ 12 ॥

->॥ Thus ends the *Sabadi* <note>the sacred verses or spiritual sayings</note> of the venerable Jalandhari Pa ॥<-

<summary>This page concludes the teachings of the Guru Jalandhari Nath, framing the human life and body as a "field of karma" where spiritual practices like the unspoken breath-chant (Ajapa) lead to liberation.</summary>
<keywords>Jalandhari Nath, Karma, Ajapa-japa, Yoga, Sabadi, spiritual knowledge, cause and effect, meditation</keywords>
